<!-- page 7 -->
Introduction
In 2024, my office continued to stand up consistently for 
the fundamental rights of citizens in the areas of data 
protection and freedom of information. Many new pro-
jects were launched and many were successfully com-
pleted. In this Activity Report, I explain my activities and 
those of my predecessor during the reporting period.
I would like to start by describing to you what I would 
like to do differently, what my vision is, what data pro-
tection needs to do differently in order to have a future. 
Data protection is not an obstacle, but an opportunity 
and an advantage of our location because it guaran-
tees trust. Data protection means self-determination, 
personal responsibility and freedom for all citizens. Data 
protection law offers scope for action and organisation 
to achieve progress, participation, value creation for 
society as a whole and internal and external security.
Modern data protection does not stand in the way of 
digital development with benefits for all citizens, but 
rather supports it.
Rest assured: I am not opposed to digital transformation, 
but I want to support it constructively through my office, 
while upholding protection of the right to informational 
self-determination. I see data protection and freedom of 
information as cornerstones for safeguarding European 
values. I want to pave the way for a digital transformati-
on that is sensitive to fundamental rights.
To this end, I have set myself one strategic and three 
content-related objectives:
Strategically, I would like to enter into dialogue with 
society and legislators, research and industry at an even 
earlier stage and in more detail. I want to know as early 
as possible where the data protection challenges of di-
gitalisation projects lie so that I can offer solutions right 
from the start. I want to listen, clarify, take part and help 
shape processes instead of being restricted to the role of 
a “naysayer”. That was never the purpose of my office, 
even if some people have seen it
as that. It is our shared responsibility to offer regulatory 
solutions for research and development, innovation and 
value creation that are in line with fundamental rights.
My term of office is an invitation to society, legislatu-
res, research and industry to engage in dialogue. I want 
to work with you to develop constructive solutions for 
trustworthy digital innovations. I appeal to all stakehol-
ders to involve us actively in legislation and the design of 
digitalisation processes at an early stage. Where coope-
ration is already working well, we should continue along 
this common path. Where there are problems, I want to 
offer a fresh start.
In terms of content, I would like to devote particular 
attention to three areas during my term of office: health, 
artificial intelligence and security.
The most sensitive personal data is processed in the 
healthcare sector. In my view, it is therefore essential to 
consider data protection from the outset. This applies, 
for example, to access to research data, which must 
be productive but also data protection-compliant and 
trustworthy. In the healthcare sector in particular, data 
protection and data usability are still regarded as op-
posites to a large extent. I am firmly convinced that the 
success of research data access, digital health applica-
tions and electronic patient records depends on a high 
level of data protection and IT security standards. The 
fundamental rights of data subjects must be protected 
but that must not prevent the systems from functional 
at a high level at the same time. These developments are 
a key component in understanding and treating disea-
ses better and faster. I would therefore like to see more 
interaction and better mutual understanding in the he-
althcare sector in relation to user-friendliness on the one 
hand and the requirements of fundamental rights on the 
other to ensure that supervision of the Federal Police is 
carried out in accordance with European law. My office 
lacks effective remedial powers to enforce the law firmly 
in this area. In the field of AI, I recommend the creation 
of a legal basis for training AI systems.
1
33rd Activity Report 20247


<!-- page 8 -->
In all legislative measures, legislators should always 
bear in mind that data usability and data protection are 
not fundamentally at odds with each other. The GDPR in 
particular has never set out to prevent data processing, 
but instead represents a careful way to balance out fun-
damental rights. It defines the basic rules of the game by 
which market participants must abide. In a world that is 
changing rapidly in terms of technology, where the soci-
al and economic understanding of freedom and self-de-
termination is increasingly diverging, digital monopolies 
and oligopolies are maturing, initial signs of increasing 
de-globalisation are becoming apparent and Germany’s 
internal and external security is at risk, it is important to 
defend the European concept of freedom and put it into 
practice.
As you will see from this Activity Report, my office once 
again took on a wide range of tasks last year in order to 
protect the fundamental rights of citizens and defend 
our freedom. This is only possible thanks to the tireless 
and dedicated support of my staff, who now number 
around 370. I would like to thank them all very much for 
their dependable, motivating and always constructive 
collaboration.
Prof. Louisa Specht-Riemenschneider
33rd Activity Report 2024 8


<!-- page 9 -->
Recommendations2
2.1 Summary of the recommenda -
tions of the 33rd Activity Report
The Activity Report largely relates to the period before 
I took office on 3 September 2024 and therefore also 
includes recommendations that were made before 
my term of office. Other recommendations have been 
developed significantly since 3 September 2024, such as 
the recommendations on the involvement of my office in 
the implementation of the AI Regulation. In some cases, 
however, my recommendations also go beyond the work 
at specialist level and relate to aspects of future develop-
ment. In summary, I would like to make the following 
recommendations:
 → I recommend that the legislature establish a national 
AI supervisory structure as stipulated in the AI Regu-
lation of the EU as soon as possible and in doing so 
make the best possible use of the expertise available 
from my office. This is the only way to prepare for 
the complex tasks associated with AI supervision and 
to ensure that the necessary structures are establis-
hed (see No. 3.2.1).
 → I recommend that the legislature create a legal basis 
for training AI in order to ensure adequate legal 
certainty for this important future technology (see 
No. 1).
 → As already mentioned in my 32nd Activity Report, I 
recommend the introduction of remedial powers in 
the area of the intelligence services (see No. 3.3.1).
 → I recommend revisiting at least the following legisla-
tive proposals, for which the data protection provi-
sions still require tightening up: the Employee Data 
Act, the Research Data Act, implementation of the 
various EU legislative acts with data protection and 
data privacy
 → implications, the Federal Data Protection Act (BDSG) 
amendment and the Security Clearance Act (SÜG) 
amendment (see No. 5.2.1; 5.3.1; 5.4.2 and 5.5.1).
 → I recommend merging the Freedom of Information 
Act and the Environmental Information Act. In addi-
tion, I recommend further development of a Federal 
Transparency Act with proactive publication obliga-
tions and powers of instruction and enforcement for 
the Freedom of Information Commissioner to allow 
action in the event of a conflict (see No. 6.2.2).
 → I continue to recommend that the federal govern-
ment and legislators avoid creating parallel systems 
for cross-divisional data exchange and implement 
the once-only principle.
 → In view of the existing foundation of the Identificati-
on Number Act (IDNrG), the Act to Promote Elec-
tronic Government (EGovG), the Online Access Act 
(OZG) and the National Once-Only Technical System 
(NOOTS) as a central infrastructure, a standardised 
approach should be pursued in which constructive 
solutions are developed for existing data protection 
problems (see No. 7.2.1).
 → I continue to recommend that the federal govern-
ment take account of the German Bundestag’s broad 
understanding of transparency in the context of ad-
ministrative digitalisation (see Committee Document 
20(4)258 of 19 July 2023) and also ensure that initial 
storage of the ID number in a register in accordance 
with
 → Section 2 No. 1 IDNrG is made visible in the data 
protection cockpit. In addition, the necessary pre-
parations should be made at an early stage to make 
the data transmissions of the registration offices that 
(still) bear the tax number transparent as soon as the 
change is made to the ID number (see No. 7.2.1).
 → I recommend legislation for an immediate obligation 
to report security vulnerabilities to manufacturers 
with the aim of remedying them immediately or to 
establish a central coordinating body to strengthen 
IT security in Germany (see No. 7.2.4).
33rd Activity Report 20249


<!-- page 10 -->
 → I recommend swift finalisation of the amendment to 
the Airline Passenger Data Act (FlugDaG), taking into 
account the requirements of the ECJ (see No. 7.4.3).
 → For data protection security and the efficiency of the 
work of the police authorities, I recommend creating 
clear legal regulations for the large-scale Police 20/20 
(P 20) IT project (see No. 7.4.4).
 → I recommend that the German Bundestag urge the 
federal government and EU legislators to revise the 
draft regulation on chat control significantly to bring 
it in line with fundamental rights in accordance with 
the EP report of November 2023, which guarantees 
end-to-end encryption, excludes interception of 
messages on the end device (client-side scanning), 
complies with fundamental German and European 
(communication) rights and prohibits blanket and 
indiscriminate interception of private communica-
tions, or otherwise to work towards rejection of the 
draft regulation as a whole (see No. 7.3.8).
 → I recommend discussion of a reform of the Anti-Ter-
ror Filing System (ATD) and the Right-Wing Extre-
mism Filing System (RED) in consultation with my 
office and all the authorities involved (see No. 8.1.3 
- Inspection of the ATD and RED).
 → I maintain my call for the creation of a fundamental 
legal basis for the Military Intelligence Service. For 
the Bundeswehr to be able to act with legal certain-
ty, it is necessary to regulate the necessary powers 
under democratically legitimate legislation (see No. 
8.1.7).
2.2 Recommendations of the 32nd Activity Report
Recommendations of the 32nd Activity Report Status of implementation
The electronic health card (eHC) will become 
even more important as a result of the Digital Act 
passed in December 2023, because simply being 
in possession of an eHC in a medical practice 
will allow access to electronic patient records. I 
recommend that the federal government imple -
ment a regulation ensuring that eHCs are deliver -
ed securely and in person (32nd AR No. 3.1.3).
No provision has been made for eHCs to be delivered in person 
only or for eHC holders to be identified retrospectively.
I recommend that the legislature determine the 
national AI supervisory structure resulting from 
the EU AI Regulation as soon as possible and 
make the best possible use of the expertise avai-
lable in my office. This is the only way to prepare 
for the complex tasks associated with AI supervi-
sion and to ensure that the necessary resources 
are in place before the regulation comes into 
effect (32nd AR No. 3.2.1).
The AI Regulation came into force on 1 August 2024. Although 
Member States have until 2 August 2025 to designate the 
national authorities responsible for implementing the AI Re -
gulation, certain provisions of the AI Regulation, in particular 
on prohibited AI practices, will already apply directly from 2 
February 2025.  
Preparing for the new tasks and building up the necessary 
resources also requires lead time. The recommendation to 
establish a national AI supervisory structure in the near future 
is therefore all the more urgent.
I recommend that the German Bundestag urge 
the federal government and the EU legislature 
to make major changes to the draft regulation 
on chat control in line with fundamental rights 
in accordance with the EP report of November 
2023, which guarantees end-to-end encryption, 
respects German and European fundamental 
(communication) rights and prohibits the blan-
ket and indiscriminate interception of private 
communications, or otherwise to work towards 
rejecting the draft regulation as a whole (32nd AR 
No. 3.2.4).
The previous recommendation continues to apply. Although 
the German government has still not given its approval to the 
draft regulation in the EU Council, the EU Council regularly 
submits new drafts, which to date have only taken a few of my 
points of criticism into account. As long as any of the issued re -
main unresolved, I stand by my recommendation: continuous 
end-to-end encryption must be guaranteed, fundamental Ger -
man and European (communication) rights must be respected 
and the blanket and indiscriminate interception of private 
communications must be rejected.
33rd Activity Report 2024 10


<!-- page 11 -->
Recommendations of the 32nd Activity Report Status of implementation
I recommend developing a coherent overall con -
cept for all security clearance procedures, instead 
of continuing to fragment the security clearance 
law into individual provisions. This requires, in 
particular, a redefinition and extension of secu-
rity-sensitive activities. In this way, it is possible 
to address legitimate security interests while 
simultaneously safeguarding the rights of those 
subject to clearance and associated persons from 
pre-emptive and redundant screening processes 
(32nd AR No. 3.3.5).
A draft bill to amend the Security Clearance Act has been 
submitted. However, no coherent overall concept for all clea-
rance procedures has been developed as part of the legislative 
process.
The reformed eIDAS Regulation allows for flexi -
bility in shaping the national, European Digital 
Identity (EUDI) Wallet. I recommend that the fe -
deral government use this to become a pioneer in 
Europe with a Wallet infrastructure that protects 
against over-identification and uses the benefits 
of digitalisation to minimise data. (32nd AR No. 
3.4.1).
Although individual decisions have been taken on the German 
EUDI Wallet, it is not possible to make an assessment of the 
overall picture without a national legal basis and decisions on 
the design of the front end for citizens.
I recommend that the legislature set clear res -
trictions in sector-specific regulations, particu-
larly with regard to the purpose and duration 
of further electronic processing of data that has 
been read from the chip of a passport or identity 
card by police and administrative authorities. The 
legislature should only authorise public bodies to 
access the biometric photo in the chip of a pas -
sport, identity card or electronic residence permit 
if it is absolutely necessary for the fulfilment of 
particularly important tasks in the public interest 
and alternative, less intrusive procedures are not 
available (32nd AR No. 3.4.2).
The legislature has not yet taken any further steps in this 
regard. Possible legislation is therefore still pending.
I recommend that the federal government push 
for improvements to the European Commission‘s 
proposal for a regulation for establishing additio -
nal procedural rules relating to the enforcement 
of the GDPR (COM(2023) 348 final), in particu-
lar through binding requirements (including 
deadlines) for the lead supervisory authority to 
expedite complaint processing in cross-border 
cases (32nd AR No. 4.2.4).
During the legislative process, the federal government 
contributed to the inclusion of significant improvements in 
the Council‘s general approach and regularly liaised closely 
with me in this regard. The procedure is now in the trialogue 
process.
I recommend that the federal government 
promptly submit a draft of comprehensive speci-
fic legal regulations on employee data protection, 
for example, on the use of AI in the employment 
context, on limits on monitoring behaviour and 
performance, and on the handling of sensitive 
employee data. This should also cover the appli-
cation and selection process (32nd AR No. 5.2).
The federal government has continued its work on an Em-
ployee Data Protection Act and has submitted a draft bill to 
strengthen fair handling of employee data and create more 
legal certainty for employers and employees in the digital 
world of work (Employee Data Protection Act - BeschDG). Ho -
wever, the legislative procedure was not completed in the 20th 
legislative period.
2.2 Recommendations of the 32nd Activity Report
33rd Activity Report 202411


<!-- page 12 -->
Recommendations of the 32nd Activity Report Status of implementation
I recommend that the German Bundestag ad -
vocate for an amendment to the Telecommunica-
tions-Telemedia Data Protection Act (TTDSG) that 
would enhance my enforcement powers, in parti-
cular by adding an obligation similar to Article 27 
GDPR to appoint a representative in Germany to 
the TTDSG and by adding provisions for enforce -
ment against branches of service providers in 
Germany either directly or indirectly through the 
federal government (32nd. AR No. 5.5).
The recommendation applies to the TDDDG accordingly and is 
becoming increasingly urgent, as many interpersonal commu-
nication services not dependent on a number (such as mes -
senger services) do not have a registered office in Germany, in 
some cases not even in the EU.
I recommend that the legislator promptly 
evaluate the decision to extend the use of video 
conferencing for oral hearings in civil proceedings 
and various other specialised jurisdictions and, 
if necessary, provide for statutory exceptions, at 
least with regard to hearings on particularly sen-
sitive data in accordance with Article 9(1) GDPR. 
In addition to video conferencing, this applies in 
particular to their possible recording for minu-
te-taking purposes (32nd AR No. 5.8).
The legislature has taken my advice on board and provided for 
corresponding discretionary considerations in the explanatory 
memorandum to the law, which can offer important guidance 
to judges deciding on the organisation of video conferences 
and their recording for minute-taking purposes in order to pro -
tect the privacy and data protection rights of the parties invol-
ved. However, a more extensive evaluation of the regulations 
and any additions to statutory exceptions are still pending.
In addition, there is still no conclusive regulation 
on secure electronic identification of the parties 
participating in videoconferencing in judicial 
proceedings. I therefore recommend that the 
legislature promptly closes the existing regula-
tory loophole and abandon the current plans 
for a video identification procedure. This poses 
severe risks and may not be used in proceedings 
requiring a very high level of protection (32nd AR 
No. 5.8).
The legislature has taken my advice on board. It is to be taken 
into account as part of the ongoing consideration of the intro -
duction of a so-called “justice cloud” . This is part of the federal 
government’s digital strategy.
I recommend consolidating the Freedom of 
Information Act and the Environmental Informa-
tion Act. In addition, I recommend the further 
development of a Federal Transparency Act that 
includes proactive publication obligations and 
provides the freedom of information officer with 
the power to issue and enforce orders, ensuring 
action can be taken in cases of conflict (32nd AR 
No. 6.1.1).
As part of the BfDI symposium on freedom of information in 
autumn 2023, the BMI reported on the plans and considera-
tions for a Federal Transparency Act. The federal government 
did not submit a draft bill in the 20th legislative period. I conti-
nue to recommend developing the Freedom of Information Act 
into a Transparency Act in the 21st legislative period.
Where the use of complex data analysis methods 
by the police and intelligence services is deemed 
necessary, I recommend that the federal govern-
ment establish clear legal bases and suitable con-
ditions for this purpose (see also the resolution of 
the German Data Protection Conference (DSK) of 
11 May 2023) (32nd AR No. 7.1).
This recommendation has not yet been implemented in the 
area of the intelligence services.
In February 2023, the Federal Constitutional Court (BVerfG) 
ruled on the legal requirements for automated police data 
analyses and established generally applicable standards. In 
principle, these also apply to the intelligence services. To date, 
there are no specific legal authorisations for such data proces -
sing in intelligence law.
2.2 Recommendations of the 32nd Activity Report
33rd Activity Report 2024 12


<!-- page 13 -->
Recommendations of the 32nd Activity Report Status of implementation
I recommend that the second part of the intel -
ligence law reform planned for 2024 include 
precise provisions for data collection from the 
internet and its further processing by the services 
(32nd AR No. 7.3).
The federal government did not present a draft bill in the 20th 
legislative period. A special, standardised legal basis should be 
created to reflect the extent of the encroachment.
I recommend that the legislature revise the Pas -
senger Data Act in light of the CJEU‘s decision. It 
is important to provide the citizens, administrati -
ve bodies and courts with clear regulations (32nd 
AR No. 7.6).
Although the federal government has initiated the required 
amendment to the Airline Passenger Data Act, it has not yet 
finalised it.
I recommend that the legislature also introduce 
remedial powers in the area of intelligence ser -
vices (32nd AR No. 7.9).
My recommendation was not taken up.
Under current law, the lawfulness of data processing by intel -
ligence services can only be reviewed by the courts in special 
cases. With the introduction of powers to issue injunctions, 
the legislature would create the possibility of submitting data 
processing for judicial clarification.
I recommend that the legislature create clear and 
comprehensive regulations governing coopera -
tion between the supervisory bodies over the 
intelligence services (32nd AR No. 7.12).
If and because the supervision of the intelligence services is 
distributed among various institutions, these must have a 
clear and comprehensive legal basis for sharing information 
about their activities in order to ensure seamless and effecti -
ve control. This is required in terms of the “equality of arms” 
vis-à-vis the supervised bodies, which could otherwise always 
claim that a matter falls under the jurisdiction of another 
supervisory authority.
Where administrative services are offered electro -
nically, the responsible authorities should adopt 
a phased procedure and first examine whether 
the service can also be accessed without requi -
ring a user account. If a user account is required, 
it should be further assessed whether basic 
registration without authentication using the eID 
of the ID card is sufficient.
I recommend that the BMI and the BMF not 
extend the deadline for using ELSTER software 
certificates as identification in the BundID and 
the organisational account. The development of 
suitable, sufficiently secure means of identifica -
tion should be accelerated for the organisational 
account (32nd AR No. 8.1).
In the parliamentary process, the legislature has permanently 
authorised the use of Elster software certificates as a means of 
identification, even for administrative services that require a 
“substantial” level of trust. As a result, the Elster software cer -
tificates must be developed further so that they consistently 
and substantially fulfil the European requirements for means 
of identification at that security level.
I recommend that the legislature draft the on -
ce-only general clause (draft of Section 5 of the 
E-Government Act (EGovG) in a way that ensures 
that any sharing of evidence based on it must be 
displayed in the data protection cockpit when the 
ID number is used (32nd AR No. 8.2).
The recommendation was implemented in full in Section 5(3) 
EGovG. The legislature went even further and included not 
only sharing of information based on the ID number, but all 
instances of it, regardless of whether an identifier is used and 
if so, which one.
The legislature has not taken up the idea that connection to 
the data protection cockpit should be a prerequisite for the 
lawfulness of sharing information in this way.
2.2 Recommendations of the 32nd Activity Report
33rd Activity Report 202413


<!-- page 14 -->
Recommendations of the 32nd Activity Report Status of implementation
I recommend considering both the retrieval of 
data by the Federal Office of Administration (BVA) 
from the Federal Central Tax Office (BZSt) and the 
initial storage of data in the individual registers 
as a transmission within the meaning of Section 
9 of the Identification Number Act (IDNrG), and 
therefore, to display them in the data protection 
cockpit (32nd AR No. 8.2).
This recommendation was at least partially implemented by 
the legislature through an amendment to Section 10(2) of the 
Online Access Act (OZG). It has now been clarified that trans -
missions between BVA and BZSt on the basis of the IDNrG are 
to be made transparent in the data protection cockpit. Unfor -
tunately, there was no further legal clarification as to whether 
the cases of first-time retrieval of the ID number from the BZSt 
should also be reported in this respect. The federal govern -
ment has also still not committed itself to this citizen-friendly 
interpretation on the basis of the law already in force. The 
federal government should close one of the last transparency 
loopholes in the IDNrG.
I further recommend to all ministries and the 
legislature that, when the ID number or tax ID is 
used by entities outside of the financial admi -
nistration, at least the safeguards of the IDNrG – 
especially the data protection cockpit – should be 
implemented. The level of protection provided by 
the IDNrG may not be further undermined by the 
fact that entities that are not tax authorities are 
declared to be such by law (32nd AR No. 8.2).
Even though some of the corresponding legislative proposals 
failed (e.g. basic child allowance), a general interest is still 
apparent in permanently linking personal data outside the 
IDNrG system, in some cases using the tax ID, which may be 
legally distinct but is completely identical in terms of content. 
The legislature and the federal government should move away 
from this and speak out clearly in favour of creating a stan -
dardised once-only system that can be used effectively and 
jointly, despite the problems that still exist.
I recommend that the federal government 
advocate for a balance between freedom and 
security that protects fundamental rights in the 
data retention debate (32nd AR No. 8.3).
The ECJ has ruled that general and indiscriminate retention 
of IP addresses, limited to what is absolutely necessary, is 
not fundamentally inadmissible but is subject to stringent 
requirements. At European level, the High-Level Group (HLG) 
on access to data for effective law enforcement published a 
recommendation paper in the summer of 2024, which, among 
other things, calls for common standards across Europe for 
extensive data retention. At national level, there is an intensive 
political debate as to whether possible scope for storing IP 
addresses can be utilised and, if so, how. For my position and 
for an explanation of the requirements of the ECJ, see Nos. 
3.3.4 and 4.5.3.
In view of the imminent start of regular opera -
tions, I recommend that the federal government 
set up an independent agency to oversee the 
implant registry (32nd AR No. 8.14).
Neither the legislature nor the Federal Ministry of Health 
(BMG) has created an independent agency for the implant 
register. This remains the responsibility of the BMG.
I recommend that the legislature evaluate the 
retention periods for the tax identification feature 
pursuant to Section 139a of the German Tax Code 
(AO) (tax ID) in the database maintained by the 
Federal Central Tax Office, and to appropriately 
determine these periods, especially in light of 
the increasing use of the Tax ID in the context of 
register modernisation (32nd AR No. 9.2.3).
The legislature has not yet taken up my recommendation from 
the 32nd AR to evaluate the storage periods relating to the tax 
identification feature, in particular with a view to defining its 
increasing use in the context of register modernisation in an 
appropriate way.
2.2 Recommendations of the 32nd Activity Report
33rd Activity Report 2024 14


<!-- page 15 -->
Main topics3
3.1 Health
3.1.1 Health Research Data Centre
The Health Research Data Centre is about to go into 
operation. In the reporting year, I was involved in close 
dialogue with those responsible, not least due to vari-
ous legal changes. The focus here was particularly on 
the transparency of the data to be processed.
The Health Research Data Centre (FDZ) project, com-
prising a database managed by the Federal Institute 
for Drugs and Medical Devices (BfArM) as a registry 
with pseudonymised billing data of all legally insured 
persons, continues to take shape and is approaching a 
milestone. From spring 2025, it should be possible to 
conduct research using billing data transmitted to the 
BfArM by the health insurance funds on the basis of the 
Data Transparency Regulation.
I have already reported on the development of the FDZ 
in previous years. 1The entry into force of the Health 
Data Utilisation Act (GDNG) in March 20242 led to exten-
sive amendments to the relevant Sections 303a to 303e 
of the German Social Code Book V (SGB V). According 
to these amendments, it is no longer the person making 
the application that determines its legitimacy, but the 
purpose for which the application is made. For a positive 
application decision, that purpose must serve the com-
mon good as defined in Section 2 GDNG.
Another important legal basis for the FDZ is the Act to 
Accelerate the Digitalisation of the Healthcare System 
(Digital Act - DigiG)3, which has also been in force since 
March 2024. As a result of the DigiG, the ePA has been in 
place since the beginning of 2025 for anyone with statu-
tory health insurance who does not object.
1 31st AR No. 4.1.2
2 32nd AR No. 3.1.2
3 32nd AR No. 3.1.3
4 32nd AR No. 3.1.1
5 Github page of the BfArM on the PUF: https://github.com/FDZ-Gesundheit/Public-Use-File
This is an important change for data transmission to 
the FDZ. The European Health Data Space Regulation 
(EHDS) is also relevant.4 In future, researchers, inno-
vators and public institutions will be able to apply for the 
use of anonymous and pseudonymous health data for 
specific, legally defined purposes via a standardised Eu-
rope-wide system. The FDZ, known as the “Health Data 
Lab” (HDL) at international level, is involved in the pilot 
project to facilitate and establish a Europe-wide system 
of this sort.
As part of the pilot project, various participating data 
spaces are to be networked with each other. The aim is to 
enable researchers to search the metadata of the centres 
across national borders, request the data required using 
a standardised form and then analyse it.
In this reporting year, I again closely monitored the 
work of the BfArM and worked towards compliance 
with data protection principles through regular dialogue 
meetings. Thanks to this constructive interaction, I was 
able to ensure that the principles of confidentiality and 
data minimisation in particular were observed, without 
jeopardising the research function. A central element 
for secure provision of data for research purposes is a 
suitable anonymisation process that is specially adapted 
to the structure of the data. Using this procedure, the 
so-called Public Use File (PUF) was created this year and 
published on the BfArM website. The PUF is a first point 
of contact for researchers to check the extent to which 
their research questions can be addressed with the help 
of the data available in the FDZ. Among other things, 
researchers can create queries (scripts) and check their 
validity, as the file structure of the PUF corresponds to 
the structure of the original data.5 Given the sensitivity 
of health data, complete anonymisation of the data is 
generally required. The BfArM has been able to present 
33rd Activity Report 202415


<!-- page 16 -->
and implement a convincing anonymisation procedure 
for this purpose. More in-depth research questions can 
then be submitted by researchers in a strictly regulated 
application procedure. If the application is approved, re-
searchers can use real data that is tailored to their speci-
fic research questions and has passed the necessary data 
protection hurdles to find answers to those questions.
Technical and organisational measures have been de-
veloped in collaboration with the BfArM for this purpo-
se.
In addition to the PUF, the BfArM also operates a 
so-called statistics portal. This is a web application 
that provides highly summarised and aggregated data 
from the FDZ free of charge for researchers and other 
interested parties. Here, too, I advised the BfArM on how 
to prepare the data in order both to achieve satisfactory 
presentation and to minimise the risk of re-identification 
of the insured persons.
In addition to the BfArM, I also advised the Robert Koch 
Institute, as the operator of the trust centre, and the 
Federal Ministry of Health, as the supervisory authority 
of the BfArM.
I am confident that the FDZ will soon be facilitating be-
neficial research while safeguarding data protection. At 
the same time, there are still certain challenges and un-
resolved questions. For example, the question arises as 
to how applications from third countries with or without 
a base in the European Economic Area can be approved 
in accordance with data protection law. The particular 
challenge here is to develop a standardised, low-th-
reshold identification procedure for applicants that 
satisfies the high level of trust. I am in close dialogue 
with the BfArM regarding the development of possible 
procedural options that must meet the European data 
protection security standard.
3.1.2 Research Data Taskforce
The use of health data for research purposes remained 
at the centre of political and scientific debate in 2024.
The Research Data Taskforce (TFFD) of the DSK, co-chai-
red by my office and the Hessian Commissioner for 
Data Protection and Freedom of Information (HBDI), 
was established by the 102nd DSK in November 2021 as 
an expert committee. The aim is to enable flexible and 
timely processing of data protection issues arising at 
short notice in the field of health research and preparati-
on and joint coordination of corresponding publications 
for the DSK.
6 31st AR No. 5.1, 32nd AR No. 3.1.1
In the reporting year, the TFFD focussed in particular on 
the requirements for secondary use of genetic data for 
research purposes and worked out the data protection 
requirements that the German and European legislatu-
res have to observe when regulating processing of this 
data.
Other focal points were the new regulations of the 
Health Data Utilisation Act on cross-state collaborative 
research and the complex regulations relating to the 
responsibility of data protection supervisory authorities.
In addition, the TFFD has dealt in detail with the requi-
rements for data transfers to third countries (outside the 
EU/EEA) for scientific research purposes. International 
cooperation is playing an increasingly important role in 
scientific research. If personal data is processed in third 
countries as part of international research collabora-
tions, the requirements of Chapter V of the GDPR on the 
transfer of personal data to third countries must also be 
observed.
3.1.3 European Health Data Space
Following my reports on the data protection challen-
ges of the European Health Data Space (EHDS) in the 
31st and 32nd Activity Reports,6 the legislative process 
is now in the home straight.
The EU Commission published its proposal for the EHDS 
in May 2022. The EHDS is intended to create a legal fra-
mework for the provision and use of data in healthcare 
(primary use) and for research, innovation, policy-ma-
king and healthcare governance (secondary use) – both 
within EU Member States and across borders.
After several months of negotiations in the Council 
Working Party on Public Health and the corresponding 
consideration of the content by the European Parlia-
ment, a total of four political trialogues took place in the 
first quarter of 2024. On 22 March 2024, the Permanent 
Representatives Committee approved the final overall 
compromise text of the EHDS by a majority. The Euro-
pean Parliament adopted the still unedited text on 24 
April 2024. The Regulation, which has now been revised 
in terms of its legal language, is to come into force in 
the first quarter of 2025, when it is adopted once again 
by the Council of the European Union and the European 
Parliament.
From a data protection perspective, the result achieved 
in the trialogue shows significant improvements over the 
EU Commission’s original draft. It fulfils important re-
commendations of the European Data Protection Board 
and the European Data Protection Supervisor from their 
33rd Activity Report 2024 16


<!-- page 17 -->
joint opinion of 12 July 2022 as well as various other 
requirements set out by my office.
For example, the Member States can use an opening 
clause to define a right of objection for their citizens at 
national level for the primary use of their health data. 
Germany has introduced this in relation to the electronic 
patient file in the Digital Act.
Health insurance funds and private insurers must not be 
given access to personal electronic health data via the 
EHDS.
As far as secondary use of health data is concerned, the 
EHDS grants citizens the direct right to object to the use 
of their data.
Other systems based on national or Union law for data 
access by public bodies or private institutions with a 
public role can be retained, as can mechanisms that 
provide for secondary use on a contractual basis, such 
as the NAKO Health Study or the Medical Informatics 
Initiative.
In the area of primary and secondary use of genetic 
data and biobanks, opening clauses in the EHDS allow 
Member States to stipulate higher requirements for the 
lawfulness of processing at national level. Germany has 
done so in the Genetic Diagnostics Act and the Health 
Data Utilisation Act and standardised the requirement 
of consent from data subjects for processing of their 
genetic data.
In addition, various definitions in the EHDS have been 
brought into line with the GDPR, and the role of the data 
protection supervisory authorities has been strengt-
hened by obliging the digital health authority and the 
access point(s) to cooperate with them.
Unfortunately, however, there are also some legal data 
protection recommendations that have not found their 
way into the EHDS.
The recommendation to oblige manufacturers of elec-
tronic health record systems such as electronic patient 
records to have them certified by independent bodies 
was not taken up. Instead, the regulations from the EU 
Commission’s original draft on self-certification remain 
in place, subject to certain conditions.
Another recommendation was to exclude data from 
wellness apps from secondary use. This was also not 
followed, but the Member States may stipulate stricter 
requirements for access to data from such apps at natio-
nal level on the basis of an opening clause.
Once the EHDS enters into force with the corresponding 
implementation deadlines for the Member States, I will 
7 Circulars of 15 October 2024 and 26 November 2024, available at: www.bfdi.bund.de/rundschreiben
provide constructive support for subsequent national 
implementation and work to ensure that this important 
digitalisation project respects citizens’ right to informati-
onal self-determination.
3.1.4 The new objection-based ePA
The redesigned electronic patient file (ePA) can bring 
real benefits of digitalisation to insured persons. The 
switch to objection instead of consent obliges data 
controllers to inform all data subjects correctly and 
clearly about their rights. The new system for access 
rights makes it more difficult to hide individual diag-
noses.
In March 2024, the Act to Accelerate the Digitalisation of 
the Healthcare System (Digital Act - DigiG) was promul-
gated in the Federal Law Gazette. In my previous Activity 
Report, I reported on my advice to the federal govern-
ment and the legislature during the parliamentary pro-
cess on this law. A digitalised, central file can give insu-
red persons an overview of and control over their health 
data and potentially avoid duplicate examinations.
The law replaces the existing ePA, which insured per-
sons receive on application, with an objection-based 
ePA. Since 15 January 2025, all insured persons have 
been given an ePA by their health insurance fund unless 
they object to it. Now that this change has been agreed 
in terms of policy, it is important that insured persons 
receive correct information about their rights to object 
so that they are actually in a position to take a decision 
about objection. To this end, I have drawn the attention 
of the health insurance funds under my data protecti-
on supervision to their duty to provide information7: 
Objections to creating the ePA can be declared to the 
health insurance providers by the insured person via any 
communication channel (post, telephone, electronically, 
app-based, etc.). The information about the right to ob-
ject must not give the impression that the objection can 
only be lodged in one way, for example only online.
The law also lays the foundations for creating functions 
in the ePA that go beyond document collection. With the 
introduction of use cases that work on structured data, 
insured persons could gain additional benefits from 
digitalisation. The first of these use cases with which the 
new ePA was launched in January 2025 is digital support 
for the medication process.
The e-prescription service, i.e. the central repository for 
all e-prescriptions, is linked to the ePA and data on pre-
scriptions issued is automatically copied to the ePA. This 
is then turned into a medication list. For insured persons 
who are entitled to a medication plan in accordance with 
33rd Activity Report 202417


<!-- page 18 -->
Section 31a(1) sentence 1 SGB V , this can be kept in elec-
tronic form in the ePA and linked to the medication list. 
Data subjects may find it surprising that a prescription 
that they delete from the e-prescription service because 
they do not want to redeem it, for example, remains in 
the ePA. It cannot be erased individually there either. 
The user guidance should therefore be designed in 
such a way that insured persons understand the conse-
quences of their actions in every situation. gematik is 
planning to define further use cases from 2026, such as 
digital support for processing results from laboratory 
tests.
More medical practices and pharmacies will automati-
cally have access to the objection-based ePA from 2025.

33rd Activity Report 2024 18


<!-- page 19 -->
At the same time, data is added to the ePA without the 
active consent of the insured person. This means that, 
by default, a large group of people may have access to 
sensitive data. There are limited setting options to cont-
rol access. Moreover, they generally require a high level 
of digital skill and active involvement on the part of the 
insured person. As soon as the data is processed in a use 
case, it can no longer be hidden individually. An indi-
vidual prescription, for example, from which a poten-
tially stigmatising diagnosis could be deduced, cannot 
be hidden on the medication list. In order to conceal 
this diagnosis, the data subject must entirely forego the 
benefits of digital support for the medication process. 
It would therefore be preferable if potentially stigmati-
sing diagnoses were not entered in the electronic health 
record without the active consent of the insured person.
In principle, insured persons have the right to object 
to the creation of an ePA at any time; any existing ePA 
will then be deleted. Insured persons can also object 
to individual service providers accessing the ePA. They 
can also object to participation in the specific use cases. 
The first use case to be implemented is support for 
the medication process using the medication list. Data 
subjects can also only object to certain service providers 
processing data from the use cases – but only via the ePA 
app. From July 2025, the ePA must be able to transfer 
pseudonymised data to the Research Data Centre. This 
function is also activated by default and insured persons 
can object to it.
The health insurance funds have to set up ombudsper-
son offices to support insured persons with concerns 
about the ePA. Logs of use/access to the ePA can also be 
requested from them and objections to the ePA or indi-
vidual functions can be submitted. This is an important 
step for insured persons who do not use their own ePA 
app. It is not possible to view the contents of the ePA, set 
the (detailed) access rights to individual files or object to 
the access of a specific service provider to a use case in 
the ombudsperson office.
I still see room for improvement in the setting options, 
especially for less digitally aware people who do not have 
their own app.

7.1.1 Authentication options for those with statutory 
insurance
3.2 Artificial intelligence
3.2.1 AI Regulation
The EU Regulation laying down harmonised rules for 
artificial intelligence (AI Regulation) came into force 
on 1 August. The AI Regulation establishes compre-
hensive rules for AI systems that are intended to 
ensure compliance with fundamental rights while still 
encouraging innovation. Many of the requirements 
for high-risk AI systems are closely related to data 
protection law . The involvement of the data protection 
supervisory authorities in the supervisory activities of 
the Federal Network Agency has not yet been finalised.
I welcome the AI Regulation as an important cornersto-
ne of the protection of fundamental rights and freedoms 
and for promotion of innovation. Many of the requi-
rements from the joint opinion of the European Data 
Protection Board (EDPB) and the European Data Protec-
tion Supervisor in 2021 have been taken into account in 
the regulation. In particular, the bans on profiling with 
regard to future criminal offences, the use of AI to assess 
social behaviour (“social scoring”) and the restriction 
on the use of biometric real-time remote identification 
systems in public spaces strengthen the protection of 
fundamental rights.
The AI Regulation also assigns new duties and powers 
to me as the authority responsible for the protection of 
fundamental rights. For example, my office will in future 
have access to the documents that must be created to 
comply with the AI Regulation and are required for 
fulfilment of my duties, and I must be notified of reports 
of serious incidents. In addition, summarised annual 
reports on the use of biometric remote identification 
systems by the federal law enforcement authorities must 
be submitted to me. The AI Regulation also provides for 
real-world laboratories to promote innovation. Real-wor-
ld laboratories are intended to provide a controlled 
environment that facilitates the development, training, 
testing and validation of innovative AI systems for a 
limited period of time. If personal data is processed in 
such a real-world laboratory, the data protection supervi-
sory authorities must be involved. I am happy to play an 
active role in this promising instrument for promoting 
innovation and protecting fundamental rights and to set 
up real-world laboratories under my direction.
The market surveillance authorities play a central role 
in the enforcement of the AI Regulation. Each Member 
State is required to designate at least one notifying au-
thority and at least one market surveillance authority as 
the competent national authorities for supervision of the 
application and implementation of the AI Regulation.
33rd Activity Report 202419


<!-- page 20 -->
The processing of personal data is of central importance 
for both the development and operation of AI systems. 
Moreover, data protection legislation remains unaffected 
by the AI Regulation. A consistent and standardised in-
terpretation of the data protection provisions and the AI 
Regulation is essential in the interests of legal certainty. 
I am therefore committed to ensuring that the collabora-
tion between the data protection supervisory authorities 
and market surveillance authorities is clearly regulated 
and implemented in a coordinated way, both at the legis-
lative level and in terms of practical cooperation.
In the interests of legal certainty, however, it is also im-
portant that the competences of the relevant authorities 
are clearly distinguishable from one another. The legisla-
tion must make clear that the data protection superviso-
ry authorities are responsible for the interpretation and 
application of the data protection provisions of the AI 
Regulation, such as Art. 59 and Art. 10(5). Clear partici-
pation and cooperation standards are also required. Data 
protection supervisory authorities should be involved in 
procedures of market surveillance authorities in which 
the processing of personal data plays a role, so that cont-
radictory decisions are excluded.
The prerogative to assess the lawfulness of the proces-
sing of personal data lies solely with the data protection 
supervisory authorities.
       
I recommend that the legislature determine the nati-
onal AI supervisory structure resulting from the EU 
AI regulation as soon as possible and make the best 
possible use of the expertise available in my office. 
This is the only way to prepare for the complex tasks 
associated with AI supervision and to ensure that the 
necessary structures are established. 
      
Cross-reference:
3.1.2 Research Data Taskforce
3.2.2 AI as a focus topic for committee work
The topic of artificial intelligence (AI) continued to 
preoccupy national and European data protection 
bodies in the reporting year. In addition to questions 
arising from the EU’s AI Regulation, which has now 
been adopted, the data protection supervisory autho-
rities have been very much concerned with what the 
use of AI means for data subjects, data controllers and 
society as a whole. Particular attention is being paid to 
consistent positioning in order to support controllers 
in legally compliant implementation of AI applications 
8 “Artificial Intelligence and Data Protection” guideline of the DSK of 6 May 2024,  
 available at https://www.datenschutzkonferenz-online.de/media/oh/20240506_DSK_Orientierungshilfe_KI_und_Datenschutz.pdf
and to draw attention to any need for action in terms 
of legislation.
National and European data protection bodies also dealt 
intensively with AI in the reporting year.
The focus of the DSK was on creating more legal certain-
ty in the development and use of AI and improving uni-
form interpretation of data protection regulations with 
regard to AI. To this end, on 6 May 2024 the DSK publis-
hed the guidance document “Artificial intelligence and 
data protection”8 for the use of AI applications, in which 
my staff were heavily involved. Prior to deployment of 
AI, a course towards data protection compliance must be 
set in the development phase.
In the case of AI systems in particular, personal data 
may also be processed in these early lifecycle phases. To 
ensure that these emerging solutions can also be used in 
compliance with data protection regulations, the DSK’s 
Technology Working Group is currently developing a pa-
per for manufacturers and operators of AI applications 
under the direction of my office.
With new technologies such as AI, the question often 
arises as to how the existing technology-neutral requi-
rements of data protection legislation can be imple-
mented. On my initiative and with my participation, the 
EDPB has drawn up two expert reports on the state of the 
art in science and technology as part of its Support Pool 
of Experts (SPoE) programme. The first report is dedica-
ted to the question of how the rights of data subjects can 
be effectively implemented in the context of AI systems. 
A particular focus here is on the options for correcting 
and erasing personal data. Once an AI model has been 
trained, special challenges can arise in this context. A 
simple “search and replace“ or “search and erase” proce-
dure fails because it is not always clear how the corres-
ponding information is represented in the model. The 
expert report therefore addresses the challenges specific 
to AI, shows the possibilities offered by techniques such 
as filtering and unlearning and highlights where the 
limits lie. The second expert report deals with how di-
scriminatory distortions (biases) arise in AI systems and 
how they can be automatically recognised and corrected 
in various lifecycle phases of an AI system.
Based on published scientific findings and available pro-
of-of-concept code, an assessment of the state of science 
and technology in this area is also presented.
The AI Regulation contains a number of provisions that 
have interfaces with data protection, in particular with 
regard to bias. In order to support data controllers in 
fulfilling their obligations arising from this, the Techno-
33rd Activity Report 2024 20


<!-- page 21 -->
logy Expert Subgroup (TECH ESG) of the EDPB, with the 
participation of my office, is developing guidelines on 
the interplay between the AI Regulation and the Europe-
an data protection provisions. The guidelines will deal 
with fundamental aspects such as a comparison of the 
different roles in the AI Regulation and the data protec-
tion regulations. Another focus will be on the standards 
for so-called high-risk AI systems. Who is accountable 
to whom, which transparency requirements need to be 
implemented and how do the risk concepts of the AI 
Regulation and the GDPR relate to each other here?
Last but not least, the questions of automatic decisi-
on-making under the two interacting legal frameworks 
will also play a prominent role.
A standardised interpretation of the GDPR for AI is 
also an important topic at European level. Some highly 
relevant questions of principle, including the personal 
nature of AI models, were discussed by the EDPB at the 
request of the Irish Data Protection Commission (DPC) 
as part of an Art. 64(2) procedure (Opinion 28/2024 pu-
blished on 18 December 2024)9. With the support of my 
colleagues, I was intensively involved in the preparation 
and adoption of the statement on the questions submit-
ted by the DPC. My office has also dealt specifically with 
the details of the ChatGPT AI application. As OpenAI, the 
provider of ChatGPT, did not have a base in the EU until 
15 February 2024, with the result that the one-stop shop 
mechanism did not apply until this date. The ChatGPT 
Taskforce of the EDPB was therefore responsible for 
coordinating the investigations and data protection 
assessment of ChatGPT in order to ensure consistent 
application of the GDPR. My office was involved in the 
report published on 23 May 202410 on the work carried 
out by the ChatGPT Taskforce.
Cross-reference:
3.2.1 AI Regulation
3.2.3 BfDI Assessment Catalogue for AI appli -
cations
While my consulting and monitoring work has increa-
singly involved data processing by artificial intelligen-
ce (AI) systems for some time now , this topic has beco-
me even more important recently because of the rapid 
upscaling in the context of large language models in 
particular. In principle, the procedure that my office 
has used for inspections to date is also applicable to 
the AI sector. In many places, however, technological 
developments make it necessary to introduce addi-
9 Opinion of the EDPB of 18 December 2024,  
 available at: https://www.edpb.europa.eu/system/files/2024-12/ edpb_opinion_202428_ai-models_en.pdf
10 Report of the EDPB Task Force on ChatGPT of 23 May 2024,  
 available at: https://www.edpb.europa.eu/system/files/2024-05/ edpb_20240523_report_chatgpt_taskforce_en.pdf
tional assessments and adapt considerations to the 
specific features of AI. During the reporting period, 
a project group set up by my office for this purpose 
completed its work and developed an assessment cata-
logue that will serve as the basis for my future monito-
ring activities in the area of AI applications.
In my supervisory role, I have to deal closely with the 
rapid developments in the field of AI. This is the only 
way to ensure that the often complex issues are adequa-
tely assessed in terms of data protection law and that 
compliance with data protection regulations is conti-
nuously guaranteed in this dynamic environment. In 
order to support this task, a project group set up for the 
purpose and comprising members from a wide range of 
departments within my organisation has developed an 
assessment grid as the basis for my advisory and moni-
toring activities with regard to AI-based algorithms and 
applications.
As a result, an assessment catalogue has been developed 
that addresses individual aspects of controls, accompa-
nied by an explanatory document that provides back-
ground information and supports the evaluation of AI 
applications.
In addition to the AI system itself, the assessment 
catalogue also considers how it is embedded organisa-
tionally in the form of roles and processes. The basis of 
the assessment is a general profile with questions about 
the data controller, the purpose and the groups of data 
subjects, as well as technical details and system parame-
ters. This is followed by a modular construction kit that 
allows a focus on monitoring priorities. Aspects such as 
the applicable legal basis of AI-based data processing, 
the process design of effective internal data protection 
supervision and the implementation of data subject 
rights in the application can be examined in appropri-
ate depth. There are also specific issues relating to AI 
in the commissioned data processing relationship, fair, 
non-discriminatory processing and transparency, relia-
bility and security of processing. This granularity allows 
my staff to tailor monitoring and consulting specifically 
to the degree of development and particularly critical 
aspects of an AI application and thus achieve results to-
gether with the bodies under supervision in an efficient 
way.
The catalogue of questions and the instructions provide 
important reference points for designing consultations 
and inspections. The assessment catalogue has already 
been used successfully in several areas.

The dynamic development in the area of AI-supported 
processing of course results in the need to evaluate and 
update the approaches continuously and adapt them 
wherever new legal and technological developments 
and regulatory requirements will have an impact on my 
supervisory tasks. In order to ensure that this happens 
and to be able to do justice to the increasing importance 
of AI in the future, a separate AI unit was set up in my 
office in the middle of the reporting year.

3.3 Security
3.3.1 Lawsuit against the BND
For the first time, I have brought legal action to enforce 
my powers of inspection. The lawsuit is directed
against the Federal Intelligence Service (BND) and was 
brought before the competent Federal Administrative 
Court. The BND is refusing to allow me to inspect docu-
ments that are subject to my statutory right of inspection 
and knowledge of which is necessary to carry out my 
checks.
The legal action was preceded by several discussions at 
authority management level and subsequently an official 
complaint under the Federal Data Protection Act (BDSG). 
The complaint, in which my office declared the refusal 
to grant access during an inspection to be unlawful, was 
rejected by the BND’s competent supervisory authority, 
the Federal Chancellery (BKAmt), as legally inadmissib-
le. It was subsequently disregarded. By refusing to grant 
me access, the BND is interfering with my independen-
ce: It claims the right to decide on the necessary basis, 
scope and content of the inspection itself.
I have therefore had recourse to legal action to enforce 
my legal mandate. The subject of the legal proceedings 
is the scope of my right of inspection and the question of 
the validity of the so-called Third Party Rule vis-à-vis my 
office as an independent supervisory body. Legal action 
is open to me in this case because I am asserting my own 
rights of inspection.
The subject matter of the action highlights a funda-
mental problem: at present, I only have the option of 
making unenforceable complaints to the BKAmt as the 
highest federal supervisory authority responsible for the 
BND. This also applies to the other federal intelligence 
services, i.e. the Federal Office for the Protection of 
the Constitution and the Federal Office for the Military 
Counterintelligence Service, and their supervisory 
authorities, the Federal Ministry of the Interior and the 
Federal Ministry of Defence. If they do not share my 
legal opinion as a specialist supervisor of the intelli-
gence services, I generally have no way of enforcing 
my findings and the complaint comes to nothing. This 
contradicts the demand for effective control of the intel-
ligence services. For this reason, I have been calling for 
many years for my office to be granted powers to issue 
orders to the federal intelligence services in accordance 
with the BKAG.
       
As in my 32nd Activity Report, I recommend that the 
legislature also introduce remedial powers in the area 
of intelligence services. 
      
3.3.2 BfDI threatened with loss of oversight of 
intelligence services
The departments responsible for the federal intelligen-
ce services are considering withdrawing my respon-
sibility for data protection supervision of the Federal 
Intelligence Service (BND), the Federal Office for the 
Protection of the Constitution (Bf V) and the Federal 
Office for the Military Counterintelligence Service 
(BAMAD). 
This was due to be part of an amendment that would 
have essentially been justified by the implementation 
of supreme court decisions. While the Federal Cons-
titutional Court (BVerfG) is also calling for changes in 
the area of control, this change in jurisdiction is not 
specified by the court.
Taking away my responsibility under data protection 
law for monitoring the federal intelligence services
The supervision of the federal intelligence services 
under data protection law has so far been my responsi-
bility. In the 20th legislative period, I became aware that 
this responsibility was to be transferred to the adminis-
trative control body of the Independent Control Council 
(UK Council). To date, the UK Council has primarily been 
responsible for preliminary assessment of certain intelli-
gence measures taken by the BND.
I have drawn the attention of the highest federal autho-
rities responsible for the supervision of the intelligence 
services to the risks of such a shift in supervision. In 
addition to the loss of completely independent data 
protection supervision and the resulting deterioration in 
the protection of fundamental rights, a shift in supervisi-
on would lead to a loss of my unique and comprehensive 
expertise in the intelligence sector, which I have built up 
over many years.
33rd Activity Report 2024 22


<!-- page 23 -->
Effective data protection control in the security sec-
tor can only be ensured if I remain responsible for 
all federal security authorities. Such control requires 
consistent interpretation and enforcement of data 
protection law in the German and European context, as 
well as an overview of all the security agencies and their 
data processing systems. I fulfil these requirements. In 
particular, my overview of the system landscapes of the 
intelligence services and the other security authorities 
enables me to fulfil the compensatory function assigned 
to me by the Federal Constitutional Court (BVerfG) in 
monitoring secret data processing. Only on the basis of 
this overview of the intelligence services and the other 
federal security authorities can I fully understand the 
lifecycle of personal data and thus the specific use of 
personal data across all authorities.
In order to counter the concern of any double controls, I 
have made a practical, cost-effective and easily imple-
mentable legal proposal, which also fulfils the require-
ments of the Federal Constitutional Court for effective, 
objective legal control. I have suggested that the UK 
Council and I be permitted to share material.
At present, both I and the UK Council are prohibited 
from doing so. Sharing information about the content 
of inspections should prevent loopholes in inspections 
and unnecessary duplication of work for the inspected 
bodies.
BVerfG advocates (only) a preliminary independent 
assessment of intrusive measures by the intelligence 
services
The shift in supervision was to be part of an amendment 
to the laws on intelligence services, the necessity of 
which arises from recent rulings by the Federal Consti-
tutional Court. The Federal Constitutional Court has now 
ruled that, in the BfV‘s area of activity, intrusive measu-
res such as surveillance of individuals and online sear-
ches must be authorised by an independent body outside 
the intelligence service before they are used. The court 
did not say anything about the question of what subse-
quent control should be in place for the personal data 
obtained through such measures. Nor did it need to do 
so, because this form of control is objectively legal data 
protection control, of the sort that I have been carrying 
out for a long time through Department 3 “Police and 
Intelligence Services”.
With the premature end of the coalition, the implemen-
tation of this and other stipulations of the BVerfG11 is off 
the table for the time being. A new attempt is likely to be 
made in the next legislative period – and is also neces-
sary in view of the demands of the Federal Constitutio-
11 32nd AR No. 3.3.1
nal Court. I trust that the new government will recognise 
the overwhelming advantages of my retaining control.
Amendment of the intelligence service laws unfortu-
nately postponed again
It was not only the aforementioned requirement of 
preliminary assessment that could not be implemented. 
The BVerfG has made further demands, in some cases 
repeatedly, that the legislature is required to address. 
The system of intelligence service powers must be 
reorganised. It must be based on so-called intervention 
thresholds. This means that a measure that encroaches 
more profoundly on fundamental rights is subject to 
higher requirements. The associated legal basis must be 
taken into account in this context. Core area protection 
must also be ensured when deploying all intelligence 
resources. Another point was the separation of the 
Military Counterintelligence Service Act (MADG) from 
the Federal Protection of the Constitution Act (BVerf-
SchG). Currently there are many references in the MADG 
to the BVerfSchG, but these are virtually impossible to 
understand for legal practitioners. The Federal Ministry 
of Defence involved me in some constructive discussions 
at an early stage in the development of an independent 
MADG, which I very much welcome. I am available for 
further solution-based and constructive involvement.
3.3.3 Thoroughness and proportionality of the 
“security package”
With the draft of the act “to improve the fight against 
terrorism” (part of the so-called security package), the 
federal government has reacted to the attack in Solingen 
on 23 August 2024. The draft law contained authorisation 
principles that allowed significant encroachments on the 
fundamental rights of data subjects.
The main focus of the draft law for a so-called “secu-
rity package” was to enable automated comparison of 
biometric data (facial images and voices) with publicly 
accessible data on the internet. Furthermore, a legal 
basis for automated data analysis was to be created and a 
comprehensive collection of data set up for this pur-
pose. This would have meant – as I put it bluntly in the 
political debate – a “super database” held by the Federal 
Criminal Police Office. Due to the political time pressu-
re, no departmental consultation took place, a process 
to which I could have contributed my data protection 
expertise in an advisory capacity. Instead, the SPD, FDP 
and Bündnis 90/Die Grünen parliamentary groups were 
provided with a “formulation aid“ that had only been 
agreed between the Federal Ministry of the Interior and 
the Federal Ministry of Justice, which was then discus-
sed directly as a draft bill from the centre of the Bundes-
33rd Activity Report 202423


<!-- page 24 -->
tag. Partly as a result of my critical comments, the pow-
ers were then formulated in a way that was more in line 
with fundamental rights and was adopted accordingly by 
the Bundestag. However, this version was rejected by the 
Bundesrat. The legislative process came to a standstill 
at the end of the 20th legislative period; at the time this 
report went to press, it was unclear whether the propo-
sal would be pursued further politically.
These new regulations of the so-called “security packa-
ge” remain critical from a data protection perspective: 
biometric identification and automated data analysis 
would normalise serious encroachments on fundamen-
tal rights in a way which, in my view, is disproportionate. 
The group of people who could potentially be affected 
by biometric matching was defined too broadly in the 
draft law. For example, witnesses would also have run 
the risk of biometric comparison. The specifications and 
requirements for technical implementation were also 
inadequate. As modern facial recognition systems are 
generally likely to fall under the EU’s AI Regulation in 
the future, an examination of compatibility with these 
provisions would also be necessary. The regulations 
in the draft law on automated data analysis were also 
non-specific in terms of technology, meaning that self-le-
arning (AI) systems could have been used. According 
to the ruling of the Federal Constitutional Court, this 
increases the intensity of encroachment on fundamental 
rights associated with the analysis and therefore requi-
res special constitutional justification and sufficient 
legal safeguards. I appeal to the political decision-ma-
kers to ensure that laws that encroach so extensively on 
citizens’ fundamental rights are drafted with due care 
and thoroughness and that consultation is sought in the 
legislative process of a government draft.
3.3.4 Factual discussion of the “quick freeze” 
and data retention
The issue of data retention is becoming more polarised: 
for some, data retention is an indispensable instrument 
for effective investigation of internet-related crimes. For 
others, its benefits are overestimated and it is seen as a 
threat to freedoms and civil rights; they see the so-called 
quick freeze as a proportionate alternative. 
When a matter is discussed for decades, one thing is 
obvious: the issue is complex and involves difficult ba-
lancing processes. This certainly applies to the introduc-
tion of data retention and the so-called “quick freeze”. To 
put it simply, the former relies on preventative storage of 
traffic and location data for certain purposes. The latter 
is based on freezing this data with a telecommunications 
12 Judgement of the ECJ of 20 September 2022 Ref. C 793/19 and C 794/19
13 Judgement of the ECJ of 30 April 2024 - C-470/21
provider only if there is suspicion of a serious criminal 
offence, so that it can be analysed later if there is concre-
te suspicion of a particular person.
While comprehensive data retention is being cam-
paigned for at European level, the political debate in 
Germany is increasingly focussing on the introduction of 
data retention of IP addresses. In this ongoing discus-
sion, the BMJ published a second draft consultation 
document for the outlined “quick freeze” regulation in 
October 2024 as an alternative to data retention.
It is important to me to take a factual approach in this 
discussion, to conduct it on the basis of objective argu-
ments and to keep the guidelines of the European Court 
of Justice (ECJ) rulings in mind. These show what is 
legally compliant and what is not: two judgements of the 
ECJ in the Spacenet case are fundamental for the legal 
assessment.12 In the Spacenet case, the ECJ limited gene-
ral and indiscriminate retention of IP addresses to what 
is absolutely essential. Whether such indiscriminate 
data retention without cause is permissible or impermis-
sible in the opinion of the ECJ depends on how serious 
the encroachment on fundamental rights involved in IP 
address retention is in the context of the proportionality 
assessment that must be carried out and whether the 
public interest pursued is proportionate to the severity 
of that encroachment (“end-means relationship”). In 
this decision, however, the ECJ deemed it permissible 
to order an immediate backup of data in the event of 
suspicion of a specific criminal offence, thus paving the 
way for the “quick freezes”.
The judgement in the Hadopi case13 does not reverse 
this case law – as is sometimes portrayed in the public 
debate.
Rather, the ECJ clarified that if stored IP addresses are 
used solely to find the identity data of a person and if 
the online behaviour of data subjects cannot be traced 
by the storage or use of the data – as must be ensured – 
there is no serious interference with fundamental rights, 
and IP addresses may therefore be stored under less 
strict conditions. But even then, storage and access must 
be absolutely necessary and an evidence-based legal 
basis is required.
An objective discussion of the topic of data retention 
and the “quick freeze” also includes the question of 
whether data retention actually generates added value 
for law enforcement authorities in view of the existing 
circumvention options. With regard to the “how” of 
data retention, the definition of an absolutely necessary 
retention period in particular would require an empi-
33rd Activity Report 2024 24


<!-- page 25 -->
rically verifiable basis. I would like to point out in this 
connection that even law enforcement agencies have 
reached the empirical conclusion that the success rate 
in investigations does not increase significantly after 
a period of around 2-3 weeks.14 There is no empirical 
basis for a storage duration for IP addresses of several 
months, which is part of the discussion. The potential of 
the “quick freeze”, which would be less constitutionally 
intrusive, should also be included in the discussion. IP 
address storage without cause – regardless of whether 
it has been declared admissible by the ECJ – increases 
the public perception of being under surveillance. This 
perception alone is likely to trigger conformist beha-
viour, which is not conducive to a free society. I will 
therefore continue to follow the discussion and possible 
legislative processes surrounding IP address storage and 
do everything in my power to find solutions that respect 
fundamental rights.
