4.1 Report from the DSK
The Conference of Independent Federal and State Data 
Protection Supervisory Authorities (DSK) pursues 
the goal of protecting fundamental data protection 
rights, achieving uniform application of European and 
national data protection legislation in Germany and 
playing a part in its further development. To this end, 
the DSK issues decisions on data protection policy 
issues, passes resolutions on the interpretation of data 
protection regulations and gives statements, guidance 
and application tips. The chair of the DSK generally 
changes annually . In 2024, the chair was held succes-
sively by the State Data Protection Commissioners of 
Bremen, Schleswig-Holstein and Hesse.
The DSK passed three decisions during the reporting 
period. These relate to better protection of patient data 
when hospitals are closed, the right to a free first copy of 
patient files and the use of facial recognition systems by 
the security authorities.
It also passed a resolution. This concerns the transfer 
of personal data in the context of an asset deal. An asset 
deal is the sale of a company in such a way that busi-
ness and other assets are transferred to the acquiring 
company. This is distinct from a share deal, in which 
only shares in a company are transferred. In addition, 
the DSK prepared four position papers on the topics of 
“National responsibility for the regulation on artificial 
intelligence”, “Requirements for the secondary use of 
genetic data for research purposes”, “Legal data protec-
tion limits on the use of payment cards to grant benefits 
under the Asylum Seekers Benefits Act” and “Privileged 
status of scientific research under the GDPR”.
Furthermore, the DSK presented five sets of guidance 
on the supervisory authorities’ main areas of focus: the 
“Guidance on obtaining self-disclosure information from 
prospective tenants”, “Guidance on artificial intelligence 
and data protection”, “Guidance on data processing in 
connection with wireless meters”, “Guidance for digital 
15 All EDPB opinions are available (only partially translated) at: https://edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/ consistency-findings/opinions_en
service providers” and “Guidance on selected issues 
from the new Online Access Act”.
A new version of the standard data protection model was 
also developed.
Further information on all topics dealt with by the DSK 
can be found on the website www.datenschutzkonfe-
renz-online.de.
4.2 European Data Protection 
Board
4.2.1 General report from the EDPB
In this reporting year, the European Data Protecti-
on Board (EDPB) continued its work on harmonised 
application of the General Data Protection Regula-
tion (GDPR) across Europe, focusing on interaction 
with new legal acts regulating artificial intelligence, 
implementation of the European data strategy and the 
digital services package, among other things. Unlike 
in previous reporting years, the focus of the EDPB’s 
publications was on issuing opinions and less on the 
adoption of guidelines.
In 2024, the EDPB consolidated its high frequency of 
plenary sessions and met a total of twelve times. The 
meetings always take place alternately as video confe-
rences and face-to-face events in Brussels. There are also 
numerous meetings of the EDPB working groups (Expert 
Subgroups), in which my staff participate actively.
Opinions in the consistency procedure
As in previous years, the EDPB has issued numerous 
opinions in the so-called consistency procedure in 
accordance with Art. 4(1) GDPR15. The majority of these 
relate to:
 → binding internal data protection regulations submit-
ted by Member States (Art. 47 GDPR);
33rd Activity Report 2024 26


<!-- page 27 -->
 → accreditation of certification bodies (Art. 43(3) GDPR) 
and
 → of bodies for monitoring compliance with rules of 
conduct (Art. 41 GDPR).
In addition, the EDPB has published four important opi-
nions under the optional procedure pursuant to Art. 
64(2) GDPR. The decisions concern issues with far-rea-
ching effects, e.g. cases where an impact is expected in 
several Member States.
Opinion 04/2024 on the concept of the main establish-
ment of a controller in the Union pursuant to Art. 4(16) 
point (a) GDPR16 stipulates, among other things, that 
decisions on the means and purposes of data processing 
must be taken in a central administration located in the 
EU in such a way that it can be understood as the “main 
establishment” within the meaning of the GDPR.
In its Opinion 08/2024 on the “Effectiveness of consent 
in the context of “consent-or-pay” models of large 
online platforms”,17 the EDPB takes the view that offe-
ring only a paid alternative to services that involve the 
processing of personal data for behavioural advertising 
purposes should not be the default approach for data 
controllers. When developing alternatives, large online 
platforms should consider providing individuals with an 
“equivalent alternative”
that does not involve paying a fee. In addition, the EDPB 
provides elements to assess the criteria for informed, 
specific and unambiguous consent that large online 
platforms should consider when implementing “consent 
or payment models”.
Opinion 11/2024 on the use of facial recognition tech-
nologies by airport operators and airlines to stream-
line the passenger flow at airports18 analyses the com-
patibility of the processing of biometric data with the 
principle of storage limitation, the principle of integrity 
and confidentiality, data protection through technology 
and default settings and the security of processing. To 
this end, the EDPB examines whether the processing 
of passengers’ biometric data is compatible with four 
different types of storage solutions, from those that store 
biometric data only in the hands of the individual to 
those that rely on a centralised storage architecture with 
16 EDPB Opinion 04/2024 of 13 February 2024 on the concept of the main establishment, available at:

17 EDPB Opinion 08/2024 of 17 April 2024 on “consent or pay” , available at:  
 https://www.edpb.europa.eu/system/files/2024-11/ edpb_opinion_202408_consentorpay_en.pdf
18 EDPB Opinion 11/2024 of 23 May 2024 on facial recognition, available at:

different modalities. In all cases, passengers’ biometric

In Opinion 22/202419, the EDPB has taken a position

processors. The opinion was issued at the request of 
the Danish data protection supervisory authority in the 
context of a procedure under Art. 64(2) GDPR,
which any supervisory authority can initiate if the ques-
tions raised are of Europe-wide relevance. The Danish 
supervisory authority raised several questions regarding 
the interpretation of Art. 28 and 44 GDPR, which inpar-
ticular concerned the accountability of the controller 
when using several (sub)processors connected in a 
“chain”. It also dealt with issues relating to the contrac-
tual structure of the agreement on commissioned data 
processing. The questions concerned both processing 
within the European Economic Area (EEA) and constel-
lations involving third countries.
Opinion 28/2024 deals with certain aspects of data pro-
tection legislation in connection with the processing 
of personal data in the context of AI models. Further 
details can be found in the section on national and Euro-
pean committee work on AI.
Guidelines of the EDPB in 2024
The EDPB adopted three guidelines in the reporting 
year:
Guidelines 01/2023 on Art. 37 of the JHA Directive20 
in the preparation of which my office played a lea-
ding role, were confirmed without significant changes 
following a public consultation. In particular, there is a 
discussion of the concept of “appropriate safeguards”, 
which is mentioned in Art. 37 of the JHA Directive as a 
central requirement for third country transfers by law 
enforcement agencies. In this context, the EDPB empha-
sises that the level of protection guaranteed in the EU 
may not be undermined by the transfer of personal data 
to a third country. I welcome the fact that this will also 
ensure high data protection standards for transmissions 
in the JHA area.
Guidelines 01/2024 on the processing of personal data 
on the legal basis of a legitimate interest pursuant to 
33rd Activity Report 202427


<!-- page 28 -->
Art. 6(1) point (f ) GDPR21, on which my staff worked as 
co-rapporteurs, were adopted by the EDPB in October 
2024. The guidelines contain clarifications on which 
aspects controllers should take into account when 
examining the requirements of Art. 6(1) point (f) GDPR. 
At the same time, they provide information on how Art. 
6(1) point (f) GDPR can be applied in specific processing 
contexts (e.g. in the area of fraud prevention, direct mar-
keting or information security). Finally, the guidelines 
clarify the relationship between Article 6(1) point (f) 
GDPR and data subjects’ rights. They thus make an im-
portant contribution to consistent application of Art. 6(1) 
point (f) GDPR within the EU. The final text is expected 
to be adopted by the EDPB once the public consultation 
has been completed in early 2025.
Guidelines 02/2023 on the applicability of Art. 5(3) of 
the ePrivacy Directive22 were adopted following a public 
consultation. It has now been explicitly clarified that the 
guidelines are not intended to analyse application of the 
exceptions to the consent requirement under Art. 5(3) of 
the e-Privacy Directive and that the applicability of Art. 
5(3) of the e-Privacy Directive does not necessarily mean 
that consent must be obtained.
EDPB taskforce on the interplay between data protecti-
on, competition and consumer protection
The EDPB taskforce on the interplay between data pro-
tection, competition and consumer protection, which 
was founded in March 2023, examined the links and 
synergies between these legal areas in the reporting year 
and developed positions and guidance on this. Under 
the joint leadership of my office, it has produced a paper 
with frequently asked questions (FAQs) on cooperation 
between data protection authorities and competition 
and consumer protection authorities for internal use 
by the data protection supervisory authorities. This 
serves to guide the data protection supervisory autho-
rities and sets out the reasons and situations in which 
cooperation between the authorities makes sense. The 
requirements of the judgement of the European Court 
of Justice in the Meta v. Federal Cartel Office case23 are 
put into practice here for the cooperation between data 
protection supervisory authorities and competition au-
thorities by presenting various options for cooperation 
and offering practical assistance. For example, the FAQs 
contain a checklist for the next steps in the data protecti-
on supervisory authority and a template for cooperation 
agreements.
21 Guideline 01/2024 of 20 November 2024, available at:  
 https://www.edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/documents/public-consultations/2024/ guidelines-12024-processing-personal-data-based_en
22 Guideline 02/2023 of 7 October 2024, available at: 
 https://www.edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/our-documents/guidelines/ guidelines-22023-technical-scope-art-53-eprivacy-directive_en
23 Judgment of the ECJ of 4 July 2023, C-252/21, available at: 
 https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX%3A62021CJ0252
In addition, a position paper on the interplay between 
data protection and competition law is being drawn up 
by the taskforce, also under the co-leadership of my 
office. This will in part reflect the FAQs and serve as a 
public position for the EDPB.
The taskforce is also working with the EU Commission 
to develop guidelines on the interaction between the EU 
Digital Markets Act (DMA) and the GDPR. The guidelines 
are intended to clarify how so-called gatekeepers, i.e. 
companies that provide centralised platform services 
and have been designated as gatekeepers by the EU 
Commission, should interpret and apply the GDPR when 
processing personal data within the scope of the DMA.
A recurring role of the taskforce is to prepare and 
consolidate common positions of the EDPB and the 
European Data Protection Supervisor in preparation for 
participation in the meetings of the advisory body of the 
EU Commission provided for in the DMA, the so-cal-
led High-Level Group. For example, the advisory body 
adopted a position on the use of artificial intelligence by 
gatekeepers. At its meetings, it also discusses the reports 
of its subgroups on obligations for gatekeepers arising 
from the DMA, such as the interoperability of various 
messenger services.
EDPB strategy 2024-2027
The EDPB has defined its strategy for the years 2024 to 
2027. This is orientated structurally around the four pil-
lars of the previous strategy, so that there is continuity:
 → The 1st pillar comprises improving harmonisation 
and promoting compliance with the provisions of the 
GDPR
 → The 2nd pillar comprises strengthening a common 
culture of enforcement and effective cooperation
 → The 3rd pillar comprises data protection in the de-
velopment of the digital and cross-regulatory area
 → The 4th pillar comprises a contribution to the global 
dialogue on data protection
In addition to a focus on artificial intelligence, the 
strategy directs the attention of the EDPB among other 
things to interaction with new legal acts regulating ar-
tificial intelligence, the implementation of the Europe-
an data strategy and the digital services package.
In this context, it is important to me that we work 
towards consistency in the EU’s digital legislation. The 
33rd Activity Report 2024 28


<!-- page 29 -->
legal acts resulting from the EU digital strategy, in parti-
cular the Digital Services Act, Data Governance Act, Data 
Act and AI Regulation, are not sufficiently harmonised 
with each other or with the GDPR. There are overlaps, 
differing definitions and unclear demarcations in the
supervisory structures. This makes it more difficult for 
providers, stakeholders and the authorities involved to 
comply with and monitor the requirements and for data 
subjects to exercise their rights.

3.2.2 AI as a focus topic for committee work
4.2.2 Regulation laying down additional pro -
cedural rules for the enforcement of the GDPR
With the proposal for a regulation establishing ad-
ditional procedural rules for the enforcement of the 
GDPR, the European Commission has responded to the 
request of the European Data Protection Board (EDPB) 
for a harmonisation of national procedural rules and 
an improvement in cooperation between the supervi-
sory authorities in cross-border cases. The legislative 
process is progressing thanks to the positions taken 
by the European Parliament and the Council of the 
European Union, with many improvements compared 
to the European Commission’s proposal.
The European Commission’s proposal for a “Regulation 
laying down additional procedural rules for the en-
forcement of Regulation (EU) 2016/679” of 4 July 2023 
(COM(2023) 348 final, hereinafter the “RPR”) is a res-
ponse to the EDPB’s so-called Vienna Declaration, which 
aims to improve cooperation in the enforcement of data 
protection in the EU.24 To this end, aspects of national 
procedural law are also to be harmonised.25 26
The project is of considerable importance for better and 
faster enforcement of the GDPR in the processing of 
cross-border cases. I have therefore been closely invol-
ved in the legislative process this year and contributed 
to the EDPB’s opinion on the negotiating positions of the 
European bodies.
Parliament and Council in relation to the trialogue27
It is pleasing that both the Parliament and the Council 
have proposed improvements and have also taken into 
account the positions of the EDPB and the supervisory 
24 Vienna Declaration of the EDPB of 28 April 2022, available at: 
 https://edpb.europa.eu/system/files/2022-04/ edpb_statement_20220428_on_enforcement_cooperation_en.pdf
25 See Annex of the EDPB of 10 October 2022, available at: 
 https://edpb.europa.eu/system/files/2022-10/ edpb_letter_out2022-0069_to_the_eu_commission_on_procedural_aspects_en_0.pdf
26 32nd AR No. 4.2.4
27 Statement of the EDPB 4/2024, available at: 
 https://www.edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/our-documents/statements/ statement-42024-recent-legislative-developments-draft_en
authorities. These include in particular the introducti-
on of binding procedural deadlines, more harmonised 
rules in the cooperation procedure, strengthening of the 
procedural positions of complainants and avoidance of 
unnecessary additional bureaucratic steps.
I will continue to monitor the legislative process closely 
and advocate for the necessary improvements. The 
fundamental rights of citizens can be protected more 
effectively through faster and more coordinated law 
enforcement and cooperation.
4.2.3 Report from the CSC
In the Coordinated Supervision Committee (CSC), the 
national supervisory authorities and the European 
Data Protection Supervisor coordinate their supervi-
sory activities for large-scale European IT systems and 
certain EU institutions. Since 2021, the deputy chair of 
the CSC has been one of my staff. Its increasing range 
of responsibilities presents the CSC with major orga-
nisational challenges, particularly with regard to the 
necessary resources.
The CSC serves to exchange information, ensure 
uniform interpretation of relevant legal principles and 
coordinate joint controls. Together with the respective 
country representatives, I am actively involved in the 
regular meetings of the CSC and preparation of joint 
documents. In addition, one of my staff was re-elected as 
deputy chair of the committee at the end of the previous 
reporting period.
The area of responsibility of the CSC includes in parti-
cular the Schengen Information System (SIS), Europol, 
Eurojust, the European Public Prosecutor’s Office and 
the Internal Market Information System (IMI). In this 
reporting year, the CSC also took over coordination of 
the Visa Information System (VIS) from the VIS Super-
vision Coordination Group (SCG). In future, the CSC will 
also take over coordination of Eurodac and of parts of 
the Customs Information System (CIS) from the previous 
SCGs.
In addition, the CSC will be responsible for the planned 
EU Entry/Exit System (EES), the European Travel Infor-
mation and Authorisation System (ETIAS), the European 
Criminal Records Information System for Third-country 
Nationals and Stateless Persons (ECRIS-TCN), police 
cooperation under the Prüm II Regulation and the EU in-
33rd Activity Report 202429


<!-- page 30 -->
teroperability framework. Although these systems have 
not yet gone live, the CSC is already actively fulfilling its 
role as a forum for exchange of information and coordi-
nation in the context of the ongoing implementation.
Its increasing scope necessitates changes in the organi-
sation. In future, discussions and work will increasingly 
take place in working groups. The CSC has also had two 
deputy coordinators since this reporting year. Overall, it 
is evident that the CSC is continuously being given addi-
tional responsibilities for new and existing EU systems, 
but hardly any additional resources. There are limits to 
what can be done internally to counter this shortfall.
I would like to present a brief selection of the work of 
the CSC below:
At the end of the reporting period, the annual statistics 
on the exercise of data subjects’ rights from the Mem-
ber States under the SIS regulations were collated and 
analysed for the first time in the SIS area. In addition, 
information on the interpretation of Art. 12 of Regula-
tion (EU) 2018/1861 on the use of log data by the super-
visory authorities is currently being collected with the 
involvement of my staff. Furthermore, the report on the 
Europe-wide coordinated checks on alerts for covert and 
specific checks pursuant to Art. 36 of the SIS II Decision 
was finalised and adopted.28 My staff were also directly 
involved in this.
In the area of the EES, the members regularly exchanged 
information on the status of implementation. The EU 
Commission also reported on the EES information cam-
paign and Frontex on the development of an EES app. 
The CSC also sent two letters to the EU Commission, in 
which the obligation to involve the supervisory autho-
rities in the information campaign was underlined. 29In 
the area of the ETIAS, the CSC performed an advisory 
function with regard to challenges in its implementati-
on, for example through internal dialogue on the topic of 
data subject rights, but also externally through partici-
pation in technical expert groups and workshops and by 
addressing the EU Commission on pressing problems 
relating to the ETIAS data protection impact assessment, 
data subject rights and responsibilities.30 In the CSC, 
the EDPB coordinates representation of the EDPB in the 
so-called ETIAS Fundamental Rights Guidance Board.

 https://www.edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/ our-documents/csc-documents/report-article-36-alerts-schengen-information-system_en

As far as Eurojust is concerned, information is currently

In the context of the IMI, recommendations were adop-

4.2.4 Developments in the area of internatio -
nal data transfers
Significant developments in the area of international 
data transfers also took place in 2024. At the beginning 
of the year, the European Commission published its 
report on the review of the eleven adequacy decisions 
issued before the GDPR came into force. In July , the 
first review of the adequacy decision on the EU-U.S. 
Data Privacy Framework followed, with significant par-
ticipation from my office. The European Commission 
published its report on this in October, while the EDPB 
published its own report in November. At international 
33rd Activity Report 2024 30


<!-- page 31 -->
level, my office initiated and drafted two important do-
cuments to further promote the concept of Data Free 
Flow with Trust (DFFT), which were adopted by the 
G7 Data Protection Authorities Roundtable in Rome in 
October and by the Global Privacy Assembly in Jersey 
in October.
1. Developments on the EU-U.S. Data Privacy Frame-
work
In my previous Activity Report, I reported in detail 
on the adequacy decision on the EU-U.S. Data Privacy 
Framework (DPF)33 which came into force in July 2023. 
The adequacy decision on the DPF is the successor to 
the adequacy decision on the Privacy Shield, which 
was declared invalid by the European Court of Justice 
in 2020.34 Since the entry into force of the adequacy 
decision on the DPF, personal data within the scope of 
application of the adequacy decision can once again be 
transferred to US companies and organisations certified 
under the DPF without the need for further transfer 
instruments pursuant to Chapter V GDPR.
One year after the adequacy decision came into force, its 
first review took place in Washington, D.C. in July 2024. 
Representatives of the US authorities and, on the EU 
side, the European Commission and the EDPB took part. 
As announced in my previous Activity Report, I have 
been intensively involved in this first review through 
my staff, taking the lead on the topic of “Government 
Access”.
Following the review, both the European Commission35 
and the EDPB36 published a review report. The European 
Commission states in its report that the US authorities 
have established the necessary structures and procedu-
res to ensure the effective functioning of the DPF. At the 
same time, it points out that experience of its practical 
application after one year was still limited and explains 
which developments it considers necessary and intends 
to monitor.
The EDPB states in its report that many implementation 
steps have already been taken. Nevertheless, it notes that 
further implementation measures will need to be taken 
in future, clarifications will be necessary on individu-
33 Commission Implementing Decision (EU) 2023/1795 of 10.07.2023 pursuant to Regulation (EU) 2016/679 of the European Parliament and of the Council on the 
 adequate level of protection of personal data under the EU-US Privacy Shield Framework (notified under document number C(2023) 4745) (Text with EEA  
 relevance), available at: 
 https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/dec_impl/2023/1795/oj
34 Judgement of the ECJ of 16 July 2020, C-311/18, available at: 
 https://curia.europa.eu/juris/document/ document.jsf?docid=228677&mode=lst&pageIndex=1&dir=&occ=first&part=1&text=&doclang=DE&cid=40595668
35 Report from the Commission to the European Parliament and the Council on the first regular review of the functioning of the adequacy decision on the  
 EU-US data protection framework, Brussels, 9 October 2024, COM(2024) 451 final, available at: 
 https://eur-lex.europa.eu/ legal-content/DE/TXT/?uri=CELEX%3A52024DC0451&qid=1731661085429
36 EDPB report on the first review of the EU-U.S. Data Privacy Framework, version 1.1 of 4 November 2024, available at: 
 https://www.edpb.europa.eu/system/files/2024-11/edpb_report_20241104_reportonfirstreviewofeu-u.s.dpf_en.pdf
37 op. cit. in particular page 4, recitals 7 ff., recitals 64 ff.
al points and developments in US law will need to be 
monitored.
With regard to the data protection principles to which 
the certified US companies must adhere (the so-cal-
led “commercial part”), the EDPB report comes to the 
conclusion that the essential steps of the certification 
procedure and the various legal remedies have been 
implemented. Further need for implementation is iden-
tified with regard to strengthening proactive controls 
for compliance with data protection principles by the 
US authorities. At the same time, the EDPB considers it 
necessary for further guides to understanding the prin-
ciples of data protection law to be published. This relates 
in particular to questions regarding to forwarding data 
on and the concept of personal data.37 
The main subject of the first review in the area of “Go-
vernment Access” was the implementation of the data 
protection standards that were introduced into US law as 
a result of the so-called Schrems II judgement of the ECJ 
(Case C-311/18). The deficits identified by the ECJ in this 
decision are to be remedied by a principle of proportio-
nality to be observed by the US intelligence services and 
a new complaints procedure. Of particular importance 
to the EDPB is whether these changes in US law are now 
also effective in practice. Implementation of the new 
complaints procedure was evaluated positively. The 
Data Protection Review Court, which is central to this 
process, is now fully staffed and operational following 
the appointment of eight judges. However, there is still a 
need for clarification regarding the specific application 
of the new proportionality principle by the US intelli-
gence services. This aspect requires continued careful 
monitoring. Another significant data protection-related 
legal development on government access also requires 
close monitoring. In April 2024, one of the relevant legal 
bases for the collection of personal data transferred 
from the EU to the USA for intelligence purposes was 
amended. The EDPB has raised concerns about a lack of 
legal clarity here, which could create uncertainty about 
the actual scope of authorised data collection.
According to the GDPR, adequacy decisions must be 
reviewed at least every four years with regard to the way 
33rd Activity Report 202431


<!-- page 32 -->
they are functioning. As far as the adequacy decision on 
the DPF is concerned, both the EDPB and the European 
Commission consider an earlier review to be expedient.
Publications of the EDPB on the DPF
The EDPB has published various documents to inform 
data subjects about how they can assert their rights 
under the DPF. There are forms and information 
documents on the complaints procedure vis-à-vis US 
organisations/companies in commercial matters38 and 
on the complaints procedure regarding the processing of 
personal data by the US intelligence services.39 
If data subjects wish to assert a breach of the law with 
regard to data transfers under the DPF, there are various 
ways to do so. Data subjects can contact the DPF-cer-
tified company directly or file a complaint with the 
Department of Commerce or the Federal Trade Com-
mission (the US authority responsible for protection of 
competition and consumer rights).
The EDPB has also produced a paper with answers to 
frequently asked questions (FAQs) on the DPF, which 
provides useful information for private individuals40 and 
companies.41
An overview of the EDPB publications mentioned above 
and further information on the subject, in particular on 
the various complaints procedures, can be found on my 
website.42

2. Report of the European Commission on the ade-
quacy decisions adopted under Directive 95/46/EC
In January 2024, the European Commission published a 
report43 and the accompanying working document 44on 
the review of the eleven adequacy decisions already 
issued before the GDPR came into force. These decisions 
were adopted under the Data Protection Directive 95/46/
EC. They are adequacy decisions for Andorra, Argenti-
na, Canada (in relation to the private sector only), the 
Faroe Islands, Guernsey, the Isle of Man, Israel, Jersey, 
New Zealand, Switzerland and Uruguay. The European 
Commission has reviewed these decisions and concludes 
in its report that an adequate level of data protection 
continues to exist in all eleven countries. In a letter45 to 
the European Commission, the EDPB, which was not 
formally involved in the review of the adequacy decisi-
ons, emphasises various aspects that should be given 
particular consideration in future reviews and decisi-
ons. Among other things, the EDPB is in favour of the 
Commission providing a more detailed explanation of 
the review of the principles relating to the rule of law in 
future.
3. Data Free Flow with Trust
The issue of trustworthy international data transmission 
was once again at the centre of discussions in important 
international forums in the field of data protection this 
year, in particular in connection with the concept of 
so-called Data Free Flow with Trust (DFFT). My office 
33rd Activity Report 2024 32


<!-- page 33 -->
initiated and took the lead in drafting two documents in 
this regard, which were adopted at this year’s roundtable 
meeting of the G7 data protection authorities and the 
Global Privacy Assembly.
Cross-references:
4.5.2 G7 DPA Roundtable, 4.3 46. Annual Conference of 
the Global Privacy Assembly
4.2.5 Coordinated Enforcement Framework 
Action 2024
The European data protection supervisory authorities 
also carried out a joint enforcement action (“Coor-
dinated Enforcement Action”, CEA) in this reporting 
period. This is based on a decision of the European 
Data Protection Board (EDPB) of October 2020 with the 
purpose of establishing a Coordinated Enforcement 
Framework (CEF).46 At my suggestion, the EDPB chose 
the implementation of the right of access under Art. 
15 GDPR by data controllers as the topic for its third 
coordinated enforcement action.
The right of access is one of the most important data 
protection rights of data subjects. It enables them to ac-
cess information about the processing of their personal 
data by data controllers and to check whether this pro-
cessing is lawful. The right of access can therefore serve 
as a basis for data subjects to exercise further rights, 
such as the right to rectification or erasure of their 
personal data. Moreover, the right of access is one of the 
most frequently exercised rights of data subjects. The 
corresponding processes are often the subject of comp-
laints to the data protection supervisory authorities.
The EDPB has issued guidelines on the right of access 
47to help with consistent implementation of this right. 
As co-rapporteur on the EDPB, my office played a key 
role in the development of these guidelines.48 The recent 
case law of the European Court of Justice (ECJ) has also 
further harmonised the standards for implementing the 
right of access.
The aim of the CEA 2024 was to investigate how cont-
rollers implement the right of access in practice. The 
intention was also to offer an assessment of whether 
individual aspects of the EDPB guidelines should be ad-
apted or clarified and whether further measures to raise 
awareness of data controllers or data subjects by the data 
protection supervisory authorities may be useful.

https://www.edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/our-documents/other/ edpb-document-coordinated-enforcement-framework-under-regulation_en

https://www.edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/our-documents/guidelines/guidelines-012022-data-subject-rights-right-access_de
48 31st AR No. 3.3.5
49 EDPB report on the CEA 2024 – Implementation of the right of access by controllers, of 16 January 2025, available at: 
https://www.edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/our-documents/other/coordinated-enforcement-action-implementation-right-access_en
My office took on the role of lead rapporteur on the 
EDPB for the CEA 2024. In addition, as part of my res-
ponsibility for data protection supervision of the federal 
administration, I surveyed various controllers about 
their implementation of the right of access. My findings 
from this – as well as the findings of seven other German 
supervisory authorities involved in the CEA 2024 and 22 
supervisory authorities from other European countries 
– were incorporated into a joint report by the EDPB. In 
this report,49 recommendations for action were drawn 
up for the EDPB, for controllers and for data subjects. In 
addition, national supervisory and enforcement measu-
res can be taken on the basis of the knowledge gained.
4.3 46th Annual Conference of the 
Global Privacy Assembly
In autumn 2024, the Information Commissioner of 
Jersey hosted the 46th Annual Conference of the Global 
Privacy Assembly (GPA). The discussions centred on 
fundamental issues relating to international data 
protection and approaches to new digital technologies. 
The conference adopted a resolution on trustworthy 
cross-border data transfers (“Data Free Flow with 
Trust”) proposed by me.
The GPA is the largest international association of data 
protection authorities and has over 130 members from 
all over the world. From 28 October to 1 November 2024, 
the Channel Island of Jersey played host to the 46th 
Annual Conference of the GPA, organised by the Jersey 
Information Commissioner’s office.
The presentations and panel discussions at the conferen-
ce focussed on issues relating to data protection-compli-
ant design of applications that use artificial intelligence 
functions and algorithms. One important aspect in this 
context was the protection of vulnerable groups. Further 
light was also shed on what the data protection supervi-
sory authorities need to do to be well prepared for the 
challenges of the future.
This includes increased cooperation with each other and 
with supervisory authorities in other sectors, e.g. the 
competition authorities.
In one of the closed sessions of the conference, I took 
part in a panel discussion on the topic of “LLMs (Large 
Language Models): Innovative Technologies and the 
33rd Activity Report 202433


<!-- page 34 -->
Future of Privacy” and pointed out the risks, but also the 
possible solutions associated with this technology from a 
data protection perspective.
As is customary every year, I also reported on the acti-
vities and latest papers of the “International Working 
Group on Data Protection in Technology”, which is led by 
my office and is an independent working group within 
the GPA environment.
The 46th Annual Conference of the GPA adopted a total 
of five resolutions:
 → Resolution on “Data Free Flow with Trust” (DFFT)
 → Resolution on data protection certification procedu-
res
 → Resolution on neurotechnology and data protection
 → Resolution on surveillance technologies
 → Resolution on the rules and procedures of the GPA
The resolution on DFFT was initiated by my office and 
drawn up in consultation with the European Data Pro-
tection Supervisor. It recognises the need for internati-
onal data flows for society, research and the economy, 
but also emphasises that this can result in risks for the 
protection of personal data and privacy. The resolution 
therefore calls on legislatures and authorities to develop 
instruments for secure cross-border data transfers and 
to pay particular attention to the following core areas 
of DFFT (“essential elements of DFFT”): rights of data 
subjects, security of processing, access to effective and 
enforceable legal remedies, independent supervision 
and access to personal data by public authorities. The 
resolution is the first GPA declaration explicitly dedica-
ted to the DFFT concept. DFFT is also a key issue at the 
level of the digital ministers of the G7 countries and the 
OECD.
As the GPA ’s statutes only allow two consecutive terms 
on the GPA ’s executive committee, I stepped down from 
the Executive Committee at the end of the 46th Annual 
Conference after four years as an elected member. The 
Data Protection Authority of South Africa was elected 
as a new member. For Europe and the EU countries, the 
Bulgarian data protection authority will continue to sit as 
a member of the Executive Committee.
The next annual conference of the GPA will take place in 
Seoul, Republic of Korea, in September 2025.

4.4 Berlin Group
The International Working Group on Data Protection 
in Technology (IWGDPT), also known as the “Berlin 
Group”, has held two meetings under my leadership 
and adopted four thematic working papers. The Group 
has further sharpened its focus on important future 
technologies.
The IWGDPT was founded in 1983 at the initiative of 
the then Berlin Data Protection Commissioner as an 
independent group of experts on the subject of “data 
protection in telecommunications” and is therefore also 
known as the “Berlin Group”. In recent years, the Group’s 
field of work has expanded to cover the entire area of 
technology, with a special focus on important future 
technologies that will be ready for the market within the 
next few years and could therefore become relevant for a 
large number of users. A “Future Foresight” discussion is 
held regularly for this purpose.
A special feature of the Berlin Group is its heterogeneous 
composition of members from the fields of data protecti-
on authorities, science, research, non-governmental and 
international organisations. This makes it possible to 
bring together diverse and outstanding expertise for the 
Group’s various working papers.
The aim of the Berlin Group is to present target 
group-based recommendations and guidelines for data 
protection-compliant handling of relevant future techno-
logies at an early stage in its working papers, so that the 
parties involved, such as legislators or developers of new 
technologies and services for example, can take these 
into account in their further decisions and procedures.
Since 1989, the Berlin Group has had a special relations-
hip with the Global Privacy Assembly (GPA) with a re-
gular “update report” from the chair during the regular 
annual conferences of the GPA. I presented this report 
to the Berlin Group at the 46th GPA Annual Conference 
2024. My office took over as chair of the Berlin Group in 
March 2021.
Four working papers published by the Berlin Group
Under my leadership, we were able to coordinate and 
publish working papers on facial recognition technology 
and digital central bank money in the Berlin Group in 
June 2024. In December 2024, we were able to publish 
further working papers on large language models (LLMs) 
and data sharing.50 
Facial recognition technologies are becoming increa-
singly widespread throughout the world, especially in 
view of current developments in the field of artificial 
33rd Activity Report 2024 34


<!-- page 35 -->
intelligence. This is a biometric technology that ana-
lyses people’s facial features and compares them with 
corresponding databases for identification purposes. 
In this way, it is possible not only to identify people 
in individual cases, but also to link information about 
their movement patterns, political views or health data 
over extended periods. Many different types of personal 
data can therefore be collected without the knowledge 
of data subjects and individual profiles of individuals 
can be created. In particular, their use in public spaces 
comes with high risks for the freedoms and rights of the 
people concerned. The same applies to facial recogniti-
on technologies that are intended to recognise emotions 
or even derive character traits from certain biological 
characteristics and are rejected by the Berlin Group in 
view of their inaccuracy and the extremely high risk of 
discrimination.
Digital central bank money refers to digital currencies 
backed by central banks. In contrast to the usual com-
mercial bank money used in digital payment transac-
tions, however, this is genuine central bank money 
which can only be created by the central bank. It can 
therefore be seen as a digital version of cash. Digital 
central bank money is becoming increasingly popular 
worldwide. The EU has also proposed the introduction 
of the digital euro.51 If there is insufficient considerati-
on of data protection, however, governments, central 
banks and the financial institutions involved could gain 
unprecedented access to the financial information of 
individuals and groups of individuals. If the digital euro 
is to be a success, citizens must be able to trust that the 
privacy-enhancing properties of cash will be preserved 
as far as possible and that not all of their transactions 
will be monitored or even subject to data retention.
LLMs are mathematical models that are developed using 
artificial intelligence (AI) data processing techniques 
to perform tasks connected with natural language. The 
state of the art has evolved considerably in recent years, 
with some LLMs demonstrating human-level and even 
expert-level natural language processing capabilities for 
certain tasks. As LLMs are complex technologies that 
entail various risks for data protection and privacy and 
any proportionate analysis must consider the technology 
from multiple perspectives. It is not only necessary to 
analyse LLMs in terms of the technology itself
but also from the perspective of the risks that it poses to 
privacy and data protection, including recommendations 
for reducing or eliminating those risks. Only with an un-
derstanding of LLMs from these different perspectives 
51 Commission proposal for the introduction of the digital euro, available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/DE/TXT/? uri=CELEX:52023PC0369
will data protection authorities be able to regulate and 
respond to this new situation effectively.
The idea of “data sharing” is based on the observation 
that data can be combined with other data sources to 
gain new insights, develop new products and services 
and drive social and economic growth. By collecting 
more comprehensive and diverse data sets, companies 
can also advance innovation and growth in the broa-
der social interest. Similarly, sharing of data by public 
authorities can enable better coordination and response 
to crises such as pandemics or natural disasters. At the 
same time, transferring data is often accompanied by 
the processing of personal data. Strict compliance with 
data protection principles is of the utmost importance 
when disclosing personal data. It is an essential pillar for 
maintaining trust between the data subject and the data 
user.
The “by-design” approach involves embedding data 
protection and ethical considerations into the design of 
data systems, thus ensuring that data is handled res-
ponsibly from the outset. By adopting this approach, 
organisations can improve their data management and 
build trust with data subjects, ultimately leading to more 
effective and responsible use and sharing of data.
The published working papers describe possible uses of 
the technologies under consideration and present risks 
and challenges as well as practical recommendations for 
data protection-compliant implementation.
Berlin Group meeting in Oslo and Brussels
At the meetings of the Berlin Group in Oslo in June 2024 
and in Brussels in November 2024, new priorities were 
selected in addition to the work on working papers. This 
includes the topics of “global opt-out preference signals” 
(e.g. in internet browsers),
“Confidential Cloud Computing / Trusted Execution En-
vironments”, the Digital Estate (“Data after life” or “Data 
post mortem”) and “Joint Communication and Sensing”.
The group also organised a discussion on “Future Fore-
sight”, which my office supported with a presentation. 
This is intended to identify relevant future technologies 
in the medium term.

4.5 Other committees
4.5.1 ETIAS Fundamental Rights Guidance 
Board
The future European Travel Information and Authori-
sation System (ETIAS) will affect around 1.4 billion 
third-country nationals from over 60 countries when 
they travel to one of 30 European countries. The orga-
nisational and technical implementation is currently 
underway . The ETIAS Fundamental Rights Guidance 
Board is committed to safeguarding fundamental 
rights, in particular the protection of personal data 
and non-discrimination.
ETIAS is aimed at third-country nationals who wish 
to enter the EU and are exempt from the visa require-
ment.52 The system is designed to check whether the 
presence of these people poses a risk to security, a risk 
of illegal immigration or a high epidemic risk. This 
check is carried out in particular by means of so-called 
ETIAS monitoring rules. This is a profiling algorithm 
based on specific risk indicators. It will also be possible 
to use the ETIAS large-scale database for the purpose of 
preventing, detecting and investigating terrorist offences 
and other serious crimes.
The requirements of the ETIAS Regulation are currently 
being consolidated and implemented at EU level and in 
the Member States. This also concerns numerous federal 
and state authorities in Germany.53 Preparations for the 
introduction of the ETIAS have been ongoing for several 
years. The introduction date has already been postponed 
several times.
In the reporting year, it was announced that the ETIAS is 
expected to go into operation in 2025.
The ETIAS Fundamental Rights Guidance Board was 
established in November 2022.54 The European Data 
Protection Supervisor, the European Union Agency for 
Fundamental Rights, the European Data Protection 
Board (EDPB), the Fundamental Rights Officer and a re-
presentative of the Consultation Forum for Fundamental 
Rights of Frontex, the European Border and Coast Guard 
Agency, are represented on the committee. The EDPB is 
represented by one of my staff, who was also re-elected 
as Chair of the ETIAS Fundamental Rights Guidance 
Board in the reporting year.
52 Regulation (EU) 2018/1240 establishing a European Travel Information and Authorisation System (ETIAS), available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/DE/TXT/?uri=CELEX%3A32018R1240&qid=1739900858368
53 See Article 2 Sections1 and 2 Federal Law Gazette 2023 Part I No. 106, available at: 
https://www.recht.bund.de/bgbl/1/2023/106/VO.html
54 31st AR No. 3.5.3
55 ETIAS Fundamental Rights Guidance Board on the Frontex website: 
https://www.frontex.europa.eu/what-we-do/etias/ etias-fundamental-rights-guidance-board/
56 Communiqué on the G7 Roundtable 2024 of 11 October 2024, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/EN/G7/ 2024_Communique.pdf
The Board met four times at Frontex headquarters 
during the reporting year and took part in numerous 
other Frontex working groups in an advisory capacity. 
It advises ad hoc in the meetings, but also develops 
and adopts positions and guidelines on various issues 
of importance for the implementation of the ETIAS on 
request or on its own initiative. For example, the guide-
line on discrimination risks in the context of the ETIAS 
monitoring rules was adopted and a second guideline on 
safeguarding the right to an effective legal remedy and a 
fair trial. The Board’s advisory activities also focused on 
data protection impact assessments, responsibilities and 
data subjects’ rights. along with the methodology for the 
ETIAS monitoring rules.
Further information on the ETIAS Fundamental Rights 
Guidance Board, its annual reports and programme of 
work are also available online.55

4.2.3 Report from the CSC
4.5.2 G7 DPA Roundtable
The data protection authorities of the G7 countries (G7 
Data Protection Authorities – DPAs) came together for 
their annual roundtable meeting in Rome in October 
2024 as part of the Italian G7 presidency . The discus-
sions focussed in particular on the topics of Data Free 
Flow with Trust (DFFT), i.e. the issue of trustworthy 
international data transmission, and current develop-
ments and data protection challenges in the field of 
artificial intelligence (AI).
In preparation for the conference in Rome, the data 
protection authorities of the G7 countries worked 
together in three working groups on the topics of “Data 
Free Flow with Trust” (DFFT), “Emerging Technologies” 
(current technological developments) and “Enforcement 
Cooperation” (international cooperation in the area of 
law enforcement). As in previous years, the results of the 
working groups were summarised in a communiqué.56 
Data Free Flow with Trust
With regard to DFFT, the communiqué recognises the 
economic and social benefits that arise from cross-bor-
der data flows. At the same time, however, the G7 DPAs 
33rd Activity Report 2024 36


<!-- page 37 -->
also point out the risks to the protection of personal data 
and privacy that such data transfers entail. The G7 data 
protection authorities therefore emphasise once again 
that trust is an essential component of the DFFT concept 
and that high data protection standards are a basic 
prerequisite for the free movement of data. To this end, 
the G7 data protection authorities reaffirm the approach 
of working towards commonalities and elements of con-
vergence between existing regulatory approaches and 
transfer instruments in order to promote the interaction 
(interoperability) of different legal systems and instru-
ments.
To develop this approach to implementing the DFFT 
concept, which is shared in particular by the digital 
ministers of the G7 countries, the G7 DPA Roundtable 
conducted a comparative analysis of different certificati-
on mechanisms for cross-border data transfers in 2024,57 
which was adopted unanimously in Rome. The project 
was initiated by my office and developed together with 
the French data protection authority (Commission Natio-
nale de l’Informatique et des Libertés - CNIL) within the 
framework of the DFFT working group of the G7 DPAs. 
The analysis points out similarities and differences 
between certifications under the GDPR and the so-cal-
led Global Cross-Border Privacy Rules (CBPR) and thus 
makes a practical contribution to promoting convergen-
ce between these transfer instruments. In this context, 
my office is also the only EU data protection authority to 
take part in the meetings of the members of the Global 
CBPR system58 (the so-called Global CBPR Forum) in an 
advisory capacity, presenting the work of the G7 and ad-
vocating for a higher level of protection under the CBPR 
rules in the interests of convergence.
In 2024, my office co-chaired the DFFT working group 
of the G7 DPA Roundtable together with the Information 
Commissioner’s Office of the United Kingdom (ICO UK).
Artificial intelligence
The G7 DPA Roundtable continued its work on AI last 
year. Building on the “Statement on Generative AI” 
adopted in Tokyo in 2023 59, it focused in particular on 
57 Analysis of different certification mechanisms for cross-border data transfers by the G7 Roundtable of 11 October 2024 available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/EN/G7/2024_Comparative-analysis.pdf
58 Currently the United States, Canada, Japan, Australia, South Korea, Singapore, Taiwan, Mexico, Australia, the United Kingdom, Dubai, Bermuda and Mauritius as 
“Associates” .
59 G7 Roundtable Statement on Generative AI of 21 June 2023, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/EN/G7/ 2023_Statement-AI.pdf
60 G7 Roundtable Statement on the Regulation of AI Technologies of 11 October 2024, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/ Downloads/EN/G7/2024_Statement-Trustworthy-AI.pdf
61 G7 Roundtable Statement on Artificial Intelligence and Children of 11 October 2024, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/ SharedDocs/Downloads/EN/G7/2024_Statement-AI-Children.pdf
62 Action Plan 2024 of the G7 Roundtable of 11 October 2024, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/EN/G7/ 2024_Action-Plan.pdf
questions of AI governance and the specific impact of AI 
technologies on children and adolescents.
The Roundtable adopted two declarations on this sub-
ject, in which my office played a key role.
With the “Statement on the Role of Data Protection 
Authorities in Fostering Trustworthy AI”,60 the G7 DPAs 
make it clear that data protection authorities must be 
closely involved in the regulation of AI technologies 
in order to enable and ensure the lawful use of AI in a 
way that respects fundamental rights. Data protection 
authorities already have extensive expertise in areas that 
are central to AI regulation, for example in relation to 
data-based processing and issues of fairness, transparen-
cy and security of technological systems. The “Statement 
on AI and Children” 61 points out potential data pro-
tection-related effects and risks of AI technologies for 
children and adolescents and calls on developers and 
users of AI systems to take these aspects into account 
when developing AI systems and to design AI systems in 
an age-appropriate manner.
In addition to the communiqué issued in Rome, the G7 
DPA Roundtable has also adopted an action plan62 of con-
crete measures to translate the positions of the G7 data 
protection authorities into practical results.
4.5.3 High-Level Group on EU data retention
The EU working group “High-Level Group on access 
to data for effective law enforcement” has developed 
recommendations on access to online data by law en-
forcement authorities. My office sends an expert to the 
European Data Protection Board (EDPB). In particular, 
new proposals must take into account the case law of 
the European Court of Justice (ECJ) on data retention.
The High-Level Group on Access to Data for Effective 
Law Enforcement (HLG) was established in June 2023 on 
the initiative of the Swedish Council presidency and the 
European Commission. In accordance with its mandate, 
the HLG pursues the goal of ensuring the availability of 
effective law enforcement instruments for combating 
crime and contributing to the improvement of public 
security in the digital age while fully respecting funda-
33rd Activity Report 202437


<!-- page 38 -->
mental rights. To this end, the committee is to make 
recommendations for further development of EU law.63
The HLG meets at plenary level and in three working 
groups. Its work has essentially been carried out by 
representatives of the law enforcement authorities in 
the Member States. The EDPB has also sent experts. I 
am represented by one of my colleagues as an EDPB 
expert on Working Group 2 (“Access to data at rest in a 
provider‘s system”), which deals with the access of law 
enforcement authorities to data in the systems of service 
providers.
In June 2024, the HLG published 42 recommendations 
for further development of policy and legislation in the 
European Union as a preliminary result of its work. 
64The recommendations relate in particular to the topics 
of encryption, cooperation between business and law 
enforcement authorities and the need for harmonised 
rules on data retention.
On 4 November 2024, the EDPB issued a statement on 
the recommendations.65 My colleagues acted as lead 
rapporteurs on the EDPB. The statement addresses the 
EDPB’s initial but not definitive concerns, as some of the 
measures proposed by the HLG could lead to signifi-
cant encroachments on the fundamental rights of data 
subjects.
The EDPB expresses understanding for the desire to har-
monise regulations in the EU, but at the same time urges 
strict compliance with the requirements of ECJ case law.
Furthermore, the EDPB is calling for all technical requi-
rements of providers of electronic communications ser-
vices that have the potential to encroach on individual 
fundamental rights always to be made on the basis of a 
legal provision that safeguards those fundamental rights 
and that is regarded as necessary and proportionate in 
a democratic society, so that a measure directed only 
against individuals or small groups of people does not 
weaken the protection of the confidentiality of commu-
nications for all users without distinction. 
With reference to the Podchasov ruling of the ECHR, the 
EDPB also points in its statement to potentially dispro-
portionate encroachments on fundamental rights and 
risks to technical security that could result from brea-

on%20Access%20to%20Data%20for%20Effective%20Law%20Enforcement_en.pdf

on%20access%20to%20data%20for%20effective%20law%20enforcement_en.pdf
king away from end-to-end encryption for all users of

The concerns expressed by the EDPB in the statement 
were only partially addressed in the HLG’s “Concluding 
Report” published at the end of November. 66The report 
calls on the European Commission to introduce Europe-

I will provide critical support for this progress and work 
to ensure that the protection of privacy is not under-
mined by weakening the security of communications at 
a technical level.
