Legislation5
5.1 Health and research
5.1.1 Federal Institute for Prevention and 
Education in Medicine
With the Act to Strengthen Public Health, the federal 
government introduced a law this year to establish 
the Federal Institute for Prevention and Education in 
Medicine (BIPAM). The BIPAM is to take over the tasks 
previously performed by the Federal Centre for Health 
Education (BZgA) as well as parts of the previous role 
covered by the Robert Koch Institute (RKI). I take a 
critical view of some of the data processing involved in 
this transfer of duties.
The BIPAM Establishment Act enables the newly estab-
lished federal institute to process data comprehensively, 
in particular health data, which may only be processed 
as a special category of personal data in accordance with 
Art. 9 GDPR under strict conditions. BIPAM is to process 
data for the purpose of analysing the state of health of 
the population, the health effects of climate and the 
environment and health-related behaviour in order to 
develop preventative measures based on the findings.
In order to legitimise any processing of health data that 
the BIPAM is to carry out in the future, a specific legal 
basis is required that meets the standards of the GDPR. 
This also applies in particular to personal data that was 
previously processed by the RKI on the basis of decla-
rations of consent from data subjects and is now to be 
transferred to the BIPAM and further processed there. 
Both in the run-up to and during the departmental 
consultations, I have repeatedly pointed out that the use 
of existing declarations of consent brings with it legal 
uncertainties, as the extent to which these can be carried 
over (change of controller) is not established, in parti-
cular with regard to the strict requirements of Art. 9(2) 
point (a) GDPR.
As a result, I was at least able to ensure that a note was 
added to the explanatory memorandum to the effect that 
a specific examination is still required in each individual 
case to determine whether the specific declarations of 
consent can actually be used as the legal basis for data 
processing by the BIPAM.
On 17 July 2024, the draft Act to Strengthen Public Health 
was passed by the Federal Cabinet. According to this, 
the Federal Institute should be established by 1 January 
2025, although the discussion about a possible name 
change of the institute to the “Federal Institute of Public 
Health” (BIÖG) had not yet been finalised.
Following the break-up of the governing coalition at 
the end of 2024, the bill did not reach the Bundestag, 
meaning that it remains to be seen whether and to what 
extent the proposed legislation will be pursued in the 
21st legislative period.
5.1.2 Act to Strengthen Healthcare Provision
The Act to Strengthen Healthcare Provision in Mu-
nicipalities (Healthcare Provision Strengthening Act 
- GVSG) creates additional legal foundations to combat 
misconduct in the healthcare sector in a more targeted 
way.
The existing legal bases of Section 197a of the German 
Social Code Book V (SGB V) for combating malpractice 
in the healthcare system are extended by the GVSG. This 
primarily concerns data from service providers, al-
though the data of insured persons may also be affected. 
Ad hoc sharing of data between so-called “anti-mal-
practice organisations” and other public bodies in the 
healthcare sector will be expanded in strict compliance 
with the purpose limitation that the exchange of data 
mut be essential for the prevention, detection, identifi-
cation or combating of misconduct in the healthcare sec-
tor. Independently of any specific case, anti-malpractice 
organisations may amalgamate their own databases with 
those of other health insurance funds, the state associa-
tions of the health insurance funds and the
National Association of Statutory Health Insurance 
Funds (GKV-SV) so that they can serve as training ma-
33rd Activity Report 202439


<!-- page 40 -->
terials for artificial intelligence that will later be able to 
recognise recurring patterns of malpractice.
At the same time, the regulation includes a mandate for 
the GKV-SV to submit a concept to the Federal Ministry 
of Health (BMG) for the establishment of a centrali-
sed nationwide fraud prevention database, which will 
provide the health insurance funds with information 
on issues or discrepancies that point to malpractice in 
the healthcare system. The GKV-SV is to commission an 
external expert opinion for this concept, which should 
also address aspects of data protection law and the need 
for further legislative regulation. In discussions with 
the BMG, I was promised that I would be involved in the 
review of the fraud prevention database concept.
Furthermore, the BMG has adjusted data protection in 
two places at my suggestion: in the case of the ad hoc 
transmission of data of insured persons, pseudonymisa-
tion already takes place before transmission if there are 
no indications of personal involvement in malpractice, 
followed by anonymisation as soon as the processing 
purpose permits. In the case of the ad hoc transfer of the 
data of insured persons to the merged database for arti-
ficial intelligence training, any reference to the insured 
person must be erased before the transfer.
5.1.3 Act to Reform Emergency Care
In the current legislative process for the reform of 
emergency care, some of my points of criticism have 
been taken up – others, however, remain unresolved.
The Act to Reform Emergency Care pursues several 
health policy reform objectives: On the one hand, the 
existing 116117 appointment service number is to be 
combined with roles in the area of acute case arrange-
ments (acute control centre). The acute control centre 
is to be networked with the rescue control centres as a 
component of a health management system. 
A legal basis is also being created for processing per-
sonal data in this context. This will enable the acute 
control centres to record and store calls. Initially, it was 
planned that even conversations that were only for the 
purpose of agreeing a meeting would also be recorded. 
I criticised this in my statement and in my discussions 
with the Federal Ministry of Health (BMG). The BMG 
has tightened this up so that the intention is now only to 
record calls that involve acute cases.
Another component of the reform project includes 
integrated emergency centres, which will be located at 
approved hospitals. These centres consist of the emer-
gency department of an authorised hospital, an emer-
gency practice of the Association of Statutory Health 
67 32nd AR No. 3.3.5
Insurance Physicians and a triage centre, and provide 
needs-based initial medical care around the clock. The 
emergency department, the emergency practice and the 
triage centre are to be digitally networked in order to fa-
cilitate transfer of patients’ personal data without media 
discontinuity. In this regard, I have repeatedly criticised 
the lack of clarity of individual regulations. However, my 
criticism was not taken up with regard to the provision 
of Section 123(2) sentence 5 SGB V new version. This 
standard stipulates that the cooperation partners of the 
integrated emergency centre are authorised to process 
the personal data required for needs-based management 
of those seeking help and to transfer this data among 
one another. As part of my advisory mandate, I pointed 
out to the legislature that, in my opinion, it is not clear 
from the regulatory text whether only master data can 
be transferred or health data too.
My criticism of Section 133a(3) sentence 3 SGB V (new 
version) was also not taken up, according to which ser-
vice providers transmit personal data of those seeking 
help to the control centre under certain conditions. 
Here, too, it seems that sensitive health data will be 
transferred. It is also not clear to callers which data is 
to be transmitted in this context. Against this backg-
round, a regulation that makes the further processing 
of the data transparent would have been desirable. I 
have assured the BMG that I will be available for further 
consultations.
5.2 Security
5.2.1 Proposal to amend the Security Clearan -
ce Act
In many areas, the Security Clearance Act (SÜG) is not 
fit for purpose. Following its review , the federal gover-
nment presented a draft law that unfortunately missed 
the opportunity to update the regulations sufficiently 
and make them fit for the future. The draft failed to 
create legal certainty and thus trust, particularly in 
terms of data protection law . Despite some effective 
approaches, the initiative falls far short of its potential.
In my previous Activity Report, I had already expres-
sed the concern that the legislature might miss the 
opportunity to modernise the SÜG.67 It is not only the 
increasing risk level that makes a fundamental revision 
necessary, but also proper digitalisation.
My inspection and consulting activities give me a com-
prehensive insight into the security clearance process. I 
was able to contribute my experience to the departmen-
tal consultations and at least some of my suggestions 
33rd Activity Report 2024 40


<!-- page 41 -->
were taken on board. In this context, I particularly 
welcome measures to reduce replication of clearance 
procedures. The new version of Section 20 SÜG also 
makes the SÜG more practicable; the creation of a legal 
basis for digital data processing for the purpose of task 
fulfilment was long overdue.
Overall, however, the draft falls far short of its potential. 
The SÜG still lacks a coherent overall concept. Referen-
ce to Section 1(2) No. 4 SÜG as the legal standard will 
continue to be made in future. This is used in particular 
to check all the employees of an authority. It remains 
unclear whether this is to be a one-off or permanent 
clearance. 
Moreover, the opportunity has not been taken to revise 
the definition of security-sensitive activities within the 
meaning of Section 1(2) SÜG and to adapt it to the reali-
ties of life. This would have placed the scope of applica-
tion of the SÜG on a new, solid foundation and expanded 
it.
As part of a comprehensive reorganisation, it would also 
have made sense to include the provisions on self-pro-
tection recently established in the BVerfSchG and BNDG. 
These supplement Section 4 SÜG (in conjunction with 
the federal government’s classified information direc-
tive) and are part of material protection of classified 
information that has been regulated in the SÜG alongs-
ide protection of classified information in relation to 
personnel since 2017.
Furthermore, I consider the inclusion of associated per-
sons, which goes along with an expansion of the scope 
of the clearance process, to be disproportionate to the 
extent planned.
The number of associated persons to be included will 
probably be significantly higher than the number of 
multiple clearance checks in classified information 
and sabotage protection avoided by the new regulation. 
Equating the legal status that associated persons have 
in sabotage protection with their status in protection of 
classified information is not plausible. It is not evident 
that security risks that apply to other persons involved 
in the area of sabotage protection are the same as in the 
area of classified information protection.
It would also be appropriate to provide me with effective 
remedial powers. As I currently only have the instru-
ments of official complaints, requests for a statement 
and warnings at my disposal, the legal situation here is 
still incoherent. In the event of data protection viola-
tions, a fine may be imposed on companies under the 
GDPR. This does not apply if personal data is processed 
in connection with a security clearance check. As a 
result, processing within the scope of a security clearan-
ce check can be controlled considerably less effectively 
than any other data processing.
Internet research
Furthermore, the significant expansion of internet 
research is questionable from a data protection per-
spective. The elimination of gradation leads to further 
encroachment on the rights of persons who are subject 
to a simple security clearance check. In addition, checks 
are carried out twice as often (with each update). Any as-
sociated person is treated in the same way as the person 
undergoing the check. I consider the current regulation 
on research in exceptional cases under Section 12(5) 
sentence 2 SÜG to be adequate.
In addition, there are extended disclosure requirements 
for usernames, electronic contact details and, for the 
first-time, the option of automated photo comparison of 
individuals within the meaning of Section 3(3) SÜG. The 
obligation to provide information without a time limit 
can lead to the provision of a large number of email 
addresses if, for reasons of data security, a new email 
address was assigned for each use. It would have made 
sense, in my opinion, to have limited the obligation to 
provide usernames and contact details to those that have 
been used in the last twelve months.
I also consider the extension of the research to all pub-
licly accessible internet platforms, including social net-
works, to be too broad without any concrete definitions. 
It is now entirely unclear to individuals which areas of 
the internet will be searched and by what means.
Individual privacy settings and sharing information in 
closed areas only provide limited protection. However, if 
it is no longer clear where protected spaces exist in the 
digital world, this can lead to a so-called “chilling effect”, 
similar to video surveillance in public spaces. Data sub-
jects adapt their behaviour and consciously or uncons-
ciously refrain from exercising their basic rights and 
freedoms for fear that their own deviation from “nor-
mal” behaviour could be seen as “suspicious”. However, 
the SÜG specifically does not want blanket monitoring. 
Appropriate definitions would create legal certainty as 
to the content that may be accessed and the means that 
may be used to do so. It is not only unclear what is still 
publicly accessible, e.g. during registration or an admis-
sion procedure, but also what the distinction between 
social networks and individual communication is.
Digitalisation
The SÜG distinguishes between a file (security file or 
security clearance file) and a file system. It is already 
permissible to keep a security file in electronic format 
under Section 18(6) sentence 1 SÜG. No change was the-
refore necessary. A distinction must be made between a 
33rd Activity Report 202441


<!-- page 42 -->
file and a file system. In the past, a file system was used 
by public authorities solely for the purpose of being able 
to find, identify and process a file more quickly (prelimi-
nary administration). In future, file systems will also be 
permitted for easier processing (task fulfilment).
Under the guise of digitalisation, the draft law suggests a 
mixture of various processing purposes. As a result, the-
re is a risk that personal data from the security clearance 
check will be collected at different locations without 
being managed in the security file. This would be the 
case, for example, if individual documents are stored un-
sorted on a drive and an email inbox is used to store let-
ters or letters are stored and sorted according to creator 
or format regardless of the associated security clearance 
check. Several checks have shown that precisely cases 
such as these are already occurring. Here too, a modern 
structure for the law, taking account of the processing 
purposes of process management, task fulfilment and 
documentation, would have provided more clarity.
Processing of personal data of third parties
The additional authorisation to store personal data 
of uninvolved third parties that is not required raises 
serious data protection concerns. There have been 
no objections to the storage of information relating 
to associated third parties – parties to proceedings, 
authors or addressees of official or other letters – if this 
is necessary to assess that information. The situation is 
different with personal data that is irrelevant and not 
required once the check has been completed. Processing 
encroaches disproportionately on the fundamental right 
to informational self-determination of third parties who 
have no connection at all to the specific security clearan-
ce check. It is true that their data may not be specifically 
requested. However, the principle of data minimisation 
is clearly ignored here.
5.2.2 Modernisation of the Federal Police Act 
still pending
The Federal Police Act is in urgent need of revision, 
not only in view of European and constitutional data 
protection requirements. I have been involved in the 
legislative process from the very beginning. The first 
reading in the German Bundestag and an expert hea-
ring took place in 2024. By the end of the 20th legislati-
ve period, nothing more had happened and the project 
remained unfinished again.
68 31st AR No. 4.5
69 Judgement of the Federal Constitutional Court of 20 April 2016 (Ref. 1 BvR 966/09, 1 BvR 1140/09), available at: 
https://www.bundesverfassungsgericht.de/SharedDocs/Entscheidungen/DE/2016/04/rs20160420_1bvr096609.html
70 BT-Drs. 20/10406, available at: 
https://dserver.bundestag.de/btd/20/104/2010406.pdf
71 32nd AR No. 3.3.2
Unfortunately, the legislature has once again failed to fi-
nalise the restructuring of the Federal Police Act (BPolG), 
which was restarted in 2022 and had already failed in the 
19th legislative period. The revision of the law, which 
largely dates back to 1994 (when the Federal Police was 
still called the Federal Border Protection Force), is still 
urgently needed:
the implementation of Directive (EU) 2016/680 of 27 April 
2016 (JHA Directive) with regard to effective remedial 
powers of the data protection supervisory authorities 
has not yet taken place in relation to the BPolG; all I can 
currently do is complain formally to the Federal Mi-
nistry of the Interior about violations in this area. Art. 
47(2) of the JHA Directive, on the other hand, provides 
for further effective remedies such as ordering the 
rectification or erasure of personal data and restricting 
processing. The deadline for implementation expired in 
May 2018, and the EU Commission has already initiated 
two treaty infringement proceedings against the Federal 
Republic of Germany in this context.68 In addition, the 
ruling of the Federal Constitutional Court on hypotheti-
cal re-collection of data69 has not yet found its way into 
the BPolG.
Both points were included in the draft of a law to rest-
ructure the BPolG70 although in my opinion the remedial 
powers provided for my office did not comply with Euro-
pean law. Along with an additional materiality threshold, 
the obligation to object to data protection violations in 
advance and the explicit exclusion of the power to issue 
an erasure order in the explanatory memorandum to 
the law would also be an inappropriate restriction on 
the effectiveness of remedial powers in contravention 
of European law. My office had already criticised this 
in the past with regard to corresponding provisions in 
the Federal Criminal Police Office Act and the Customs 
Investigation Services Act.
I have been constructively involved in the legislative 
process since the departmental consultation and some 
of my suggestions have been taken into account in the 
latest draft version.71 I was able to comment on the 
remaining points of criticism at a public hearing of the 
German Bundestag’s Committee on Home Affairs in 
April 2024.
Here I raised concerns about, among other things, the 
abolition of the so-called “opening order” (Section 36 
BPolG) when new IT systems are introduced, under 
which I must be consulted in advance according to the 
33rd Activity Report 2024 42


<!-- page 43 -->
current legal situation. Involving my office at an early 
stage ensures that data protection concerns are taken 
into account from the outset, thus avoiding time-consu-
ming and costly subsequent adjustments.
I also commented on the planned expansion of the use 
of so-called bodycams to include places that are not 
open to the public, the extension of the transfer of air-
line passenger data and the future obligation to carry out 
security clearance checks on all persons working for the 
Federal Police.
One positive aspect of the draft law is that the parti-
cularly intrusive instruments of telecommunication 
surveillance of sources and online searches have been 
dispensed with.72
Regrettably, the proposed legislation could not be finali-
sed in the 20th legislative period of the German Bun-
destag. However, a revision of the BPolG is still urgently 
required, not only in light of the data protection conside-
rations mentioned above.
The law is generally in significant need of modernisa-
tion. I therefore appeal to the legislature to finalise the 
project at the third attempt. I will be happy to provide 
further advice on data protection issues.
5.2.3 BKA II decision of the Federal Constituti -
onal Court
The Federal Constitutional Court (BVerfG) has made a 
fundamental decision on data that the Federal Crimi-
nal Police Office (BKA) stores in the police information 
network for precautionary reasons. This confirms my 
advice and the findings of my inspection and will have 
far-reaching effects.
The BVerfG has declared the regulations according 
to which the BKA stores personal data in the police 
information network to be partially incompatible with 
the Basic Law.73 This primarily concerns the storage of 
information about suspects for precautionary purposes. 
The issue here is that data about suspects can be stored 
in the information network even if the suspicion of a 
criminal offence against them cannot be substantiated. 
This means that individuals whose data is stored in the 
information network are not necessarily convicted of-
fenders. Minor offences are also included in principle.
Retaining the data of suspects for precautionary purpo-
ses requires special justification. The court has clarified 
that precautionary storage is an independent encro-
achment on fundamental rights and that this may only 
72 BfDI statement of 18 April 2024, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/DE/DokumenteBfDI/ Stellungnahmen/2024/StgN_Neustrukturierung-Bundespolizeigesetz.html
73 Judgement of the BVerfG of 1 October 2024 – 1 BvR 1160/19, available at: 
https://www.bundesverfassungsgericht.de/SharedDocs/Entscheidungen/ DE/2024/10/rs20241001_1bvr116019.html
be authorised by law if the police authority makes an 
independent predictive decision.
This is associated with basic statements on purpose 
limitation, storage thresholds and erasure regulations.
I see the judgement as confirmation of my previous legal 
opinions, which have also been reflected in many years 
of consulting and monitoring practice. The judgement 
will have a direct and significant impact on the police 
information network in particular.
The outcome of the decision will therefore also affect 
the reorganisation of the BKA Data Regulation. I am 
in favour of ensuring that data categories cannot be 
changed to counteract the above decision. In particular, 
it must be ensured that the predictive decision required 
by the court – a so-called negative prediction – cannot be 
circumvented. I am also in favour of a clear data struc-
ture. This must facilitate police work. However, it must 
not go beyond what is necessary for fulfilment of tasks 
and must always be non-discriminatory.
In addition, the court declared a provision on covert 
interventions against contacts of dangerous persons to 
be invalid. I will take this into account in my monitoring 
of covert investigative measures.
5.3 Home affairs and justice
5.3.1 First Act to Amend the Federal Data Pro -
tection Act
At the beginning of 2024, the federal government intro-
duced its draft bill “to amend the Federal Data Protecti-
on Act” in the German Bundestag. My office supported 
the legislative process and was particularly keen to 
implement the changes identified as necessary by the 
Data Protection Conference (DSK) in its evaluation of 
the Federal Data Protection Act (BDSG).
The draft of a first law to amend the Federal Data Protec-
tion Act is intended to take account of the agreements 
relevant to data protection law in the coalition agree-
ment for the 20th legislative period and to implement 
the results of the evaluation of the BDSG by the Federal 
Ministry of the Interior. This includes, for example, ins-
titutionalisation of the DSK, which is provided for in the 
draft law. In addition, the DSK is expressly to be granted 
control over its own rules of procedure. The federal 
government did not include any further steps, such as 
33rd Activity Report 202443


<!-- page 44 -->
making DSK decisions more binding on its members or 
setting up an office for it.
The draft law contains some necessary clarifications, 
which I welcome. However, it falls short in particular in 
terms of the revisions identified as necessary by the DSK 
in its evaluation of the BDSG.
The draft bill contains important clarifications on 
Section 18 BDSG that I proposed for cooperation of the 
German supervisory authorities on European matters. 
The supervisory authorities of the federal states and my 
office should also always speak with one voice at Euro-
pean level in order to give more weight to the German 
positions there. Consequently, I welcome the clarificati-
on that a common position of the German supervisory 
authorities in European matters is required not only in 
the consistency procedure (Art. 63 to 65 GDPR), but also 
in the cooperation procedure (Art. 60 GDPR) and the 
urgency procedure (Art. 66 GDPR).
Further corrections to unsatisfactory regulations in the 
BDSG are, however, necessary. The main points to be 
mentioned from my statement submitted in the parlia-
mentary procedure are as follows:
 → Expanding and strengthening the powers of supervi-
sory authorities under the BDSG with the effect that 
it is possible to enforce measures by coercive means 
against public bodies, for example by creating a sec-
tor-specific exemption regulation within the meaning 
of Section 17 VwVG, and to order immediate enforce-
ment by deleting Section 20(7) BDSG. In addition, the 
provision of Section 43(3) BDSG should be repealed, 
thus enabling fines to be imposed on public bodies. 
So far, it has only been possible to punish violations 
of data protection regulations by public bodies with 
a warning. 
Under the GDPR, warnings are only intended for 
minor offences, while fines are to be imposed for 
more serious offences. This second level of sanctions 
is currently not available in relation to public bodies.
 → Extending my supervisory responsibility for vio-
lations committed by employees of public federal 
bodies who act as controllers (so-called employee 
excess). According to current legislation, I am not 
responsible for the corresponding processing of per-
sonal data by the person employed in the event of an 
employee excess. Pursuant to Section 40 BDSG, the 
data protection supervisory authority of the respecti-
ve federal state is responsible, as the person con-
cerned does not meet the requirements of the bodies 
named in Section 9(1) BDSG. To ensure consistency 
74 ECJ, judgment of 7 December 2023, case no. C-634/21 - SCHUFA Holding (scoring), available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/de/ TXT/?uri=CELEX:62021CJ0634
through one supervisory authority, I believe that the 
extension of my responsibility described above, for 
example in a supplementary provision in Section 9 
BDSG, is necessary.
 → The new regulations on the scoring procedure 
included in Section 37a BDSG-E which, according 
to the explanatory memorandum to the law, are 
based on the ECJ ruling of 7 December 2023 in the 
matter of SCHUFA Holding,74 give cause for concern 
both from a fundamental point of view and from 
a number of individual perspectives, and I have 
suggested appropriate improvements. These include 
reviewing whether this provision is in line with the 
requirements of Art. 23 GDPR on the restriction of 
data subjects’ rights and the incorporation of a requi-
rement for formal certification of the scientifically 
accepted mathematical-statistical methods on which 
the scoring is based by an independent body.
As a result of the premature end of the 20th legislative 
period of the German Bundestag, the draft law has come 
to a halt. I hope that the 21st German Bundestag will 
take up the proposal again promptly and consider the 
amendments that I have proposed.
5.3.2 NIS-2 Implementation and Cybersecuri -
ty Strengthening Act
The NIS-2 Implementation and Cyber Security Strengt-
hening Act (NIS2UmsuCG) aims to strengthen IT secu-
rity in Germany by imposing cyber security measures 
and reporting obligations on numerous organisations. 
In view of the close links to IT security , this also affects 
data protection.
The Directive on measures for a common high level of 
cyber security across the Union (NIS2 Directive) adopted 
by the EU in December 2022 establishes rules at Europe-
an level for so-called critical and essential organisations 
and requires, among other things, the implementation 
of cyber security measures and reporting procedures 
for significant security incidents. The EU Member States 
were required to transpose the NIS2 Directive into 
national law by 17 October 2024. Unfortunately, imple-
mentation could not be completed in Germany within 
this period.
Cyber and information security is of great importance 
in digitalisation. Digitalisation and cyber security are in-
extricably linked to the issue of data protection. Threats 
or even breaches of IT security also regularly affect the 
protection of personal data. Data protection cannot be 
achieved without properly functioning IT security. It is 
33rd Activity Report 2024 44


<!-- page 45 -->
therefore sensible and necessary to coordinate the two 
areas closely. The NIS2UmsuCG thus offers an oppor-
tunity to provide better protection for the government, 
the economy and society in the digital space.
The core of the NIS2UmsuCG is the new version of the 
Act on the Federal Office for Information Security (BSI 
Act – BSIG). The BSI has the central role of monitoring 
the planned measures, supporting the organisations 
concerned and acting as the central body for informa-
tion security at national level. Its role is also to collect 
information on attacks and vulnerabilities. Vulnerabili-
ties are security loopholes that third parties can exploit 
to gain unauthorised access to or influence ICT products 
or services. They pose a major threat to IT security 
and therefore also to data protection. In this context, 
my office has already pointed out in the past that the 
opportunity should be taken within the framework of 
the new BSIG regulation to outline a clear path from the 
detection of a loophole to its closure and to prescribe its 
consistent and immediate closure.75 The risks of leaving 
a loophole open simply outweigh the benefits of keeping 
it open. Unfortunately, the government draft of the 
NIS2UmsuCG did not include any specifications on this. 
In my public statement of 4 November 2024,76 I therefore 
asked the Bundestag Committee on Home Affairs, which 
is the lead committee, to add corresponding provisions 
to the law.
The new version of the BSIG planned by the NIS2Um-
suCG not only provides for new powers and data 
processing for the BSI, but also some restrictions on 
the rights of data subjects under data protection law. I 
have therefore also suggested that these long-standing 
restrictions be evaluated in terms of their necessity for 
the work of the BSI and their proportionality with regard 
to the rights and freedoms of data subjects.
Protecting information security is not just the respon-
sibility of one public authority. IT security and data 
protection supervisory authorities must work together to 
achieve the highest possible level of security. The NIS2 
Directive therefore provides for forwarding of relevant 
information from the IT security supervisory authorities 
to the data protection supervisory authorities. Unfor-
tunately, the government draft of the NIS2UmsuCG has 
only implemented this inadequately and is thus out of 
line with the Directive. I pointed this out in the statem-
ent mentioned above and made specific suggestions for 
improvement.
75 Letter from the BfDI of 18 September 2023 to six ministries of 03.07.2023, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/ Downloads/DE/DokumenteBfDI/Dokumente-allg/2023/Schwachstellen-schließen-Sicherheit-stärken.pdf
76 Statement of the BfDI of 4 November 2024, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/DE/DokumenteBfDI/ Stellungnahmen/2024/NIS2-UmsuCG-Bundestag
77 32nd AR No. 8.1
Unfortunately, the NIS2UmsuCG could not be passed 
because the Bundestag election was brought forward. 
The legislative proposal must therefore be restarted in 
the 21st legislative period. 
A new draft should retain the positive impetus from the 
formulation guidance for the bill made public in the 
Bundestag Committee on Home Affairs and also take up 
the suggestions for improvement made by experts in the 
committee hearing and by me in my written statements. 
For example, the regulations on how the BSI and the 
federal administration deal with vulnerabilities in the 
formulation guidance should be incorporated into a new 
draft, as should the provisions on transferring infor-
mation from the BSI to the data protection supervisory 
authorities in line with the Directive. In addition, the 
coordination between the NIS 2 supervisory authorities 
and the data protection supervisory authorities in cases 
involving fines should be clearly regulated in order to 
avoid the imposition of double financial penalties. If any 
further powers of the BSI involving encroachment on 
fundamental rights are included in the new draft, they 
must be proportionate. The constitutionally essential 
balancing of the weight of intervention can be achie-
ved, for example, by involving independent supervisory 
authorities in the exercise of powers.
In the new legislative period, I will again play a const-
ructive role in the deliberations on the law and, once the 
law has been passed, I will advocate for it its implemen-
tation in a way that takes appropriate account of the 
interests of both cyber security and data protection.
5.3.3 OZG Amendment Act
The legislature has now passed the OZG Amendment 
Act, which was still undergoing parliamentary consul-
tation when my previous Activity Report was publis-
hed. Some changes have been made to the draft in the 
law that has been passed.
In my previous Activity Report, I analysed the draft 
amendment to the Online Access Act (OZG) from the 
legal perspective of data protection.77 The law has now 
been passed and has come into force. The original draft 
was amended at the recommendation of the German 
Bundestag Committee on Home Affairs and the Media-
tion Committee. The following amendments relating to 
the OZG should be emphasised in particular.
Centralisation of citizen accounts for the BundID, in 
future DeutschlandID
33rd Activity Report 202445


<!-- page 46 -->
The draft initially envisaged that the regional state 
accounts would be discontinued after a fixed transitio-
nal period of three years and that in future the federal 
government alone would provide one centralised citizen 
account, the BundID. The adopted law now provides 
for the possibility of a state account being automati-
cally transferred (“migrated”) to a federal account. 
The period of three years in which this can happen 
only begins when the conditions for such automated 
migration have been established. As migration will also 
raise data protection issues, the relevant supervisory 
authorities should be closely involved. At the same time, 
the law provides for the BundID to be developed into 
the “DeutschlandID” in future. It remains to be seen 
whether there will be specific changes and what they 
will entail in terms of content.
ID card as a means of identification for the citizen 
account
As in the draft, the law stipulates that citizens must 
authenticate themselves with their electronic ID card, 
i.e. with a high level of trust, if an administrative service 
requires identification and authentication (see below for 
the exceptions). On the recommendation of the Home 
Affairs Committee, a regulation was added according 
to which the ID card only needs to be used for initial 
authentication. After that, other means of authentication 
can also be used, which are approved for this purpose 
for a limited period by the Federal Ministry of Home 
Affairs (BMI) following a review by the Federal Office for 
Information Security (BSI). According to the explanato-
ry memorandum, the aim of the regulation is to create 
“low-threshold authentication options” until the EUDI 
Wallet is established. I am in favour of ensuring that this 
regulation does not lead to a reduction in the level of 
security.
Elster as a means of identification for the citizen ac-
count
According to the draft law, identification with Elster 
software certificates should be possible until 30 June 
2026 for administrative services that require at most a 
“substantial” level of trust. Indefinite extension of this 
deadline was an option for the BMI and the Federal Mi-
nistry of Finance (BMF). Contrary to my recommendati-
on, the time limit provision has been removed from the 
law. This means that authentication with Elster software 
certificates is always possible for administrative ser-
vices that require a “substantial” level of trust. General 
authorisation of Elster software certificates for adminis-
trative services at the “substantial” level of trust should 
be rejected, however, as the certificates do not provide a 
correspondingly reliable means of identification. As an 
alternative, consideration should be given to improving 
Elster software certifications so that they always do meet 
the “substantial” level of trust.
Elster as a means of identification for the organisation 
account
Unlike the citizen account, use of the organisation ac-
count is mandatory for companies within the meaning of 
the Corporate Base Data Register Act. This was already 
included in the draft law. According to the draft version, 
however, Elster software certificates were only to be per-
mitted as a means of identification for the organisation 
account for a transitional period of five years, although 
this period could be extended indefinitely by the BMI 
and the BMF. Contrary to my recommendation, this 
deadline has also been removed so that Elster software 
certificates can also be used permanently for authenti-
cation for the organisation account. The same concerns 
exist in this respect as in the case of the citizen account. 
However, I welcome the fact that the law stipulates that 
the organisation account must not be used if a “higher” 
level of trust – which I take to mean a trust level of “high” 
– is required in individual cases.
5.3.4 Register Census Act
In May 2024, the Federal Ministry of the Interior (BMI) 
once again submitted a draft for a Register Census 
Authorisation Act for departmental consultation. This 
is intended to be the basis for legitimising not only 
the preparation and implementation of the upcoming 
2031 census, but also the collection of information for 
all future population censuses and all data transmis-
sions relating to corresponding information provision 
obligations under applicable EU law . However, it was 
not possible to reach department-wide agreement on 
the draft bill before the premature end of the legislati-
ve period.
I also expressly welcome the approach pursued by the 
draft law of drawing the information which is in-
dispensable for political policy, business and society, 
entirely from existing registers in future, instead of 
obtaining it at great expense by means of supplemen-
tary surveys of sections of the population, as has been 
the case to date. The potential susceptibility to error 
of survey procedures, regardless of whether they are 
carried out by survey officers or online via a correspon-
ding internet portal, was confirmed in the previous 2022 
census and generally has a direct impact on the right to 
informational self-determination of data subjects.
Nevertheless, I have raised considerable concerns about 
the draft law. My main criticism is directed against the 
planned establishment and permanent operation of a 
comprehensive population statistics database under 
the responsibility of the Federal Statistical Office. With 
33rd Activity Report 2024 46


<!-- page 47 -->
this very extensive collection of personal data, the main 
contents of the data of all registers in this country would 
be mirrored centrally at federal level and continuously 
updated at least once a year by means of data supplied 
by the residence registration offices. Even though I can 
see that a stock of basic socio-demographic data for 
use in a variety of social and economic policy decisions 
should be of the highest possible quality and resilience, 
this legislative objective must be proportionate to the 
resources used to achieve it.
The development and maintenance of the envisaged 
population statistics database alone would require data 
processing on a scale which – even if it takes place 
behind the wall of statistical confidentiality – would have 
to be considered a particularly serious encroachment on 
the fundamental right to informational self-determinati-
on in terms of both breadth and depth, since, unlike the 
previous population censuses, it would not be limited in 
time and the data in this database would not be anony-
mised. In this light, the arguments put forward for the 
need for such a database are not convincing. They are 
essentially based on producing and ensuring the highest 
possible data quality and by reference to expected future 
requirements under EU law. However, discussions on 
the specific content of the legal amendments currently 
being prepared at EU level have not yet been finalised, 
meaning that the date of entry into force of this future 
EU law is also not yet foreseeable.
The current draft bill also lacks a more careful conside-
ration of the chosen model and of possible alternative 
procedures, which could at least have less drastic con-
sequences for the data subject’s right to informational 
self-determination. For example, it is unclear whether 
the quality assurance elements already established in 
the legislation on register modernisation could also be 
sufficient to ensure data collection on specific dates by 
the regional registration offices, instead of also storing 
it centrally and permanently in a separate population 
statistics database.
This also does not take into account the regular data 
additions from a large number of other databases in fe-
deral and state registers under the proposed “sign of life” 
approach, which are also planned for quality assurance 
reasons and which would require the use of a unique 
personal identifier for the purpose of comparison with 
the residence register data. In this respect, too, the draft 
law proves to be problematic, as the corresponding regu-
lations provide for the use of the identification number 
in accordance with the Identification Number Act or, as 
78 Art. 8 of the Act implementing Regulation (EU) 2022/2065 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 19 October 2022 on a single market for digital 
services and amending Directive 2000/31/EC and implementing Regulation (EU) 2019/1150 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 20 June 2019 on pro-
moting fairness and transparency for business users of online intermediation services and amending other laws of 6 May 2024 (Federal Law Gazette 2024 I No. 149).
a substitute and transitional measure, the tax identifica-
tion number in accordance with the Fiscal Code, without 
at the same time specifying in the draft law the appro-
priate safeguards for the rights and freedoms of data 
subjects required under Art. 87 GDPR. 
These include, for example, protective mechanisms 
such as the involvement of an independent neutral trust 
centre or an intermediary for exclusive processing of 
uniquely personal identifying features (for the purpose 
of creating a unique personal identifier) and a connec-
tion to the data cockpit in accordance with the Online 
Access Act.
Unfortunately, my concerns and suggestions were not 
taken into account in the various revisions of the draft 
bill. I hope that, as part of a possible new legislative 
initiative, the current regulatory approach will either be 
reconsidered fundamentally or at least revised in such a 
way that unavoidable encroachments on the fundamen-
tal right to informational self-determination are adequa-
tely offset by legal requirements for suitable protective 
measures.
5.4 Economy and finance
5.4.1 Telemedia renamed Digital Services
The German Digital Services Act (DDG), which imple-
ments the EU‘s Digital Services Act (DSA) at national 
level, came into force on 14 May 2024. With the DDG, 
the previous Telecommunications Telemedia Data 
Protection Act (TTDSG) was also renamed the Tele-
communications Digital Services Data Protection Act 
(TDDDG).78
Entry into force of the TDDDG
With the entry into force of the DDG and the associated 
renaming of the TDDDG, little has changed in terms of 
the content of the previous TTDSG apart from the title. 
Only the term “telemedia” has now been replaced by 
“digital services” as a result of the amendment.
The TDDDG continues to have two main areas of applica-
tion. According to Section 1(1) No. 1 to No. 6 TDDDG, the 
law continues to regulate telecommunications secrecy 
and establishes special data protection provisions for 
providers of telecommunications and digital services. 
According to Section 1(1) No. 7 TDDDG, it also includes 
regulations for the protection of privacy in terminal 
equipment. According to Section 2(2) No. 6 TDDDG, 
terminal equipment is any device connected directly 
33rd Activity Report 202447


<!-- page 48 -->
or indirectly to the interface of a public telecommuni-
cations network for sending, processing or receiving 
messages, i.e. devices connected to the internet such as 
smartphones and laptops, but also networked devices 
such as “smart” kitchen appliances, alarm systems and 
radiator thermostats. With regard to digital services offe-
red by federal public bodies, I am also responsible under 
Section 29(2) and (3) TDDDG for enforcing protection of 
the integrity of terminal equipment within the meaning 
of Section 25 TDDDG against unauthorised access or 
storage.
Inspections and experience
In 2024, my office also monitored the digital services 
of several federal public bodies. The subjects of these 
inspections were primarily websites and apps both of the 
highest federal agencies and subordinate bodies. Unfor-
tunately, as in previous years, my staff found recurring 
violations of the principles of the TDDDG and the GDPR 
during my inspections. For example, the information 
obligations of Art. 13 GDPR are not fully observed as a 
result of the use of incomplete data protection declara-
tions. In addition, the consent required for access and 
storage under Section 25(1) TDDDG within the meaning 
of Art. 4(11) GDPR is often not obtained effectively for 
self-hosted tracking services, for example. The consent 
banners used for this purpose (so-called cookie ban-
ners) regularly do not correspond to the requirements 
of the GDPR in terms of the information provided and 
the explanation of the voluntary nature of the consent. 
This is all the more surprising given that the German 
courts79 now also provide clear guidelines on the design 
of consent banners.
In addition, my office was able to determine in the 
course of its checks using technical analyses that the 
storage period for cookies that are generally permitted 
is unnecessarily long in view of the purpose pursued. 
For example, a so-called session cookie, which stores 
information about the current session on the user’s end 
device, does not have to be stored beyond that session 
in order to fulfil its purpose. Yet my office has recorded 
storage periods of over 100 days for such cookies in some 
cases.
It has also come to my attention that, in cases where 
federal public bodies use the services of an IT service au-
thority to host their website, the necessary agreements 
within the meaning of Art. 28 GDPR are not always 
concluded. A commissioned data processing agreement 
must usually be concluded with third parties for the 
hosting of a website.
79 The following decisions are examples of this: Upper District Court of Cologne, 19 January 2024 – 6 U 80/23 and Munich I District Court, 29 November 2022 – 33 O 
14776/19.
Advice
Last year, my office once again focused on the area of re-
sults-based advice on data protection-compliant design 
of digital services. Provided that we were involved in 
good time, we were able to achieve reliable results in this 
area that were satisfactory for all parties involved.
Through circulars such as the one on “Integration of vi-
deo content on federal government websites” or internet 
articles such as “Use of the website analysis tool Mato-
mo”, my office is also proactive in informing the bodies 
it supervises of its data protection assessments.
It is regrettable, however, that I continue to perceive 
a certain resistance to accepting advice on the part of 
some federal public bodies. In the face of a sometimes 
persistent refusal to change processing procedures to 
make them data protection-compliant, my office also 
occasionally made use of its right to instruct federal 
public bodies to take specific action in accordance with 
Art. 58(2) GDPR last year. At the same time, I would like 
to focus primarily on dialogue and consultation in 2025 
in order to continue to reconcile the need for efficient 
digital public administration with the protection of citi-
zens’ rights under the GDPR.
5.4.2 The EU Digital Services Act and the Ger -
man Digital Services Act
Under the EU Digital Services Act (DSA), there have 
been new EU-wide regulations in force on the use of 
data for tracking and profiling in the context of online 
advertising since 17 February 2024. With its national 
implementation in the German Digital Services Act 
(DDG), my office has been assigned new tasks since 14 
May 2024 related to ensuring effective enforcement of 
these regulations.
The regulations of the DSA apply in particular to large 
online platforms, such as large social networks. Among 
other things, they oblige platforms to provide signifi-
cantly more transparency and make their services more 
consumer-friendly. The regulations on the use of data for 
tracking and profiling in the context of online adverti-
sing are of particular importance from a data protection 
perspective.
When the DDG, the German law implementing the DSA, 
came into force, a national coordination centre for 
digital services was set up to supervise digital service 
providers and coordinate complaints under the DSA. 
In Germany, this role falls to the Federal Network Agen-
cy (BNetzA).
33rd Activity Report 2024 48


<!-- page 49 -->
Pursuant to Section 12(3) DDG, my office is the compe-
tent authority for enforcement of the advertising bans 
on German online platforms contained in Art. 26(3) and 
Art. 28(2) DSA: firstly, the ban on displaying profiled 
advertising to minors (Art. 28(2) DSA) and secondly, the 
ban on displaying advertising to adults if special catego-
ries of personal data pursuant to Art.(1) GDPR were used 
for profiling (Art. 26 para. 3 DSA).
On the one hand, the DSA and DDG open up the possi-
bility for citizens to lodge a complaint with the BNetzA 
as the central complaints body within the meaning of 
Section 20 DDG about violations of Article 26(3) or Artic-
le 28(2) of the DSA, verification of the content of which is 
then the responsibility of my office. On the other hand, 
my staff are also proactive in approaching German on-
line platforms within the scope of our resources in order 
to provide them with support and advice on compliance 
with Art. 26(3) or Art. 28(2) DSA and thus minimise the 
risk of citizens’ rights being violated at an early stage.
In my view, the new DSA and DDG regulations on data 
protection, particularly in the area of profiling, are 
appropriate for contributing to a safer online environ-
ment for all users. My role is therefore of central import-
ance for the enforcement of these regulations.

7.3.7 Age verification in digital services
5.4.3 Automated and connected driving wi -
thout comprehensive video surveillance of 
public spaces
Automated and connected driving on the roads re-
quires a comprehensive and accurate picture of the 
vehicle surroundings for the right driving decisions to 
be made. How is this compatible with data protection?
An automated and connected vehicle must be able to 
see and hear its surroundings in order to be able to steer 
itself to suit the situation. Under my leadership, the DSK 
has already made it clear in a position paper in 2023 
how, in its view, the development of reliably roadworthy 
vehicles is possible without violating the right of a third 
party to adequate protection of privacy. For the operati-
on of an automated and connected vehicle, third parties 
must be able to assume that the video and audio data 
about the surroundings captured during the journey is 
processed in a closed system. Audio and video data may 
only be transmitted and used for specific purposes in 
clearly defined exceptional cases and to a very limited 
extent, for example to clarify the circumstances of an 
accident or unforeseen, potentially dangerous errors by 
the driver assistance system, in accordance with Sec-
tions 63a to 63f of the Road Traffic Act.
Car manufacturers and their suppliers want to be able 
to recognise possible general defects in automated 
and networked driving functions and advanced vehicle 
assistants that occur during the operation of the vehicles 
or in components they manufacture and to be able to 
investigate them forensically using event-based data. 
The knowledge gained from this is used to rectify defects 
and for general development of automated and networ-
ked driving functions and other highly sophisticated 
vehicle assistants. If this requires audio and video data 
at the time of an event (trigger), it may be permissible to 
collect audio and video data about the environment for 
the purpose of defect rectification and improvement un-
der Art. 6(1) point (f) GDPR, as the result of a balancing 
of the legitimate interests of manufacturers and their 
suppliers in the development and production of roadwo-
rthy vehicles and components against the interests and 
fundamental rights and freedoms of data subjects. Better 
protection of vulnerable road users through emergency 
brake assist or other automated and connected driving 
functions can only be achieved if the technical systems 
can correctly assess the likely behaviour on the roads of 
vulnerable people. This sometimes requires processing 
of data that enjoys special protection of fundamental 
rights. It must also be guaranteed that risks to the fun-
damental rights and freedoms of data subjects remain 
proportionate.
In situations that involve audiovisual recording of the 
surroundings by potentially all automated and networ-
ked vehicles – i.e. beyond simple development driving 
– I continue to take the line that recorded personal data 
may only be transferred from vehicles to third parties 
under specific and narrowly defined conditions and that 
protection against unauthorised use of the recorded 
data must be guaranteed by appropriate vehicle techno-
logy regulations. The audiovisual environment data is 
primarily used for vehicle control and does not have to 
leave the vehicle at any time for this purpose. Against 
this background, I very much welcome the fact that the 
manufacturers and their suppliers have presented my 
Belgian colleagues with a draft code of conduct in accor-
dance with Art. 40 GDPR for the handling of audio and 
video data, in particular for the development of advan-
ced driving assistants and automated driving functions, 
via their ACEA European interest group. Together with 
my colleagues in the Member States of the EU and in 
the German states, I am ready to help find solutions that 
enable manufacturers and suppliers to develop their ad-
vanced driving assistants and automated and connected 
driving functions without creating undue risks to fun-
damental rights and freedoms. The recording of a large 
number of third parties moving in publicly accessible 
street spaces (passers-by, gatherings, etc.) requires se-
parate and sufficiently specific legal regulation, as does 
33rd Activity Report 202449


<!-- page 50 -->
the transmission and further processing of the data, to 
establish the necessary legal certainty. This applies all 
the more with regard to vehicles the manufacturers of 
which process data mainly in a third country in which 
the right to data protection is not adequately protected 
in a manner comparable to EU standards and in this re-
spect would not be covered by any rules of conduct. The 
important increase in the road safety of vehicles through 
automated and connected driving must be accompanied 
by adequate protection of the personal data processed in 
that context.
5.4.4 New legislation to combat financial 
crime
The coalition agreement for the 20th legislative period 
includes a provision to improve the fight against 
financial crime. The Financial Crime Prevention Act 
(FKBG) is intended be a central component of this. It 
remains to be seen whether and when the proposed 
new Federal Office for Combating Financial Crime 
(BBF) will be established after the premature end of 
the coalition. In addition, another part of the project 
was at least submitted for departmental consultation 
in the context of the Act to Combat Asset Concealment 
(VVBG) 2024. With its obligation to provide informati-
on about one’s own assets, the legislation is breaking 
new ground.
The main content of the FKBG was the establishment of 
the BBF, including the Money Laundering Investigation 
Centre as a new law enforcement agency for the prose-
cution of international money laundering with a connec-
tion to Germany. I have already reported in detail about 
the legislative process in my 31st Activity Report.80 The 
original plan was for the new authority to be set up in 
2024 and to commence operation in 2025. With the pre-
mature end of the 20th legislative period of the German 
Bundestag, the progress of the legislative process is un-
certain. The extent to which my previous comments will 
be taken into account is equally uncertain. If the plans 
to set up the BBF go ahead, I look forward to supporting 
it in an advisory capacity from the outset and working 
towards its data protection-friendly organisation.
In April 2024, I was also involved in the departmental 
consultation on the draft of the Act to Combat Asset Con-
cealment. The law has not yet left the draft stage. The 
core of the proposed law was the so-called
Asset Investigation Act. In this, a new authority was to be 
created for cases with a particular risk of money launde-
ring or sanctions, the Asset Concealment Investigation 
Centre (EZV). In organisational terms, the plan was to 
80 31st AR No. 3.3.3
81 25th AR No. 9.3.1
integrate the body into the BBF. The starting point for ta-
king action is the assets for which there is evidence that 
they originate from an unlawful act. The authority was 
to be authorised to clarify the origin of the funds used to 
acquire such assets or the beneficial ownership of such 
assets by means of an administrative procedure. The 
innovation was that for the first time the investigations 
began with the object. The administrative investigation 
procedure provided for graduated powers of interventi-
on. These began with register searches and ended with 
the authority to demand information from the owner of 
suspect assets. This was framed as an obligation.
In addition to the federal government’s lack of legislative 
authority, I had and still have considerable doubts about 
the draft legislation in terms of data protection law. In 
particular, I have criticised the lack of selection review 
periods independent of events and non-compliance with 
the principle of hypothetical data re-collection in the 
context of inter-agency data transfers.
I welcome the fact that I was involved in the departmen-
tal consultation at an early stage. I found the dialogue in 
that process to be very constructive and assume that my 
suggestions will be taken into account.
I am therefore confident that the established threads 
of dialogue will be taken up again should the legislative 
process continue.
5.5 Labour and social affairs
5.5.1 Employee Data Act
In the context of implementation of the coalition 
agreement for the 20th legislative period, the Fe-
deral Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs (BMAS) in 
conjunction with the Federal Ministry of the Interior 
(BMI) presented a draft law to strengthen fair handling 
of employee data and provide more legal certainty for 
employers and employees in the digital world of work 
(Employee Data Act - BeschDG). However, as a result of 
the premature end of the legislative period, the latest 
initiative to regulate the handling of employee data has 
also failed.
I very much welcome the initiative of the BMAS and 
BMI, as it meets long-standing demands and expec-
tations and both legal and actual requirements. The 
German Data Protection Conference (DSK) called for 
the creation of an Employee Data Protection Act back 
in 2014.81 In a resolution adopted in April 2022, the DSK 
recommended the establishment of a legal framework 
33rd Activity Report 2024 50


<!-- page 51 -->
in various areas, such as the use of algorithmic systems 
including artificial intelligence (AI) and data processing 
in application and selection procedures.
Many of the issues already mentioned by the DSK can be 
found in principle in the draft Employee Data Protection 
Act. It is unfortunate that it was not possible to establish 
specific regulations on employee data protection becau-
se of the premature end of the 20th legislative period. 
The need for reform of German employee data protecti-
on, which remains long overdue, must not be forgotten, 
particularly in light of the judgement of the European 
Court of Justice (ECJ)82 83 of 30 March 2023. Among other 
things, the ECJ formulated requirements for national 
regulations that are issued on the basis of the opening 
clause of Art. 88(1) GDPR.
I hope that the plan to create specific legislation on 
employee data protection will be taken up again in the 
next legislative period. I will also be happy to provide 
constructive advice on a new legislative process.
5.5.2 Basic child allowance
The 20th German Bundestag did not pass the govern-
ment’s draft of the Introduction of a Basic Child Allo-
wance Act (BKG). From a data protection perspective, 
there were no fundamental objections to the law . If the 
proposal is to be taken up again in the 21st legislative 
period, I will continue to provide constructive advice 
to the government and parliament so that state bene-
fits for children can be granted in a simple, uncompli-
cated, digital and data protection-friendly manner.
The Act for the Introduction of a Basic Child Allowan-
ce84 was not passed in the 20th legislative period of the 
German Bundestag. To combat child poverty, the draft 
bill provided for replacement of the previous financial 
support for children with a guaranteed amount for all 
children and adolescents independent of income (for-
merly child benefit under the Federal Child Benefit Act) 
and a supplementary income-dependent and age-related 
child allowance (formerly child supplement). To simplify 
matters for applicants, data could be retrieved (semi-)
automatically from various data sources, e.g. tax data, 
social security data, employer data. The “Basic Child 
Allowance Check” is a standardised way of checking in 
advance whether a child may be entitled to the supple-
mentary child benefit without a formal application for 
it having been made beforehand. Data that is already 
82 Judgment of the ECJ of 30 March 2023 in case C-34/21, available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX%3A62021CJ0034
83 See also the DSK resolution “The need for specific regulations on employee data protection!” , available at: 
https://datenschutzkonferenz-online.de/media/en/2023-05-11_DSK-Entschliessung_Beschaeftigtendatenschutz.pdf
84 BKG draft, BT-Drs. 20/9092, available at: 
https://dserver.bundestag.de/btd/20/090/2009092.pdf
85 32nd AR No. 8.11
available in electronic form from public authorities, 
especially the financial and social authorities, was to 
be used to pre-check entitlement to the supplementary 
child allowance.
In my previous Activity Report, I had already commen-
ted on the draft Act for the Introduction of a Basic Child 
Allowance:85 in my opinion, there are no fundamental 
data protection concerns – despite the need for impro-
vements in individual points. The (partially) automated 
exchange of data between authorities is in line with the 
“once-only principle” pursued in the context of the over-
all digitalisation of the administration. The aim here is 
for the authorities to collect essential information from 
the data subject only once and then to use it for the vari-
ous purposes of the authorities. In view of the particular 
importance of the objective of effectively combating 
child poverty and the benefits to the data subjects them-
selves, I consider a statutory deviation from the consti-
tutionally guaranteed principle of initial data collection, 
which is expressly applicable in social law in Section 67a 
(2) SGB X, to be justifiable under data protection law.
I welcome and support the aim of using digital and un-
bureaucratic procedures to make it easier for families to 
receive state benefits. Should the legislature take up the 
proposal again in a comparable or modified form, the 
following aspects in particular would have to be taken 
into account for data protection-compliant design:
 → Carrying out the Basic Child Allowance Check only 
with express consent 
Of the many millions of people entitled to the gua-
ranteed child allowance, only a small proportion will 
be entitled to the supplementary child allowance. 
Many citizens will not come into consideration for 
this because of their income and are aware of that. 
In order to avoid mass processing of the sometimes 
very sensitive data of these persons in the context of 
the Basic Child Allowance Check without an applica-
tion, I recommend that the legal regulation should 
stipulate that the consent of the persons concerned 
be obtained.
 → (Automated) data collection from employers as part 
of the application process 
Recipients of social benefits often have reservations 
about disclosing the receipt of means-tested bene-
fits to their employer. They fear stigmatisation and 
may be deterred from applying for such benefits as a 
33rd Activity Report 202451


<!-- page 52 -->
result. In this respect, data subjects should also have 
the opportunity to provide the necessary evidence 
themselves if they so wish.
 → Ensuring increased protection when applying the 
“once-only principle”, in particular transparency via 
the data protection cockpit (Section 10 OZG)
 → Processing of the identification number in accor-
dance with the Identification Number Act (IDNrG) 
instead of processing the tax identification number. 
Although the tax identification number is materially 
identical to the identification number pursuant to 
Section 1 IDNrG, there is a legally fictitious diffe-
rence between the two features, with the result that 
the increased protection requirements for the use 
of the identification number do not apply directly. 
The increased protection requirements for use of the 
ID number include a very strict purpose limitation 
(Section 5 IDNrG), a structural restriction on data 
exchangeability (Section 7(2) IDNrG) and transparen-
cy via the data protection cockpit (Section 9 IDNrG).

6.1 Statistical analyses on freedom 
of information
Submissions relating to the Freedom of Information 
Act (IFG) and the Environmental Information Act (UIG)
I received a total of 595 submissions in the reporting 
period. This means that the number of submissions has 
once again increased compared to previous years. In 404 
cases, petitioners contacted me under Section 12(1) IFG 
and complained about a violation of their right to access 
information under the IFG. Applicants contacted me in 
26 cases with requests for mediation in applications un-
der the UIG (Section 7a UIG). Compared to the previous 
year, the number of requests for mediation regarding 
applications under the UIG increased. However, the figu-
res remain at a low level. In addition to appeals relating 
to violations of the right to access information, general 
enquiries were also made during the reporting period 
concerning legal information on the IFG and citizens’ 
enquiries and requests for mediation outside my remit.
In terms of the departments and their business areas, 
the submissions are distributed as shown in the follo-
wing chart. The highest number of submissions con-
cerned the BMG and its area of operation. As in previous 
years, this result is due to increased interest in issues 
relating to the past pandemic situation and the develop-
ment and use of vaccines.
IFG requests to my office
During the reporting period, I received a total of 126 re-
quests for access to information. These requests were di-
rected both at access to file contents relating to requests 
for mediation addressed to my office after their conclu-
sion, as well as to my opinions on proposed legislation. 
Compared to previous years, the number of applications 
has remained the same.
