6.2 Committees
6.2.1 Conference of Freedom of Information 
Commissioners
In the reporting year, the Saxon Data Protection and 
Transparency Officer chaired the Conference of Free-
dom of Information Officers (IFK). The IFK focussed 
on a variety of aspects of further development of go-
vernment transparency at federal and state level.
The IFK is an association of federal and state commissi-
oners providing access to files, freedom of information 
and transparency with the aim of promoting the right 
of access to information and jointly advocating for its 
further development. The IFK agrees IFG applications to 
my authority in the reporting year on common positions 
on issues relating to freedom of information. This is 
done in particular through resolutions, position papers 
and statements. At its 46th meeting in Dresden on 5 June 
2024, its 47th meeting inn Leipzig on 27 November 2024 
and by means of the circulation procedure between tho-
se meetings, the IFK adopted the following resolutions 
and practical recommendations:
At the end of April 2024, the IFK published guidelines 
on setting up and operating government transparency 
portals. The practical guide is aimed at the operators of 
the portals, bodies obliged to provide information and 
users who wish to check the quality of a platform. The 
IFK provides clear and concise recommendations and 
advice on the design of the platforms, specifies require-
ments and gives concrete implementation options. The 
guide also contains a checklist that summarises the key 
criteria, for example the technical framework condi-
tions, organisational specifications, accessibility and the 
absence of tracking.
At the end of July 2024, the IFK published recommenda-
tions on the fulfilment of the right of access to informati-
on. The IFK’s information is aimed at public bodies that 
are subject to freedom of information/transparency and 
environmental information legislation. The recommen-
dations follow the principle of “freedom of information 
by design”. Essentially, the aim is to ensure that organi-
sations that are obliged to provide information can make 
data and documents available reliably, efficiently and in 
full. To achieve this goal, the IFK recommends a series 
of organisational and technical measures focussing on 
electronic file management. This enables public autho-
rities to reduce the administrative burden of providing 
official information and speed up procedures.
In the resolution “Well informed in the super election 
year of 2024!”, the IFK pointed out that, especially before 
elections, the laws on transparency and freedom of 
information can be an appropriate means for voters to 
obtain first-hand information that gives an unfiltered 
impression of the actual work of the government and 
administration. This information can also be a valid and 
reliable basis for forming a well-founded opinion or for 
public debate that allows a well-informed and objective 
decision later on.
The resolution “Equal mandate – equal right to infor-
mation from public broadcasters!” deals with the fact 
that the “whether” and “how” of the right to information 
depends on the location of the broadcaster in question, 
even though all public broadcasters essentially have the 
same mandate. In the case of multi-state organisations, 
an effective right of access to information often fails 
due to the requirement that this must be regulated by 
23
34
6
8
55
33rd Activity Report 2024 54


<!-- page 55 -->
an interstate treaty and that there is no corresponding 
regulation. Public service broadcasting is a medium and 
factor in the formation of public opinion. The IFK is 
therefore calling on the legislature to ensure transparen-
cy in this area too by creating uniformly high standards 
throughout Germany. An exception only applies to 
journalistic and editorial activities which are protected 
by fundamental rights. 
This strengthens the independence of public service 
broadcasting.
With the resolution “Obligation of freedom of informa-
tion and transparency for municipalities in Hesse and 
Saxony!”, the IFK calls on the state legislatures of Saxony 
and Hesse to grant their citizens binding access to in-
formation, including from the municipal authorities. So 
far, this decision has been left to the individual munici-
palities, which can commit to transparency voluntarily. 
In contrast to Hesse and Saxony, the transparency and 
freedom of information laws in all other federal states 
also apply to municipal authorities. This means that the 
municipal authorities in most federal states are obliged 
to provide information in accordance with the respective 
federal state law.
Following on from an earlier resolution from 2022,86 87 
in the resolution “A modern transparency law for Lower 
Saxony now!” the IFK calls on the Lower Saxony state 
legislature to implement the proposal set out in the 
coalition agreement for the 19th legislative period of the 
Lower Saxon state parliament to create a progressive 
transparency act quickly. To this end, the IFK has made 
proposals about the provisions that such a law must 
contain.
Thuringia will chair the IFK in 2025.
6.2.2 International Conference of Information 
Commissioners
The ICIC is an international forum that deals with 
issues of freedom of information and transparency 
in governance. The 15th International Conference of 
Information Commissioners (ICIC), held in Tirana, 
Albania from 3 to 5 June 2024, took “empowering indi-
viduals through access to information: ensuring trans-
parency and inclusion in an interconnected world” as 
its main theme.
The main tasks of the ICIC include sharing best practice, 
promoting freedom of information laws and suppor-
ting freedom of information commissioners in various 
countries. 
The ICIC is made up of information commissioners, 
86 All IFK resolutions are available at www.bfdi.bund.de/ifk-entschließungen
87 31st AR No. 6.1
government representatives, social organisations and 
other interest groups who are committed to strengt-
hening information rights and improving transparency 
in administration. The conference provides a platform 
for discussion and development of joint strategies to 
promote freedom of information worldwide.
It regularly organises events, training courses and work-
shops to raise awareness of freedom of information and 
promote interaction between members.
Another important aspect is the role of the ICIC in 
supporting countries that want to improve or implement 
their own freedom of information laws. The conference 
provides a platform for discussing the challenges associ-
ated with the implementation of freedom of information 
laws, such as data protection, access to information and 
the balance between security and transparency.
In an increasingly digital world, the ICIC has a special 
role to play. Access to information and the transparency 
of governments is crucial for strengthening democracy 
and the trust of citizens.
The ICIC’s mission is to promote the principles of free-
dom of information and transparency in governance 
worldwide. The conference is committed to improving 
access to information for citizens and strengthening the 
accountability of governments.
To this end, the conference is involved in sharing best 
practice and experience among members to support the 
implementation and enforcement of freedom of infor-
mation laws. The ICIC also aims to promote cooperation 
between different stakeholders, including governments, 
social organisations and international institutions, in 
order to create a culture of openness and trust.
The ICIC 2024 in Albania brought together a wide range 
of stakeholders, including information commissioners, 
government representatives, NGOs, academics and 
activists.
My staff were involved in the conference with several 
contributions. On the one hand, these set out the most 
urgent challenges from my point of view in the cont-
ext of the panel “A balanced relationship between ATI 
(Access to Information) and data protection rights in the 
digital sector. How can we protect citizens’ personal data 
while ensuring that efforts are made to guarantee digital 
information rights?”.
I am fundamentally of the opinion that an appropriate 
balance can be achieved between data protection and 
the right of access to information. This applies to access 
33rd Activity Report 202455


<!-- page 56 -->
to both official information and environmental informa-
tion from non-public bodies.
I see challenges in the proactive provision of larger vo-
lumes of up-to-date information through a transparency 
law, as envisaged in the coalition agreement of the 20th 
legislative period, together with a legal claim to open 
data.
The idea behind open data is comprehensive – auto-
mated – access to data in open formats, its processing 
and re-contextualisation. In my opinion, this is one of 
the challenges of digitalisation in view of the tension 
between open data and data protection.
On the other hand, at the invitation of the Council of 
Europe I presented the advantages that Germany would 
have from ratifying the Tromsø Convention in the panel 
it chaired.
In recent years, I have campaigned at national and 
international level for the ratification of the Convention. 
Already at the 8th International Conference of Freedom 
of Information Commissioners in Berlin in 2013, the par-
ticipating commissioners called for as many countries as 
possible to ratify the Tromsø Convention in their “Berlin 
Declaration”.
At national level, the Conference of Freedom of Infor-
mation Officers in Germany also called for ratification in 
2013 and 2021. In my view, Germany would benefit from 
ratification at least at federal level, as the Convention 
goes beyond the current legal situation. Internationally 
– for example in the ranking of the legal basis for access 
to information compiled by the Centre for Law and De-
mocracy – Germany ranks 127th out of 140 countries.88 
The German government has rejected ratification in the 
past decade
on the basis of the argument that the existing Ger-
man Freedom of Information Act already provides an 
adequate framework for granting access to government 
information. I used five key aspects to show why I do not 
share this view.89 
Interaction with the international Information Commis-
sioners often makes it clear how important it would be 
to update the Freedom of Information Act which was 
passed in 2005. I recommend developing the Freedom of 
Information Act into a transparency law with a statuto-
ry transparency register. With these adjustments, the 
88 Ranking of the Centre for Law and Democracy on access to information, available at: 
https://www.rti-rating.org/
89 Speech at the ICIC 2024 in Tirana, manuscript available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/DE/DokumenteBfDI/ Reden_Gastbeitr%C3%A4ge/2024/ICIC-Troms%C3%B6-Convention.pdf
90 Joint Declaration of the ICIC 2024, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/EN/ICIC/ 2024_06_05_ICIC-Public-Statement.pdf
Federal Republic’s freedom of information legislation 
would at least be in the middle of the international field.
Finally, during the closed meeting of the Information 
Commissioners on the third day of the conference, 
my staff provided an overview of the activities of our 
European colleagues in the ICIC over the last year in a 
“Europe Report”.
Together with my colleagues, we have issued a public 
statement. This is entitled “Transparency and the digital 
age: the information commissioner’s role and citizen 
empowerment”.90
In this declaration, all members of the ICIC commit to 
nine positions aimed at strengthening access to public 
information worldwide.
      
I recommend merging the Freedom of Informati-
on Act and the Environmental Information Act. In 
addition, I recommend the further development of a 
federal transparency law with proactive publication 
obligations and powers of order and enforcement for 
the Freedom of Information Commissioner, allowing 
action to be taken in the event of a conflict. 
      
6.3 Sharing experience
6.3.1 Second European Case Handling Work -
shop on Freedom of Information
The second European Case Handling Workshop on 
Freedom of Information took place in Madrid from 6 to 
7 May 2024.
After I organised a European Case Handling Workshop 
on Freedom of Information for the first time in 2023 at 
the European House in Berlin, the format was continued 
successfully in 2024. From 6 to 7 May 2024, the Spanish 
Council for Transparency and Good Governance played 
host to the second Case Handling Workshop in Madrid, 
which was attended by 30 people from 13 European 
countries and the European Ombudsman. The aim of the 
workshop was to create a platform for sharing experi-
ences of practical application of issues of freedom of 
information law at European level. In my view, sharing 
best practice and findings from official case processing 
33rd Activity Report 2024 56


<!-- page 57 -->
represents an important contribution to cross-border 
promotion of transparency.
In addition to presentation of various case studies from 
official processing practice, the thematic focus of this 
year’s event was on proactive publication of informa-
tion on transparency platforms and the relationship 
between freedom of information and the protection of 
personal data. The Republic of Kosovo has expressed 
an interest in hosting the follow-up event next year. In 
my view, it is desirable to develop the Case Handling 
Workshop further and establish it as an annual format 
for Europe-wide interaction at a practical level. My office 
therefore continues to act as the secretariat to ensure 
continuation of the exchange of views.
6.3.2 Exchange of experience between federal 
authorities about freedom of information
There were multiple opportunities to share experi-
ence with the highest federal authorities and a new 
event with various higher-level federal agencies and 
institutions in Berlin and Bonn in the reporting year. 
The events are now an established part of my advisory 
services.
Both series of events create a valuable opportunity for all 
participants to exchange views on current developments 
in case law, their concrete effects on practical work and 
general questions of processing practice. I was very plea-
sed with the great interest in this advisory service and 
the open and lively discussion at both events.
In view of the positive response from participants, I 
intend not only to continue these two successful event 
formats, but also to expand my programme to other 
authorities.
I also used the two formats to inform the authorities 
involved about my role in ombudsperson activities and 
the principles, procedures and limits of the mediation 
process in accordance with Section 12(1) IFG. Particular 
attention was paid to the discussion of the effects of the 
validity of a decision under the IFG or UIG on the me-
diation procedure. In this regard, I explained that even 
if the decision is final, this does not in principle affect 
the question in the procedure according to Section 12(1) 
IFG of whether rights under the IFG or UIG have been 
violated. I pointed out that clarification of the facts is 
necessary to examine this question and the cooperation 
of the body obliged to provide information therefore re-
mains necessary. Whether and under what conditions an 
unlawful final decision can then be changed or whether 
other measures are to be taken by me is then the subject 
of further review.
There was also an exchange and expert discussion on 
the judgement of the Federal Administrative Court on 20 
March 2024, which ruled on the admissibility of anony-
mous or pseudonymous applications.
6.4 Mediation procedure
6.4.1 Confidentiality of an expert discussion 
between the BVerfG and the ECtHR – the limits 
of the mediation procedure
The mediation procedure pursuant to Section 12(1) 
IFG is an out-of-court dispute resolution procedure 
in which mutually acceptable solutions are general-
ly sought. If a legal offence under the IFG cannot be 
clearly established, a final clarification of the unresol-
ved factual and legal issues in the individual case is 
reserved for the formal appeal procedure.
A petitioner requested various documents from the 
Federal Constitutional Court (BVerfG) in connection 
with an expert discussion between judges of the BVerfG 
and a delegation from the European Court of Human 
Rights (ECHR). The BVerfG rejected the application with 
regard to manuscripts identified as being the subject 
of the application, which had been sent to interpre-
ters in preparation for the event. On the one hand, the 
manuscripts did not constitute official information, 
as they were simply a memory aid in preparation for 
the translation work. Rather, they are notes within the 
meaning of Section 2(1) sentence 2 IFG. Furthermore, 
access to information is excluded due to the confidenti-
ality of the technical discussions. In the end, it could be 
left open whether the protection of confidentiality arises 
from § 3(3) point (a) IFG (confidentiality of international 
negotiations) or § 3(3) point (b) IFG (confidentiality of 
official consultations), from a corresponding application 
of these grounds for exclusion or from a corresponding 
application of the principle of the core area of executive 
responsibility. Ultimately, the manuscripts are works 
protected by copyright, so that access to information 
pursuant to Section 6 sentence 1 IFG is precluded by the 
protection of intellectual property.
The petitioner took a different legal view on all points 
and explained this in detail and in a way that was clear 
to me. After a thorough examination of the arguments, 
I informed the parties involved that I considered the 
petitioner’s legal opinion to be convincing. At the same 
time, the legal issues raised in the mediation procedu-
re, for example with regard to the protection of special 
public interests, allowed for different legal opinions due 
to their complexity.
33rd Activity Report 202457


<!-- page 58 -->
Despite my mediation efforts, the BVerfG ultimately 
stuck to its legal assessment. Taking into account the 
limits of the procedure under Section 12(1) IFG, I there-
fore saw no possibility of continuing the mediation in an 
effective way.
The mediation procedure is an out-of-court dispute 
resolution process. In the absence of dedicated powers 
to issue or enforce orders – as available in the area of 
data protection, for example – the procedure is primarily 
geared towards consensual solutions. If a breach of the 
law cannot be clearly established because the approach 
of the body from which information is requested does 
not appear unreasonable on factual or legal grounds, 
mediation proceedings cannot generally be continued 
in any meaningful or expedient way. In cases where 
the fundamental “whether” of the claim is disputed, an 
amicable solution in the sense of mutual concession is 
out of the question. In these cases, a conclusive answer 
to unresolved legal questions remains reserved for the 
formal appeal procedure, in particular their considerati-
on in administrative court proceedings.
The matter is now the subject of administrative court 
proceedings.
6.4.2 Limits of the authorities’ obligation to 
obtain information
The performance of a role by a political foundation in 
the area of non-material support for gifted students 
does not constitute appointment of an entity covered 
by private law to carry out a task under public law wit-
hin the meaning of Section 1(1) sentence 3 IFG. Simply 
allocating state funding does not mean that a public 
authority , as the funding provider, takes ownership of 
the role of supporting gifted students as a task in the 
public interest and is using the political foundation to 
fulfil that task.
A petitioner had applied to the Federal Ministry of 
Education and Research (BMBF) for access to informa-
tion in connection with the activities of the Konrad Ade-
nauer Foundation (KAS) as an organisation that supports 
talented people on the basis of the IFG. The subject of 
the request included information on the KAS’s non-mate-
rial support programmes for gifted students.
As a political foundation in the legal form of an associati-
on, the KAS is one of 13 organisations that support gifted 
students in Germany. The organisations for supporting 
talented young people receive various types of federal 
funding for their areas of activity, provided that the 
budgetary and specific funding requirements and, in the 
case of political foundations, the requirements of the 
Act on the Financing of Political Foundations from the 
Federal Budget (Stiftungsfinanzierungsgesetz - StiftFinG) 
are met. The BMBF makes grants available to the pro-
grammes for both material and non-material support of 
gifted students and doctoral candidates in Germany.
The BMBF rejected the application, essentially stating 
that the requested information was not available from it 
or would only be collected at a later date. There is also 
no obligation to obtain information under Section 1(1) 
sentence 3 IFG in conjunction with Section 7(1) sentence 
2 IFG, as the support organisations perform their role 
independently in accordance with their own statutes and 
are not fulfilling duties of the BMBF under public law.
The petitioner contacted me and expressed the opinion 
that the KAS fulfils a public interest in the area of sup-
porting gifted student and its role is therefore covered 
by public law. The role belongs to the BMBF because it 
provides the financial resources and it is therefore using 
the KAS to fulfil that role under public law.
After a thorough examination of the arguments put 
forward, I was unable to establish any violation of rights 
under the IFG. As organisations established under 
private law, foundations affiliated with political parties 
are generally not subject to a direct obligation to provide 
information under the IFG, regardless of whether they 
are financed from public funds. Pursuant to Section 1(1)
sentence 3 IFG, a natural or legal person under private 
law is equivalent to an authority with a duty to provide 
information insofar as the authority “uses that person to 
fulfil its duties under public law”. However, according to 
Section 7(1) sentence 2 IFG, the body against which the 
claim is to be made is the competent authority whose du-
ties are being carried out by the entity under private law. 
In this case, the authority approached for access to infor-
mation has a persuasive obligation to obtain informati-
on. Otherwise, the claim for access to information would 
come to nothing because the authority formally subject 
to the claim and the third party materially obliged to 
provide information would not be the same.
However, in my opinion, the conditions of Section 
1(1) sentence 3 IFG were not met in this specific case. 
Although the term “duty under public law” within the 
meaning of Section 1(1) sentence 3 IFG must be interpre-
ted broadly, it is sufficient for it to involve fulfilment of 
an administrative task rooted in public law. A restriction 
to the area of public services cannot be derived from the 
law. In my opinion, there is therefore much to suggest 
that support for talented students is indeed a role that is 
in the public interest.
In this specific case, however, mere allocation of public 
funding does not mean that the BMBF is taking owners-
hip of this role in the public interest (“its task”) and is 
“making use of” the KAS to fulfil it. The KAS performs its 
33rd Activity Report 2024 58


<!-- page 59 -->
role as an organisation that supports talented students 
independently and autonomously. In particular, it has 
freedom of organisation in the area of non-material 
support. The assumption that the role is performed 
autonomously is also underpinned by the legal structure 
of the funding of organisations for supporting talented 
students. Unlike the area of public contracts or other 
“classic forms of cooperation”, for example, which allow 
for a degree of management and control responsibili-
ty on the part of the state, the public authority, as the 
funding provider, usually has a legal but not a material 
controlling influence. In accordance with budgetary 
regulations and the StiftFinG, a state grant always entails 
a significant federal interest in the funding. In my opi-
nion, however, the assumption that this alone fulfils the 
criteria of Section 1(1) sentence 3 IFG extends the provi-
sion too far. Further evidence is required that makes the 
legal entity under private law a cooperation partner and 
thus a quasi-governmental public authority.
In principle, the official information relating to the grant 
awarding process itself remains accessible. However, 
this is not a case that falls under Section 1(1) sentence 3 
IFG, but rather an official obligation to provide informa-
tion in accordance with Section 1(1) sentence 1 IFG.
6.4.3 The categorisation of a document does 
not cover the entire file
The categorisation of an individual document or seve-
ral documents in a file as classified has no direct effect 
on the other parts of the file.
A petitioner had applied to the Federal Ministry for 
Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ) on the 
basis of the IFG for access to the complete contents of 
the BMZ’s files on a project carried out by the Weltfrie-
densdienst, in particular to certain specifically identi-
fied information. The BMZ granted access to general 
information, e.g. the legal basis of the BMZ’s activities, 
but otherwise rejected the request. All of the documents 
in the file in question are classified as VS-NfD, which 
prevents access to the information.
The petitioner contacted me and questioned the legality 
of the classification of all documents in the file. He was 
convinced that the BMZ file to which the application re-
ferred did not exclusively contain material or documents 
classified as VS-NfD and marked accordingly on each 
page. Furthermore, the material grounds for classificati-
on were not explained sufficiently.
In the mediation proceedings, the BMZ first subs-
tantiated the material reasons justifying the need for 
classification. I could understand the disadvantages for 
the interests of the Federal Republic of Germany that it 
feared. However, I still had doubts that every document 
in the process concerned was in need of material clas-
sification. The BMZ maintained its legal opinion in this 
regard with reference to Section 20(6) of the Classified 
Information Directive (VSA). Classified information is 
to be marked in its entirety according to the highest 
classification level if the classified information consists 
of several differently classified parts.
I pointed out to the BMZ that this understanding corre-
sponded neither to the interpretation of the VSA here 
nor to my experience of practice in other organisations 
gained from consultation and inspection visits. The 
term “classified information” is not to be understood in 
relation to files, but in relation to documents. Even if 
understood differently, the marking obligation regula-
ted in Section 20(6) VSA only has a warning function if 
different parts of a classified document are classified 
differently. It does not justify classification of the other 
parts of the file. In other words, the marking simply 
follows the (highest) classification, it has no constitu-
tive classification effect. As a consequence, individual 
parts of the file could also be “unclassified”. Whether a 
material need for classification of individual documents 
that have not yet been formally classified arises from a 
narrow factual context, which in turn allows conclusions 
to be drawn about facts that require protection, must be 
considered on a case-by-case basis.
Following a further review, the BMZ concurred with 
these explanations and checked all individual docu-
ments for their need for material categorisation. Insofar 
as the documents did not require classification, access to 
information could be granted.
6.5 Advice and inspection visits
6.5.1 Advice and inspection visit in respect of 
the Federal Police Headquarters
In mid-May 2024, I conducted an advice and inspection 
visit to the Federal Police Headquarters (BPOLP) in 
Potsdam. The subject of the visit was the processing 
of applications under the Freedom of Information Act 
(IFG) and the Environmental Information Act (UIG).
The review of the BPOLP’s processing practice for appli-
cations under the IFG and UIG was based on an evalua-
tion of around half of the procedural files from 2019 to 
2023. The main result of the advice and inspection visit 
was that the application of the IFG and UIG is largely 
orientated to citizen and service requirements and that 
the procedural regulations and substantive legal require-
ments of the laws are largely observed.
Requests for access to information from the BPOLP and 
cross-directorate requests are processed centrally by 
33rd Activity Report 202459


<!-- page 60 -->
Unit 71 with the involvement of the specialist depart-
ments. This centralised processing ensures that applica-
tions submitted to the BPOLP are processed consistently 
and, with a few exceptions, without contradictions. In an 
organisational decree on the procedure for applications 
under the IFG, the BPOLP has instructed its subordinate 
authorities, the Federal Police Directorates and Federal 
Police Academy under its authority, to pass on all appli-
cations they receive in order to ensure coordination and 
uniform processing in cases of fundamental importance. 
As the police authorities specified under Section 57(1) 
BPolG are both independent authorities within the me-
aning of Section 1(4) VwVfG, I have suggested that this 
procedure be clarified in advance.
In some individual cases, my staff have identified an 
inaccurate distinction between obtaining information 
not covered by the claim under the IFG and simple pro-
cessing of information. It is true that the right to access 
information under the IFG only applies to the informati-
on held by the body obliged to provide it. However, this 
does not rule out an obligation to process information. 
At the same time, a re-contextualisation that leads to 
additional information content or generates new infor-
mation is not required under the IFG. The right of access 
to information includes mere compilation or simple 
addition of similar types of information. This distinction 
is also to be assessed independently of the administrati-
ve effort involved.
My staff also provided information and suggestions on 
the tenor of notifications, the third-party participation 
procedure and the fee forecast.
6.5.2 Advice and inspection visit in respect of 
the BMVg
In March 2024, I conducted an advice and inspection 
visit to the Federal Ministry of Defence (BMVg). Over-
all, an open and positive attitude towards freedom of 
information is evident there.
Given the large number of IFG applications, my staff 
reviewed a sample of the procedures from 2019 to 2023 
during the advice and inspection visit.
According to the findings of my staff, the IFG and UIG 
are applied at the BMVg with a focus on citizen and ser-
vice requirements and the procedural regulations and 
substantive legal requirements of the IFG and UIG are 
essentially observed.
The processing of IFG applications, including responses 
and decisions, and the involvement of specialist de-
partments is carried out centrally by a unit of the Legal 
and Organisation department in Berlin. Applications 
under the UIG are processed separately by a unit of the 
Infrastructure, Environmental Protection and Services 
Department in Bonn in view of its better knowledge of 
the area. Centralised processing ensures that IFG and 
UIG applications are processed consistently and, with a 
few exceptions, without contradictions. The specialist 
departments largely work in an effective and cooperative 
manner. This creates the conditions for prompt proces-
sing of IFG and UIG applications. There were occasional 
delays in processing applications during the review 
period.
At times, a heterogeneous picture could be observed 
with regard to the tenor and detail of the reasons given 
for the decisions. Implementation of the third-party 
participation procedures could only be partially verified, 
as these were carried out in a decentralised way by the 
specialist departments and were largely not included 
in full in the files provided. Insofar as the third-party 
participation procedures could be reviewed, the corre-
sponding letters were rather general in some cases, al-
though a high level of quality was evident in other cases. 
I have made various specific comments and suggestions 
regarding the need for improvement in the wording and 
justification of notifications and in the implementation 
of third-party participation procedures.
33rd Activity Report 2024 60

Individual topics7
7.1 Health
7.1.1 Authentication options for those with 
statutory insurance
Requirements for authentication in the healthcare 
sector, innovations in this regard and what health 
insurance companies now need to consider.
If insured persons wish to access their health data via 
the electronic patient file (ePA), they must first authen-
ticate themselves. During authentication, the system 
checks whether the person who wants to access the sys-
tem is really the person they claim to be. The obligation 
to carry out authentication arises from Art. 32 GDPR and 
applies to the health insurance funds as providers of the 
electronic health record. Authentication procedures are 
categorised into three levels depending on their security, 
starting with the “normal” trust level, through “substan-
tial” to “high”.
According to Art. 9 GDPR, health data requires particular 
protection as sensitive data. There is therefore a case for 
requiring the highest level of trust in an authentication 
process to access this data.
Authentication procedures that guarantee a high level of 
trust include the electronic identity card (eID), the elec-
tronic health card (eGK) and the health ID (referred to in 
the legislation as the “digital identity”), each in combina-
tion with the associated personal identification number 
(PIN). Despite the existence of secure authentication 
procedures, less secure authentication procedures

the Act to Accelerate the Digitalisation of the Healthcare 
System (Digital Act - DigiG), which lays the legal founda-
tion for this, in my 32nd Activity Report.91
Building on the DigiG, the Gesellschaft für Telematik 
(gematik) has developed new specifications according 
to which the biometric function of mobile devices can 
be used for the health ID instead of a PIN.92 According to 
studies by the Federal
Office for Information Security in 2022, the biometric 
functions of the mobile devices available on the mar-
ket did not reach the trust level of “high”; at best it was 
“substantial”, but in most cases the confidence level was 
only “normal”.93 Biometric authentication with a “nor-
mal” level of trust can be circumvented by the simplest 
means, such as a photo of the person concerned or a 
fingerprint taken from a glass. gematik has adopted this 
new specification despite the data protection challenges 
I have outlined.
Whether the problem of authentication using face ID 
or a fingerprint (both mechanisms have a lower level 
of protection than the PIN) can be solved by obtaining 
consent from insured persons for the less secure authen-
tication procedure – as provided for in the DigiG and the 
gematik specifications – is disputed. Hamburg Social 
Court94 and Düsseldorf Upper District Court95 accept 
the derogation of Section 32 GDPR, i.e. the possibility of 
lowering the level of authentication protection by means 
of consent. The overwhelming opinion in the literature 
rejects a complete waiver, but also accepts in part that 
at least the specific means are at the discretion of the 
33rd Activity Report 202461


<!-- page 62 -->
data subject96 (among many others: Jandt in: Kühling/
Buchner, DSGVO/ BDSG, 4th ed. 2024, Art. 32 Marginal 
39 with further references). The resolution of the 102nd 
Data Protection Conference of the Supervisory Autho-
rities of the Federal Government and the Länder (DSK) 
of 24th November 2021 only provides for exceptions in 
narrowly defined and individual cases and these require 
documentation.97 
If the gematik specifications are implemented unche-
cked, health insurance funds run the risk of violating the 
GDPR. I therefore warned the health insurance funds 
subject to my supervision accordingly on 6 May 2024. In 
the warning, I made it clear that health insurance funds 
could be in breach of Art. 32 GDPR if they allow access 
to health data in the ePA without authentication having 
taken place with the highest possible level of trust or, 
exceptionally, in an individual casein accordance with 
the aforementioned DSK resolution, which must then be 
documented.
I have also posted an article on authentication proce-
dures in the digital healthcare system on my website to 
raise awareness and educate the public.98 Here I address 
the most frequently asked questions in this context and 
explain why biometric authentication can be proble-
matic for health data from a data protection perspective.
7.1.2 Development of the medical register 
landscape
The organisation of medical registers has also under-
gone dynamic development in this reporting year. The 
Implant Register (IRD) began full operation for breast 
implants in July 2024. In addition, a uniform legal basis 
is currently being created for all medical registers in 
order to achieve harmonisation of legal requirements 
on the one hand and to give strong support to medical 
research on the other.
The IRD had already started trial operations with the 
first real data in 2023. I gave the necessary approval for 
the pseudonymisation procedure and encryption tech-
nology and advised on various individual issues. These 
concepts are now also the basis for regular operation. A 
particular focus in this reporting year was on how data 
from statutory health funds, private health insurers and 
independent healthcare providers can be merged in the 
IRD without violating the data protection rights of data 
96 Among many others: Jandt in: Kühling/Buchner, DSGVO/BDSG, 4th edition 2024, Art. 32 para. 39 with further references
97 Resolution of the 102nd DSK of 24 November 2021 on the option of not applying technical and organisational measures in accordance with Art. 32 GDPR at the 
express request of data subjects, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/DE/DSK/ DSKBeschluessePositionspapiere/102DSK_Nichtanwendung-TOM.pdf? blob=publicationFile&v=3.
98 Article on authentication procedures in the digital healthcare system, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/DE/Buerger/Inhalte/ GesundheitSoziales/eHealth/Authentifizierungsverfahren_dig_Gesundheitswesen.html
99 See the resolution of the DSK of 23 November 2024, available at: 
https://www.datenschutzkonferenz-online.de/media/en/ 2023-11-23_DSK-Entschliessung_medRegister.pdf
subjects. Now that the IRD has started full operation on 
1 July 2024, initially as planned for breast implants, it 
is to be extended to endoprostheses for hips and knees 
and aortic valve implants on 1 January 2025. The Second 
Regulation amending the Implant Register Operating 
Regulation of 21 October 2024 has created the neces-
sary legal basis for this. It remains problematic that the 
registry is still provisionally located in a project group 
directly within the Federal Ministry of Health (BMG) 
itself. The recommendation for an independent registry 
therefore remains in place.
The BMG’s draft bill for a law to strengthen medical 
registers and improve the use of register data (Register 
Strengthening Act) of 9 October 2024 represents a welco-
me legal advance in the register system.
The Register Strengthening Act is intended to create a 
sustainable, high-quality medical register infrastructure. 
There are currently around 400 medical registers in Ger-
many. Only very few of these registers work on the basis 
of specific legal foundations, such as the state cancer 
registers and the implant register. Most medical registers 
therefore collect personal data on the basis of general 
data protection legislation and operate in a heteroge-
neous web of standards imposed under EU, federal and 
state law. In addition, the usability of registers is limited 
by the fact that many utilisation purposes require a 
representative or complete database, which cannot 
currently be guaranteed. The Health Data Utilisation Act 
and the Act to Accelerate Digitalisation in the Healthcare 
Sector have already taken important steps towards better 
use of health data at national level. These are supported 
by the regulation on the European Health Data Space. 
The draft of a register strengthening law complements 
the existing legal basis by providing a uniform legal fra-
mework for all registers, establishing legal and technical 
standards and advancing research by bringing together a 
wide range of data.
I therefore fundamentally welcome the creation of a 
standardised legal regulation, even if there are still some 
points that need to be revised in terms of data protection 
law. I once again drew the attention of the legislature to 
the resolution of the Conference of Independent Federal 
and State Data Protection Supervisory Authorities of 
22/23 November 2023 on the framework conditions 
and recommendations for the statutory regulation of 
medical registers.99 Due to the premature dissolution of 
33rd Activity Report 2024 62


<!-- page 63 -->
the 20th German Bundestag, this legislative process was 
unfortunately not finalised. I am calling for the project 
to be taken up again in the 21st legislative period.
I will continue to give my constructive support to the 
dynamic development of the register system in the 
healthcare sector. Promotion of research, the quality as-
surance of the working methods of the registries and the 
protection of highly sensitive personal health data have 
high priority. These objectives are mutually supportive. 
My cooperation with the registries and trust offices and 
the legislature is always determined by this goal.
7.1.3 Selected decisions in the area of health
Some of my decisions pursuant to Art. 58(2) GDPR 
concern matters of general interest. In this reporting 
year, I dealt with issues such as the requirements for 
sending health data by email and the obligation to test 
software in advance.
Sending health data by email
My office issued a warning because a public body sent a 
booking confirmation for medical appointments cont-
aining the names of doctors by email without end-to-end 
encryption.
According to Art. 32 GDPR, data controllers must take 
technical and organisational measures (TOMs) to ensure 
a level of protection appropriate to the risk. Under Art. 
32(1) point (a) GDPR, this also includes encryption. If 
email messages are sent in which there is a high risk 
associated with unauthorised disclosure of the personal 
data they contain, end-to-end encryption is required.100 
This applies in particular to health data within the 
meaning of Art. 4(15) GDPR. This is particularly sensitive 
and requires protection. Considerable risks may arise 
in connection with the processing of that data101 Health 
data is defined as data from which the health status of a 
person can be inferred.102 This also includes information 
that a person has booked an appointment with a particu-
lar doctor. From this it can be concluded that the person 
booking the appointment has a need for treatment. 
Furthermore, by researching the doctor’s specialism, it 
is possible to narrow down what type of illness may be 
involved. Unauthorised disclosure of this information 
can lead to stigmatisation for the persons concerned, 
for example if it becomes known that the person has 
100 DSK guidance “Measures for the protection of personal data during transmission by email” of 27 May 2021, bullet point 4.2.2, available at: 
https://www.datenschutzkonferenz-online.de/media/oh/20210616_orientierungshilfe_e_mail_verschluesselung.pdf
101 Recitals 51 and 75 GDPR.
102 ECJ, judgement of 4 October 2024, ref. C-21/23, marginal 83, available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX%3A62023CJ0021
103 Final decision of the European Data Protection Board: EDPBI:SE:OSS:D:2023:652, available at: 
https://www.edpb.europa.eu/ our-work-tools/consistency-findings/register-for-article-60-final-decisions/decision-no-652_en
booked an appointment with a specialist in psychiatry 
and psychotherapy.
In the decision to issue a warning, my office is in line 
with the Swedish supervisory authority, which also 
issued a warning in a similar case in which an email con-
taining health data was sent without end-to-end encryp-
tion.103 In our case, the controller understood our point 
of view and has since adapted its processes. The appoint-
ment confirmations no longer contain any information 
about the doctor with whom the appointment has been 
booked.
Obligation to test software in advance
In another case, I issued a warning to a public body 
because it had made a new version of its smartpho-
ne app available via the app stores with a third-party 
software development kit (SDK) integrated into it for the 
first time without testing it in advance or checking that it 
does not entail unintentional data processing.
According to Art. 32 GDPR, data controllers must imple-
ment TOMs to ensure a level of protection appropriate 
to the risk. Under Art. 32(1) point (b) GDPR, this also 
includes TOMs to ensure the confidentiality of systems 
and services. Confidentiality risks exist in particular 
with software that can transfer data over the internet. 
Controllers must therefore use TOMs to ensure that the 
software is tested appropriately to prevent unintentional 
data transfers. The IT Guide to Basic Protection publis-
hed by the BSI contains general recommendations on 
software testing. It specifies that software tests should 
not only be carried out when the software is released 
for the first time, but also when significant changes are 
made to it. One such significant change is the initial 
integration of an SDK from a third-party provider. On the 
one hand, SDKs can make application development ea-
sier if existing third-party program code can be used. On 
the other hand, this external program code can harbour 
dangers. It can conceal unwanted functions with which 
the SDK manufacturer is able to transfer personal data 
to itself.
In the case in question, it would have been the respon-
sibility of the controllers to check by means of tests that 
the SDK installation would not result in any unwanted 
data transfers or to arrange for such tests to be carried 
out. As the controller failed to meet their obligations in 
this regard, I issued them with a warning accordingly. 
33rd Activity Report 202463


<!-- page 64 -->
The controller has accepted the warning and will ensure 
that apps are tested in future before they are made avai-
lable to users.
7.1.4 Genome sequencing pilot project
The pilot project for comprehensive diagnostics and 
therapy identification using genome sequencing for 
rare and oncological conditions in accordance with 
Section 64e SGB V was launched in this reporting year. 
In August 2024, I granted the necessary consent for 
the design of the declaration of consent for the use of 
patient data for research purposes.
Genome sequencing is a promising medical approach 
to diagnosis and effective identification of treatments, 
especially for rare and oncological conditions. Genetic 
diagnostics should therefore be available as standard 
care for all patients in the long term. As part of a pilot 
project, provision of this diagnostic test as a health 
insurance benefit will initially be trialled for a limited 
period of five years. This is a voluntary procedure finan-
ced by the health insurance funds for eligible insured 
persons.
As genetic data is very sensitive data within the meaning 
of the GDPR, the data protection framework for this 
pilot project must be solid. For the implementation of 
genome sequencing relevant to the pilot project, Section 
64e(15) SGB V clarifies that the provisions of the Genetic 
Diagnostics Act (GenDG), in particular on information, 
consent and communication of the results of genetic 
examinations and analyses, remain unaffected. The 
Federal Institute for Drugs and Medical Devices (BfArM), 
as the competent authority, has formulated a participati-
on declaration with information on the genome sequen-
cing and consent from patients for participation in the 
pilot project. I was involved in drafting the declarations 
of participation and consent and supporting the medical 
staff.
Additional consent is required for the further use of pa-
tient data in research. In accordance with Section 64e(6) 
sentence 5 SGB V , the declaration of consent must be 
drawn up with my approval.
For this purpose, the BfArM intended to use the mo-
del consent form of the Medical Informatics Initiative 
(MII-Broad-Consent)104 of version 1.6d or higher. I have 
had intensive discussions with the BfArM about the 
permissibility of using this broad consent in the specific 
case of the pilot project. In particular, it had to be ensu-
red that the use of the MII Broad Consent was compa-
104 See also the DSK resolution of 15 April 2020, available at: 
https://www.datenschutzkonferenz-online.de/media/ dskb/20200427_Beschluss_MII.pdf
105 32nd AR No. 8.2, pages 99-101
tible with the relevant normative requirements. This is 
because the law stipulates an application procedure that 
is under the control of the BfArM. Only authorised users 
who have undergone this application procedure are au-
thorised to process the data for the purpose of scientific 
research. During the dialogue with the BfArM, it became 
clear that the implementation of this legally prescribed 
application procedure, the associated authorisation of 
data use and the control by the BfArM is also required 
under the MII Broad Consent. The service providers – 
primarily university hospitals from a number of federal 
states – store the clinical and genome data collected on 
a data platform operated by the BfArM as the platform 
provider. Applicant research institutions can submit 
applications for use to the BfArM via a portal. Once the 
applications have been successfully reviewed, the data 
from the data platform and the results generated from 
the networked analysis of all data on the data platform 
are made available to the authorised users for further 
research purposes. On the one hand, this procedure 
meets the requirements stipulated in Section 64e SGB 
V , on the other, genome and analytical data that is very 
valuable from a research perspective is made accessible 
for scientific research.
7.2 Home affairs and justice
7.2.1 Register modernisation
In the reporting year, those involved in register moder-
nisation began to work in more detail on implemen-
ting the once-only principle. This has created not only 
new technical challenges, but also challenges in terms 
of data protection law . Fundamental considerations 
regarding the distribution of identification numbers 
and providing transparency have still to be settled.
Supporting the modernisation of the register also played 
a major role in my work in 2024. The initial indications 
are unchanged, although it is increasingly being percei-
ved by the public as one of the largest digitalisation 
projects in Germany. The fundamental data protection 
assessment is still differentiated.105 Although the move 
away from the direct collection principle to the once-on-
ly principle is associated with increased encroachment 
on the right to informational self-determination, this can 
certainly be offset by innovative measures in favour of 
transparency, control and prevention of the capacity to 
merge data. At the same time, the decision to use the tax 
identification number (tax ID) as a general identification 
number (ID number) still poses a significant constitu-
33rd Activity Report 2024 64


<!-- page 65 -->
tional hurdle, as a standardised, cross-sector identifier 
would not allow effective prevention of merging of data 
in all directions. But even against the background of this 
rather mixed picture, my staff, together with representa-
tives of the Data Protection Conference, have once again 
been involved in many aspects of register modernisati-
on.
OZG Amendment Act
On 23 July 2024, the Act to Amend the Online Access 
Act (OZG Amendment Act) was promulgated, which I 
had already supported closely in the previous reporting 
period.106 For register modernisation, the so-called on-
ce-only general clause of Section 5 of the E-Government 
Act should be highlighted in particular. This regulation 
allows all public bodies to retrieve evidence and data 
directly from the federal, state and municipal registers, 
provided that this is requested by citizens in the context 
of an OZG administrative service. After detailed consul-
tation, it was possible to ensure that in future all trans-
missions on this legal basis, whether with or without 
an ID number, will be displayed in the data protection 
cockpit (DSC). The same applies to those of the register 
modernisation authority (RegMoB). The once-only ge-
neral clause that has now been adopted is a far-sighted, 
citizen-friendly regulation because it takes a compre-
hensive approach to administrative digitalisation that 
creates transparency, and I therefore expressly welcome 
it. For transfers to other EU member states on the basis 
of the Single Digital Gateway Regulation, however, there 
is still a transparency loophole in current legislation.
While the IT Planning Council (IT-PLR) was in favour of 
exchanging evidence in real time (synchronously), the 
legislature decided also to allow evidence to be exchan-
ged at different times (asynchronously) when in contact 
with citizens. Unfortunately, this means that the preview 
function, which is very important for transparency, is 
only available to a limited extent when accessing evi-
dence. For these cases, an equally low-threshold type of 
inspection should be defined and regulated.
Overall Management of Register Modernisation
General
In 2024, I also supported the Overall Management of 
Register Modernisation (GS RegMo) in the implementa-
tion of this major project. In particular, I worked with 
the Steering Committee for Overall Management and the 
Legal Programme Division (PB Legal). This was com-
bined with the continuous involvement of the Contact 
Group on Register Modernisation of the Data Protection 
Conference (contact group), which I chair.
106 32nd AR No. 8.2, pages 102 f.
107 32nd AR No. 8.2, page 101
NOOTS State Treaty
Once again, the plans to set up a National Once-Only 
Technical System (NOOTS) played an important role. The 
NOOTS will form the central technical infrastructure 
for the exchange of information between various public 
bodies.
The focus in 2024 was on designing a viable legal basis 
for the NOOTS in the form of a planned federal-state 
treaty. Intense discussion took place in this connection 
between the PB Legal, the IT PLR and the Contact Group 
from August 2024. Unfortunately, these consultations 
did not lead to a data protection-compliant result in all 
respects. In November 2024, the IT-PLR adopted a draft 
for the state treaty that provides neither for purpose 
limitations with direct effect to the NOOTS nor for 
the establishment of a central contact point for data 
subjects. Instead, the draft is limited to initially obliging 
only online services under the OZG and registers within 
the meaning of the IDNrG to connect to the NOOTS. The 
connection of further public bodies for other purposes, 
which will also only be defined in the further course of 
establishment of the NOOTS, clearly remains an open 
question. This also involved possible use of the NOOTS 
in intervention management. Such an expansion would 
jeopardise the balance that has been struck so far bet-
ween the risks and benefits of administrative digitalisa-
tion. The comprehensive data exchange system required 
for the implementation of the once-only principle would 
not be sufficiently offset by data protection law in the 
context of intervention management.
Even the establishment of the NOOTS as a cross-sector, 
low-threshold exchange system for personal data bet-
ween different government agencies is an encroachment 
on the fundamental rights of the individual, which must 
be offset appropriately.107 Without direct data collection 
from citizens, technical means of transparency and cont-
rol, coupled with structural barriers to merging data, 
must perform this important balancing function in the 
context of register modernisation. However, this balance 
is specifically geared towards application-based service 
administration and not towards any form of intervention 
administration, in which the state takes action on its 
own initiative. If the state initiates data transfers on its 
own initiative, effective transparency and control by citi-
zens is impossible or at best only conceivable downstre-
am. The deeper intervention caused by the establis-
hment of this comprehensive exchange system alone 
would thus not have any counterbalance. This broad area 
of application has a particularly significant effect with 
regard to the work of the police and security authorities. 
33rd Activity Report 202465


<!-- page 66 -->
The lack of a clear exclusion clause in the State Treaty, 
as provided in Section 5 IDNrG for example, will result 
in stricter requirements of the design of the technical 
and organisational measures in the NOOTS, although it 
has not yet been conclusively determined whether this 
alone will enable a data protection-compliant design. 
Federal and state legislatures should expressly regulate 
this exclusion.
Finally, the lack of a common point of contact for data 
subjects makes it difficult to effectively implement the 
rights of data subjects in accordance with Art. 12(1) 
GDPR, according to which information must be provided 
by the controller in a comprehensible, easily accessible 
and clear manner. This is only partially the case with the 
compartmentalised regulations on responsibility in the 
context of OZG services: the online service accepts the 
application on its own responsibility. If certain evidence 
is required to respond to the application, the NOOTS-af-
filiated registers will provide this, albeit under their 
own responsibility. The NOOTS itself will carry out the 
respective transmissions as the controller and only in 
the final step will the specific authority responsible be 
involved. Even if this fragmentation is an unavoidable 
consequence of the division of labour between several 
public bodies involved in administrative digitalisation, it 
is not easy for data subjects to see where the boundaries 
between the individual steps are and who they need to 
contact accordingly. The federal and state legislatures 
should also make further improvements on this point, 
perhaps in the context of an amendment to the IDNrG, 
which is probably necessary anyway (see the following 
section).
Concept of secure connection nodes/NOOTS consulta-
tion
For cross-divisional transmissions using an ID number, 
an additional security measure is defined in Section 7(2) 
IDNrG, according to which transmissions must be made 
in encrypted form via exchanges. These must be inde-
pendent of the public bodies that wish to communicate 
with each other and must not be aware of the content of 
the messages. In the discussion about the technical im-
plementation, the technical NOOTS programme division 
of the GS RegMo proposed an architecture in which the 
transmission would no longer take an actual route via 
an exchange, but instead the exchange would only issue 
a certificate of admissibility, which would be checked be-
fore the communication link between the public bodies 
is established (the so-called token solution). The change 
is to be supported by the introduction of so-called secure 
connection nodes (SAKs). The SAKs should constitute 
a mandatory technical infrastructure provided by the 
NOOTS, which is not operated centrally but locally by 
each connected communication participant. Local ope-
ration means that each body can still view the content 
of the communication in plain text in the SAK. One of 
the reasons for this proposed architecture was better 
scalability and openness to further developments and 
changes to the transport protocols used.
The PB Legal and my office initially agreed that this 
proposed architecture did not fulfil the requirements of 
Section 7(2) IDNrG, as the transmissions would then no 
longer run via an exchange. A legal amendment would 
therefore be necessary in any case. At the same time, 
my reviews showed that this alternative approach has 
the potential to be at least as effective an obstacle to 
merging data as the exchanges currently defined, if not 
more so. A corresponding development of this approach 
could thus play an important part in making the overall 
system of register modernisation, which still suffers 
from the flaw of a uniform ID number, considerably 
more data protection-compliant. However, some of the 
requirements must be fulfilled by the combination of 
token exchange and SAK. Permanent and unmanipula-
ted operation and use of these facilities must be ensured 
technically and must not depend on arbitrary decisions 
by the connected authorities or individual employees. 
The SAKs must not themselves lead to an increase in the 
capacity to merge data. Only the responsible communi-
cation participant must be permitted access to the mes-
sages entered there in plain text. Even technical service 
providers of the public body should not be able to view 
the contents of the transmission. Verification of the ab-
stract transfer authorisation already provided for in the 
current legal situation must also extend to the coherence 
between the stated and actual purpose of the transfer 
and, as far as the reason for the transfer is concerned, 
must also cover the actual initiation of the process by the 
citizen. Particularly with regard to these verifications, 
the design of the SAK with its access to readable data 
offers great opportunities, which could ultimately lead 
to an improvement in the level of data protection and an 
effective reduction in constitutional encroachment as a 
result of the once-only principle. However, it is precisely 
this access that entails the risk of even wider dissemina-
tion of the personal data in question.
Over the course of the year, GS RegMo took this archi-
tectural approach further and presented it as part of a 
wider consultation on the NOOTS, in which my office 
was also involved. Consultations on this will continue 
in 2025. Despite the uncertainties so far, I can still see 
a great opportunity in the new approach. This is parti-
cularly the case because previous architectural plans 
provide for permanent integration of the exchange, even 
if the transmission required is not cross-divisional. I will 
be happy to continue providing advice on this topic in 
the future.
33rd Activity Report 2024 66


<!-- page 67 -->
Ordinance on administrative areas
Last year, I reported on the ongoing drafting of an ordi-
nance to define administrative areas within the meaning 
of Section 7(2) IDNrG.108 However, the provisional decisi-
ons on the architecture of the NOOTS and the exchange 
could possibly eliminate the need for this legal regulati-
on. If the exchanges were used across the board, there 
would no longer be any need for areas. The law already 
in force would have to be adapted to this approach.
Sub-projects
In the current reporting year, several important sub-pro-
jects of the GS RegMo made further progress. These 
include the planned introduction of the ID number and 
connection to the DSC by the Federal Employment Agen-
cy, the Federal Motor Transport Authority, the German 
Statutory Accident Insurance Agency and the German 
Pension Insurance Agency. I have already made initial 
contact with the corresponding project team at the Reg-
MoB and I am planning to extend cooperation in 2025.
NWR pilot project
Another topic, which my office already reported on in 
the previous Activity Report, was the pilot project for 
storage of ID numbers and the connection to the DSC 
of the National Weapons Register (NWR).109 The NWR 
was selected for the pilot project primarily because it is 
operated by the Federal Office of Administration (BV A), 
which also acts as the RegMoB. One of the focal points 
in terms of data protection law was the evaluation of 
so-called initial storage, i.e. the initial introduction of 
the ID number into the register. In contrast to the BMI 
and BV A, my office took the view here that this first-time 
introduction of the ID number should also be display-
ed in the data protection cockpit (DSC), not just any 
subsequent transmissions. In this context, the effects of 
the OZG Amendment Act are still the subject of ongoing 
reviews and the particularly transparent approach of the 
new regulations also argues in favour of including initial 
storage in the DSC.
There is, however, no debate about making subsequent 
transmissions using the ID number transparent in 
the DSC. This requirement became a problem at the 
beginning of 2024, as the DSC was not yet operationally 
connected to the register due to various circumstances, 
but a further exchange of data between the NWR and the 
identification number database at the Federal Central 
Tax Office (BZSt) was nevertheless supposed to take 
place. In March 2024, my office therefore issued a formal 
warning to the BV A regarding these intended transfers 
108 32nd AR No. 82, page 102
109 32nd AR No. 8.2, pages 103 f.
110 32nd AR No. 8.2, pages 103 f.
and pointed out that they may not take place until they 
can be made transparent in the DSC. The reasons for the 
delay were, on the one hand, the lack of a reliable legal 
regulation for the appointment of the body responsible 
for the DSC and, on the other hand, security concerns on 
the part of the NWR regarding the end-to-end encryp-
tion of the DSC and the resulting requirements for its 
own technical infrastructure. Unfortunately, the latter 
problem in particular could not be resolved by the end of 
the reporting period, meaning that the pilot programme 
involving the NWR is currently still on hold. In view of 
the particular importance of ensuring transparency via 
the DSC, I will continue to work intensively on this topic 
in the future.
Data protection cockpit (DSC)
Support for the DSC also continued through the Contact 
Group. One of the focal points was the finalisation of the 
legal ordinance on determining the body responsible 
for operation of the DSC. The DSC was mainly developed 
under the leadership of the state of Bremen. For consti-
tutional reasons, the legislature decided in July 2024 to 
designate the BV A as the responsible body. As a result, I 
have been involved in the project not only in an advisory 
capacity, but also in the role of the supervisory authori-
ty. With this decision, all the prerequisites for the start 
of live operation of the DSC were met, as it had already 
been technically operational since 2023.110 However, 
the additional security concerns that then arose during 
the implementation of the DSC’s end-to-end encryption 
(see above) delayed commissioning with the NWR as a 
connected register until further notice. Ultimately, the 
BMI and BV A therefore decided to allow the DSC to go 
online without any register at the end of 2024.
Work also continued on the future development of the 
DSC. The main focus was on the design of the so-called 
master data information, i.e. information about the 
data of a person permanently stored in a register (the 
DSC otherwise only accesses data that is generated by 
specific transfers). As the master data information is al-
ready applicable law under Section 10(2) OZG, it must be 
implemented as quickly as possible, especially in view 
of the fact that storage of the ID number is now planned 
for several registers for 2025. In my opinion, however, it 
would be illegal to use the ID number without a master 
data information system.
The breakdown of the governing coalition and the fact 
that the budget for 2025 has not yet been passed me-
ans that the outlook for the DSC is even more negative. 
According to the findings of my office, the provisional 
33rd Activity Report 202467


<!-- page 68 -->
funds available to the DSC will neither be sufficient for 
regular minimum operation nor for the development of 
the master data information system. However, as already 
explained in relation to the NWR pilot project (see 
above), the DSC is a mandatory prerequisite for lawful 
processing of the ID number as a central transparency 
instrument.
If this problem is not solved promptly by the next federal 
government, there is a risk of major delays in the imple-
mentation of register modernisation.
Exchange of information with the Federal Central Tax 
Office
An exchange of information with the BZSt took place in 
March of the reporting year. As the operator of the iden-
tification number database, the BZSt plays an important 
role in the implementation of register modernisation.
The exchange focussed in particular on communication 
with the RegMoB. One of the most important data pro-
tection findings was that the RegMoB enquiries do not 
contain any register-specific data and that they are gene-
rally only stored until final processing by the BZSt and 
then completely erased. The creation of a “shadow data-
base” is therefore largely ruled out. Other aspects of the 
meeting concerned the introduction of the ID number 
in the residence register (in the form of a reallocation of 
the tax ID already stored) and in the civil status registers 
(probably with considerable assignment problems) and, 
finally, the establishment of a quality assurance process 
in the event that the registers receive data that contra-
dicts the BZSt by other means. These issues in particular 
will be the subject of further discussions in the future.
Outlook
In addition to the areas already mentioned, which requi-
re continued support from me, I expect to see further 
fundamental changes to the use of the ID number in 
the near future. As already mentioned in the previous 
Activity Report, the creation of parallel systems that rely 
on the (identical) tax ID instead of the ID number would 
be a serious constitutional problem for further adminis-
trative digitalisation.111 Even if my fundamental reserva-
tions about the use of a standardised identifier remain 
(see above), solutions should be found that build on the 
existing system of the ID number and the NOOTS, since 
at least some important data protection measures have 
been taken into account from the outset in these, which 
is not the case with the tax ID.
111 32nd AR No. 8.2
112 Judgement of the ECJ of 7 December 2023, C-26/22 and C-64/22, available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/C/2024/917/oj/eng
113 Judgement of the ECJ of 26 September 2024, C-768/21, available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/DE/TXT/? uri=CELEX%3A62021CJ0768&qid=1739617269549
      
I continue to recommend that the federal government 
and the legislature avoid creating parallel systems for 
cross-sector data exchange and that they implement 
the once-only principle. With a view to establishing 
foundation of the IDNrG, EGovG, OZG and NOOTS as 
the central infrastructure, a standardised approach 
should be pursued in which constructive solutions are 
developed for existing data protection problems. 
      
      
I also recommend that the federal government take 
into account the German Bundestag’s broad under-
standing of transparency in the context of administra-
tive digitalisation (see committee document 20(4)258 
of 19 July 2023) and also make the initial entry of the 
ID number in a register in accordance with Section 
2(1) IDNrG visible in the data protection cockpit. In 
addition, the necessary preparations should be made 
at an early stage for making the data transmissions of 
the registration offices (still) using the tax ID transpa-
rent as soon as the conversion to the ID number takes 
place. 
      
7.2.2 ECJ judgements on the right to lodge a 
complaint
In several court rulings, the European Court of Justice 
(ECJ) has further outlined the scope of the right of data 
subjects to lodge a complaint and the obligations of 
data protection supervisory authorities.
In the reporting year, the ECJ dealt with both the right of 
data subjects to lodge complaints and the enforcement 
obligations of data protection supervisory authorities in 
several rulings. According to Art. 77(1) GDPR, every data 
subject has the right to lodge a complaint with a data 
protection supervisory authority if they consider that the 
processing of their personal data infringes the GDPR. In 
its decisions, the ECJ has clarified that the right to lodge 
a complaint is not a mere right of petition or petition-li-
ke right.112 113 Rather, the decision on a complaint is a 
legally binding decision, the correctness of which may 
be reviewed in full by the courts. Some German courts 
had previously ruled differently and were of the opinion 
that the correctness of the authorities‘ decision could at 
best be reviewed on the grounds of arbitrariness. The 
ECJ did not subscribe to this view.
33rd Activity Report 2024 68


<!-- page 69 -->
The ECJ has also ruled that, in the event of a data pro-
tection breach by a supervised body, the data protection 
supervisory authority is obliged to respond in an appro-
priate manner in order to remedy the inadequacy identi-
fied. In this context, it refers to the enforcement measu-
res pursuant to Art. 58(2) GDPR. In this respect, the data 
protection supervisory authority is obliged to take one 
or more enforcement measures if these are appropriate, 
necessary and proportionate. At the same time, however, 
it cannot be ruled out that it may be justified in exceptio-
nal cases to refrain from enforcement measures because 
of the special circumstances of the individual case. In 
principle, the data protection supervisory authority can 
choose the appropriate and necessary means. Courts can 
only review this discretion for errors of judgement, in 
particular regarding compliance with the limits descri-
bed above by the data protection supervisory authority. 
At the same time, the ECJ denied a subjective right of 
complainants to a fine. Fines are only imposed in the 
public interest.
I expressly welcome the decisions of the ECJ. The dis-
pute between the German higher courts regarding the 
nature of a complaint under Art. 77 GDPR has thus been 
clarified. The decisions reflect the importance of the 
right to lodge a complaint and clarify the statutory en-
forcement obligations and supreme court expectations 
of effective data protection supervision. At the same 
time, they ensure proportionality in special exceptional 
cases. To prevent data protection breaches from occur-
ring in the first place, however, it is all the more import-
ant that my advisory services are accepted and that I 
am involved at an early stage, especially in government 
projects.
7.2.3 Data protection in the German Bundes -
tag
Since the introduction of the GDPR, the question has 
arisen as to whether it also applies to the German 
parliaments – including the German Bundestag – and 
which independent body monitors its application. 
Now the European Court of Justice (ECJ) has given its 
ruling.
For decades, the question has arisen as to which data 
protection regulations apply to parliaments and their 
respective members, whether this applicability also in-
cludes the core area of parliamentary work and who mo-
nitors compliance with these regulations. Even with the 
introduction of the GDPR, the unresolved questions were 
not explicitly regulated. The parliaments of the Member 
States were not mentioned in its scope of application, 
114 Judgement of the ECJ of 16 January 2024, C-33/22, available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/DE/TXT/? uri=CELEX%3A62022CJ0033&qid=1739617197928
nor was any provision made for providing the corres-
ponding data protection supervision. In particular, the 
general supervisory authorities were not excluded as 
incompetent in this respect, as Art. 55(3) GDPR stipula-
tes comparable supervision of processing carried out by 
courts in the context of their judicial activities.
My office has always accepted the applicability of the 
GDPR to the core area of parliamentary work and has 
carried out the necessary data protection supervision in 
this respect. Enquiries and complaints addressed to me 
about the German Bundestag have been and continue to 
be dealt with in all cases and with a clear focus. The ran-
ge of data processing concerned extends from enquiries 
and petitions to parliament and its members, through 
the presence of MPs on the internet and social networks, 
to their activities as employers. Unfortunately, it was 
unclear for a long time how far my supervisory powers 
extended. Given the special position of the German 
Bundestag and its directly elected members, I have fo-
cussed on my advisory role vis-à-vis parliament. Notwi-
thstanding this, in recent years I have succeeded almost 
without exception in clearing up the data protection 
violations reported to me and taking precautions to en-
sure that these do not occur in future wherever possible. 
This has allowed the suggestions of many citizens to be 
implemented and data protection in the parliamentary 
sphere to be improved overall. In addition, my office has 
been in constant contact with parliament over the years 
to support it in clarifying outstanding issues, particularly 
with regard to data protection supervision.
The ECJ has now provided clarity.114 I very much welco-
me the fact that the highest European court has confir-
med that the GDPR fundamentally applies directly to the 
parliamentary sphere.
In my opinion, parliamentary requirements must be 
taken into account appropriately in data protection su-
pervisory decisions in order to leave the core legislative 
function of the democratically elected parliament unaf-
fected. With a clear focus on information and advice, my 
office can work towards compliance with data protection 
regulations from the outset and in an appropriate way.
33rd Activity Report 202469


<!-- page 70 -->
7.2.4 Security loopholes jeopardise the pro -
tection of personal data
Security loopholes and poorly maintained systems 
pose a significant risk to the protection of personal 
data. In order to minimise this risk, it is essential for 
security loopholes to be closed as quickly as possible 
by controllers by means of updates and that knowled-
ge of security loopholes is used immediately to close 
them.
In this reporting year, security breaches have continu-
ed to pose a serious threat to personal data. Security 
vulnerabilities are exploited faster than updates can be 
installed or manufacturers can provide updates. Both 
the long response times of manufacturers after reports 
of loopholes and the poor quality of the updates provi-
ded have been particularly noticeable in this reporting 
period. In this reporting year, for example, potential data
