protection incidents within the meaning of Art. 33 GDPR 
were reported that were based on a known security 
vulnerability that the BSI had already warned about. The 
controller had already installed a corresponding update, 
but this had become obsolete in the meantime because 
the manufacturer had to make further improvements. 
Nevertheless, outdated and poorly maintained systems 
continue to pose a major problem.
Every security vulnerability first represents a potential 
risk of a breach of data protection law. Whether a securi-
ty vulnerability has been exploited and personal data has 
been affected is often difficult for controllers to determi-
ne and sometimes takes a very long time. The long time 
required for clarification is problematic, as countermea-
sures to curb possible misuse of personal data cannot be 
taken in any meaningful way. One reason for this is that 
attackers may also exploit security loopholes in combi-
nation with compromised access data to install back-
doors. In some cases, the attackers only become active 
a long time after this installation has taken place. Add to 
this the time it takes for these malicious activities to be 
noticed. In such cases, it is often no longer possible to 
determine from the available data whether and to what 
extent activities have taken place in the meantime, nor 
therefore the extent to which the protection of personal 
data may have been violated.
In view of the increasing dependence on certain manu-
facturers and software-as-a-service solutions, individual 
security loopholes and errors affect many organisations. 
This holds true whether the cause is unintentional mal-
functions or security loopholes. Particularly in the case 
of security vulnerabilities in such widely used products, 
large-scale attacks are evident. During the reporting 
period, several Article 33 notifications were received 
115 In the following, statements about the online ID card always refer to all three technically identical documents.
relating to the same vulnerability with different control-
lers.
It is vital for the protection of personal data that secu-
rity loopholes are closed immediately. To this end, it 
is essential for security vulnerabilities to be reported 
immediately to the manufacturer or, if appropriate, to 
a central organisation such as the BSI. Controllers must 
install updates immediately. The BSI’s warning notices 
are proving to be effective in this respect, on the basis of 
which controllers can check their systems and identify 
corresponding exploitation of security vulnerabilities, as 
Article 33 reports to my office reveal.
Furthermore, it is important that controllers take techni-
cal and organisational measures to restrict and limit the 
opportunities for successful attacks. This includes, in 
particular, data minimisation at the time of collection, 
the earliest possible erasure, and also measures such as 
pseudonymisation and anonymisation. To ensure the 
security of processing, the controller must also exercise 
caution when selecting the products used. The manufac-
turer’s handling of security vulnerabilities should also be 
used as a selection criterion.
      
In order to strengthen IT security in Germany , I recom-
mend that the legislature enshrine in law an immedi-
ate obligation to report security vulnerabilities to the 
manufacturer or a central coordinating body with the 
aim of rectifying them immediately . 
      
7.2.5 The European Wallet for digital identity 
and its national implementation in Germany
The reformed eIDAS Regulation extends the eID sys-
tems of the Member States for electronic identification 
to include electronic wallets. The rights of citizens set 
out in the regulation must now be effectively imple-
mented in implementing acts and technical specifi-
cations. This is the only way to build trust in this key 
technology of digitalisation.
In March 2024, amendments to the EU Regulation on 
electronic identification and trust services for electronic 
transactions in the internal market (eIDAS Regulation) 
were adopted. The Member States are obliged accor-
dingly to provide citizens with a wallet for their digital 
identity (EUDI Wallet). So far, the Member States have 
only made eID systems available. In Germany, this is the 
online ID function of the ID card, the electronic residen-
ce permit and the eID card for citizens from EU Member 
States.115 Digital wallets go one step further than pure 
eID systems. They will be used to store digital informa-
33rd Activity Report 2024 70


<!-- page 71 -->
tion and characteristics that go beyond identity data and 
can be presented to make use of services.
It should then be possible to store not only the ID card 
in an app, but also any other attributes such as driving 
licences, access authorisations and certificates, mem-
berships and concert tickets. I welcome this further de-
velopment of a state-regulated secure digital identity, as 
it can be used as the “key” to all the digitalised areas of 
life. It is important that citizens’ rights are safeguarded.
EUDI Wallet
The eIDAS Regulation also takes a sound approach here: 
the use of the EUDI Wallet is voluntary and there is also a 
non-discrimination clause stating that citizens must not 
be disadvantaged in their access to public and private 
services if they choose not to use the EUDI Wallet (Art. 
5a(15) sentence 1 and 2 eIDAS Regulation). Where the 
wallet is used, citizens generally have the right to use 
a pseudonym. Identification may only be required if a 
legal obligation stipulates this (Art. 5, Art. 5a(4) point (b) 
and Art. 5b(9) sentence 2 eIDAS Regulation). It is also 
envisaged that all data processing in connection with the 
personal data in the wallet will be under the sole control 
of the user (Art. 5a(4) point (a) eIDAS Regulation). Ac-
cording to the general understanding of this regulation, 
every request from a body to make use of the wallet 
must be confirmed by the wallet user. Before confirming 
the data transfer, there is also an option to release only 
part of the data requested (so-called “selective disclosu-
re”). For traceability, the wallet must display a list of all 
bodies with which a connection has been established, 
the data those bodies have received and an easy way 
to request the bodies to erase the data on a common 
dashboard. In the event of suspected unlawful requests, 
the wallet must facilitate reporting to the competent 
data protection authorities (Art. 5a(4) point (d) eIDSA Re-
gulation). Users must be able to store their data on their 
end device and the wallet must support the right to data 
portability (Art. 5a(4) point (f) and (g) eIDAS Regulation).
In addition to these rights for citizens, there are also 
regulations for the organisations that want to use data 
from the wallet, the so-called relying parties. In order 
for citizens to be able to recognise who is asking them to 
disclose data, relying parties must register. This mea-
sure also protects against attackers who interfere with 
the communication in order to access or change data. 
Relying parties may only request data from citizens that 
they have provided during the registration process (Art. 
5b(1) to (3) eIDAS Regulation).
These sensible regulations are being set out in more 
detail in implementing acts and technical specifications. 
In particular, the right to pseudonymous use has unfor-
tunately not yet been taken into account to the necessary 
extent in drafts of technical guidelines. I am committed 
to ensuring that the rights from the regulation are trans-
lated into effective technical measures.
The target announced by the Commission of all Member 
States providing a wallet by 2026 is very ambitious. In or-
der for the wallet to gain the trust of citizens, a thorough 
and careful approach must be taken and the wallet must 
be a finished, complete product when it is released.
German EUDI Wallet
The Member States have room for manoeuvre when 
choosing the specific technology for the wallet and on 
the question of the operator model: the Member States 
can provide the wallet themselves, commission it or 
make use of an independent solution (Art. 5a(2) eIDAS 
Regulation). In Germany, the Federal Ministry of the 
Interior has collected information in a consultation pro-
cess and via a prototype competition, in preparation for 
answering these questions. Leaving the question of the 
operating model to one side, the decision was taken to 
use signed data for the architecture, which caused some 
controversy at the outset. The authenticity of signed 
data is guaranteed, so that it could have a higher value. 
Alternatively, the authenticity of the data can also be 
determined by using a secure communication channel. 
The data is then only verifiably genuine for the specific 
identification process. Viewed in isolation, the latter pro-
cedure, which is also used for the online ID function, is 
preferable in that it offers a certain degree of protection 
against misuse/use for other purposes. However, if Art. 
25(1) GDPR is applied as the standard, controllers must 
weigh up which technical and organisational measures 
are to be implemented in order to achieve a level of 
protection appropriate to the risk. In the federal govern-
ment’s decision on the architecture for the EUDI Wallet, 
the decision was made in favour of proof of authenticity 
by means of a signature.
In the event of malicious behaviour on the part of a 
relying party or a data leak, significant damage can 
be caused to data subjects if their ID data is misused. 
The potential damage for data subjects is high for both 
signed and unsigned data. However, the incentive for 
unlawful sale of data or illegal data collection could 
presumably be higher. But signed data cannot be used 
in any other meaningful way: for example, it cannot be 
used again for identification in the EUDI Wallet system.
The core of every wallet is the method of national 
identification that provides the ID data. In Germany, the 
online ID function is a secure and data protection-com-
pliant system. The online ID function is decentralised: 
when citizens identify themselves online, there is a 
direct link to the body to which they are identifying 
themselves. No data has to be loaded from a central 
33rd Activity Report 202471


<!-- page 72 -->
location. The online ID function is card-based. The ID 
card must be held up to a smartphone, for example, for 
every interaction. Although there have long been efforts 
to store the ID card in smartphones securely and the 
law on ID cards and electronic proof of identity (Perso-
nalausweisgesetz - PAuswG) already provides for this in 
Section 10a, smartphones with chips that support this 
are still not particularly widespread. The German wallet 
will therefore probably support a system known as an 
“evolutionary solution”. The missing security function of 
the smartphone hardware is offset by a central server. I 
am advising the Federal Office for Information Security, 
which is responsible for
the technical arrangements, with a focus on measures to 
reduce the risk of traceability and profiling on this cent-
ral server. I am committed to ensuring that the decentra-
lised solution is implemented when secure smartphone 
hardware is available.
7.2.6 Anonymisation
Anonymisation creates scope for research, value crea-
tion and innovation. Advice on anonymisation issues is 
therefore particularly important to me.
Put simply, the essence of anonymisation is the removal 
of any personal reference from data. The use of anony-
misation techniques is therefore intended to ensure that 
data subjects cannot be identified.
The advantages of these technologies for data processors 
are obvious. The data protection requirements for per-
sonal data no longer apply to anonymised data. Anony-
misation thus creates scope for research, value creation 
and innovation. This makes it an important instrument.
There are countless applications for it in all areas of our 
social and economic life, especially where data needs to 
be aggregated and statistical correlations presented. Ho-
wever, the anonymisation procedure is not always easy. 
This is because there is always a desire to maximise the 
informative value of the data at the same time.
The complexity of such a task means that a lot of advice 
is needed. In the year under review, I provided advice 
on legal and technical issues relating to the anonymisa-
tion of personal data in several projects. In addition, I 
worked intensively in this area within the framework of 
the Conference of Independent Federal and State Data 
Protection Authorities and the European Data Protection 
Board with the aim of reaching an agreement on a com-
mon national and international position.
7.2.7 Legal clarity for requests under the Free -
dom of Information Act via Frag-den-Staat
In its ruling of 20 March 2024, the Federal Administra-
tive Court (BVerwG) provided legal clarity regarding 
the admissibility of the standard collection of postal 
addresses for applications under the Freedom of Infor-
mation Act (IFG).
In my decision of 11 February 2020, I issued a data pro-
tection supervisory warning to the Federal Ministry of 
the Interior (BMI). The background to this was that the 
BMI had demanded that all IFG applicants provide their 
postal addresses across the board. One applicant had 
submitted an IFG application to the BMI via the platform 
Frag-den-Staat operated by the Open Knowledge Foun-
dation Deutschland e. V . The platform offers citizens a 
low-threshold digital service for submitting IFG applica-
tions to public authorities. For this purpose, the platform 
creates an email address, which usually consists of the 
applicant’s first and last name and a sequence of num-
bers and which is used to submit the IFG application 
electronically to the relevant public authority. The Open 
Knowledge Foundation Deutschland has taken measures 
to prevent misuse of its platform.
In principle, administrative procedures are not subject 
to any formal requirements, but must be carried out 
simply, expediently and swiftly (Section 10 sentence 2 
VwVfG). The decision in a procedure does not neces-
sarily have to be issued in paper form and does not 
necessarily have to be sent by post (Section 37 VwVfG). 
An exception to this under the IFG – because of the 
necessity of third-party involvement, for example, or to 
charge a fee – was not apparent in the specific case. A 
decision can therefore also be sent by email to the email 
address of the applicant at Frag-den-Staat. Nevertheless, 
the BMI had made further processing dependent on the 
provision of a postal address or an email address of a 
commercial provider. The applicant then gave the BMI 
both their postal address and another email address 
from a large commercial provider. However, in doing 
so they asked the BMI to send the decision to the email 
address provided if possible. Contrary to the request, the 
BMI sent its decision to the postal address. The applicant 
was informed that the requested information was not 
available. In my opinion, this could easily have been 
sent to the applicant’s email address at Frag-den-Staat. 
The request for the address and inevitably its further 
storage simply to send out negative information seemed 
unnecessary to me and is a violation of the principles of 
data minimisation and necessity.
The BMI’s action to contest my warning was success-
ful in the first instance. But the Higher Administrative 
Court of North Rhine-Westphalia ruled in my favour in 
33rd Activity Report 2024 72


<!-- page 73 -->
the second instance and found the BMI’s discretionary 
decision to send the letter by post to be legally flawed. 
The Federal Administrative Court has, however, now 
endorsed the BMI’s view in the appeal.116 It is of the 
opinion that necessity under data protection law should 
only be assessed after the authority has made a discreti-
onary decision. What is necessary under data protection 
law depends on the form of disclosure chosen by the 
authority.
The Federal Administrative Court’s decision provides 
greater legal clarity for citizens and the federal adminis-
tration and is therefore to be welcomed in principle. At 
the same time, I can also understand the perspective of 
the citizen concerned in this specific case, who wants 
to use a digital platform but then has to give a postal ad-
dress only to be informed by letter that the information 
they want is not available. In my view, this is not simple, 
expedient or swift – and not a good example of how the 
federal government can achieve its digitalisation goals. 
I hope that public authorities will increasingly focus on 
citizen-centred, digital administration and make greater 
use of the option of responding to IFG applications 
digitally. I will also continue to campaign for a low-th-
reshold, modern transparency law.
7.2.8 The new “Mein Justizpostfach” service
The “Mein Justizpostfach” (My Judicial Mailbox) ser-
vice enables citizens to send legally binding messages 
and documents electronically to courts, authorities 
and other bodies that are networked with it. From a 
data protection perspective, I can still see room for 
improvement in the service and the underlying infra-
structure.
Since October 2023, the Federal Ministry of the Interi-
or (BMI) has offered the “Mein Justizpostfach” (MJP) 
service. This is a free mailbox service in the BundID (in 
future DeutschlandID), which enables communication 
with networked bodies – courts, authorities, lawyers and 
public notaries, for example – via the EGVP infrastruc-
ture (Elektronisches Gerichts- und Verwaltungspostfach 
(Electronic Court and Administration Mailbox)). To be 
able to use the service, citizens must authenticate them-
selves with their electronic ID card. This ensures that the 
identity of all mailbox owners is verified so that docu-
ments can be transmitted to the respective recipients 
with legally binding effect by electronic means.
The MJP thus offers real added value for citizens, but 
also exemplifies the challenges of such “distributed 
services”. This term refers to services that are perceived 
by users as coming from a single source, although they 
116 Judgement of the Federal Administrative Court of 20 March 2024, 6 C 8.22, available at: 
https://www.bverwg.de/de/200324U6C8.22.0
are technically made up of several components that are 
themselves operated by different organisations. With 
the MJP, the mailbox component is operated by the BMI, 
but messages are sent using the EGVP infrastructure and 
other components are operated by the federal and state 
justice administrations. In such cases, providers and 
operators must carefully check who is solely or jointly 
responsible for the processing of personal data in the 
service and to what extent. This must be made transpa-
rent to users in the data protection notices by naming 
the controllers and providing information about data 
recipients so that they know which bodies process the 
data under their own responsibility and which bodies 
they can or must contact to exercise their rights. This 
was not guaranteed sufficiently for the MJP when a data 
breach occurred immediately after the service went 
live. Data subjects then contacted me because they were 
unable to identify which other bodies were responsible 
for processing their data on the basis of the data protec-
tion notices and therefore – in contrast to the BMI – had 
assumed that processing was the sole responsibility of 
the BMI. I am currently investigating how to categorise 
responsibility for the MJP.
I also see a need for action under data protection law 
with regard to the EGVP services that the MJP uses to 
facilitate addressing and sending of messages. The EGVP 
infrastructure was originally developed solely for sen-
ding messages between courts and public authorities, 
with the result that there were no provisions for the par-
ticipation of citizens. The EGVP was therefore originally 
designed in such a way that each mailbox owner appears 
in a directory with a selected data set. Other mailbox ow-
ners can view this directory data. This has not changed 
since the service has been opened up to citizens, with 
the result that the first and last names and addresses of 
citizens who have such a mailbox are currently visible 
to holders of other mailboxes (e.g. courts, authorities, 
lawyers and public notaries). However, the entries are 
not visible to other citizens. This applies regardless of 
whether there is a communication relationship between 
the parties involved. This should be changed in the me-
dium term so that directory data can only be viewed by 
citizens if and as long as a communication relationship 
exists between the specific parties involved.
33rd Activity Report 202473


<!-- page 74 -->
7.2.9 Early involvement in planned new 
regulations to prevent electricity and gas 
disconnections
When the electricity or gas supply is switched off in 
private households, it poses major challenges for those 
affected. The conditions under which electricity and 
gas disconnections can be avoided by involving social 
services have been regulated in an appropriate way 
in the amendment to the Energy Industry Act (EnWG), 
passed by the federal cabinet following extensive 
consultation with my office.
On 13 November 2024, the federal government adop-
ted the draft of a comprehensive Act to Amend Energy 
Industry Law in the areas of end customer markets, 
grid expansion and grid regulation, the so-called EnWG 
amendment, presented by the Federal Ministry for Eco-
nomic Affairs and Climate Protection (BMWK). The new 
regulation in question was not adopted in the legislative 
period at that time due to the early elections.
One regulation in the amendment is intended to pre-
vent disconnection of the energy supply to household 
customers who do not pay their bills despite reminders. 
In such cases, energy suppliers are authorised under 
certain conditions to have the energy supply cut off 
four weeks after prior warning. The amendment to the 
EnWG passed by the cabinet now includes a provision 
that allows data on defaulting household customers to 
be passed on to social services under strict conditions in 
order to avoid electricity or gas disconnections (Sections 
41f and 41g EnWG government draft).
My office was closely involved in the development of this 
new regulation by the BMWK even before the normal 
coordination between the departments. This is because 
the planned regulation is caught between data protecti-
on and the protection of those affected by electricity and 
gas disconnections. As a result of this comprehensive 
and constructive discussion, we arrived at a regulation 
that creates an appropriate balance between the two pro-
tected interests. The resulting government draft contains 
a graduated solution: firstly, those affected are given the 
opportunity to consent voluntarily to their data being 
passed on to local social services. As an alternative, it is 
also possible to conclude an instalment payment agree-
ment or to present a special need for protection to avert 
disconnection of the electricity or gas supply. Only if all 
of these options have been rejected and the bill has still 
not been paid can energy suppliers, under certain condi-
tions, inform local social services that the electricity or 
gas supply is about to be cut off with the aim of averting 
it. In accordance with my advice, the BMWK has thus 
117 Judgement of the ECJ of 20 October 2022, C306/21, available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/DE/TXT/? uri=CELEX%3A62021CJ0306&qid=1739620497817
decided against an early legal obligation for energy sup-
pliers to involve social services and has limited the scope 
of data that may be passed on to prevent disconnection 
of the energy supply to what is necessary.
7.2.10 Data protection in the Bundestag elec -
tion
The 2025 Bundestag election was held on the basis of 
amended electoral law . The revision of the Federal 
Electoral Regulations (BWO) was carried out with due 
consideration for data protection, with my advice 
requested at an early stage.
The need to amend the BWO arose following the reform 
of the Federal Election Act (BWG) in 2023. My office was 
involved in the revision of the BWO by the Federal Minis-
try of the Interior (BMI). In addition to various regula-
tory objectives that are not motivated by data protection 
law, the intention was in particular to adapt the BWO to 
the requirements of the GDPR and to improve protection 
of personal rights, e.g. for election candidates A judge-
ment of the European Court of Justice (ECJ) had to be 
taken into account here. In 2022, the ECJ clarified that 
the processing of personal data during elections is not 
excluded from the scope of the GDPR, which therefore 
also applies directly to federal elections.117 The BWO did 
not reflect the fact that the rights of data subjects are ba-
sed directly on the GDPR, although they are specifically 
set out in election law.
The new version of the BWO aims to make practical im-
provements to data protection. Improved protection of 
personal rights to prevent potential dangers is provided 
for people who are standing as candidates. In future, the 
standing for district elections will only involve stating 
candidates’ place of residence, not their full address. 
This welcome measure serves to protect them. It makes 
it more difficult for potential attackers to watch their 
home address. The BWO’s approach of providing data 
controllers with templates to inform data subjects is also 
welcome. This can help to ensure a uniformly high level 
of data protection during elections and the preparations 
for them in practice. A review of the revised templates 
was suggested to the BMI, particularly with regard to the 
provision of complete and comprehensible information.
I am delighted that the BMI involved my organisation 
in the deliberations about the planned changes at an 
early stage. My colleagues therefore had the opportunity 
to provide various comments on the protection of the 
rights and freedoms of natural persons with regard to 
the processing of personal data. I hope that this example 
33rd Activity Report 2024 74


<!-- page 75 -->
will set a precedent and that my offer of early advice on 
data protection law will be taken up more often.
7.3 Economy and finance
7.3.1 European Blockchain Sandbox
Real-world laboratories facilitate and accelerate the 
transfer of innovation into practice. As testing grounds 
for innovation and regulation, they offer potential that 
is particularly important for the digital transformation 
of the economy and society .
Since mid-2023, my office has been taking part in a re-
al-world laboratory on the topic of blockchain (European 
Blockchain Regulatory Sandbox) at the invitation of the 
European Commission.
As part of this real-world laboratory, representatives of 
selected projects from the blockchain sector meet with 
supervisory authorities to discuss regulatory aspects of 
innovative applications of blockchain and distributed 
ledger technologies in a secure and confidential dialo-
gue. The aim of the project is to provide a framework 
for regulators, supervisory authorities and blockchain 
innovators to engage in a regulatory dialogue and iden-
tify obstacles from a legal and regulatory perspective in 
a secure and confidential environment.
 → For the participating supervisory authorities, the re-
al-world laboratory offers an opportunity to gain in-
sights into new applications of blockchain technolo-
gy while providing the participating companies with 
a better understanding of the legal requirements in 
connection with their projects. I particularly welco-
me the fact that the real-world laboratory is helping 
to ensure that data protection factors are taken into 
account at an early stage in projects, in line with the 
principle of “data protection by design”.
 → In summer 2024, my office and four of the other par-
ticipating supervisory authorities received the “Most 
Innovative Regulator Award”, presented by a panel of 
independent academics from various European uni-
versities, for our contributions to the first “cohort” 
of the real-world laboratory. A Best Practices Report 
with results from the first cohort was published on 
the EU Commission’s website.118 
My office is also taking part in the second “cohort” of the 
sandbox, which is running at the moment.
118 Best Practices Report of the European Commission: 
https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/display/EBSISANDCOLLAB/ Best+practices+report+2023+-+Part+B
7.3.2 Messenger services
Messengers are available today in a wide range of vari-
ations, designs and system integrations and are one of 
the most widely used means of communication. I am 
responsible for the data protection supervision of mes-
senger services of federal public bodies and of mes-
senger services that are telecommunication services. 
In addition to checks on legally compliant use of these 
services, my office has dealt with new data protection 
issues arising in the context of interoperability and 
conducted a public consultation on the Standardised 
Messenger Audit catalogue.
Inspections of messenger services of the public autho-
rities of the federal government (BwMessenger & Wire 
Bund)
In addition to commercial messenger services offered 
for private and business communication, I am respon-
sible for those used within the federal government’s 
public administration. In the past year, my colleagues 
have been involved in two inspections concerning data 
protection in relation to internal messenger services of 
the administration.
One of the inspections concerned the BwMessenger ser-
vice. The Bundeswehr (German Federal Army) developed 
this using the Matrix open source protocol. It is ope-
rated in Bundeswehr data centres by BWI GmbH. The 
service is available to members of the Bundeswehr for 
official communication. In my inspection, I examined 
in particular the legal basis on which the Bundeswehr 
carries out its data processing in connection with the 
operation of the messenger service for members of the 
Bundeswehr.
The Bundeswehr obtained the consent of its employees 
for this purpose. On the one hand, my staff established 
that consent had not been obtained effectively. During 
the registration process, a box was ticked to indicate 
consent to the terms of use and the privacy policy. This 
means that data subjects are unable to make a suffi-
ciently clear distinction between giving consent and 
entering into a contract. On the other hand, I found that 
the consent was also ineffective from the perspective of 
employee data protection. This is because the questi-
on of the voluntary nature of consent must take into 
account the dependency of the employee in the emplo-
yment relationship and the circumstances under which 
the consent was given. Section 26(2) sentence 2 BDSG 
stipulates that voluntary consent may exist in particular 
if the employee obtains a legal or economic advantage 
as a result of the data processing or if the employer and 
33rd Activity Report 202475


<!-- page 76 -->
employee are pursuing similar interests. In response to 
my findings, the Bundeswehr will now adjust its checks, 
information and documents for the use of BwMessenger 
to ensure that the data processing that takes place for 
the provision of the service is necessary for fulfilment 
of its public duties and for the purposes of fulfilling the 
rights and obligations of the employment relationship.
The second inspection concerned the Wire Bund service 
and the question of whether this service constitutes a 
telecommunications service, who the provider of such a 
service is and which body is responsible under data pro-
tection law. To this end, my office reviewed whether the 
documents provided by the BMI and the ITZBund cor-
rectly represented the allocation of roles. My colleagues 
determined that the Wire Bund in its current form of 
purely internal administrative use is not a telecommu-
nications service. The controllers under data protection 
law are the respective federal public bodies using the 
service. The ITZBund acts as a processor.
In both cases, I was able to ascertain that the controlled 
organisations are well positioned in terms of data protec-
tion law and were cooperative. It became clear that there 
is a strong desire to make the services compliant with 
data protection regulations.
As is customary during visits by my office, I gave the 
bodies the opportunity to ask questions in an open 
dialogue immediately after the inspections, including 
questions beyond the subject area of the inspection. In 
this process, I was able to find out which topics and issu-
es are currently preoccupying stakeholders.
Remedial measures against two well-known and fre-
quently used services
Following complaints from data subjects, I initiated 
proceedings against two well-known and widely used 
messenger service providers that are not based in the 
EU. The one-stop-shop procedure does not apply to cont-
rollers not established in the EU.119
The background to the complaints in both cases was that 
the providers did not have a data protection officer wit-
hin the meaning of Art. 37 GDPR and did not provide a 
German privacy policy within the scope of Art. 13 GDPR. 
In addition, one provider did not name a representative 
in the EU in accordance with Art. 27 GDPR, while the 
other did not provide the contact details of the controller 
in the privacy policy.
While one of the two messenger service providers 
responded to the hearing and provided a privacy policy 
in German and the contact details of the controller, 
my office did not receive any feedback from the other 
119 See also https://www.bfdi.bund.de/DE/BfDI/Inhalte/Datenschutzpfad/DSGVO.html?nn=252102
provider. A decision against this service is now legally 
binding.
With regard to the outstanding issues, in particular 
the lack of a representative office in the EU and a data 
protection officer, my office is continuing to work 
towards establishing a legally compliant situation. This 
is difficult with services that are not based in the EU and 
that do not respond to correspondence from my office. 
Nevertheless, my office will make use of all the resour-
ces available to it in these cases.
In my opinion, violations of the obligation to designate 
contact persons are particularly serious, as data subjects 
are denied the opportunity from the outset to contact the 
messenger service provider and/or the data protection 
officer in the event of suspected violations of the provi-
sions of the GDPR and other data protection regulations 
and are unable to assert their rights as data subjects 
under Chapter 3 of the GDPR. This is even more the case 
in relation to electronic communication via messen-
ger services, as this is a particularly sensitive area in 
terms of fundamental rights. Message content regularly 
concerns domestic and private activities. Traffic data 
can also reveal a comprehensive picture of the private 
circumstances of users of messenger services.
I will report on the progress of the proceedings in due 
course.
Interoperability of messenger services
As in other areas of digital markets, large service provi-
ders with a significant market share have also emerged 
in messenger services over time. To protect competition, 
the European Union has imposed special requirements 
on so-called gatekeepers through the Digital Markets Act 
(DMA).
One of these requirements for gatekeepers providing 
number-independent interpersonal communications 
services (NIICS) is the obligation to ensure interopera-
bility with other providers of NIICS in accordance with 
Art. 7 DMA. Once a gatekeeper has been appointed by 
the EU Commission, it must facilitate various stages of 
interoperability with other market participants over 
time.
Starting with the opportunity to exchange text, image 
and voice messages between two individual end users, 
this must also be made possible for groups after two ye-
ars. Four years after appointment, voice and video calls 
between groups must be implemented as the final step. 
In addition, these functions must be implemented while 
maintaining the existing level of security.
33rd Activity Report 2024 76


<!-- page 77 -->
Along with major challenges for technical implementati-
on, complex questions also arise from a data protection 
perspective. For example, cross-provider discoverability 
(user discovery) of two users of different services is a dif-
ficult question. Not only must the user’s intention be ta-
ken into account and reflected in a data protection-com-
pliant manner, but the exchange of data must also be 
evaluated from a data protection perspective in order to 
facilitate discoverability from a technical perspective. 
Data protection requirements must be reconciled with 
technical necessity without losing the practicality of the 
approach.
Furthermore, the service providers have implemented 
numerous measures to prevent e.g. technical misuse by 
distribution of SPAM or bot accounts. These mechanis-
ms, some of which have been heavily optimised, must 
now be adapted to the new circumstances of interoper-
ability. Any requests for further data exchange between 
the services here must be checked for technical neces-
sity, for example, and, last but not least, admissibility 
under data protection law.
As these issues do not always concern data protection 
alone but also impinge on other subject areas, I have 
dealt with many of them in close cooperation with other 
authorities. In addition to the ongoing bilateral discussi-
ons with the BSI on this topic, the issue of interoperabi-
lity is one of the focal points of the Digital Cluster Bonn. 
Together with the BNetzA and the BSI in particular, I am 
actively working on common positions which can also be 
brought to the European Data Protection Board (EDPB).
However, I have not only dealt with these issues in the 
context of inter-agency cooperation. The second Jour 
Fixe on Data Protection and Messenger Services in May 
2024 focussed on the topic of interoperability. In parti-
cular, technical issues were discussed with the participa-
ting authorities and providers of messenger services.
This highlighted that there is still a long way to go before 
users can communicate with each other across different 
messenger services.
Public consultation on the Standardised Messenger 
Audit - Frontend model
I would like to create a uniform standard for inspection 
and verification of messenger services.
To this end, my staff already developed a standardised 
audit catalogue for front-end applications through the 
EDPB “Support Pool of Experts” format in 2023.
A uniform audit standard with sufficient flexibility for 
individual systems is very complex and extensive. In 
order to give other national and international super-
visory authorities, providers, users and developers of 
messenger services the opportunity to draw my attenti-
on to possible special and marginal cases, the catalogue 
underwent public consultation. It was important to me 
to invite both specialist users and society at large to par-
ticipate in the design of criteria for data protection-com-
pliant messenger services.
I would like to thank everyone who participated for their 
numerous responses. My colleagues will now analyse 
them and publish the results on my website. They will be 
incorporated into the revision and further development 
of the audit catalogue alongside experience from practi-
cal implementation.

7.3.3 Position paper on pseudonymisation of 
electricity meter data
With the introduction of smart meters, consumption 
readings are no longer calculated on an annual basis, 
but at a minimum of every quarter of an hour. 36,500 
consumption readings per year make it possible to 
profile consumption behaviour and therefore require 
particularly careful handling.
The use of electricity consumption data is compre-
hensively regulated under data protection law by the 
Metering Point Operation Act (MsbG). This means that 
only those measuring systems that meet the high cyber 
security requirements of the Technical Guidelines of the 
Federal Office for Information Security may be used, 
and that data may only be sent to the authorised bodies 
to the extent that is necessary for the fulfilment of their 
respective tasks.
The amendment to the law at the beginning of 2023 
stipulated that the consumption values recorded by the 
smart meter every quarter of an hour are to be sent to 
the respective authorised bodies in pseudonymised form 
on a daily basis for the previous day for planning pur-
poses and for the purposes of balancing the electricity 
suppliers’ electricity market transactions. I already made 
it clear during the legislative process that for pseudony-
misation to prevent the households associated with the 
respective consumption profiles from being identified 
effectively, the data must already be pseudonymised 
when it is sent. The procedure for pseudonymisation 
must be specified by the Federal Network Agency in 
consultation with my office.
During the preparatory discussions with the Federal Net-
work Agency (BNetzA), it emerged that swift implemen-
tation is unlikely given the large number of municipal 
utilities and network operators affected. In my position 
33rd Activity Report 202477


<!-- page 78 -->
paper120 for a Determination by the Federal Network 
Agency pursuant to Section 47 para. 2 no. 13 MsbG on 
pseudonymisation pursuant to Section 52 para. 3 MsbG 
of 28 June 2024, I made clear the conditions under which 
the quarter-hourly values can still be used for the in-
tended purposes during the transition period. The most 
important prerequisite here is the strict organisational 
separation of the balancing and planning bodies from 
the bodies that have to deal with personally identifiable 
information, for example for billing. This position is 
supported by the BNetzA.
The BNetzA has explained to me that it plans to reorga-
nise the market rules for the quarter-hourly balancing 
of electricity volumes so that in the medium term there 
will only be one body, which will not also be involved in 
the processing of connection-related data. This in turn 
will provide the other bodies with cumulative consump-
tion readings over time, which are therefore no longer 
connection-related, to carry out their work. I am very 
much in favour of this approach because the current 
legally established daily distribution of quarter-hourly 
readings to the respective authorised body is no lon-
ger necessary and the associated risk of improper data 
processing is reduced. The relevant statutory regula-
tions could be repealed with the implementation of the 
BNetzA ’s proposal.
7.3.4 Analyses of Android apps using the ex -
ample of children’s smartwatches
In the reporting year, detailed technical experience 
of suitable analytical tools and methods was collected 
through individual investigations of Android apps 
and IoT devices. It was possible to customise some 
open-source investigative tools to meet specific needs 
or develop them to include functional extensions. 
Further tools, for documenting and analysing the re-
sults of investigations for example, have been designed 
and implemented.
My office has been operating an IT laboratory since 
spring 2023 and it is currently being put into regular 
operation. In the laboratory environment, the staff of 
my office are able to analyse the data flows of products – 
such as apps, applications or devices – by using suitable 
investigation tools.
The experience gained from the investigations carried 
out in the reporting year made it possible to define a 
general investigation process. The aim is to test and opti-
mise the process further next year so that it can be used 
120 Position paper of 28 June 2024, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/DE/DokumenteBfDI/Dokumente-allg/2024/ Positionspapier-Pseudonymisierung-Z%C3%A4hlerstand-
g%C3%A4nge.pdf
more easily when investigating other IoT devices. The 
process is divided into the following phases:
1. Product selection: After analysing the market, cha-
racteristics and criteria are defined for the selection 
of products, followed by their procurement.
2. Test planning: The conceptual phase comprises the 
definition of suitable scenarios and actions and a 
catalogue of the characteristics to be examined and 
criteria for their evaluation.
3. Test procedure: The operational phase comprises the 
preparation and execution of the technical investiga-
tion and documentation of the results.
4. Evaluation: Evaluation of the characteristics defined 
in phase 2 on the basis of the evaluation criteria, 
taking into account the documented results.
5. Analysis report: Target group-specific summary of 
the evaluation, e.g. to raise awareness and educate 
the public or to expand the knowledge underpinning 
investigation tools and methods already available in 
the data protection community.
 
 
Laboratory environment  
 
The infrastructure of the laboratory environment 
provides virtual machines as test environments that 
can be used to analyse IT applications and hardware 
in a dynamic way. An open source product is used 
as the virtualisation solution. The virtual machines 
have free, unfiltered access to the internet; this can 
be important for investigations, as the rest of the 
federal authorities‘ IT is connected to the so called 
federal networks and can only leave these via proxies 
that are filtered in some circumstances. Access to the 
network of the laboratory environment is established 
via a VPN connection; the access protocol also allows 
locally connected USB devices, such as Android 
devices for use of ADB or USB WLAN sticks for use as 
a hotspot, to access the virtual machine.  
When investigating some of the apps for controlling and 
configuring children’s smartwatches, the main aim was 
to test my colleagues’ technological toolbox and develop 
further ideas and tools (phases 2 to 4). The methods 
developed, tested and proven in the process were made

knowledge management system of my office.
The aim was therefore not to gain a comprehensive 
overview of the market or even to investigate specific

The staff of my office wrote an article for the magazine

7.3.5 Requirements of secure video conferen -
cing services
Last year, there were several media reports about 
security vulnerabilities relating to a widely used video 
conferencing service. My office helped to clarify the 
facts of the case from a data protection perspective 
and raised awareness by publishing security notices 
and specific recommendations for action.
Whether privately or professionally: video conferencing 
has become an important tool for communication and 
collaboration. This made it all the more worrying when, 
half way through the year, security loopholes were re-
ported in a major video conferencing service.
Security loopholes could be exploited to gain unauthori-
sed access to information from meetings. The titles of 
meetings, the start time and planned duration and the 
name of the person who created the meeting were all 
affected. In some cases, it was also possible to dial in to 
meeting rooms by telephone without entering a pass-
word. The security loopholes were closed both by system 
adjustments and by adapting the recommendations for 
the basic operational settings.
Insecure video conferencing services are gateways for 
criminals – and therefore also a threat to data protecti-
on. The security of a video conference does not depend 
exclusively on the video conferencing tool used, but also 
on whether appropriate measures have been taken ope-
rationally in terms of the arrangements for and content 
of a video conference. The use of passwords for access to 
conference rooms should be emphasised in particular. 
This is particularly the case if it is possible to dial in by 
telephone. So-called waiting lobbies are recommended 
to check eligibility for participation in advance.
In order to guarantee the protection of personal data 
when using video conferencing, providers must ensure 
compliance with the requirements of data protection 
and telecommunications legislation. In this connection, 
I have prepared the recommendation document “Legal 
data protection requirements for commercial providers 
of video conferencing services”. This is intended as an 
aid for providers of commercial video conferencing 
services and gives an overview of the important legal 
and technical requirements. The recommendations are 
published on my homepage and are also intended to 
serve as a benchmark for future inspections.123

In the reporting year, my office organised two mee-

data protection supervisory authorities to share infor-

tive interaction and also helped those responsible for 
the IT labs to get to know one another.
Together with the State Data Protection Commissioner 
of Lower Saxony, my office set up opportunities for the 
IT laboratories of the German-speaking data protection 
supervisory authorities to share their experiences. The 
invitation was issued by the Technology Working Group 
of the Conference of Independent Federal and State 
Data Protection Authorities. Two meetings were held in 
the reporting year – one at my office in Bonn and one at 
the liaison office in Berlin. The meetings were attended

tection supervisory authorities, the Broadcasting Data 
Protection Officer, the Data Protection Authority of the 
Principality of Liechtenstein, the European Data Protec-

The cornerstones of the collaboration were defined at

At the first meeting, various technical approaches and

create an audit process. This topic was then continued 
and finalised at the second meeting.
The second meeting focussed on testing IoT devices and

where participants had the opportunity to test IoT 
devices and apps with PiRogue mini-computers and the

also provided the opportunity for those responsible for

7.3.7 Age verification in digital services
Discussions about the use of age verification methods 
in digital services have been gathering pace since the 
EU Digital Services Act (DSA) came into force and the 
negotiations on the so-called draft CSA regulation were 
held.124 Against this background, I have been working 
intensively this year on the question surrounding 
the admissibility of age verification methods under 
data protection law . I also invited the German bodies 
involved in the implementation of the DSA to a joint 
workshop at my office in May 2024 and we drew up a 
document of points to take forward.
There is no doubt that it is important that minors are 
adequately protected from risks such as grooming, bul-
lying and exposure to inappropriate content when using 
digital services and that the digital world is as safe a 
place as possible for them. A number of measures come 
into consideration for this purpose. In my opinion, age 
verification methods should not be seen as a one-size-
fits-all solution.
The use of age verification methods regularly involves 
data processing and therefore requires a legal basis. 
This may result from a legal obligation. However, this is 
only applicable to some digital services, including those 
with access to content that puts minors at risk, gambling 
or the purchase of certain products such as alcohol, 
tobacco products or weapons. So-called online platforms 
are obliged under the DSA to take proportionate child 
protection measures. However, the DSA does not include 
a general obligation to use age verification methods that 
involve the processing of additional personal data. It 
may be that an obligation arises for high-risk, very large 
online platforms categorised as such by the European 
Commission.
Outside of legal obligations, service providers must use 
a different legal basis and regularly weigh up the options 
if they want to use age verification methods. Their 
considerations must prioritise the best interests of the 
child, which, in addition to protection against risks, also 
include the right to participation and empowerment in 
a digital environment and the privacy and informational 
self-determination of children.
33rd Activity Report 2024 80


<!-- page 81 -->
When considering whether a method of age verification 
can be used, its efficacy must also be examined in view 
of the often low-threshold circumvention options. Other 
measures, such as content moderation or reporting 
procedures, are often less intrusive and even better at 
achieving effective protection of minors. It is particular-
ly important that the services have strict standard data 
protection and child-friendly default settings. This inclu-
des, in particular, prevention by default of mechanisms 
controlled by algorithms that can promote excessive 
use. Personalisation of content and advertising that is 
possible with the help of user profiling should not be 
used by default in order to protect children. Displaying 
advertising based on profiling of minors is prohibited 
by Art. 28(2) DSA in general. Furthermore, the profiles 
should not be publicly visible and contact from strangers 
should be prevented.
The assessment should also include a consideration of 
whether it may be possible to take risk minimisation 
measures only for certain areas or individual functions 
that actually pose a risk to children.
Otherwise, the general provisions of the GDPR apply. 
Age verification methods that process sensitive data or 
are based exclusively on an automated decision and are 
used as absolute barriers to access are generally not per-
mitted. Age verification methods are also generally not 
permitted if they are associated with unique identifica-
tion, profiling, tracking or cross-service linking of data. 
They should be designed in such a way that they only 
pass on information as to whether users are authorised 
to access a service or certain content. No information 
should be passed on about whether the person is a 
minor or how old they are. In addition, detailed informa-
tion should be provided about the methods and the data 
processing associated with them, in particular to make 
users aware of the difference between an age check and 
online identification.
Overall, I believe that age verification methods should 
only be used with caution, as they are associated with 
considerable social implications such as jeopardising 
anonymity or pseudonymity on the internet and entail 
considerable encroachment on the fundamental rights 
of children and adolescents in particular. Service pro-
viders should initially take less invasive and possibly 
more effective measures to protect children, such as the 
measures of data protection and child-friendly default 
settings or reporting procedures mentioned above.
At least as important as technical measures for digital 
services are socio-political measures, such as raising 
awareness at an early stage and education of parents 
125 Points on age verification, available at:: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/DE/DokumenteBfDI/Dokumente-allg/2024/ Punktepapier_gem-deut-Position-Altersverifikation.pdf
and guardians, in order to enable children to use digital 
services in an age-appropriate manner.
In May of this year, I invited the agencies involved in 
implementing the DSA – the Federal Network Agency, 
the Federal Ministry for Family Affairs, Senior Citizens, 
Women and Youth, the Federal Centre for Child and 
Youth Media Protection and the State Media Authority 
of North Rhine-Westphalia – to a joint workshop. At this 
session, we developed a joint position on the topic of age 
verification in digital services. The points raised can be 
viewed in a document on my website.125
Cross-references:
4.2.1 General report from the EDPB, 5.4.2 The EU Digital 
Services Act and the German Digital Services Act
7.3.8 “Chat monitoring” versus fundamental 
rights
The EU draft regulation on the tracing of online child 
sexual abuse material (CSAM) was pushed back by the 
European legislature by another year. While the EU 
Parliament’s report of November 2023 addresses the 
most critical points in terms of data protection policy , 
the Member States were unable to agree on a common 
position in the Council of the EU. The changes propo-
sed so far are not yet viable from the perspective of 
data protection policy , despite numerous ideas.
In my previous two Activity Reports, I reported on the 
plans of the European Union (EU) to adopt a regulati-
on on preventing and combating online child sexual 
abuse (CSA Regulation) and criticised points that were 
problematic in terms of data protection law. Protecting 
children from abuse is an extremely important goal both 
offline and online and must be supported as one of the 
foundations of a democratic society. At the same time, 
the European legislature’s plans go far beyond this goal 
and would mean the introduction of blanket surveillance 
of private communications without cause. In practice, 
the draft regulation presented by the EU Commission 
in May 2022 envisages that providers of messenger and 
hosting services will be obliged to search the content 
of their service without cause by breaking end-to-end 
encryption or by reading it on the end device (so-called 
client-side scanning). According to the Commission’s 
draft, it should be possible to search text and audio 
messages. However, breaking the encryption undermi-
nes any form of confidential communication and would 
lead to security loopholes that could also be exploited by 
criminals.
33rd Activity Report 202481


<!-- page 82 -->
While those in favour of the draft legislation never tire 
of arguing that it only provides for issuing of so-called 
discovery orders against individual service providers, 
the consequence of such an order is that all communica-
tions of the service in question would be searched. In the 
case of one of the largest and most popular messenger 
services, this would potentially affect around 60 million 
citizens in Germany alone.
Adaptation of the regulatory approach
Although the EU Parliament’s Home Affairs Committee 
agreed on a provisional position on 14 November 2023126 
(report), the Council of the EU has not yet been able to 
agree on its own position (general direction). The EU 
Parliament’s report includes some positions that protect 
fundamental rights and, in my view, significant impro-
vements over the Commission’s draft regulation. This 
applies in particular to the change to using discovery or-
ders in a targeted way only and against people or groups 
about whom there are concrete suspicions. Even though 
there is still room for improvement in the detail here,127 
the report is a step in the right direction.
In the negotiations in the Council of the EU, the presi-
dencies have tried to reconcile the sometimes widely 
divergent positions of the Member States. The consi-
derable data protection concerns that I have repeatedly 
raised remain unchanged.
One of the proposals made during the Belgian presiden-
cy of the Council of the EU in the first half of 2024 was 
the introduction of a so-called flagging system to com-
pensate for the still very high error rates in the planned 
technologies to be used to detect CSA material. Instead 
of reporting users together with the content found direc-
tly to the responsible law enforcement authorities, a flag 
should first be set and a report should only be made to 
the authorities if the system is triggered again. However, 
this system does not address the core problem and is 
an ineffective treatment of symptoms. Given the large 
number of messages and the error rate of up to 12 per 
cent,128 reports of wrongly suspected persons (so-called 
“false positives”) are not significantly reduced in this 
way. In the worst case and with simple implementation, 
merely sending malicious content repeatedly is enough 
to trigger the system. I will continue to campaign to ch-
126 Summary of the position of the LIBE Committee on the CSAM Regulation, available at: 
https://www.europarl.europa.eu/news/en/ press-room/20231110IPR10118/child-sexual-abuse-online-effective-measures-no-mass-surveillance
127 Joint statement of 1/2024 by the EDPB and EDPS, available at: 
https://www.edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/our-documents/ statements/statement-12024-legislative-developments-regarding-proposal_en and my press release, 
available at: https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Pressemitteilungen/DE/2024/01_CSA-Verordnung.html
128 European Commission, Working Documents of the Commission Departments, Impact Assessment Report, accompanying documents to the proposal for a Re-
gulation of the European Parliament and of the Council laying down rules on preventing and combating child sexual abuse, SWD(2022) 209 final, page 283, available 
at: https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/? uri=CELEX%3A52022SC0209
129 For the definition of grooming, see also European Commission, Working Documents of the Commission Departments, Impact Assessment Report, accom-
panying documents to the proposal for a Regulation of the European Parliament and of the Council laying down rules on preventing and combating child sexual 
abuse, SWD(2022) 209 final, page 3, available at: https://eur-lex.europa.eu/ legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX%3A52022SC0209
ange the actual violation of fundamental rights – blanket 
scanning of all message content without cause – to a 
level that protects those fundamental rights, for example 
by taking up the proposals of the EU Parliament.
In addition to the flagging system, other proposed 
amendments to the draft regulation were made in an 
attempt to attenuate the severity of the threatened 
encroachments on fundamental rights. As an alternati-
ve to client-side scanning, one proposal envisages that 
users must consent to the monitoring of their com-
munications as a prerequisite for using a service or a 
requirement without which the service can only be used 
to a very limited extent. Apart from the fact that this 
idea is unlikely to meet the necessary requirements for 
the voluntary nature of consent, this proposal does not 
minimise the core problem of blanket mass surveillance 
without cause.
After taking over the EU Council presidency in the 
second half of 2024, Hungary presented further ideas 
to the other EU Member States regarding the draft 
regulation. Building on the last draft of the previous 
presidency, for example, proposals included limiting 
the scope of discovery orders to known CSAM, revie-
wing the risk categorisation of services and requiring 
some service providers to cooperate in the development 
of scanning technologies. The restriction of discovery 
orders to known CSAM and thus the reduction in the 
risk of “false positives” is a positive development from a 
data protection perspective. But even with this proposal, 
discovery orders would still not be issued on the grounds 
of suspicion or be targeted at specific persons or groups, 
but would continue to apply to all users of a service. In 
addition, this draft also provided for breaking of end-to-
end encryption and client-side scanning. I continue to 
see considerable data protection problems in this.
According to the proposal of the Hungarian presiden-
cy, scanning for unknown CSAM and specific contact 
between adults and minors with the intention of abuse 
(grooming)129 – and thus the problem of the high error 
rate of the technologies in question – is simply to be 
rolled out and transferred to a transitional CSAM Interim 
Regulation (Regulation (EU) 2021/1232), with extension 
of the period of validity. The Interim Regulation is inten-
33rd Activity Report 2024 82


<!-- page 83 -->
ded to enable service providers to scan, but not to make 
scanning mandatory. However, this regulation does not 
constitute a valid legal basis for CSAM scanning, as the 
regulation itself states in recital 10. This will only pro-
long the legal uncertainty that already exists. As a result, 
the Hungarian proposal also failed to reach agreement 
in the Council.
I am very pleased that the federal government has so far 
stuck to its red lines and campaigned in the EU Council 
for a major adjustment to the proposed regulation. In 
April 2023, for example, the federal government took 
a critical stance against many points of the draft re-
gulation and made its approval of the current Council 
draft dependent on an amendment to the main points 
of criticism. In the current reporting year, the federal 
government has continued to uphold these points of cri-
ticism, thereby helping to ensure that a draft regulation 
that violates fundamental rights is not adopted. It is now 
important that the federal government also maintains 
this course in the 21st legislative period. I am happy to 
continue to offer my active advice to the federal govern-
ment on a way of achieving the goal of combating child 
pornography in the CSA Regulation, which I expressly 
support, while protecting fundamental rights.
      
I recommend that the German Bundestag urge the 
federal government and the EU legislature to revise 
the draft regulation on chat control significantly in line 
with fundamental rights and in accordance with the 
EP report of November 2023, which guarantees end-to-
end encryption, excludes the reading of messages on 
end devices (client-side scanning), safeguards funda-
mental German and European (communication) rights 
and prohibits blanket reading of private communi-
cations without cause, or otherwise to work towards 
rejecting the draft regulation as a whole. 
      
7.3.9 Delivery to automated parcel machines 
under the new Postal Act
With the new Postal Act (PostG), precise guidelines 
for the delivery of letters and parcels have come 
into force for the first time, which apply to all postal 
service providers. Recipients of post can now instruct 
service providers to deliver parcels in particular to 
provider-neutral automated stations, for example. At 
the same time, the law stipulates that an objection can 
be made to delivery to a provider’s own station if this is 
not wanted by the person concerned.
On 19 July 2024, a completely revised PostG came into 
force in the form of the Postal Law Modernisation Act. In 
addition to the previous PostG, the Universal Postal Ser-
vices Ordinance (PUDL V), the Postal Services Ordinance 
(PDL V) and the Postal Security Act are no longer in force. 
These regulations have largely been incorporated into 
the new PostG or transferred unchanged. This also ap-
plies to the central standards of particular relevance to 
me, Sections 67-71 PostG, which contain data protection 
regulations.
Sections 12 and 13 PostG introduce new regulations 
that specify exactly how letter and parcel consignments 
should be delivered. To date, only universal service 
operators or market-dominating providers of postal ser-
vices have been subject to the PUDL V and PDL V . These 
regulations now apply to all postal service providers in a 
significantly expanded form.
For example, recipients of parcels now have the right to 
instruct postal service providers to deliver parcels to a 
provider-neutral automated station under Section 13(2) 
No. 2 PostG. Companies do not have to comply with this 
only if delivery cannot be made to such a station for 
reasons for which its provider is responsible.
At the same time, in the course of the legislative process 
my office has worked to ensure that people who do not 
want or are unable to use automated parcel stations can 
prevent parcels from being delivered to such stations. 
According to the new PostG, postal companies are 
generally allowed to deliver parcels to their own stations 
without an agreement if the recipient was not present 
at the delivery address and another form of delivery 
was not possible. According to Section 13(3) sentence 
3 PostG, however, it must be possible to object to such 
parcel delivery on a one-off or permanent basis if the 
stations can only be used with a technical device belon-
ging to the recipient, e.g. their own smartphone.
In addition, data subjects must be informed of this right 
to object in a transparent way by the respective provider 
in accordance with Section 13(3) sentence 4 PostG.
In my view, it would have been more citizen-friendly to 
stipulate in the PostG that parcels can only be delivered 
to automated parcel stations if the recipient so wis-
hes. However, the right of objection introduced in the 
parliamentary procedure allows people who do not use 
a smartphone or do not want to download the corres-
ponding app to their smart phone to continue to receive 
delivery of parcels without using additional technical 
equipment.
7.3.10 50th Telecommunications Jour Fixe
For 25 years, my office has hosted a Telecommunica-
tions Jour Fixe. The Jour Fixe serves as a forum for 
dialogue with the telecommunications industry on 
current data protection issues.
33rd Activity Report 202483


<!-- page 84 -->
A wonderful anniversary: the 50th Telecommunications 
Jour Fixe was held in autumn 2024. Organised every 
six months, it serves as a forum for dialogue with the 
telecommunications industry on current data protection 
issues. The group of participants is made up in particu-
lar of data protection officers from telecommunications 
companies and representatives of industry associations. 
Other federal authorities also regularly take part, such as 
various ministries, the Federal Network Agency and the 
Federal Office for Information Security.
Dialogue and interaction have been the guiding princi-
ples of the Jour Fixe from the very beginning. Dialogue 
formats such as these are of benefit to all parties. They 
are important to me because they have a positive impact 
on my advisory and control activities. The effects of my 
supervision can be discussed directly. How do planned 
legal projects work and how can new requirements be 
operationalised in a data protection-compliant manner? 
My staff can then build directly on the results of the Jour 
Fixe during forthcoming consultation and inspection 
visits to the companies.
In recent years, topics such as data retention and the 
consequences of the Schrems II judgement of the Eu-
ropean Court of Justice have been perennial hot topics. 
Citizens’ complaints that reach me in the area of tele-
communications focus on the exercise of data subjects’ 
rights, in particular the right to data access. This topic 
has therefore also been a frequent focus of the Jour Fixe 
– and will undoubtedly remain so in the future. The sco-
pe and limits of the right to access information are cons-
tantly being consolidated by the courts. New judgements 
therefore require prompt discussion with the industry. 
Companies can then answer customer enquiries in a 
legally compliant way so that, ideally, a complaint to my 
office is not even necessary.
The positions developed with the industry and the 
participating authorities are often published in compact 
form. These publications can have a lasting impact on 
data processing in companies. My office’s guide to data 
protection-compliant storage of traffic data130 from 2012 
is one such example. Another is my “Position on requests 
for information from telecommunications service provi-
ders”.131
I will continue to operate the successful model of the 
Telecommunications Jour Fixe. The feedback from the 
industry indicates that there is also great interest in its 
continuation.
130 24th AR No. 6.7
131 BfDI position on requests for information from telecommunications service providers, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/DE/Fachthemen/ Inhalte/Telefon-Internet/Datenschutzpraxis/Auskunftsrecht_Telekommunikationsanbieter.html
7.3.11 No AI training at Meta
Meta informed data protection authorities in the 
summer of 2024 that it wanted to make use of its users’ 
data for the purposes of AI training. After many critical 
questions from me and my European colleagues, Meta 
has suspended the announced AI training until further 
notice.
Meta announced in June 2024 that it wanted to carry out 
AI training in future with user data from its Facebook, 
Instagram and WhatsApp services. Meta intended to 
use the legal basis of legitimate interest (Art. 6(1) point 
(f) GDPR) for its evaluation of user data. This raised 
many critical questions: whether, for example, analysis 
for AI training purposes is covered by the legal basis of 
legitimate interest. And whether third parties who are 
affected by posts from meta users that are processed 
for AI training have been informed in a transparent way 
about the planned data processing. It also remained 
unclear until the end which data Meta wanted to use 
from the WhatsApp messaging service for its AI training, 
especially as communication content cannot be read due 
to end-to-end encryption.
After my European colleagues from the European Data 
Protection Board (EDPB) and I submitted various critical 
questions to the Irish data protection supervisory autho-
rity (DPC), which took the lead on this issue, and the DPC 
then forwarded these to Meta, Meta decided to cancel 
the planned AI training until further notice. Meta has yet 
to provide a detailed answer to the questions posed by 
me and my European colleagues.
7.3.12 Subscription models for large online 
platforms
Triggered by a judgement of the European Court of 
Justice (ECJ), the discussions of the European Data 
Protection Board (EDPB) on “consent-or-pay” models, 
which are designed to obtain legal data protection 
consent, have attracted considerable attention. In the 
reporting year, the EDPB issued a statement on the 
use of such models on large online platforms for the 
purposes of behavioural advertising.
Pay a monthly fee or reveal your surfing behaviour – 
more and more digital services are presenting their 
users with this choice. The service providers want to 
display personalised advertising based on the consent 
of their users and thus finance the service. In practice, 
the vast majority of users agree to extensive processing 
33rd Activity Report 2024 84


<!-- page 85 -->
if their only alternative is to take out a subscription.132 
In many cases, tracking leads to a very significant and 
extensive recording and evaluation of user behaviour 
in digital space, which has consequences not only for 
the fundamental rights of individual users, but also 
for society as a whole. For example, personalisation of 
advertising and content by algorithms can lead to the 
formation of so-called filter bubbles. Consent-or-pay 
models bring the risk of privacy and data protection 
becoming commodities that are reserved for those who 
can afford them.
In its ruling of 4 July 2023, the ECJ dealt with the legality 
of data processing for the purposes of behavioural adver-
tising. The Court of Justice doubted whether, in the case 
presented, the practice could be covered by any legal 
basis other than consent. In the course of its delibera-
tions, the ECJ stated in an “obiter dictum” (Latin for „said 
in passing“) that users must have “the freedom to refuse 
consent to certain data processing operations individu-
ally in the course of concluding a contract if they are not 
necessary for the performance of the contract, without 
being forced not to use the service offered by the opera-
tor of the online social network at all, which implies that 
they are offered an equivalent alternative which does not 
involve such data processing operations, where approp-
riate for a reasonable fee”.133
In light of this ECJ judgement, the Dutch, Norwegian 
and Hamburg supervisory authorities requested an 
opinion from the EDPB pursuant to Art. 64(2) GDPR on 
the effectiveness of data protection consent for large 
online platforms under “consent-or-pay” models. These 
are models in which users must either consent to the 
processing of their personal data for the purposes of 
behavioural advertising or pay a fee in order to use the 
service in question.
With an application under Art. 64(2) GDPR, supervisory 
authorities, the chair of the EDPB or the Commission 
may request that a matter of general applicability or 
with implications in more than one Member State be 
examined by the EDPB. The EDPB must then submit its 
opinion within eight weeks, although this period may be 
extended by a further six weeks in view of the complexi-
ty of the matter – as was the case in this procedure.
132 See, for example, Spiegel Online’s own information, available at: 
https://devspiegel.medium.com/wie-unser-pur-angebot-f%C3%BCr-werbefreies-lesen-ankommt-f92abaa0640d or the information of contentpasst, see Morel/San-
tos/Fredholm/Thunberg, Legitimate Interest is the New Consent - Large-Scale Measurement and Legal Compliance of IAB Europe TCF Paywalls, page 3, available at: 
http://arxiv.org/pdf/2309.11625
133 Judgement of the ECJ of 4 July 2023, C-252/21, available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/DE/TXT/? uri=CELEX%3A62021CJ0252&qid=1739628145948
134 EDPB opinion 08/2024, available at: 
https://www.edpb.europa.eu/our-work-tools/our-documents/opinion-board-art-64/opinion-082024-valid-consent-context-consent-or_de
In its opinion,134 the EDPB addresses the effectiveness of 
consent to data processing for behavioural advertising 
purposes in the case of “consent-or-pay” models of large 
online platforms. The EDPB highlights the voluntary 
nature of consent as a problem, in particular with regard 
to the disadvantages of refusal (payment of a fee or 
non-use), lock-in and network effects, power imbalan-
ces between providers and users and conditionality (i.e. 
the question of whether consent is required for access 
to goods or services even though the processing is not 
necessary for the performance of the contract). Further-
more, the EDPB is concerned with the question of when 
an alternative offered by the provider (possibly in return 
for a fee) without consent to data processing is equiva-
lent and with further requirements of consent such as 
granularity, specificity and informed decision-making.
The EDPB concludes that in most cases it will not be 
possible for large online platforms to meet the GDPR’s 
requirements for effective consent if they only present 
users with the binary choice of either consenting to the 
processing of personal data for the purposes of behavi-
oural advertising or paying a fee. Large online platforms 
should therefore consider offering another free alterna-
tive without behavioural advertising, e.g. in the form of 
advertising that processes less (or no) personal data.
Following completion of the opinion, the EDPB’s Key 
Provisions Experts Subgroup was given the mandate of 
developing further guidelines on the topic of “consent 
or pay” for use by controllers other than large online 
platforms and for processing purposes other than beha-
vioural advertising.
Further discussions will show whether and how “con-
sent-or-pay” models can be used in other contexts for 
effective consent and as a financing model for digital 
services. The more services introduce such models, the 
more pressing the question becomes of the extent to 
which users will only be able to guarantee their privacy 
and data protection in the digital world for a fee in fu-
ture. I will continue play an active part in these discus-
sions.
