7.4 Security
7.4.1 Implementation of the EU Smart Borders 
programme by border control authorities
Introduction of the European border control Entry-
Exit System (EES) and European Travel and Authorisa-
tion System (ETIAS) planned for 2024 had to be post-
poned again at short notice. Preparations are being 
completed by the competent national authorities, in 
particular the Federal Police. I continued to support 
the implementation process during the reporting 
period.
With its Smart Borders programme, the EU Commission 
intends to increase automation and digitalisation of the 
control process at external borders. In addition to ad-
apting and linking existing databases, two new systems 
will also be set up. My office has been monitoring the 
planned changes with a critical eye for some years.135
Two of these systems were originally due to be launched 
in 2024:
the EES is intended largely to eliminate manual stam-
ping of passports; instead, a so-called EES dossier is 
created for every third-country national on entry into 
the Schengen area.
The stored data records include master data (name, 
date of birth, etc.), fingerprints and photographs of the 
person. In addition, the date of the border crossing is re-
corded and the authorised period of stay is automatically 
calculated at the same time.
The ETIAS affects third-country nationals who are 
exempt from a visa requirement and who will in future 
have to apply for a travel authorisation before travelling 
to the Schengen area. The applications are automatically 
compared with other databases in the central system, 
and if a match is found, the so-called national ETIAS 
centre (in Germany the Federal Police Headquarters) 
decides whether to grant the authorisation.
The EES was scheduled to go into operation in the se-
cond half of the year after the two major sporting events 
in summer 2024 (European Football Championships and 
Olympic Games), with the ETIAS following six months 
later. In September, Germany, France and the Nether-
lands informed the responsible EU commissioner that 
they were not ready to put the systems into operation 
due to a lack of adequate testing opportunities, with the 
result that the launch has now been further delayed.
The Federal Police is involved in implementing the 
systems in the border control procedures, which are 
135 27th AR No. 1.3
136 32nd AR No. 7.3
already partially automated. In addition to creating and 
linking new interfaces and integrating the systems into 
existing border control applications, organisational 
adjustments to processes are also required, and emplo-
yees must be made aware of and trained in the use of the 
systems.
As the supervisory authority, my office has new tasks to 
fulfil; for example, the underlying EU regulations pro-
vide for regular mandatory inspections of how the EES 
and ETIAS are being used. In addition, there is an obli-
gation to publish statistics on the receipt and processing 
of applications by data subjects to exercise their rights, 
which the competent national authorities – the Federal 
Office of Administration and the Federal Police – must 
report to me.
My employees already gained an overview of the imple-
mentation of the new European requirements during 
an information visit to Düsseldorf Airport in 2023. This 
provided an opportunity, for example, to take a look at 
the self-service terminals intended for the EES registra-
tion process.
In 2024, the Federal Police involved me in the consul-
tation procedure for several implementation orders in 
accordance with Section 36 of the Federal Police Act, 
which concern the implementation of the aforementi-
oned changes in the border control applications of the 
Federal Police. From a data protection perspective, it 
was necessary to tighten up one or two points here. My 
office will continue to monitor developments in this area 
from a critical perspective, provide advice and review 
handling of the systems, including by means of the new 
mandatory inspections.
7.4.2 OSINT observations on the internet
In my previous activity report, I reported under the hea-
ding “Internet searches for national security – there are 
limits here too!” that intelligence services in particular 
regard monitoring the internet as an important tool in 
their work, but that the right to informational self-deter-
mination must be observed here too.136 This also applies 
to the police and law enforcement authorities. The topic 
is a perennial issue both in terms of its use as a tool by 
the security authorities and in my monitoring activities.
OSINT research (OSINT stands for “Open Source In-
telligence”), i.e. the collection and analysis of publicly 
accessible data on the internet, is an important tool for 
all security authorities. In fact, a lot of information, 
including personal data, is much easier to obtain on 
the internet than in the real world. This is not only due 
33rd Activity Report 2024 86


<!-- page 87 -->
to technological developments and the extent of their 
dissemination, but also to the communication behavi-
our of many people. Under the supposed protection of 
an anonymous environment, people behave differently 
than in direct conversation and content is exchanged 
surprisingly freely with others they have never met. 
Chats are often used like a fleeting conversation, but can 
still be viewed much later and are sometimes visible to 
a much larger audience than assumed. This also applies 
to profiles on social networks, e.g. photos, references 
to hobbies, professional activities or family members. 
So what is freely accessible in the sense of OSINT is so-
metimes not the same as online editions of print media, 
for example, which are intended for a broad audience. In 
many cases, the term “social media intelligence” (SOC-
MINT) has therefore become established for research of 
social networks, in contrast to traditional research into 
publications, because the information gathering of the 
security authorities has had to adapt to this reality.
In view of the diversity of internet services, the limit at 
which an encroachment on the right to informational 
self-determination becomes so serious that recourse to 
a general clause is no longer possible and a special legal 
basis is required can only be answered in each individu-
al case.
It is doubtful, for example, whether the general investi-
gation clause of the Code of Criminal Procedure, which 
authorises the public prosecutor’s office to conduct „in-
vestigations of any kind“, actually justifies any encroach-
ment on the right to informational self-determination 
in individual cases. I therefore provide detailed advice 
on this issue both in the legislative process and in the 
application of the law by the bodies I monitor.
Federal police and law enforcement authorities
In view of the difficulty in defining the extent of the 
encroachment, my colleagues have obtained extensive 
information about the various investigative instruments 
from the Federal Criminal Police Office. Furthermo-
re, in coordination with the State Commissioner for 
Data Protection and Freedom of Information of North 
Rhine-Westphalia, an information visit was made to the 
Cybercrime Centre and Contact Point of the Cologne 
Public Prosecutor‘s Office. In an extensive discussion, all 
participants were able to explore the practical and legal 
problems regarding the use of OSINT searches in law 
enforcement. These findings have been incorporated 
into my inspection activities.
Federal intelligence services
Following an invitation from the University of the 
Bundeswehr Munich to attend the “OSINT Forum 2024”, 
my office gave a presentation on the data protection 
requirements for OSINT in the context of intelligence 
law. In a constructive discussion with representatives of 
the public authorities and commercial enterprises who 
were also invited, it became clear that specific OSINT 
data processing methods differ greatly depending on the 
application and purpose. At the same time, the different 
forms of OSINT data processing have in common the 
fact that there are no clear legal regulations as to when 
and for what purpose which form of OSINT research 
may be used to gain and consolidate knowledge. Unclear 
legal requirements create legal uncertainty on the part 
of users and thus jeopardise compliance with data pro-
tection standards.
In the reporting year, I also carried out various inspec-
tion and advisory visits to the BND, which also followed 
on from a complaint from 2023 regarding data proces-
sing without an adequate legal basis. The Federal Office 
for the Protection of the Constitution (BfV) and the Fe-
deral Office for the Military Counterintelligence Service 
also see investigation of public data on the internet as an 
important focus of their work. I have discussed critically 
with the authorities whether the intelligence services 
can rely exclusively on general powers to search public 
data in all cases, given the constant development and 
diversification of internet services. On the practical side, 
I recognise that the intelligence services need adequate 
technical solutions for their activities in this environ-
ment in order to manage the volume of unstructured 
data. In my inspection and advisory work, I scrutinise 
these newly created file systems with a particular focus 
on their application in the area of social networks. I al-
ready reported in my last activity report on the planned 
use of a file system by the BfV for research in social 
networks. This system not been introduced for various 
reasons.
Internet research of authorities involved in security 
clearance checks
I also carried out an inspection and advisory visit to the 
BfV in relation to the BfV’s internet research as a coope-
rating authority in the area of security clearance checks. 
Internet research is an essential component of secu-
rity clearance in uncovering security-related findings. 
Although there is a legal basis for carrying out internet 
searches under the Security Clearance Act (SÜG), there 
are no clear definitions to differentiate between public 
and non-public areas or to distinguish between social 
networks and individual communication.
Due to the growing importance of the internet for the 
intelligence services as participating authorities, the 
option of using internet research under the SÜG will 
expand massively. This applies both to the quality and 
the quantity of the research. Unfortunately, the original-
33rd Activity Report 202487


<!-- page 88 -->
ly planned draft law to amend the SÜG does not provide 
clear definitions. When conducting internet searches 
under the SÜG, it should not be overlooked that, in 
addition to the personal data of the persons concerned 
and associated persons, a large amount of personal data 
of third parties is always collected and processed that 
has no connection to the security clearance check in 
question.

7.4.3 Amendment of the Airline Passenger 
Data Act
Almost two years after the landmark judgement of the 
European Court of Justice (ECJ),137 the Federal Minis-
try of the Interior (BMI) initiated the departmental 
consultation process for amendment of the Airline 
Passenger Data Act (FlugDaG) in June 2024.
On 21 June 2022, the ECJ issued a ruling following a 
referral from the Belgian Constitutional Court, accor-
ding to which although Directive (EU) 2016/681 (PNR 
Directive) of 27 April 2016 on systematic processing of 
passenger name records (PNR data) for the prevention, 
detection, investigation and prosecution of terrorist 
offences and serious crime remains valid, it must be in-
terpreted in a very restrictive way. This interpretation is 
binding for the implementation of the PNR Directive in 
all Member States and is therefore also the standard for 
application of the FlugDaG, which serves to implement 
the Directive in Germany. The Passenger Information 
Unit (PIU) has been set up in Germany at the Federal 
Criminal Police Office.
Since the adoption of the PNR Directive in 2016, I have 
regularly drawn attention to problematic passenger 
data processing, as I have done in previous Activity 
Reports.138 I would like to emphasise, for example, the 
inclusion of all intra-EU flights stipulated in the FlugD-
aG and the five-year storage period for passenger data 
– comparable to data retention – which the ECJ also 
addressed in its landmark ruling, considering the corre-
sponding provisions of the Directive to be in conformity 
with European law only if they are interpreted narrowly. 
Since the judgement was issued, I have therefore empha-
sised the urgency of adapting administrative practice 
in the processing of passenger data and amending the 
FlugDaG both to the Federal Ministry of the Interior 
137 Judgement of the ECJ of 21 June 2022, Ref. C-817/19, available at: 
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/DE/TXT/?uri=oj:JOC_2022_340_R_0005
138 26th AR No. 2.3.2, 27th AR No. 1.3, 28th AR No. 6.4, 29th AR No. 6.6, 29th AR No. 6.24, 31st AR No. 7.1 and most recently 32nd AR No. 7.1
and in the European Data Protection Board (EDPB). In 
June 2024, the Federal Ministry of the Interior initiated 
departmental consultation on the amendment of the 
FlugDaG.
On a positive note, I would like to emphasise that 
extensive adjustments have already been made at 
administrative level to the procedures for processing 
passenger data by the PIU as a result of the PNR ruling. 
For example, passenger data is now only stored for six 
months instead of five years and is then erased. All old 
data records were also erased by the PIU. In addition, 
the PNR data of all intra-EU flights is no longer proces-
sed across the board, but only for selected flights. The 
BMI and PIU have acted much faster than other Member 
States here. Even though adjustments have already been 
made in practice, however, the possibility of an interpre-
tation that conforms with the Directive does not release 
the parties from the principles of certainty and clarity of 
the law and thus from the need to adapt the FlugDaG its-
elf to the requirements of the ECJ. In this respect, swift 
amendment of the FlugDaG remains a necessity in light 
of the ECJ judgement.
In August 2024, I sent the federal government my 
second report on the so-called template inspection in 
this context. In accordance with Section 4(3) sentence 9 
FlugDaG, I report to the federal government every two 
years on monitoring of the creation and application of 
the templates for comparison with PNR data. I did not 
identify any significant deficiencies in data processing 
during the reporting period. However, I have once again 
pointed out the extent to which the processing of PNR 
data encroaches on fundamental rights. For example, 
around 453 million passenger data records of 125 million 
passengers were processed in 2023. Even if the storage 
period is no longer always five years in practice, the 
data is stored for at least six months with no grounds for 
suspicion. On the other hand, 1,454 previously unknown 
suspects were identified. This number requires critical 
scrutiny on the basis of proportionality considerations – 
also in relation to further encroachments on fundamen-
tal rights.
Together with my European colleagues on the EDPB, I 
am lobbying for the national PNR systems to be revised 
and the legal regulations to be adapted accordingly. 
Germany has already taken a lead role in Europe thanks 
to the practical adjustments to the PIU. The revised 
FlugDaG could serve as a role model. In this respect, I 
recommend that the legislature finalise the amendment 
33rd Activity Report 2024 88


<!-- page 89 -->
of the FlugDaG swiftly, taking into account the require-
ments of the ECJ.
      
I recommend that the legislature finalise the amend-
ment of the FlugDaG swiftly , taking into account the 
requirements of the ECJ. 
      
7.4.4 Police 20/20
The joint data centre of the federal and state police 
authorities has reached a new stage of development. 
The planned functions are intended to make personal 
data visible to other police authorities in certain cases. 
At the political level, it would be desirable to establish 
additional, clear legal bases for the project.
I report regularly on the overall Police 20/20 programme 
(P 20), as previously in my 32nd Activity Report (No. 7.2). 
Once again this year, I can report on a positive note that 
the P 20 project group at the Federal Ministry of the In-
terior (BMI) regularly involves the federal and state data 
protection authorities in the overall programme and 
sub-projects and that constructive interaction has been 
established in general.
Development of the data centre:
In mid-2024, staff from my office took part in informati-
on events for the joint data centre of the federal and sta-
te police authorities, to which the BMI had invited them. 
The joint data centre is at the heart of P 20. In future, all 
personal data from all police authorities will be stored 
separately for each client in the data centre.
In the meetings, the BMI presented the structure of the 
data centre and the technical architecture and explained 
certain features. The intention is to break through the 
client separation, i.e. the technical separation between 
the individual police authorities, by means of so-called 
contextualisation. In concrete terms, this means that the 
police authorities can grant each other access to their 
respective databases in certain cases.
I can see some interesting approaches in the develop-
ment of the data centre that are worth considering 
further. In principle, a shared data centre can be an 
opportunity for data protection. However, the legal basis 
on which the data centre can be operated and its fun-
ctions used must first be clarified. I still see a need for 
clarification, particularly regarding contextualisation.
139 Judgement of the BVerfG of 1 October 2024, 1 BvR 1160/19, available at: 
https://www.bundesverfassungsgericht.de/SharedDocs/Entscheidungen/DE/2024/10/rs20241001_1bvr116019.html
140 Judgement of the BVerfG of 20 April 2016, 1 BvR 966/09 & 1 BvR 1140/09, available at: 
https://www.bundesverfassungsgericht.de/SharedDocs/Entscheidungen/DE/2016/04/rs20160420_1bvr096609.html
141 32nd AR No. 7.2
I see the possibility in this that the storage thresholds 
under the Federal Criminal Police Office Act (BKAG) may 
be circumvented. I have asked the BMI for a statem-
ent on the points that require clarification. I had not 
received an answer by the editorial deadline. Particular 
care must be taken here to ensure that the principle of 
identifying a specific purpose is adhered to. In particu-
lar, the legal and constitutional limits on data retention 
for precautionary purposes must be observed. The 
Federal Constitutional Court issued an important ruling 
on this in autumn 2024 (ruling of 1 October 2024, 1 BvR 
1160/19).139
A separate module is planned that will automatically 
take into account the principles of hypothetical data 
re-collection. These are requirements issued by the 
Federal Constitutional Court.140 141 They are intended to 
ensure that data from particularly intrusive investigative 
measures can only be used if this is necessary for the 
prosecution of sufficiently serious offences. I see this 
technical approach of a separate module as a benefit.
I am concerned about the question of the legal basis. 
This exists insofar as the data centre is part of the police 
information network pursuant to Sections 29 et seq. of 
the BKAG. However, if the police authorities at federal 
and state level wish to store additional data, this can only 
be done on the basis of a network of standards under 
police legislation at federal and state level. Storage must 
also be regulated in combination with a large number of 
agreements on commissioned data processing. It would 
make sense to create clear legal regulations here, in the 
interests of both data protection and efficient police 
work. For constitutional reasons, these must provide for 
separation of data in a way that is technically multi-cli-
ent compatible and suitable for the purpose.
At the same time, central responsibility for technical 
operation can be regulated. This would allow the police 
authorities a high degree of legal certainty and efficien-
cy. The necessary adjustments from a data protection 
perspective are aligned with the interests of the police 
and I can see a consensus with the P 20 project group 
here in terms of content.
Strategic component of the Police Information and 
Analysis Network (PIA V-S)
The PIA V-S is intended to enable the federal and state 
police authorities to carry out nationwide strategic 
analyses. Among other things, first-time drug users and 
repeat users are to be recorded and counted as “real 
33rd Activity Report 202489


<!-- page 90 -->
offenders”. Since 2019, there has been a disagreement 
between the BMI and the Federal Criminal Police Office 
(BKA) on the one hand and my office on the other as to 
whether the PIA V-S processes personal data in the form 
of pseudonymised data or whether the data is anonymi-
sed.142 The BKA presented the system to my staff. I have 
come to the conclusion that the data is processed in 
pseudonymised form. The reason for this is that anony-
mised data is generally not appropriate for counting real 
offenders and being able to assign events that occur over 
time to them. As a consequence, a legal basis is required 
if personal data is to be processed further. In certain 
cases, I believe the general police clause of Section 16(1) 
BKAG to be a suitable legal basis for this. This is particu-
larly the case if the processed data is also stored in the 
nationwide police information network (INPOL-Z) and 
therefore already meets certain storage thresholds of the 
BKAG. If this is not the case, analyses are only possible 
to a limited extent.
Among other things, the requirements of supreme court 
rulings must be observed in this context. No complex 
analyses are carried out with the PIA V-S. It is mainly 
used for case number surveys with a strong encryption 
method. The PIA V-S is not being pursued further within 
the P 20 programme. The BKA continues to operate the 
PIA V-S in its central function.
PIA V-S Politically Motivated Crime (PIA V-S-PMK)
The PIA V-S-PMK is a reporting system in the area of 
national security based on the same technology as the 
PIA V-S, but with a different design.143 In future, the po-
lice authorities of the federal states will use the system 
to deliver personal data from the area of national securi-
ty to the BKA electronically. This is intended to develop 
the criminal police reporting service. In a similar way to 
the PIA V-S, I consider its application on the basis of the 
general police clause of Section 16(1) BKAG to be permis-
sible only to a limited extent. Comprehensive analyses 
cannot be based on the general clause.
      
In the interests of data protection certainty and the ef-
ficiency of the work of the police authorities, I recom-
mend creating clear legal regulations for the large-sca-
le Police 20/20 (P20) IT project.

7.4.5 Security clearance checks in business
In many cases, the shortcomings that exist in data 
protection law in the area of security clearance in 
business are due to an unnecessary lack of clarity 
in the regulations, which results in uncertainties in 
application of the law . It is therefore my aim to avoid 
such deficiencies in particular through constructive 
advice, continuous professional dialogue with all those 
involved and active public relations work.
In the area of the Security Clearance Act (SÜG), one of 
my tasks is to ensure that non-public bodies, i.e. com-
mercial enterprises, also comply with data protection 
regulations (Section 36a(2) sentence 1 SÜG). This affects 
a large number of companies that carry out security 
checks on their staff. In this reporting year, my staff the-
refore met with representatives of trade associations and 
companies once again and responded to a large number 
of requests for advice during and outside of inspections 
and proactively made recommendations.
In principle, I see a great willingness in the private sec-
tor to implement all the requirements of data protection 
law in practice. As a result, my recommendations from 
consultations and inspections are generally implemen-
ted in full and quickly. Associations and commercial 
enterprises have a significant interest in legally compli-
ant data processing, not least to exclude liability risks 
and official sanctions.
In addition, I received support from representatives of 
the business community and from the Federal Ministry 
for Economic Affairs and Climate Protection (BMWK), 
e.g. through the provision of documents that I was able 
to use to address the documentation deficiencies of 
companies, for which I was grateful given the labour-in-
tensive nature of the work.
My training courses at the Federal Academy for Public 
Administration, the BMWK security seminar and in 
other working groups were also very well received. The 
same applies to the guidance and inspection reports 
published on my website.144 With these measures, I want 
to make knowledge of data protection law accessible to 
a wide range of people and enable legally compliant and 
effective data processing for both commercial enter-
prises and other stakeholders in the security clearance 
process.
In this reporting period, as in the past,145 I have repe-
atedly found that many of the shortcomings in data 
33rd Activity Report 2024 90


<!-- page 91 -->
protection law that I have identified are predominantly 
due to uncertainties in the application of the law on 
the part of both businesses and public clients. Business 
representatives with whom my staff meet regularly 
criticise in particular the lack of clarity and complexity 
of the regulations in the area of security clearance and 
the lack of knowledge on the part of public sector clients 
about the processes involved in protecting secrets in the 
business world.
According to business representatives, these circum-
stances not only make it more difficult for companies 
to carry out the legally compliant personnel planning 
that is urgently needed in times of increased security 
risks, but also have a negative impact on the handling 
of personal data. For example, I have found that public 
clients, misjudging the legal situation or supposedly in 
order to simplify the process, induce commercial enter-
prises to carry out unauthorised data processing. To 
illustrate this, I refer you to my working aid and tabular 
overview146 of data processing by security officers (SiBe), 
sabotage protection officers (SaBe) and other authorised 
persons in accordance with Section 25 SÜG, which exp-
lains, among other things, the differentiation between 
protection of classified information, sabotage protection 
and other security-related activities and between respon-
sible bodies and SiBe/SaBe appointments.
The reason for this circular was that I had previously 
discovered during my data protection inspections that 
there were considerable uncertainties on the part of 
both companies and the authorities with regard to the 
role and powers of companies in relation to security 
clearance checks. Authorities were asking security 
officers and/or sabotage officers in companies for their 
extensive involvement in security clearance checks, even 
though there was no legal basis for this. This led to data 
protection breaches on both sides. For example, security 
declarations were inspected and checked in companies 
and security files were kept, even though the require-
ments of Section 26 SÜG and Section 30 in conjunction 
with Section 18 SÜG were not met.
The SÜG amendment discussed in the 20th legislative 
period should be taken up again in the new legislative 
period. This would provide an opportunity to improve 
the system and clarify the regulations. I am happy to 
provide further constructive advice on this.147
(Proactive) consulting and professional dialogue with all 
stakeholders in the field of security clearance checks will 
therefore continue to be at the forefront of my activities 
146 Overview of Sections 24-31 SÜG, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/DE/Arbeitshilfen/Arbeitshilfen-S%C3%9CG/_Anlage-zu-S%C3%9CG-24-bis-31.html
147 32nd AR No. 3.3.5
148 31st AR No. 12.4
in this area in the future, as they have been for sever-
al years. Experience has shown that the protection of 
informational self-determination can be achieved most 
effectively in this way, particularly in view of the large 
number of companies in my area of responsibility.148

7.4.6 BfDI receives complete FIU report
With the help of my data protection remedial powers, a 
written exchange of views and in personal discussions, 
I persuaded the Federal Ministry of Finance (BMF) to 
send me information that had been withheld from me 
for a long time. The statutory obligations to provide in-
formation to me could therefore be fulfilled by mutual 
agreement.
In spring 2023, my staff learnt that the final report on 
the assessment of processing backlogs at the Financial 
Intelligence Unit (FIU) of the Federal Ministry of Finan-
ce was available. I then requested this from the BMF for 
scrutiny. The report was sent as a classified document 
with some passages redacted. The Federal Ministry of Fi-
nance justified this by stating that an unrestricted trans-
fer would provide deep insights into the FIU’s working 
methods and that disclosure would jeopardise the public 
interests of the Federal Republic of Germany. Even a 
higher classification could not eliminate the danger.
This was followed by an exchange of letters in which 
the opposing points of view were again exchanged and I 
requested that a complete version of the report be sent 
to me. Ultimately, I felt compelled to take action under 
supervisory law and formally objected to the failure to 
send an unredacted version. Section 16(4) sentence 1 no. 
2 of the Federal Data Protection Act obliges supervised 
bodies to provide me with all information necessary 
for the fulfilment of my duties. It is also not possible for 
the BMF to invoke rulings of the Federal Constitutional 
Court on endangering the interests of the state in this 
specific case, as the requirements are not met. My au-
thority has effective procedures in place to prevent the 
disclosure of official secrets.
Unfortunately, this action under data protection law did 
not initially lead to a rethink on the part of the BMF.
The FIU took a different view. This was followed by cons-
tructive, solution-orientated discussions which were able 
33rd Activity Report 202491


<!-- page 92 -->
to dispel any existing concerns. As a result, the FIU sent 
me the full report in summer 2024. The BMF also had 
no further objections. In my view, the discussions have 
broken down existing reservations and created greater 
mutual trust. This has already led to successful, straight-
forward cooperation with the BMF in a similar case this 
year. I am therefore confident that similar issues can be 
resolved constructively together with the BMF and FIU 
in the future.
7.4.7 Erasures successfully completed by the 
FIU
During the reporting period, I was able to achieve 
significant improvements in the erasure of personal 
data by the Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU). On the 
one hand, regular erasures are now carried out in the 
information pool. On the other, erasure of the data 
of obligated parties in accordance with the Money 
Laundering Act (GwG) is to be made possible on a ca-
se-by-case basis. Finally , individual erasures were also 
completed in relation to citizen complaints.
Compliance with erasure deadlines for the informati-
on pool
The FIU’s activities focus on collecting and analysing 
reports on suspicious financial transactions below the 
threshold of initial suspicion for criminal proceedings. 
These reports contain a large amount of personal data. 
In 2023 alone, the FIU received 322,590 reports. The FIU 
stores the reports in its so-called “information pool”.
Pursuant to Section 37(2) GwG, the FIU is obliged to era-
se personal data if storage is inadmissible or knowledge 
of it is no longer required for the fulfilment of the task. 
According to Section 37(4) GwG, the erasure require-
ments must be checked during individual case proces-
sing or according to set deadlines.
An inspection was carried out for the first time in this 
connection in 2021. One of the findings was that no 
technical mechanism for compliance with the erasure 
periods had been implemented in the specialist applica-
tion used. A formal complaint about this was submitted 
to the Federal Ministry of Finance in accordance with 
Section 16(2) of the Federal Data Protection Act.149
Since then, I have been closely involved in the procedure 
for erasing personal data at the FIU. Following my com-
plaint, the FIU commissioned a technical solution. For 
various reasons, however, this has only been in operati-
on for a short time.
In October 2024, the FIU confirmed to me that the 
erasure routine had been resumed. Since 21 October 
149 30th AR No. 8.2.9
2024, it has been carried out every day. This means that 
the FIU has now implemented the requirement from my 
inspection report.
Erasure of data of obligated parties under the GwG 
 
Obligated parties under the GwG must register with the 
FIU’s electronic reporting portal, “goAML-Web”. From 
there, the data is automatically transferred to the FIU‘s 
specialist application. If there is no longer a need to pro-
cess the data – e.g. as a result of a change in personnel 
– the FIU must erase this data.
In the context of a citizen’s complaint, I discovered that 
there was no technical process for this either. I have 
also formally complained about this to the BMF. After an 
intensive discussion, erasure was initially implemented 
for individual cases. As of December 2024, the FIU has 
assured me that a standardised erasure function is in 
place.
Erasure in individual cases 
 
As explained at the outset, the FIU is obliged under 
Section 37(4) GwG to verify the erasure requirements 
when processing individual cases. The right to erasure 
exists in accordance with Section 37(2) GwG, irrespective 
of fixed deadlines, whenever storage is not (or no longer) 
necessary for the fulfilment of the FIU’s tasks.
This means that personal data must be erased if it is 
established that the suspicious activity report in ques-
tion is not related to a criminal offence. The FIU has 
so far opposed this and argued that storage beyond the 
specified erasure periods is always necessary to carry 
out its work.
Improvements were also achieved here in the reporting 
period. In one case, the data was erased after the obliga-
ted party corrected the original suspicious activity report 
in a subsequent report and thus withdrew it.
In another case, the complainant simultaneously took 
administrative action and successfully argued before 
Cologne Administrative Court that the transaction was 
based on a typical professional procedure. In both cases, 
the data was ultimately erased before the regular dead-
line expired.

7.5 Labour and social affairs
7.5.1 Proof of eligibility for social tickets
Proof of eligibility is intended to give recipients of ci-
tizens’ allowance and other social benefits discounted 
access to education, sport and culture and, in particu-
lar, the option to purchase a discounted ticket for local 
public transport. Simple, digital solutions are also 
available for job centre clients, but protection of social 
data must be observed.
During the reporting period, I dealt in detail with the 
procedure for issuing entitlement certificates for social 
tickets in the state of Berlin, insofar as social data from 
job centres organised as joint institutions in Berlin is 
processed for this purpose. At the request of the Senate 
Department for Labour, Social Affairs, Equality, Integ-
ration, Diversity and Anti-Discrimination and in close 
coordination with the Berlin Commissioner for Data 
Protection and Freedom of Information, my staff have 
been involved in intensive consultations to support the 
evaluation of various proposals for digital procedures.
Originally, the so-called “Berlinpass” was issued by the 
municipal offices. This was then replaced by certifica-
tes of eligibility issued directly by the social security 
authorities providing the benefits. This change has led 
to delays in the issuing of proof of entitlement, meaning 
that recipients of social benefits are currently forced to 
show their benefit certificate during a ticket inspection, 
for example.
I also approached the Federal Employment Agency 
in order to explore the possibilities associated with a 
reorganisation of the job centres‘ benefit notices, for 
example. A data protection-friendly option that could 
be implemented quickly would be to print QR codes on 
the benefit notices of recipients of SGB II benefits. The 
code could then serve as digital proof of eligibility for 
discounted monthly tickets and could be scanned at 
ticket machines, for example. Automated transmission 
of social data from the job centres to Berlin’s transport 
companies, for which prior consent is generally requi-
red, would therefore be unnecessary.
As the issue of granting a “social ticket” is also live in 
other federal states, I would welcome a standardised 
nationwide approach.
7.5.2 Data protection breach at Deutsche Post 
AG’s pension service
In November 2023, Deutsche Post AG’s pension service 
incorrectly assigned personalised welcome letters to 
new pensioners and a corresponding pension certi-
ficate when printing and sending out around 25,000 
pension certificates. As a result of this erroneous as-
signment, sensitive social data that can be taken from 
the pension certificate (first name, surname, date of 
birth and pension insurance number) was disclosed to 
unauthorised third parties.
In November and December 2023, numerous data 
protection complaints were submitted to me by data 
subjects under Art. 77 GDPR. Deutsche Post AG also 
reported the data protection breach to me in accordance 
with Art. 33 GDPR in November 2023. After a full clarifi-
cation of the facts and a legal review, I determined in the 
course of the data protection supervisory proceedings 
that I initiated that Deutsche Post AG had violated Art. 
6(1) GDPR by unlawfully disclosing the data. Further-
more, the technical and organisational measures taken 
in accordance with Art. 32 GDPR were not suitable to 
ensure a level of protection appropriate to the high risk 
inherent in mass processing of sensitive personal data. 
In view of the violations identified, I issued a warning 
to Deutsche Post AG in the reporting period pursuant to 
Art. 58(2) point (b) GDPR.
I have refrained from issuing an instruction, as Deut-
sche Post AG responded immediately to my intervention 
and implemented additional measures to ensure a level 
of protection commensurate with the risk in future. In 
addition, the data subjects were informed by post by 
the office responsible in the form of a letter of apology 
and asked to return the pension certificate in a prepaid 
envelope.

8.1 Advice and inspection visits in

8.1.1 Inspection of storage in the case proces -
sing system of the Federal Police
An inspection of data stored in the case processing sys-
tem of the Federal Police did not reveal any significant 
violations. Only minor deficiencies in the recording of 
personal data were identified and it was not necessary 
for me to issue a formal complaint.
Inspection of the storage of personal data in the @rtus-
Bund case processing system at the Berlin Federal Police 
Directorate, which began in early summer 2023, was 
completed during the reporting period. The inspection 
focussed on cases relating to politically motivated crime. 
On the basis of the legal principles and the internal 
recording specifications of the Federal Police, my staff 
checked in particular the correct assignment of personal 
roles (accused, witnesses, contact persons, etc.) and the 
calculation of erasure periods.
As a result, I only identified minor data protection defi-
ciencies in the recording of cases, which did not indicate 
any overarching organisational problems, but were due 
to processing errors in individual cases. On the contrary, 
I was able to ascertain that there is a significant aware-
ness of the problem on the part of the Federal Police, 
who prepared for and supported my advice and inspecti-
on visit with by dedicating a large number of personnel 
to it. The Federal Police made a recognisable effort to 
clear up the errors discovered and showed itself to be 
open to advice from my staff. Some of the infringements 
found were corrected in the case processing system 
during the inspection visit.
Overall, I was able to exercise my judgement in not rai-
sing any official complaints under data protection law. In 
the inspection report, I made a number of recommen-
dations in an advisory capacity for further steps to raise 
the awareness of employees and possible adjustments to 
be made to the software, which the Federal Police either 
complied with or undertook to review.
8.1.2 SÜG inspections confirm greater need 
for advice
During the inspections I carried out in this reporting 
year in accordance with the Security Clearance Act 
(SÜG), I was able to identify positive developments 
in compliance with data protection regulations in 
security clearance procedures as a result of my advice. 
Identified deficiencies in data protection can be reme-
died through better staffing of the inspected bodies, 
technical support and additional basic and advanced 
training.
In the reporting year, my organisation verified complian-
ce with the data protection requirements of the Security 
Clearance Act (SÜG) at 17 locations. Seven companies 
with secrecy and sabotage protection in the defence in-
dustry, personnel provision, IT/IT security, telecommu-
nications and property security sectors and ten federal 
authorities were inspected. 
Four checks had not yet been completed at the time the 
Activity Report went to press. Three inspections begun 
in the previous year were completed in the reporting 
period.
In 13 cases, I issued formal complaints due to breaches 
of data protection law, seven of which related to the pu-
blic sector. With regard to repeated data protection defi-
ciencies and violations in security clearance procedures 
identified under the SÜG, I can largely make reference to 
my Activity Reports from previous years. However, there 
were also one or two new issues.
New findings from the inspection visits
A recurring topic was the cooperation between autho-
rities and companies in security clearance reviews in 
business. In one case, a non-public body was keeping 
security files without a legal basis. In another case, a 
public body had actively involved external companies 
in security clearance procedures for external personnel 
33rd Activity Report 2024 94


<!-- page 95 -->
and, without a legal basis, assigned tasks relating to 
personnel security and sabotage protection checks to the 
companies, such as obtaining and pre-checking security 
declarations. As a result, the company created security 
files for the persons concerned, in contravention of 
the provisions of Section 18(1) SÜG. Outside the scope 
of application of Sections 24-31 SÜG, i.e. in the case of 
activities of external personnel in an authority that are 
sensitive from a security point of view, commercial com-
panies cannot participate in security review procedures 
and are not authorised to process data under the SÜG, 
even if they have appointed officers in accordance with 
Section 25 SÜG. Under the provisions of the SÜG, only 
the competent body can work on these cases. As this 
practice has been described to me by business represen-
tatives as a “common approach”, I have issued a circular 
on data processing by security officers, sabotage protec-
tion officers and other authorised officers pursuant to 
Section 25 SÜG.150
In one case, the personnel administration office arran-
ged for regular security checks for replacement candida-
tes from application procedures to be carried out by the 
security officer. There was initially no direct intention to 
hire these people and therefore certainly no concrete in-
tention to entrust them with a sensitive security activity. 
This procedural practice has since been discontinued 
following my intervention.
My staff once again came across lists of security files 
that had been destroyed or transferred to other depart-
ments dating back over a period of 10 years during two 
inspections in the BMVg’s operating area in this repor-
ting year, even though my office had already advised last 
year that there is no legal basis for this under the SÜG. 
This procedure is set out in a binding internal service 
regulation. The BMVg had already promised to revise the 
relevant internal directives but had still not done so at 
the time of the inspection, so I was compelled to issue a 
formal complaint in this regard.
Another inspection revealed that the “PVSPlus” per-
sonnel administration system may be used for the 
organisational area of personnel secrecy and sabotage 
protection as a tool for process management (resubmis-
sion management and deadline control) in accordance 
with Section 20(1) SÜG. However, the technical design of 
the process found in the specific case that was inspected 
proved to be unsuitable for reliable deadline manage-
ment, as only a very short time window was left for the 
person responsible for the classified information and 
sabotage protection officers to process the case after the 
person concerned left the authority. After that, there was 
150 Circular of 2 May 2024 on the Fifth Section of the SÜG, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/DE/ DokumenteBfDI/Rundschreiben/Sueg/Rundschreiben-f%C3%BCnfter-Abschnitt-S%C3%9CG
no practical way of accessing the deadlines and resub-
missions that had been entered. In the case of the body 
inspected, this led in numerous cases to files remaining 
in storage beyond the statutory destruction deadline 
and a confusing accumulation of outdated deadlines 
and resubmissions that could not be erased, making it 
unnecessarily difficult to process them in an effective 
way.
In one case, employees communicated work-related con-
cerns about personnel who had undergone security clea-
rance via a messenger service. My office asked the body 
responsible to raise the awareness of staff once again 
regarding the use of messenger services and to prohibit 
exchange of internal company information regarding se-
curity clearance checks. My staff also recommended that 
compliance with this directive be monitored by means of 
adequate technical and organisational measures.
In one case, the security officer was appointed on a part-
time basis and only spent a small proportion of their 
working time on the area of security. In addition, this 
area of responsibility was inadequately staffed overall. A 
number of data protection violations were attributable 
to this. I called on the body concerned to ensure that the 
work of the security and sabotage protection officer was 
carried out on a full-time basis in future.
In one case, no deputy had been appointed for the 
sabotage protection officer. This hindered my inspec-
tion work. Above all, however, there is a risk in such a 
scenario that, in the event of the (extended) absence of 
the sabotage protection officer, security-related informa-
tion will not be dealt with promptly, which may have an 
impact on material security.
In one case, Outlook was used extensively as a “supple-
mentary file”. In addition to numerous documents from 
the security clearance procedures, internal correspon-
dence was also stored there, in some cases with personal 
data of uninvolved third parties. I have asked the body 
inspected to check the folders in Outlook once a month 
for unauthorised personal data in order to prevent fu-
ture infringements.
In another case, the personnel of a company with secu-
rity clearance were largely not proficient in German and 
German was obviously not the working language. The 
forms and instructions for completing them are current-
ly only available in German there. In this particular case, 
it was doubtful that the competent body had fulfilled its 
obligation under Section 11(1) sentence 2 SÜG, according 
to which, among other things, the subject of the process 
must be informed of the purpose of the collection of in-
33rd Activity Report 202495


<!-- page 96 -->
formation, the obligation to provide information under 
the SÜG and any obligation to cooperate under service, 
labour law or other contractual provisions, and other-
wise of the voluntary nature of provision of any infor-
mation. I have advised the body in question to provide a 
courtesy translation in such cases.
The above findings show one thing clearly: the need for 
advice continues unabated, as does the requirement for 
training on the part of the responsible bodies and com-
panies that are subject to secrecy and sabotage protecti-
on. Very often, the staff responsible at the inspected bo-
dies, predominantly in the public sector, complain about 
a shortage of personnel, a lack of technical support or a 
lack of training opportunities.
Citizens who are (jointly) affected by a security clea-
rance check also repeatedly come to me with questions 
and problems regarding assertion of their rights as data 
subjects; it is not uncommon for them to complain about 
duplicate checks or (security) clearance checks with 
no legal basis. One of my inspections this year was also 
initiated on the basis of a citizen submission.
Positive developments and new advisory formats
I am pleased to report that there were also positive 
developments in the reporting year as a result of my 
advisory activities and other measures taken by my 
authority. As a result of my complaint, the Customs 
Administration, the Federal Office of Justice and the 
Federal Ministry of Health each increased the number of 
staff in the organisational units responsible for classified 
information and sabotage protection. As the supervisory 
authority, the Federal Ministry of the Interior immedi-
ately stopped the reviews of replacement candidates in 
the application process at the Federal Office for Migrati-
on and Refugees by decree after receiving my inspection 
report. In order to build on these positive developments, 
I will be attaching even greater importance to data 
protection information and advice during my inspecti-
on visits in the future and further expanding my public 
relations work.
Independently of the inspections, my office conducted 
an information and advisory visit to the Federal Office 
for the Protection of the Constitution and the Federal 
Intelligence Service in relation to the self-protection 
provisions introduced in the Federal Intelligence Service 
Act and the Federal Intelligence Service Act with effect 
from 1 January 2024 and 30 December 2023 respectively. 
The implementation of the new regulations presented 
by the authorities in this context did not have any data 
protection shortcomings.
151 31st AR No. 9.4.4
This year, my staff also organised an informal discussion 
with the data protection officer and the security officer 
of the Federal Ministry of Defence (BMVg) on topics re-
lating to security checks in the BMVg’s area of operations 
for the first time. The discussion focussed in particular 
on the planned introduction of an electronic security 
file.

8.1.3 Inspection of the Anti-Terror Filing 
System and the Right-Wing Extremism Filing 
System
As in previous years,151 I also carried out the man-
datory checks of the Anti-Terror Filing System (ATD) 
and the Right-Wing Extremism Filing System (RED) in 
this reporting period. In the inspections that began at 
the end of 2023, I took an inter-agency advisory and 
inspection approach this time, primarily for reasons of 
efficiency .
As far as the ATD is concerned, the federal authorities 
monitored by me are the Federal Criminal Police Office 
(BKA), the Federal Police (BPOL), the Customs Criminal 
Police Office (ZKA), the Federal Office for the Protection 
of the Constitution (BfV), the Federal Office for the Mi-
litary Counterintelligence Service (BAMAD) and the Fe-
deral Intelligence Service (BND); for the RED, I monitor 
the BKA, the BPOL, the BfV and the BAMAD. I have not 
identified any data protection shortcomings at the ZKA, 
the BND, the BAMAD, the BfV or the BPOL. All proces-
sing was in accordance with the legal requirements. The 
inspections had not actually been completed at the time 
this report was prepared. However, it is already clear 
that there are no major data protection violations.
New inter-agency inspection approach
The authorities are connected to the ATD and RED in 
different ways; not all of them use an automated storage 
interface. In addition, the files saved come from various 
source files. I have also noted variations in documen-
tation practices on the part of the security authorities 
involved.
Although it is not easy to implement a uniform advisory 
and inspection approach in this context, I wanted to take 
an overarching approach for the federal authorities that 
I monitor this time in order to standardise and maximise 
the efficiency of the individual advisory and control acti-
vities in the best possible way. In order to streamline the 
time-consuming on-site inspections at some of the bo-
dies, I wanted to obtain an overview of the current data 
33rd Activity Report 2024 96


<!-- page 97 -->
inventory and the changes to both filing systems during 
the inspection period by means of a written procedure to 
allow more precise preparation. Delivery of this infor-
mation proved to be difficult and lengthy.
Although my office complies with all the requirements 
for protection of classified information, the authorities 
involved had reservations about sending the informati-
on because of the volume of data requested. Although I 
understand this in principle, I consider the refusal to be 
a restriction of my inspection rights for no objective rea-
son. A compromise was finally found so as not to push 
back the start of the inspection even further. It was not 
possible to achieve the efficiency gains I had hoped for 
because of the compromise. Based on my experiences 
here, there is obviously a particular risk that my advisory 
and inspection activities can be hampered by a reluctan-
ce to provide documentation.
Nevertheless, my new inspection approach enabled 
me to determine whether multiple agencies had stored 
data about the same people by analysing the log data 
across the board. I have scrutinised the storage systems 
particularly closely at the authorities concerned and am 
pleased to report that the multiple storage systems were 
in accordance with the legal requirements. As a result of 
my new approach, I was able to improve the quality of 
advice and inspection, despite the delay in submission 
of the documents. I would therefore like to continue to 
optimise future consultations and inspections in order 
to fulfil my statutory duty to provide advice and inspec-
tions to the required extent and with the necessary level 
of detail as efficiently as possible. The bodies under my 
supervision should continue to support me actively and 
remain open to my information and advice.
The ATD and RED are hardly used
The opportunity to compare the use made of the ATD 
and RED by the various federal authorities as a result of 
my overarching inspection approach once again confir-
med the impression repeatedly described in my previous 
reports that they are not particularly useful instruments 
for the security authorities and are accordingly used 
only little. In view of the significant encroachment on 
fundamental rights because of the large number of agen-
cies connected to them and the sensitive nature of the 
data stored in them, I am persisting with my call152 for a 
reform of both filing systems.
152 31st AR No. 9.4.4
153 32nd AR No. 9.1.9
      
I recommend that the legislature discuss a reform of 
the ATD and the RED together with my authority and 
all the authorities involved. 
      
8.1.4 Inspection of the Joint Counter-Extre -
mism and Counter-Terrorism Centre
In the reporting year, I began monitoring the Joint 
Counter-Extremism and Counter-Terrorism Centre 
(GETZ) on the basis of the new transfer regulations for 
the intelligence services.
I began my inspections of one of the Joint Centres back 
in 2022/23 with the Joint Counter-Terrorism Centre 
(GTAZ), in which various federal and state security agen-
cies work together. In that context I realised that some 
forms of work at the GTAZ cannot be brought into line 
with the legal provisions for data protection.153 Practi-
cal recommendations were also made at that time to 
strengthen data protection.
I welcomed the fact that the federal government inclu-
ded the creation of an explicit legal basis for the Joint 
Centres in the coalition agreement and offered them 
advice in this area. It is regrettable that this proposal was 
not taken up in the 20th legislative period.
Since, however, the legislature reformed the transmis-
sion regulations in the area of the intelligence services 
with the Act on the First Part of the Reform of Intelligen-
ce Service Law of 22 December 2023 and adapted proto-
col collation regulations, I took the decision to inspect 
another Joint Centre in this reporting year, despite the 
lack of special statutory transmission regulations, in the 
form of the GETZ.
The aim of the GETZ is to combat right-wing, left-wing 
and foreign extremism and espionage and prevent its 
proliferation. Meetings of special working groups are 
held at different intervals for all four of these phenome-
na. In addition to the 16 State Offices of Criminal Investi-
gation and 16 State Offices for Protection of the Consti-
tution, the following federal authorities are represented 
in the GETZ: the Federal Office for the Protection of the 
Constitution, Federal Office for the Military Counterin-
telligence Service, Federal Intelligence Service, Fe-
deral Criminal Police Office, Federal Public Prosecutor 
General, Federal Police, General Customs Directorate/
Customs Criminal Police Office and the Federal Office 
for Migration and Refugees. 
Accordingly, my advisory and inspection team consists 
33rd Activity Report 202497


<!-- page 98 -->
of colleagues from the units responsible for these agen-
cies.
As part of the inspection, I check across all the areas of 
four working groups – including the Situation working 
group, in which current situational findings are exch-
anged – that the federal authorities represented in the 
GETZ comply with the statutory requirements for the 
transfer of personal data and document their actions 
accordingly. To this end, my inspection team first arran-
ged for a presentation of all the GETZ working groups 
and their procedures during an information visit and 
clarified any open questions. The inspection team took 
part in several meetings of the working groups both on 
the information visit and during the inspection period 
itself. As the on-site inspection was still ongoing at the 
time of going to press, I will report on the results of my 
inspection in the next Activity Report.
8.1.5 Advice and inspection in respect of the 
Federal Office for the Protection of the Consti -
tution (BfV)
The reporting period was again punctuated extensi-
vely by monitoring, advisory and information visits to 
the Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution 
(BfV). I carried out an inspection of the data stored in 
the intelligence information system (NADIS). Inspec-
tion and advice on the BfV’s electronic files continu-
ed, with a focus on searches for individuals, and that 
process was recently completed successfully . However, 
the procedures for obtaining covert information and 
internet searches and the various BfV filing systems 
were also scrutinised in order to provide the BfV with 
the best possible support in data protection matters. 
Furthermore, I completed an inspection of data pro-
cessing in connection with the reporting of the BfV .
NADIS inspection
The NADIS is the joint filing system of the network of the 
Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution and 
the state Authorities for the Protection of the Constituti-
on (VS-Verbund) – more recently also with the inclusion 
of the Federal Office for the Military Counterintelligence 
Service (BAMAD). The NADIS contains records of all per-
sons and properties that are considered relevant for the 
surveillance role of the Office for the Protection of the 
Constitution. The filing system allows the information 
to be linked and is therefore an important analytical tool 
for the authorities. During my consultation and inspecti-
on this year, I reviewed specific storage of personal data 
in the NADIS, focussing on the initial storage process.
154 32nd AR No. 9.1.7
155 31st AR No. 7.8
The inspection did not reveal any significant violations 
of data protection regulations with regard to the subject 
of the inspection. On a positive note, it is evident that all 
of the records checked can be considered plausible.
However, I also advised the BfV on the potential for im-
provement with regard to the practice of documenting 
initial storage. The BfV is now attempting to standardise 
its storage regulations throughout the agency.
Storage in the NADIS, its requirements and the precise 
organisation and presentation of this central database 
of the VS network will remain important topics for my 
consulting and inspection in the future.
Electronic files of the BfV
It has now been possible to complete the follow-up 
inspection of the BfV’s electronic files described in my 
previous Activity Report.154 My consultation and inspec-
tion focussed mainly on the options for searching for 
individuals in the electronic files of the BfV . Searches 
of this sort are only permissible within the narrow legal 
framework of the Federal Protection of the Constituti-
on Act (BVerfSchG). My positive initial impression was 
confirmed by the end of the inspection. I was unable to 
identify any material data protection violations.
The inspection approach was developed in consultation 
with the BfV as compensation for technical measures 
and my office closely observed the BfV’s self-monitoring 
procedure resulting directly from this in its follow-up 
inspection. The BfV is currently carrying out further fol-
low-up checks for the new monitoring period itself and 
will report the (interim) results to me. With regard to the 
future electronic file and document management system 
in the network of constitution protection agencies (Ver-
bund-DMS), I continue to recommend the development 
of technical organisational measures to limit full-text 
searches.
I am confident that the measures to raise awareness 
recently carried out by the BfV among its employees 
and the advisory and inspection work carried out by me 
will again lead to a positive result and thus underline the 
benefits of my advisory and inspection visits, especially 
on site.
Information letters of the BfV
In my 31st Activity Report,155 I explained that the transfer 
of personal data in the context of the BfV’s reporting 
procedures does not comply with legal requirements. 
Some personal data was included in the reports without 
a recognisable legal basis for all data transfers. No 
adequate differentiation was made between the very 
33rd Activity Report 2024 98


<!-- page 99 -->
different recipients and the respective areas of responsi-
bility of the BfV (e.g. left-wing or right-wing extremism) 
and the information letters were always sent to a large 
distribution list.
As part of a written inspection, I reviewed the reports 
for several months and analysed the data processing 
contained in them. I then advised the BfV on which 
transfers were legally permissible. As a result, the BfV 
has adapted its procedures and significantly restricted 
the use of personal data. I was therefore able to comple-
te the inspection in spring 2024 without any complaints.
Inspection of data retrievals from the AZR
The Central Register of Foreigners (AZR) is the central 
information hub for legislation on foreigners and asylum 
and contains over 26 million personal data records of fo-
reigners who have been in Germany for more than three 
months. Personal data of EU citizens is also stored in the 
AZR if, for example, they are not entitled to freedom of 
movement. The register is kept by the Federal Office for 
Migration and Refugees (BAMF).
It was not only the particularly large amount of data 
in the AZR, but also the storage of extremely sensitive 
data (e.g. health data, fingerprints) that prompted me 
to check the BfV‘s access to the AZR and to provide data 
protection advice and carry out an inspection. Federal 
intelligence services are authorised to retrieve data from 
the Central Register of Foreigners using the so-called 
automated procedure in accordance with Section 22(1) 
sentence 1 no. 9 of the Central Register of Foreigners 
Act (AZRG). With the necessary authorisation from the 
Federal Office of Administration (BV A), which acts as 
a service provider for the BAMF, the BfV can view and 
process extensive data in the AZR via direct access. The 
advantage of direct access for the BfV is that data can be 
retrieved and stored without the BV A or the BAMF being 
informed. In contrast to transfers that require a prior 
request or application procedure, the necessity of the 
retrieval is assessed and controlled exclusively by the 
BfV and not by the BV A or the BAMF.
My data protection advice and inspection was still ongo-
ing at the end of the reporting period. 
However, the BfV has already made improvements to 
the procedure and specified documentation of AZR 
retrievals in more detail. For example, the authorisation 
concept provided for by law (Section 22(3) sentence 3 
AZRG) has been adapted and made more transparent. 
The procedure for authorising employees to retrieve 
data from the AZR has also been changed as a result of 
my advice. I am currently unable to provide any further 
information due to the ongoing inspection and for rea-
sons of confidentiality.
Inspection of internet investigations
Internet investigations, also known as Open Source 
Intelligence or OSINT for short, are one of the BfV‘s 
main tasks. As a result, it is also a permanent focus of 
my advice and inspection activities. During the reporting 
period, I monitored the various measures ranging from 
the inspection of generally accessible information to 
intrusive investigation on the internet by obtaining infor-
mation secretly. This area is subject to constant change 
due to the continuous development of platforms and 
the frequently changing nature of the use of the various 
communication channels.
During my inspection, I examined the current approach 
of the BfV and paid particular attention to the question 
of whether the depth of the intervention is justifiable in 
relation to the findings. I have found that the legal basis 
for the collection and further processing of data from 
the internet by the BfV is not always clear.
There is no question that the processing of freely acces-
sible data on the internet is possible in principle for the 
BfV within the scope of fulfilment of its role. However, 
the powers of the BfV must be clearly defined both by a 
definite legal regulation and by updated internal instruc-
tions and must be appropriate to the purpose of the pro-
cessing. As things stand at present, this is only the case 
to a limited extent. There is a need for legal clarification 
and clear internal guidelines.
Analogue covert information gathering
In addition to information gathering on the internet 
as described above, intelligence services also continue 
to collect information in the real world. If this is done 
secretly, it is referred to as intelligence gathering. Only 
some of this is explicitly regulated in the BVerfSchG, 
such as the use of undercover agents or persons of trust 
(also often referred to as V-persons).
A complete list of these intelligence gathering procedu-
res, as regulated in Section 8(2) sentence 4 BVerfSchG, 
can only be found in a BfV service regulation, which is 
subject to the approval of the Federal Ministry of the 
Interior (BMI). This regulation is classified. I can there-
fore only report in very abstract terms at this point that 
I inspected the use of one such procedure during the 
reporting period. I have not identified any shortcomings 
in terms of data protection during my own checks.
However, the procedure in question is not explicitly 
described in the aforementioned service regulation. The 
BfV subsumed it under a procedure mentioned there by 
name. I could not understand this approach and cont-
acted the BMI as the responsible supervisory authority. 
In my view, every intelligence tool must be explicitly 
named and the requirements for its use defined.
33rd Activity Report 202499


<!-- page 100 -->
I cannot understand why the BMI did not see any need 
for action here and referred to a pending amendment 
to the BVerfSchG, which unfortunately had not yet been 
initiated during the reporting period.
Information visits regarding various BfV filing systems
If the BfV wishes to store personal data in filing systems, 
I must be involved in a filing consultation procedure in 
accordance with Section 14 BVerfSchG before the system 
is put into operation. In the last reporting year, I already 
reported on a tool for monitoring the internet in this 
context, for which I was involved early on in the procure-
ment process and checked the “proof of concept” (PoC), 
i.e. the test phase of the filing system, on site.156
As part of this PoC, I identified several data protection 
violations and shortcomings that would have led to a 
complaint when the filing system was put into operation. 
These related, among other things, to the lack of techni-
cal and organisational measures in accordance with 
Section 27 No. 2 BVerfSchG in conjunction with Section 
64(1) sentence 1 BDSG. I cannot go into further details 
for reasons of confidentiality.
However, since the BfV decided shortly after my consul-
tation and inspection to discontinue the PoC and not to 
introduce the filing system, I refrained from making a 
formal complaint.
In this reporting year, I had an information meeting at 
which I was shown the filing system that is now to be 
introduced as an alternative to the discontinued proce-
dure. The new PoC, in which the software will undergo 
extensive technical and functional testing, had not been 
finalised at the time of going to press. I will continue to 
support this process closely in an advisory capacity and 
evaluate it in terms of data protection law.
This filing consultation procedure is not the only one in 
the year under review involving procedures for moni-
toring and reconnaissance on the internet. This is the 
result of the diversity of internet services, which is coun-
tered here by specialist investigative procedures.
In addition to the new filing systems for actual internet 
monitoring, there is also another filing system in an 
ongoing consultation process that is to be introduced for 
analysis by the Office for the Protection of the Cons-
titution due to the rapidly growing size and variety of 
unstructured data. Here, too, reasons of secrecy prevent 
a more precise description. However, it is obvious that 
this entire area of data collection on the internet and the 
handling of complex, unstructured data sets that arise in 
the process is currently a focus of my work.
156 32nd AR No. 9.1.7
Cross-reference: 
 
7.4.2 OSINT observations on the Internet
8.1.6 Advice and inspection in respect of the 
BAMAD
The Federal Office for the Military Counterintelligence 
Service (BAMAD) has extensive intelligence service 
powers. As with the Federal Office for the Protection 
of the Constitution, the relevant data protection issues 
here include reconnaissance on the internet and the 
connection of the authority to the intelligence service 
information system (NADIS). The fact that this can 
lead to friction is particularly evident from processing 
submissions from citizens who ask me for support in 
the context of requests for information.
I have a duty to provide citizens with individual sup-
port when they request information from the federal 
intelligence services. I thus represent the rights of data 
subjects, as they do not have their own rights of access 
and information from the services may sometimes be 
subject to restrictions.
In this context, it is also possible for me, within my area 
of responsibility, to review reported facts by means of 
concrete inspection measures, to evaluate them in terms 
of data protection law and to inform and advise BAMAD 
as the responsible body. The necessary clarification of 
the facts is initially carried out by my employees as part 
of an initial written contact with the responsible data 
protection department at the BAMAD. Any additional 
consultations and on-site inspections required are coor-
dinated by my staff and carried out if necessary. This en-
sures regular, comprehensive and targeted assessment 
of the underlying issues and advice on how to handle 
them in compliance with data protection regulations.
This is important because, unfortunately, it has been 
shown time and again that the right to access is often in-
terpreted restrictively on the part of the BAMAD. In my 
opinion, more information could certainly be provided 
in many cases instead of non-disclosure or only partial 
disclosure, without revealing intelligence working me-
thods that are secret or violating confidentiality regula-
tions.
In my view, there are signs of a positive trend from my 
advice and inspection activities in relation to BAMAD. 
In the cases I dealt with, it was possible to satisfy the 
further information requirements of petitioners and to 
resolve data protection issues by mutual agreement on 
several occasions. I very much welcome that. I hope that 
the BAMAD will be even more open and proactive in re-
33rd Activity Report 2024 100


<!-- page 101 -->
sponding to requests for information in the future. This 
creates the legal certainty required for data protection 
and data subjects at the same time. I will continue to be 
available to the BAMAD in an advisory capacity.
Connection of BAMAD to NADIS
In the course of 2023, full connection of the BAMAD to 
the NADIS, the joint filing system of the Federal Office 
for the Protection of the Constitution and the State 
Authorities for the Protection of the Constitution, was 
completed. I had monitored the interlinking of the file 
systems of the various federal intelligence services from 
a critical standpoint.157 The BAMAD had announced that 
the permissibility of its retrieval of data from the NADIS 
would be guaranteed by several technical and organi-
sational security instances. During this year’s reporting 
period, I therefore carried out a review of the use of 
the NADIS by the BAMAD. At the time of going to press, 
this inspection was still ongoing, so I will not be able to 
report my findings until the next Activity Report.
Reconnaissance on the internet
Anti-constitutional activities and activities that threaten 
national security are increasingly shifting to the internet 
and social networks in particular. In response to this, the 
collection and analysis of freely available information 
and information that is not freely accessible on the inter-
net has been an important part of intelligence activities 
for a number of years. I have therefore also inspected 
the processing of personal data in connection with the 
internet work of one BAMAD department.
I did not identify any breaches of data protection law. 
From the point of view of data protection law, it is to be 
welcomed that BAMAD has issued an internal depart-
mental regulation for the area of internet processing by 
the department that I inspected, which also includes an 
order form as an annex. However, in the course of my 
advice and inspection activities, I found that in practice, 
arrangements were not being made in accordance 
with the department’s own regulations. This was either 
because the order form was not being used at all or 
because it was signed by a person who was not authori-
sed to issue the order.
As the BAMAD had already accepted the deficiencies 
during the inspection and undertaken to remedy them, I 
refrained from making an official complaint about them 
in accordance with Section 16(2) sentence 2 BDSG – des-
pite the large number of cases – and instead only issued 
some practical recommendations.
