7.4.2 OSINT observation on the Internet
8.1.7 Discussion of the Military Intelligence 
System
For some time now, I have been calling on the legislature 
to create a simple legal basis for Military Intelligence 
(MilNW). Alongside the Federal Intelligence Service 
(BND), the Federal Office for the Protection of the Con-
stitution (BfV) and the Federal Office for the Military 
Counterintelligence Service (BAMAD), MilNW is the 
fourth intelligence service at federal level. However, 
while the BND, BfV and BAMAD each fulfil their manda-
te on the basis of a specific law, MilNW does not have the 
same specific legal basis.
MilNW is made up of different areas of the Bundeswehr, 
which – like an intelligence service – are responsible for 
gathering information worldwide. The task and objecti-
ve of this intelligence gathering and reconnaissance is 
to cover the military information requirements of the 
federal government, of the management of the Federal 
Ministry of Defence (BMVg) and of the military com-
mand levels such as commanders.
Absence of a simple legal basis
In close cooperation with the BND, MilNW represents 
the foreign military intelligence service of the German 
armed forces. This role has become even more import-
ant in times of change like the present. The cooperation 
extends to personnel and technical matters and conti-
nuous sharing of information and analysis. The BND also 
supports the Bundeswehr in mandated foreign deploy-
ments.
Although the MilNW operates in part exactly like the 
BND, the federal government has not seen the need to 
create a fundamental legal basis for it. At the latest since 
the judgement of the Federal Constitutional Court on the 
BND’s foreign telecommunications reconnaissance in 
2020,158 I believe that the need to create an explicit legal 
basis for the MilNW has been confirmed by the highest 
court.
I therefore maintain my call for the creation of a fun-
damental legal basis for data protection reasons. For 
MilNW to be able to act with legal certainty, it is neces-
sary for parliament to define its data processing powers.
I am happy to offer my support and advice in this regard.
33rd Activity Report 2024101


<!-- page 102 -->
Inspections of MilNW
In addition to my call for the creation of an explicit 
legal basis for MilNW , I carried out two data protecti-
on consultations and inspections at MilNW during the 
reporting period. The framework for these is provided 
by the general data protection provisions of the GDPR, 
which can be applied where there is no alternative.
One inspection which started in 2023 and was comple-
ted in 2024 related to MilNW’s central data processing 
system. Several data protection violations and deficien-
cies were identified. I issued an official complaint about 
the data retention practice that I identified, involving an 
associated breach of employee data protection, in view 
of the seriousness of the violation. Given the acceptan-
ce and willingness of the representatives of MilNW to 
remedy the other violations and deficiencies I identified 
as quickly as possible, I have refrained from making any 
further official complaints. I have made several practical 
recommendations in an advisory capacity instead. The 
BMVg’s statement on my inspection report confirms that 
all of the points I listed have already been addressed or 
will be addressed in the near future. I will check whether 
my recommendations have been fully implemented.
My second consultation and inspection related to a file 
that is used for early crisis detection and situational 
analysis. It began with the Competence Centre for Early 
Crisis Detection at the University of the Bundeswehr 
Munich introducing itself to my staff. At a subsequent 
meeting, my colleagues checked the actual data set. I did 
not identify any serious breaches of data protection. I 
will remain in close contact with the BMVg regarding the 
practical recommendations I have made offering advice 
and information about how to handle the filing system.
      
I maintain my call for the creation of a fundamental le-
gal basis for Military Intelligence. For the Bundeswehr 
to be able to act with legal certainty , it is necessary 
to regulate the necessary powers in democratically 
legitimised laws. 
      
8.1.8 Information and advisory visit to the 
BND
As last year, I also took a closer look at one division 
of the Federal Intelligence Service (BND) this year. I 
decided on the division responsible for information 
gathering at the BND. It first coordinates information 
gathering and then processes the information in a 
targeted way in specialist units.
159 32nd AR No. 9.1.10
The BND has established a new structure with six 
centralised divisions. Last year, I inspected one of these 
divisions – the analysis division.159
In this reporting year, I focussed on the area of informa-
tion gathering. In order for the BND to fulfil its intelli-
gence mission, information must first be gathered. The 
BND is authorised to use various means for this purpose. 
In addition to open information, satellite images and 
human sources, these also include technical tools such 
as telecommunications reconnaissance and cyber ope-
rations.
During an information and advisory visit, I arranged for 
the various organisational units and processes in the 
area of information gathering to be presented to me. The 
meeting was characterised by transparency and open-
ness. All questions relating to data protection law were 
answered conclusively.
8.1.9 No disruption to the security authori -
ties’ work as a result of the BfDI’s monitoring 
and advisory activities 
I often face the accusation that my advisory and 
inspection practices lead to a great deal of additional 
work for the supervised bodies, which considerab-
ly disrupts the real work of the body in question. I 
strongly disagree with this point of view . Not least 
because of the compensating function I have, my su-
pervisory activities are essential for safeguarding fun-
damental democratic rights, particularly in the area of 
security; and I always focus on providing advice and 
minimising the workload for the bodies I supervise.
Supervisory bodies are an essential feature of our de-
mocracy. We find them in parliament, in the courts, in 
the media and, of course, in public life. In addition, the 
organisation of the administration provides for an inter-
nal administrative control structure through technical, 
legal and service supervision.
Within this structure, my office has a special responsibi-
lity for safeguarding the fundamental right to informati-
onal self-determination.
I am aware that the security authorities are subject to a 
high level of scrutiny. However, the security authorities 
do process particularly sensitive personal data and have 
extensive powers that can have serious consequences 
for data subjects and thus encroach significantly on the 
fundamental right to informational self-determination.
Particularly in the area of the intelligence services, this 
information processing usually takes place in secret 
and without the knowledge of the data subjects. The 
33rd Activity Report 2024 102


<!-- page 103 -->
Federal Constitutional Court has therefore granted me 
a compensating function. My supervisory activities are 
intended to compensate for what individuals cannot do 
themselves because they are unaware of the processing 
of their data. It is precisely because of this context that 
my advisory and inspection activities are necessary.
On behalf of data subjects, I independently verify that 
the security authorities are complying with the legal re-
quirements that apply to them. This is usually the case.
It is undoubtedly true that my on-site visits and writ-
ten enquiries lead to work for the agencies I inspect. 
However, my visits and enquiries are also an opportunity 
for the agencies under inspection to clarify unresolved 
questions. Issues and requirements relating to data 
protection law can often be discussed in detail during 
my on-site visits in particular, so that the agencies have 
more legal certainty and therefore more confidence in 
their actions following the consultation and inspection 
visit. When it comes to future projects, they are aware of 
their room for manoeuvre within the “red lines” that I 
have outlined.
As a matter of principle, I do not expect a large number 
of staff to be present during my inspection, information 
and advisory visits, nor do I expect elaborate presenta-
tions or “dress rehearsals” before my visits. On the cont-
rary, I endeavour to keep the workload at the agencies I 
inspect as low as possible. To gain an impression of data 
processing systems, for example, it is often enough for 
me to simply inspect the system and obtain a general 
idea of its features.
I give advance notice of my visits, take into account the 
availability of the stakeholders involved, make on-si-
te appointments with the organisations I inspect and 
specify the information in which I am interested before 
each visit. My visits and enquiries often affect various 
departments of the body under inspection, with the re-
sult that – in addition to data protection – other organisa-
tional units are often the focus of a visit.
The time and effort involved in my work for the su-
pervised bodies is therefore proportionate to my legal 
mandate in our free and democratic society. The bodies 
I inspect should be aware of this role and their respon-
sibility when they encroach on the right to informatio-
nal self-determination and understand my work as an 
inherent condition for legitimising lawful processing of 
personal data.
Notwithstanding this clarification, which in my view is 
urgently needed, I would also like to mention explicitly 
160 See the statistical report of the European Agency for the operational management of large-scale IT systems in the area of freedom, security and justice (eu-LI-
SA), available at: https://www.eulisa.europa.eu/our-publications/reports
161 32nd AR No. 7.7
once again that I have an excellent cooperative relati-
onship with many public bodies in my inspection and 
advisory visits.
8.1.10 Data protection in the Schengen area: 
Supervision of the SIS
With 91 million stored alerts and 15 billion searches 
carried out in 2023,160 the Schengen Information Sys-
tem (SIS) is one of the most important large-scale IT 
systems in the EU. The SIS is used to exchange infor-
mation in the areas of security and border manage-
ment in Europe. Against this backdrop, my focus in the 
current reporting year was once again on providing 
advice and monitoring the SIS.
Following the commissioning of the expanded, third 
generation of the SIS161 it is important to monitor how 
the agencies connected to it process personal data in the 
system. In the year under review, I carried out an inspec-
tion of the Federal Police in this context.
The subject of the audit was the alerts relating to refusal 
of entry and residence stored in the SIS in accordance 
with Article 24(1) point (a) of Regulation (EU) 2018/1861.
No significant data protection deficiencies were iden-
tified. However, I have worked in an advisory capacity 
to ensure that the central concept of a serious criminal 
offence as the prerequisite for an alert is interpreted and 
understood in a sufficiently restrictive way. The inspecti-
on also revealed that there is a need for better documen-
tation of when and why data subjects are not informed 
about an SIS alert.
In addition, my staff have started reviewing the new 
alert categories introduced in the SIS in 2023. To this 
end, German alerts were checked in the reporting 
year for compliance with Art. 40 of Regulation (EU) 
2018/1862. Alerts can be issued for unknown wanted per-
sons for identification using fingerprints or palm prints 
that are associated with a very high degree of probabi-
lity with the perpetrator of an act of terrorism or other 
serious criminal offence. In order to ensure lawful data 
protection-compliant application of the regulation, these 
alerts and the data protection framework surrounding 
them are currently being reviewed. I will report on the 
results of the inspection in my next Activity Report.
At European level, I deal with data protection issues rela-
ting to the SIS on an ongoing basis as part of the Coor-
dinated Supervision Committee (CSC). One of the topics 
discussed there was the evaluation of the Europe-wide 
coordinated checks of alerts for the purpose of covert 
33rd Activity Report 2024103


<!-- page 104 -->
and targeted checks in the SIS.162 A report that was pre-
pared with the close involvement of my staff summarises 
the audit findings and makes clear recommendations to 
the responsible bodies.163
My colleagues also carry out Schengen-wide checks on 
the SIS as part of the Schengen evaluations.164 After par-
ticipating in the expert mission in Finland in 2023, we 
took part in the evaluation in Hungary in the reporting 
year.
I have also repeatedly dealt in detail with the processing 
of personal data in the SIS in the course of handling 
complaints. In total, my staff processed around 150 en-
quiries and complaints about the SIS in 2024.

4.2.3 Report from the CSC
8.1.11 Advice and inspection in respect of the 
GBA
My advice and inspection in respect of the Attorney 
General at the Federal Court of Justice (GBA) did not 
lead to any objections. The subject was data trans-
missions by the GBA in accordance with the Order on 
Notifications in Criminal Matters (MiStra) and the In-
troductory Act to the Courts Constitution Act (EGGVG).
The Courts Constitution Act authorises courts and public 
prosecutors’ offices to transmit personal data ex officio 
to public authorities for purposes other than criminal 
proceedings. For example, information about certain 
criminal proceedings against holders of firearms licen-
ces can be communicated to the firearms authority. An 
obligation exists if this is stipulated in special regulations 
or in the administrative provisions of the MiStra. Under 
certain circumstances, the public prosecutor’s office 
must notify the accused that a transmission of informati-
on has taken place.
No significant data protection deficiencies were iden-
tified during the inspection. I was able to confirm that 
the files of the GBA are kept very conscientiously and 
thoroughly. Only in one case was an accused individual 
not informed of the transfer. As this was an error in an 
individual case and the GBA had already taken measures 
to prevent similar errors before the inspection date, I 
refrained from issuing a formal complaint and merely 
made practical recommendations in an advisory capa-
city.

8.1.12 Inspection of the Federal Criminal 
Police BKA in the area of politically motivated 
crime on the left-wing political spectrum
In the area of politically motivated crime (PMK), the 
Federal Criminal Police Office (BKA) differentiates 
between various political movements. An inspection of 
the offences classified as “left-wing” and the persons re-
gistered in relation to them did not reveal any structural 
problems but did raise questions in individual cases.
The BKA currently has a total of five filing systems with 
different purposes in the area of left-wing politically mo-
tivated crime. My inspection focused on the PMK-links-Z 
central database, the PMK-links-S criminal prosecution 
database and the storage of persons classified by the 
police as so-called “threats” and “persons of interest”.
The PMK-links-Z database is managed in the BKA ’s stan-
dard case processing system (eFBS). One positive aspect 
to emphasise – especially in comparison to an inspection 
carried out in 2012165 – is the implementation of negative 
predictions, i.e. a specific justification of why the person 
in question is likely to commit further offences. Howe-
ver, I take a critical view of the data quality. Although 
this is not a mass procedure with 171 persons registered 
at the time of the inspection, my staff found errors such 
as double entries and an incorrect date of birth in as 
many as six of 124 personal data records checked.
In terms of content, it was noticeable that a large pro-
portion of the data stored at the time of the inspection 
was attributable to actions by the “Letzte Generation” 
group. In the case of one of these datasets, I reserved 
the right to make a formal complaint because, in my 
opinion, there was no underlying criminal offence and 
the facts needed to be clarified further.
I have made a formal complaint about data stored in re-
lation to a significant offence. It is not possible to prove 
that the individuals whose data is stored actually cont-
ributed to the offence. Although the public prosecutor’s 
investigation has been closed for some years and there is 
no further evidence against the persons in question, the 
Federal Ministry of the Interior (BMI) and the BKA make 
reference to a residual suspicion that continues to justify 
the storage.
I have inspected the public prosecutor’s investigation 
files on both of these critical cases and have found 
my opinion confirmed. The BKA had not reviewed the 
cases, but had relied on partly outdated and inaccurate 
33rd Activity Report 2024 104


<!-- page 105 -->
information provided by the state police authorities. In 
view of the considerable extent of the encroachment on 
rights, I see significant potential for improvement on 
the part of the BKA. The BKA has now erased one of the 
cases.
The BKA stores various investigation proceedings in 
the PMK-links-S system on the basis of Section 483 of 
the Code of Criminal Procedure (StPO). I only reviewed 
a random sample here. I had to object to the storage of 
one so-called contact person. The associated investiga-
tion proceedings have been closed for some time and I 
no longer consider it necessary to store the data of the 
person concerned. Here, too, the BMI and BKA take a 
different view.
I have also reviewed data retention in the context of 
persons categorised by the police as so-called “threats” 
and “persons of interest” in the area of extremist left-
wing criminality. Classification and categorisation is 
carried out exclusively by the state criminal investigation 
departments. At the time of the inspection, 84 people 
were affected, ten of whom are recognised as “threats”. I 
have been able to check all the data stored about persons 
classified as “threats”. In addition, I have examined 14 
further sets of data stored about “persons of interest”, 
defendants and convicted persons. I was unable to iden-
tify any data protection problems.
8.1.13 Inspection of covert measures of the 
BKA
I carry out a mandatory inspection of so-called covert 
measures of the Federal Criminal Police Office (BKA) 
every two years. This inspection serves in particular 
to compensate for the fact that the persons concerned 
usually only become aware of such measures at a late 
stage, if ever.
The mandatory inspection left a very positive impression 
overall. The subject of the inspection was counter-terro-
rism measures carried out under the fifth section of the 
Federal Criminal Police Office Act (BKAG), under Section 
34 BKAG for self-protection during law enforcement 
measures and under Section 64 BKAG for protection 
purposes.
No breaches of data protection law were identified. The 
audit covered a total of seven proceedings in which 
covert measures were carried out. It was possible to 
review all orders. I was also able to check the complete 
documentation. The BKA had recorded all the measures 
taken comprehensively and well. The necessary notifica-
tions of persons affected and their documentation were

also reviewed and these measures have been carried out 
with due care.
8.2 General advice and inspection 
visits
8.2.1 Sharing experiences with the bDSBs of 
the statutory health insurance funds
Discussion of current data protection issues with 
official data protection officers (bDSBs) of the statutory 
health and long-term care insurance funds.
In June 2024, official data protection officers (bDSBs) 
from the statutory health and long-term care insuran-
ce funds met with my staff in Bonn to discuss current 
issues relevant to data protection law in statutory health 
insurance. Representatives of the Federal Social Security 
Office also took part in the event.  
I reported on this in a circular dated 17 July 2024.166
The focus was on the issues arising from Art. 15, 33 and 
34 GDPR in the daily practice of health and long-term 
care insurance funds, along with current legislative 
proposals and their implementation. At the same time, 
some challenging technical issues such as the integra-
tion of video content on public sector websites, criteria 
for the use of sovereign clouds, Microsoft applications 
and the challenges and solutions in the use of artificial 
intelligence were the subject of this meeting. The pro-
fessional and personal interaction with the bDSBs of the 
various health and long-term care insurance funds was 
informative and stimulating for everyone involved. A 
repeat of an event of this sort is planned for 2025.
8.2.2 Advice and inspection visits in respect of 
statutory health and long-term care insurance 
funds
I draw positive conclusions from my advice and in-
spection visits to the statutory health and long-term 
care insurance funds in my area of responsibility du-
ring the reporting period. The health insurance funds 
are aware that they must ensure a higher level of data 
protection.
In the reporting year, I carried out four on-site inspec-
tions, one advisory visit and twelve written questionnai-
re inspections in relation to the statutory health and 
long-term care insurance funds in my area of responsi-
bility.
33rd Activity Report 2024105


<!-- page 106 -->
The on-site inspections at the health insurance funds 
focused on sickness benefit and rehabilitation discharge 
management, handling of digital identities and redesign 
of the security authentication of an online customer por-
tal and an app. In addition, a key inspection was carried 
out in the area of statutory long-term care insurance.
The advisory and inspection visits give my colleagues on 
site important insights into the work processes and the 
associated data protection organisation of the supervised 
bodies.
This always includes confidential discussions with the 
data protection officers. The focus here is on giving 
advice.
In addition, written questionnaires were used to check 
the organisation of data protection at the health and 
long-term care insurance funds. This format comple-
ments the on-site inspections and thus ensures a more 
detailed inspection. Cross-comparisons are possible, as 
several supervised sites can be considered in parallel for 
specific inspection topics.
Overall, my impression was positive. The statutory he-
alth and long-term care insurance funds have increased 
their data protection requirements because health data 
needing special protection is processed and, in addition 
to the GDPR, regulations under social legislation such 
as social confidentiality must also be observed. These 
challenges are essentially being met - also in view of the 
advances in digitalisation.
8.2.3 Advice and inspection practice of tele -
communications providers
Many companies in the telecommunications industry 
readily take up my advice and utilise the consulting 
services I offer. Other providers are less cooperative.
A broad spectrum of reactions is evident in the super-
vision and counselling of telecommunications provi-
ders. Many providers accept my advisory services and 
take data protection into account at an early stage. Any 
deficiencies that are identified are rectified following an 
informal notification. My staff regularly provide practi-
cal tips and advice on possible solutions.
However, there are also some providers which are 
already aware that areas of their data processing are not 
legally compliant. The necessary adjustments are always 
postponed until there is an inspection. Then, suddenly, 
they are under time pressure. There are few companies 
that refuse to work with me. In these negative cases, I 
am compelled to make use of my remedial powers under 
the GDPR. If instructions are not followed, I impose 
167 Website of the GovTech Campus: https://govtechcampus.de/
fines, which are then collected by the enforcement 
authorities. However, this is only the last resort and is 
fortunately rarely necessary.
One telecommunications provider stood out as a particu-
lar problem in 2024. The company sent out letters about 
contracts that had supposedly been concluded, followed 
by reminders. I received numerous complaints about 
this. Data subjects often could not recall having con-
cluded a contract at all. If data subjects then exercised 
their right to access information in accordance with Art. 
15 GDPR, they did not receive the information to which 
they were entitled regarding the processing of their 
personal data. The right of access is intended to give data 
subjects an overview and thus a degree of control over 
the specific processing of their personal data. They are 
entitled to a summary and a copy of the personal data 
(e.g. a copy of the contract concluded). I am committed 
to using all legal means at my disposal to help data sub-
jects to exercise their right to access information.
Another provider also persistently refuses to fulfil the 
data subject rights of a customer. This provider had to 
pay a four-figure fine. It could have avoided this by fulfil-
ling the custo mer’s right to access information.
8.2.4 The Cloud-Reallabor
The public sector is increasingly considering swit-
ching to the cloud. Moving certain processes to the 
cloud can be more economical than running them on 
their own infrastructure. However, it is also beco-
ming apparent that many infrastructure providers are 
increasingly moving their solutions completely to the 
cloud so that in-house operation is no longer possib-
le. For public authorities in particular, moving to the 
cloud is often associated with challenges. These can be 
of a regulatory nature, but strategic issues are also re-
levant here: public administration must be able to act 
independently in the long term and must not become 
dependent on individual providers.
The GovTech Campus offers representatives from public 
administration, science, business and the social sector 
a platform to modernise the digitalisation of public 
administration in the context of various projects.167 At 
the beginning of 2024, the “Cloud-Reallabor” was laun-
ched there to develop blueprints for secure use of cloud 
solutions. The project members are from public admi-
nistration, in particular social services, various cloud 
providers from the USA, Germany and other European 
countries, and the German Federal Office for Informa-
tion Security (BSI). I support the project in an advisory 
capacity.
33rd Activity Report 2024 106


<!-- page 107 -->
Firstly, the members from public administration check 
the cloud providers’ services for usability. In addition, 
certain application scenarios are being tested and ana-
lysed in practice by the project participants in various 
proofs of concept (PoCs). In two PoCs, for example, two 
cloud providers are working together with a representa-
tive of the public administration to test secure switching 
of applications between clouds. Another PoC is trialling 
the added value of using confidential computing, a tech-
nology that enables data to be processed in a specially 
protected environment, on public cloud platforms.
My authority advises the Cloud-Reallabor on data protec-
tion considerations. Data protection must be considered 
from the outset in accordance with the principles of 
data protection by design and data protection by default. 
Initial interim results were published at the end of 
2024.168 A further report is planned for the end of 2025, 
and continuation of the project beyond that date will be 
considered in the course of the year.
8.2.5 Data protection inspection at Überlingen 
tax office
Personal data of all citizens is affected by the tax 
administration system. This begins immediately after 
birth with the allocation of a tax identification number 
and usually continues throughout life, in the form, 
for example, of annual income tax assessments by the 
relevant tax office. The tax administration system is 
therefore a focus of my supervisory activities, which 
also include regularly advising and inspecting local tax 
offices.
During the reporting period, the Überlingen tax office 
was advised and inspected by my office. In addition 
to general data protection advice and monitoring, the 
focus was on electronic file management in the collec-
tion procedure. In view of the largely positive results, 
only general information and recommendations were 
required.169 One recommendation, for example, was 
that the awareness of tax office staff of data protection 
legislation should be raised not only on an ad hoc basis, 
but if possible at regular intervals too. Centralised do-
cumentation of discretionary decisions in the collection 
procedure was also suggested to make tracking easier 
where necessary.
I found some shortcomings in terms of data protection 
law in relation to the official data protection officers 
(bDSBs) responsible for the Überlingen tax office. Art. 
38(2) GDPR stipulates that the controller must support 
the data protection officer in the fulfilment of their role 
168 Publications of the Real-World Cloud Lab: https://reallabor.cloud/insights/
169 Report on the inspection of Überlingen tax office, available at: 
https://www.bfdi.bund.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/DE/DokumenteBfDI/ Kontrollberichte/Kontrolle-Finanzamt-Ueberlingen-2024.html
by providing the resources necessary to do so. In order 
to make use of the many rights and powers of the GDPR, 
bDSBs need sufficient time to perform their tasks. I am 
generally of the opinion that, as far as public bodies are 
concerned, bDSBs should be exempted entirely from 
other duties if the number of employees exceeds 500, 
so that they are able to complete their duties properly. 
I consider this staffing level to be necessary simply 
because of the mandatory tasks involved in ensuring 
protection of personal data and acting as a contact point 
for my office.
Unfortunately, the personnel resources made available 
to the bDSB of the Überlingen tax office proved to be 
inadequate. With around 100 employees, the Überlin-
gen tax office performs a number of central tasks in the 
tax system. Accordingly, the bDSB also has a variety of 
advisory, monitoring and cooperation duties to fulfil. It 
should be emphasised here that data protection officers 
should certainly not restrict themselves to responding 
to requests from their organisation or to complaints and 
submissions from data subjects. A bDSB is required who 
will act on their own initiative, actively contribute to data 
protection-related planning and monitor compliance 
with data protection regulations without being asked to 
do so.
I also have to take into account the fact that the bDSB of 
the Überlingen tax office is also a joint bDSB for several 
other authorities in accordance with Art. 37(3) GDPR. 
For example, the bDSB of the Überlingen tax office is 
responsible for 72 agencies with a total of over 17,000 
employees. In their area of work, they can therefore 
only draw on a total of around 0.6 full-time equivalents 
(FTEs) to fulfil their many duties. They themselves can 
commit only around 0.3 FTEs. Separate information 
about the time devoted to Überlingen tax office has not 
been provided. However, this is likely to amount to only 
a small fraction of the time available and is obviously 
insufficient.
Following my advice and inspection, the Baden-Würt-
temberg tax authorities decided not to restrict their 
review of the staffing situation I identified to Überlingen 
tax office, but rather to review and adequately resource 
the area of joint bDSBs as a whole. I will support this 
process attentively and constructively. Ideally, it will be 
possible to draw general conclusions from this about the 
staffing levels required for joint bDSBs in the financial 
administration system as a whole, which can be applied 
to other federal states. Corresponding developments can 
also be seen in other administrative areas. The results 
33rd Activity Report 2024107


<!-- page 108 -->
may therefore provide valuable general information 
about appropriate staffing of bDSB offices, thereby effec-
tively strengthening the enforcement of data protection 
regulations overall.
8.2.6 Inspection of the „ANSWER“ IT system of 
the Federal Central Tax Office (BZSt)
Artificial intelligence (AI) systems are already part of 
everyday working life for many controllers. They are at 
the centre of my inspection of the ANSWER system of 
the Federal Central Tax Office (BZSt).
The tax administration authorities regularly play a 
pioneering and key role in digitalisation of the public ad-
ministration. Steps are being taken in several areas here, 
including in the field of artificial intelligence.
The BZSt’s “Analysis and Evaluation” (ANSWER) sys-
tem was the subject of an advisory and inspection visit 
by my office. Particular attention was paid to the AI 
components used. As a central IT system, ANSWER is 
intended to support specialist departments with pro-
cessing procedures as required. The ANSWER system is 
currently mainly used to carry out text analyses for the 
department DAC-6170 which is responsible for automatic 
exchange of tax structures. The expansion of the ANS-
WER system is making steady progress and integration 
of another department is already being planned.
It is pleasing to note that data protection is a high priori-
ty in the ANSWER process. The department involves the 
local official data protection officer (bDSB) frequently 
and at an early stage. They in turn maintain a regular 
dialogue with my office.
However, there is still room for improvement in some 
areas. For example, I recommended refining the rights 
and roles policy in relation to individual, high-ranking 
roles in order to avoid general access to the data availa-
ble or accessible in ANSWER from other departments. 
The fact that AI methods are finding their way into the 
working environment of the bDSBs is a new develop-
ment. I have therefore also recommended that the BZSt 
supplement the resources of the bDSB with training 
specifically related to AI.
My office will continue to provide constructive support 
for the ANSWER process in the future.

8.2.7 Inspection of the German Embassy in 
London
Operation and use of the international portal in the 
passport system was the focus of my advice and in-
spection visit to the German embassy in London.
The Federal Foreign Office (AA) is implementing the 
requirements of the Online Access Act through the inter-
national portal. In future, the portal will make it possible 
to apply for all services at our foreign missions. The 
various functions are being rolled out in stages. In this 
context, applications for adult passports were piloted at 
the embassy in London on 1 December 2023.
During the visit by my colleagues on 15 and 16 May 2024, 
the embassy staff explained to us that use of the interna-
tional portal is voluntary and happens before an actual 
passport application is made. Applicants can submit 
the documents in advance via the portal so that the 
diplomatic mission abroad can check and confirm the 
completeness of the documents before the application is 
submitted.
At the personal appointment at which the official ap-
plication is submitted, the processors can transfer the 
details, including the documents justifying the appli-
cation, to the RK- Pass 171 processing system for actual 
processing.
This procedure complies with the Passport Act (PassG), 
as the application itself is not made via the international 
portal. The data processing as such is based on Art. 6(1) 
point (e) GDPR in conjunction with Section 19(2) PassG. 
There are therefore no data protection concerns about 
implementation of the system.
The AA regularly involves me in its planning for further 
developments of the system, so I will also continue to 
support the expansion of the international portal from a 
data protection perspective.
During my visit, I found only minor data protection 
deficiencies. These concerned, for example, a general 
regulation on the storage of various lists and the use 
of standard forms and notices. These issues have since 
been rectified.
33rd Activity Report 2024 108


<!-- page 109 -->
8.2.8 Data protection in proceedings to prohi -
bit associations
Processing of personal data in proceedings to prohibit 
associations was the particular focus of an advice and 
inspection visit to the Federal Ministry of the Interior 
(BMI).
From 9 to 11 April 2024, my employees completed an 
advice and inspection visit to the BMI. The inspection 
related to the processing of personal data in proceedings 
to ban associations and the general organisation of data 
protection at the BMI. Neither area revealed any signifi-
cant data protection deficiencies.
The BMI is home to an extremely wide range of areas 
such as Public Security and the Federal Police, Digital 
Administration, Sport and Home Affairs, to name but a 
few. This regularly results in a need for extensive data 
protection advice and coordination with my office. In 
addition to the team of official data protection officers, 
the BMI has also set up an operational data protection 
division for this purpose. In the context of my inspec-
tion, I welcomed the establishment of this division at 
a central location and at the same time suggested that 
it be strengthened in terms of personnel. The BMI has 
already followed this suggestion and taken initial steps 
accordingly.
In Article 9(2) of the German Basic Law (Grundgesetz - 
GG), our constitution prohibits associations the purposes 
or activities of which violate criminal law, are intended 
to disrupt constitutional order or run counter to the idea 
of international understanding. Bans are determined in 
accordance with Section 3 of the Association Act. The 
opportunity to establish the prohibition of an unconsti-
tutional association in a constitutional procedure is an 
expression of the protective democracy enshrined in the 
Basic Law. In order to fulfil its role as the prohibition 
authority for associations the organisation or activities 
of which extend beyond the territory of one federal state, 
the BMI can call on the assistance of security agencies at 
federal and state level.
The implementation of proceedings to ban associations 
requires processing of personal data in various ways. 
In particular, my staff were provided with informati-
on about the transfer of data between the authorities 
involved, file management and secure storage of do-
cuments, and gave advice in these areas. In addition, I 
was provided with an example of the potential handling 
of personal data of third parties in proceedings to ban 
an association. I did not identify any data protection 
deficiencies.
8.2.9 Advice and inspection visit in respect of 
the Federal Statistical Office (StBA)
In view of the changing role of the Federal Statistical 
Office (StBA), the implementation of the compartmen-
talisation and separation principle represents a new 
challenge.
The core task of the StBA is the production of statistics. 
In recent years, the establishment and maintenance of 
administrative registers has also been added as a new 
area of activity. Since July 2021, the StBA ’s Administrative 
Data Information Platform (VIP) has provided a compre-
hensive overview of the data held by German administ-
rative bodies. Since October 2022, the StBA has been the 
register authority for the Security Personnel Register 
(BW A), in which all parties operating in the security 
industry register their companies and security person-
nel. The basic register for companies is currently being 
set up as an additional administrative register under the 
responsibility of the StBA.
The expansion of the Federal Statistical Office into an 
administrative register authority means that, from a data 
protection point of view, compliance with the principle 
of compartmentalisation and separation is coming into 
focus. My colleagues therefore monitored the implemen-
tation of this requirement at the Federal Statistical Office 
in Wiesbaden and Bonn during the reporting year.
33rd Activity Report 2024109


<!-- page 110 -->
 
 
Principle of compartmentalisation  
and separation 
 
My colleague s are developing tools for analysing and 
The principle of compartmentalisation and separati -
on was estab lished by the Federal Constitutional 
Court in the so called census ruling* as a constitutio -
nal principle in connection with the performance of 
official statistical tasks. Compliance with this is a 
necessary condition for the legality of any encroach -
ment on the fundamental right to informational self 
determination. 
 
“Compartmentalisation and separation” in this sense 
refers to the spatial, human resource and organisati -
onal separation of statistical operations from non 
statistical bodies. Accordingly, the pro cessing of 
individual statistical data records must take place in 
a separate division from other administrative 
structures. The principle of compartmentalisation 
and separation protects data processing for the 
purposes and at the same time strengthens the trust 
of persons obliged to provide information in official 
statistics. 
 
   
* Judgment of the BVerfG of 15 December  
The StBA has analysed the significance of the compart-
mentalisation and separation principle and its concrete 
effects on the performance of the authority’s various 
duties in detail.
The concrete implementation of the separation of the 
new (administrative) areas of responsibility of the office 
from the established statistical tasks requires correspon-
ding measures in organisational, spatial, human resour-
ce and technical terms. Compartmentalised performan-
ce of tasks is already common practice at the StBA. The 
impressions gained from the inspection show that there 
is potential for improvement in physical separation at 
the headquarters in Wiesbaden and that the organisati-
onal structure can also be optimised. I will continue to 
advise the StBA on this.
The evaluation of the inspection results had not been 
completed in full at the time of the editorial deadline for 
this report.

9.1 Personnel development 2024
In the reporting year, I continued to focus on recru-
iting additional personnel. This has enabled me to 
continue to grow my office.
In 2024, my department was also represented at vari-
ous careers fairs, such as the faculty careers days in 
Bonn and Cologne, the departmental day at the Federal 
University of Applied Sciences in Brühl and the company 
day at Bonn-Rhein-Sieg University of Applied Sciences. 
The exhibition stand was completely renovated and 
modernised to highlight the public welfare-orientated 
and varied activities and career opportunities within my 
authority even more clearly.
Another measure to expand the range of job applicants 
was the revision of the “Working for the BfDI” section of 
the website. In addition to specific examples in the form 
of job profiles, which provide an insight into everyday 
working life, and FAQs on the application process, 
potential applicants can also find out about the many 
benefits of working for my office. I would like to thank 
the colleagues who took part in the in-house photo 
shoot for their openness and commitment to the entire 
organisation.
In the 2024 reporting year, I conducted 32 recruitment 
processes (both individual and collective recruitment 
processes). I received a total of 661 applications. Of the-
se, 296 applicants were invited to interview.
Under the budget, I was allocated a total of 417.9 posts in 
the 2024 reporting year. These comprise 397.4 perma-
nent posts for civil servants and 20.5 posts for pay-scale 
employees.
In addition to a total of 12 planned and unplanned 
staff departures, I was also pleased to welcome 54 new 
colleagues in 2024. I expect 13 more new additions from 
application processes that have already been completed 
in the coming weeks. As of 31 December 2024, my office 
therefore had a total of 369 employees.
9.2 Press and public relations 
work
Communication with citizens is an important part of 
my legal mandate. The members of my press and pu-
blic relations team are very active in various ways and 
on numerous channels. It is important to me that my 
agency not only provides information, but also opens 
up opportunities for dialogue.
Press work
In the reporting year, my press office received a large 
number of enquiries relating to the change in the identi-
ty of the Federal Commissioner for Data Protection and 
Freedom of Information.
A large number of enquiries were also about content-re-
lated issues. Overall, enquiries about the healthcare sec-
tor stood out, which is clearly related to the introduction 
of electronic patient records for all people with statutory 
health insurance from
January 2025. At the same time, many media professio-
nals are also concerned with the data protection aspects 
of the digitalisation of healthcare. These relate to both 
technical solutions and practical issues, such as asser-
ting the rights of data subjects.
Another focus of the enquiries was the topic of artificial 
intelligence (AI). The wider distribution and more com-
mon use of the technology have certainly contributed to 
this. Meanwhile, a number of existing procedures and 
tools have now also been designated as AI by the media 
or the bodies using them and thus once again became 
the centre of attention.
Last but not least, my press office always receives many 
enquiries when the media and social media themselves 
become an issue. For example, there were many enqui-
ries about the use of the TikTok platform by the Federal 
Chancellor and about the European Data Protection 
33rd Activity Report 2024111


<!-- page 112 -->
Board’s deliberations over subscription models for social 
media providers.
 
 
“Consent or pay” and  
“pay or okay” models  
 
“Consent or pay” and “pay or okay” models canbe 
found on various websites, such as those ofmedia c 
ompanies and social media providers. Inthese 
models, users often only have two optionsfor 
accessing website content: they either pay acertain 
amount of money (“pay”) or they consentto their 
data being used for profiling andpersonalised 
advertising (“consent” or “okay”).As a rule, this 
selection is made via a windowsimilar to a cookie 
banner.Different versions of such models have 
emerged inpractice. For example, the payment 
option can bedesigned as a one off payment (e.g. 
access to anarticle in a n online newspaper) or as a 
regularsubscription (e.g. use of a social network for 
amonthly or annual payment). In addition, the 
choicebetween “consent” or “pay” often only 
coversaccess to the basic website or the basic 
service. Anadditional payment is often required to 
accessadditional content on the website, such as 
special articels, sections or services.  
My agency issued 16 press releases during the reporting 
period and was invited to attend the federal press confe-
rence twice. It also wrote 9 guest articles and essays for 
various media. My press office answered 302 enquiries 
by email and 269 by telephone.
Website and social media
I have made some changes to my website to give the 
information a clearer focus on target groups. To this 
end, the structure has been adapted and new content 
provided in the areas of security, telecommunications 
and digital services.
I have also added new forms. These are clearer, easier 
to use and facilitate the work of my staff. I would like to 
continue to improve the features and user-friendliness 
of the forms.
In addition to the website as my primary online channel, 
I continue to run the Mastodon instance social.bund.
de. I would still very much welcome a takeover of this 
extremely successful project by the federal government, 
which also uses it (at least in part). Usage figures on 
Fediverse continue to rise steadily, albeit not quite as fast 
as in 2023. Nevertheless, I reach over 45,000 citizens via 
my channel social.bund.de/@bfdi.
I can see that there is a high level of interest in using the 
instance from the fact that I repeatedly have to deny ac-
cess to institutions because they do not fulfil our criteria. 
As the strength of Fediverse lies in its decentralised na-
ture, I would welcome seeing more instances in different 
areas, for example rescue and emergency services, state 
authorities and academic bodies.
The data protection forum (https://bfdi.bund.de/forum) 
also continues to enjoy great popularity among data pro-
tection experts. I will continue to operate both platforms 
within the scope of the financial resources available to 
me.
Visitor groups
My staff at the Berlin liaison office looked after groups of 
visitors comprising members of the German Bundestag. 
Four groups of up to 50 participants attended.
In 2024, we welcomed a delegation from the data protec-
tion supervisory authority of Sri Lanka to Bonn to share 
experiences.
Information materials
In 2024, the focus was on a general revision of existing 
information materials, particularly with regard to pos-
sible new information channels. A start has been made 
on reorganising the distribution channels. In view of the 
demand for specific information, new formats have been 
prepared that will be available in 2025.
As in previous years, interest in the BfDI Pixi books 
continues unabated. Almost half a million Pixi books 
were made available in the reporting year. Demand from 
primary and secondary schools in particular has increa-
sed. Teaching materials will therefore be available for 
the Pixi Knowledge books “What is data protection?” and 
“What is freedom of information?” in 2025.
9.3 Events organised by the BfDI
Public events continue to be an integral part of my 
office’s public relations work. In various formats, my 
colleagues and often I myself enter into dialogue with 
many different groups – from children on World Child-
ren’s Day to decision-makers in politics and society at 
our forums.
Festival of Democracy
On 25 May, my office took part in the Festival of De-
mocracy with its own stand. The grounds of the Villa 
Hammerschmidt in Bonn attracted many thousands of 
visitors despite the unfavourable weather. In addition to 
direct interaction with citizens, my staff also offered a
i
33rd Activity Report 2024 112


<!-- page 113 -->
practical demonstration of data protection laboratory 
tests and various activities for children.
World Children’s Day
As in 2023, I co-hosted World Children’s Day on 20 Sep-
tember 2024 in Bonn City Library. This year’s motto was 
“Into the future with children’s rights”. Once again, we 
organised morning and afternoon events. Two primary 
school classes from Bonn took great pleasure in success-
fully completing my data protection quiz and coming up 
with secure passwords.
I was impressed by the large number of visitors at the 
family event in the afternoon. The children were mainly 
interested in our mega puzzle, while their parents wan-
ted to know exactly what was going on and asked specific 
questions about data protection-related topics.
Events like these prove time and again to be an ideal way 
of embedding the issue of data protection into everyday 
life and thus into the awareness of children and families 
at an early stage. This is not only a part of my legal man-
date under Section 14(1) no. 2 BDSG and Art. 57(1) point 
(b) GDPR, but also an independent expression of my 
desire to ensure that my office serves society as a whole. 
Educating children about data protection and raising 
their awareness of the topic is extremely important in 
enabling them to use digital media and cope with the re-
sulting challenges in a secure and self-determined way.
Strategic Foresights
On 5 December, I organised a Strategic Foresight event 
in cooperation with the Weizenbaum Institute for the 
first time. The board of directors of the Weizenbaum 
Institute met with numerous academics in Berlin in this 
context. Strategic Foresight topics included democratic 
digitalisation strategies, access to health data and soci-
ally beneficial development and application of artificial 
intelligence.
Strategic Foresights are multi-phase processes that deal 
with possible future developments in certain subject 
areas. They serve to promote dialogue between internal 
and external experts. Our Strategic Foresights process is 
based on an academic concept and is organised as fol-
lows: (1) topic identification; (2) participatory process of 
identifying the problems; (3) internal reflection process; 
(4) data protection positioning and continuous evaluati-
on.
I will continue to expand the range of the Strategic Fore-
sights over the next few years, particularly in my focus 
on the areas of artificial intelligence, health and security.
Police symposium
On 12 September, my office organised a symposium 
on the work of the police. The day in my Berlin liaison 
office was based thematically around the question: 
“does automated data analysis and innovative AI police 
work entail the potential for discrimination?”. In short 
presentations and discussions, experts from research 
and practice analysed the current state of automatic data 
analysis in police work and criminal proceedings and 
highlighted possible developments. The speakers inclu-
ded: Prof. Christoph Krehl, former judge at the Federal 
Court of Justice, Markus Hartmann, Senior Public Prose-
cutor and Head of the North Rhine-Westphalia Cybercri-
me Centre, Gül Pinar, a lawyer specialising in criminal 
law and Alexander Poitz, Deputy Federal Chair of the 
Police Union. The first round of discussions focussed in 
particular on criminal prosecution. Does the use of new 
technologies lead to better administration of justice and 
are the new methods of investigation compatible with 
data protection law?
The afternoon panel began with presentations by Tobias 
Wiemann, Head of the Legal and Constitutional Affairs 
Division of the Federal Ministry of the Interior, Eric Töp-
fer, research associate at the German Institute for Hu-
man Rights and Prof. Michael Bäuerle, LL. M., professor 
at the Hessian University of Applied Sciences for Police 
and Administration and Deputy Director of the Hessian 
Centre for Responsible Digitalisation. The second round 
of discussions then focussed on automated data analysis 
and AI methods in police work. The possibilities and 
limitations of automated data analysis were discussed 
here.
You can find a recording of the event on my website.
Spring Policy Forum
In March 2024, we welcomed guests to an event at the 
new Berlin liaison office at Spittelmarkt for the first 
time. Attendees at the Spring Political Forum were not 
only met with a new event area, but could also enjoy an 
interesting evening on the topic of “Exclusively digital? - 
How far does the right to an analogue life go?”
Prof Reinhold Popp, futurologist at the Sigmund Freud 
Private University in Vienna, gave the opening keynote 
speech “Digital phobia - Where does our fear of change 
come from?“. He pointed out that fear of the future also 
arises from the conviction that digitalisation is destined 
to happen or that there is no alternative. It is up to us to 
decide for ourselves how we want to shape the future as 
a society and as individuals.
Carolin Kleinert (Berlin Startup Association spokesper-
son), Rena Tangens (Digitalcourage e. V .), Prof. Reinhold 
Popp and Prof. Ulrich Kelber then discussed the phen-
33rd Activity Report 2024113


<!-- page 114 -->
omenon of so-called digital coercion. The debate was 
chaired by freelance author and journalist Falk Steiner. 
According to Prof. Kelber, digitalisation is taking place in 
big steps. But digital solutions sometimes still need ana-
logue versions so that nobody is left behind or excluded. 
He pointed to inadequate technical access and a lack of 
data protection as problem areas.
Finally, Rena Tangens presented the “Digitalcourage” 
initiative, which is campaigning to enshrine the right to 
an analogue life in the German constitution.
You can find a recording of the event on my website.
Autumn Policy Forum
My Autumn Forum had the title “Data in the service of 
patients” and addressed one of the key areas for my term 
of office: the compatibility of data use and data protecti-
on in medicine. The focus was on the electronic patient 
file (ePA).
In his impressive keynote speech (“Digitalisation of the 
Austrian healthcare system using the example of the 
ELGA electronic health record – where do our neigh-
bours stand and what issues do they have?”), Dr Franz 
Leisch, who played a key role in the electronic health re-
cord in Austria, presented the discussions and develop-
ments in his country.
In a session chaired by Teresa Sickert, I then discussed 
how data use and data protection might be reconciled 
in the healthcare sector with Prof. Eva Winkler, Chair of 
the Central Ethics Commission of the German Medical 
Association, Dr Florian Hartge, Managing Director of 
gematik, Michaela Schröder, Member of the Executive 
Board of the Federation of German Consumer Organisa-
tions and Dr Leisch. There was agreement that compre-
hensive digitalisation can only succeed if the technical 
solutions are supported by legal expertise and citizens 
can have confidence in new digital solutions. Effective 
data protection plays a very important role here.
You can find a recording of these events on my websi-

9.4 Facts and figures on the repor -
ting year
The statistics also provide insights into my activities. 
It is clear that the workload of my office has increased 
recently .
Complaints and enquiries
A total of 8,670 complaints and enquiries were addressed 
to me in the reporting year. This shows a further increa-
se in the number of cases, which is essentially reflected 
in all of my departments.
There was a clear increase in the volume of both general 
enquiries and complaints under the GDPR and BDSG 
(see table). Only during the introduction of the GDPR in 
2018 did my office record a higher number of incoming 
cases. In addition to written and electronic enquiries 
and complaints, my staff advised people by telephone in 
5,221 cases.
Advice and inspection
As a supervisory authority, advisory and inspection 
activities are important areas of work for my office and 
sometimes rely heavily on personal contact with the 
controllers under my supervision. The number of advice 
and inspection meetings in the reporting year was on a 
par with the previous year. The instrument of written in-
spections, which was widely used during the pandemic, 
continues to be employed where it has proven expedient.
It is gratifying that my office is valued by the bodies 
under its supervision as an advisory contact. This is 
reflected in the high number of advisory and informati-
on visits. Specific problems and data protection-compli-
ant solutions are discussed at these meetings. Issues are 
often brought to my attention by the organisations under 
my supervision. My aim is to expand my information 
and advisory services further and to place data protecti-
on with a focus on solutions at the centre of my activi-
ties.

Formal involvement in proposed legislation
Under Section 21 of the Joint Rules of Procedure of the 
Federal Ministries (GGO), lead ministries must involve 
me at an early stage in the drafting of legislation insofar 
as it impinges on my areas of responsibility. The stati-
stics for the reporting year show an increased level of 
participation in formal legislation.
Instances of involvement under Section 21 GGO
This shows the customary higher volume at the end 
of a legislative period, which results in corresponding 
demands on my office.
My aim is to take part in drafting legislation even before 
the formal involvement of the lead departments by pro-
viding solution-based advice in a spirit of trust.
I was also involved in legislation by the German Bun-
destag. I was called in as an expert by committees in 9 
cases. At a national level, I was also asked to review file 
orders from security authorities in 90 cases.
At European level, I was involved in the drafting of 6 
regulations and one directive.
Committee meetings
Through my membership of organisations such as the 
Data Protection Conference (DSK), the European Data 
Protection Board (EDPB) and many other – often inter-
national – bodies, I am in contact with a wide variety of 
stakeholders in the field of data protection.
In the reporting year, my staff chaired a total of 259 mee-
tings as (co-)chairs/rapporteurs and also attended 713 
meetings as committee members. My office submitted 
10 draft decisions and resolutions to these bodies.
9.5 Development of Future Fore -
sight at the BfDI
New technologies are often only recognised as re-
levant to data protection when they are already in 
use. Timely recognition of data protection relevance 
therefore requires (systematic) tracking of technologi-
cal developments, a requirement that is being met in 
successive phases by the “Future Foresight” unit that 
was established in my office in March 2024. In the first 
phase of Future Foresight, my staff identified the 6G 
mobile communications standard, which is due to be 
introduced in 2030, as one of the technological trends 
with a potentially significant impact on data protecti-
on and presented it at the 74th meeting of the Berlin 
Group in November 2024, as a result of which 6G has 
been included as the topic for a future working paper 
of the Berlin Group.
Technological data protection faces the fundamental 
problem that new technologies are often only recogni-
sed as relevant to data protection after they have been 
implemented. This is partly due to the fact that they 
often follow a so-called “hype cycle”. According to the 
hype cycle, technological developments typically lead to 
exaggerated expectations at the beginning and reach an 
initial climax, followed by disappointments, before rea-
listic applications are found, all of which makes prompt 
and well-founded evaluation from a data protection 
perspective more difficult. It is therefore expedient to 
ensure technological data protection through legislation 
that is as technology-neutral as possible, while providing 
scope for technology-specific assessments of key future 
developments that are relevant to data protection. This is 
taken into account in existing legislation at national and 
European level insofar as Recital 15 of the GDPR justifies 
the aforementioned technological neutrality and moni-
33rd Activity Report 2024 116


<!-- page 117 -->
toring of relevant developments in information and com-
munication technology is defined as a role of the BfDI 
in the BDSG.173 This area of my office’s responsibilities is 
covered, among other things, by the “Future Foresight” 
unit established in March 2024, which takes an approach 
involving five successive phases with a forecast horizon 
of five years in combination with annual updates.
In the first phase (trend identification), the aim is to gain 
a data protection-related understanding of the conti-
nuously developing technological landscape. The aim of 
phase 1 is to identify new developments in information 
and communication technology (so-called technology 
trends) with a potentially significant impact on data 
protection.
In phase 2 (scenario development), potential (data 
protection-related) scenarios are developed for the 
technological trends identified in phase 1. These are 
hypothetical future developments that highlight as many 
alternatives as possible in a comprehensible way.
In phase 3 (impact assessment), an evaluation of potenti-
al consequences is carried out for some of the scenarios 
developed in phase 2 in order to estimate the resulting 
risks and opportunities, which are then evaluated in 
phase 4 (risk assessment).
In the final phase 5 (application), the risk assessment 
from phase 4 is used to derive suitable technical and or-
ganisational measures for potential controllers for “data 
protection by design” in accordance with Art. 25 GDPR.
A further objective based on the knowledge gained from 
implementation of phases 1-5 is to provide advice to the 
relevant federal institutions and bodies in accordance 
with Section 14(1) No. 3 BDSG in conjunction with Art. 
57(1) point (c) GDPR.
In the first iteration of Future Foresight, four technolo-
gy trends with a potentially significant impact on data 
protection were identified in phase 1. Three of these 
technology trends are currently being addressed in wor-
king papers by the Berlin Group (see Section 4), namely 
neurotechnology, extended reality and confidential 
computing. A further technology trend with significant 
data protection relevance was identified following my 
colleagues’ participation in the 83rd meeting of the DSK’s 
“Technical and Organisational Data Protection Issues” 
working group in September 2024. This is the 6G mobile 
communications standard, which is due to be intro-
duced in 2030 and which was presented in the context 
of the Future Foresight process at the 74th meeting of 
173 Section 14(1) no. 9 BDSG in conjunction with Art. 57(1) point (i) GDPR
174 Memorandum of Understanding of the Bonn Digital Cluster of 15 January 2024, available at: 
https://www.digitalclusterbonn.de/DCB/ MoU.pdf? blob=publicationFile&v=3
the Berlin Group in November 2024. As a result, 6G was 
included as a topic for a future working paper of the 
Berlin Group.
The relevance of data protection for the 6G mobile com-
munications standard arises from the fact that existing 
wireless communication is to be adapted so that it can 
also be used as wireless sensor technology (through 
radar functionality). Widespread use of this combina-
tion of wireless communication and wireless sensor 
technology (known as JCAS - Joint Communication and 
Sensing) means that, among other things, comprehensi-
ve environmental data can be collected, the processing 
of which can entail considerable data protection risks, 
especially with regard to Art. 7 GDPR (consent) and Art. 
12 GDPR (transparency).
The Future Foresight process is currently being used to 
work on scenario development and impact assessment 
for the four technology trends mentioned, to be followed 
by risk assessment and application.
9.6 A cluster of authorities for bet -
ter digitalisation
Six federal authorities in Bonn have joined forces to 
form the Bonn Digital Cluster in order to meet the 
challenges of digitalisation, which is becoming increa-
singly relevant in all areas, in a more effective way . In 
this context, they exchange views on current issues 
relating to administrative digitalisation and other digi-
tal topics. The aim is not only to ensure better coor-
dination of the increasingly overlapping supervisory 
responsibilities, but also to make the agencies’ own 
administrative digitalisation more efficient by sharing 
process experience.
At the initiative of the Federal Network Agency, the 
Federal Cartel Office and my office, these agencies, 
together with the Federal Financial Supervisory Autho-
rity, the Federal Office for Information Security and the 
Federal Office of Justice, established the Digital Cluster 
Bonn in the reporting year. To this end, the heads of the 
authorities signed a Memorandum of Understanding 
at the launch event on 15 January 2024,174 which sets 
out the common goals and basic framework for future 
cooperation.
The background to this was initially the need for better 
and more structured coordination of the new horizontal 
responsibilities for regulation and supervision resul-
33rd Activity Report 2024117


<!-- page 118 -->
ting from the European Digital Acts.175 The creation of 
communication channels and more extensive sharing 
of expertise should lead to even more coherent and 
legally secure application of the laws on digitalisation. 
This should not only benefit the agencies themselves, 
but above all those affected by the legislation, such as 
companies, public bodies and citizens.
To this end, the cluster organises itself into various 
working groups on relevant topics, which are set up by 
a steering committee with representatives from all the 
authorities. Suggestions for these working groups and 
the topics to be dealt with come directly from the cluster 
members’ specialist departments. However, it is not 
necessary for all agencies to participate in every working 
group. They can get involved according to their interests, 
needs and capacities.
In addition to the working groups relating to the Digital 
Acts – on the topic of interoperability, for example, or on 
questions of supervision in the area of the AI Regulation 
or the Digital Services Act – it also quickly became appa-
rent that the authorities also have a significant interest 
in sharing information on issues relating to internal ad-
ministrative digitalisation. In this area, the individual au-
thorities reflect the extremely varied progress of digitali-
sation. At the same time, however, the range of expertise 
required for digitalisation processes is available and can 
be brought together in the cluster. This creates a forum 
that generates synergies through transfer of knowledge 
and experience and identification of joint solutions from 
which all the cluster members can benefit. At the time of 
going to press, half of the eight existing working groups 
were therefore dealing with questions and topics relating 
to internal digitalisation, such as considerations around 
cloud strategies, opportunities for internal use of AI wit-
hin the authorities and solutions for efficient knowledge 
and change management.
9.7 AI workshop of the Digital Clus -
ter Bonn
Through the Digital Cluster Bonn, my office discusses 
issues relating to AI with the other digital authorities 
based in Bonn. My colleagues have presented a use 
case in the context of this initiative.
Inter-agency dialogue is of great importance to my 
office. In the context of the Digital Cluster Bonn, there is 
an opportunity to exchange information on digitalisation 
topics with other digital authorities in Bonn on a perma-
nent basis. In addition to my office, the Federal Financi-
al Supervisory Authority (BaFin), the Federal Office for 
175 31st AR No. 4.2 and 32nd AR No. 5.3
Information Security (BSI), the Federal Office of Justice 
(BfJ), the Federal Cartel Office (BKartA) and the Federal 
Network Agency (BNetzA) are members of this initiative.
Following the kick-off event on 15 January 2024, a com-
mittee was established to exchange ideas on AI topics. 
All of the authorities mentioned above are represented 
on this committee. The aim is to create and exploit syn-
ergies, share experiences and benefit from one another’s 
expertise.
The committee met three times during the reporting 
period. Two core areas have emerged from the meetings: 
on the one hand, all the participating authorities report 
on the use cases they have identified, i.e. areas in which 
they see a need or potential for AI applications. On the 
other hand, the participating authorities share informa-
tion about guidelines for the use of AI. My office has so 
far identified one use case for AI and presented it to the 
committee.
The use case we identified relates to the reports of data 
protection violations received by my office and their 
distribution to the relevant organisational unit. Firstly, 
it should be emphasised that there are currently no 
concrete plans to implement this use case in my office; 
it is just a preliminary consideration of the issue. The 
idea we developed envisages a program that contains AI 
components – where necessary – and is used to pro-
cess incoming reports of data protection violations. In 
particular, the content of the report is analysed. Further-
more, the program identifies who is responsible so that 
the report can be assigned to the relevant organisational 
unit. The report is always submitted to the relevant or-
ganisational unit to be processed, so that every report of 
a data protection violation will still be dealt with by my 
staff, even if this use case is implemented. A prediction 
of the severity of the data protection violation by the pro-
gram should assist with the assessment of its urgency. 
Colleagues would be able to provide feedback for conti-
nuous improvement of the programme in order to adapt 
it better to the application context through so-called 
reinforcement learning or to make other adjustments.
33rd Activity Report 2024 118


<!-- page 119 -->
The Single Contact Point (ZASt), which is based in my 
office, supports the federal and state supervisory au-
thorities in their cooperation on EU matters. The ZASt 
acts as a bridge between German and European data 
protection supervision to promote good pan-European 
cooperation.
Head of the „Statistics“ sub-working group
Data protection has been the subject of particular politi-
cal attention since the introduction of the GDPR. In ac-
cordance with Art. 97 GDPR, the European Commission 
is obliged to report regularly to the European Parliament 
and the Council on the application and effectiveness of 
the GDPR. In preparation for these reports, the Europe-
an Commission requests a large number of key perfor-
mance indicators and information from the data protec-
tion supervisory authorities. The European Parliament’s 
Committee on Civil Liberties, Justice and Home Affairs 
(LIBE) has also already approached the data protection 
supervisory authorities with a comprehensive catalogue 
of questions. The ZASt was responsible for coordinating 
the responses of the German data protection supervisory 
authorities and providing consolidated information, as 
it has gained considerable experience in compiling and 
processing statistics in this way.
At the initiative and under the leadership of the ZASt, a 
“Statistics” sub-working group was established within 
the Organisation & Structure working group of the Con-
ference of Independent Data Protection Authorities of 
the Federal Government and the Länder (DSK), which is 
working on the concept of a joint national data frame-
work. This should put the data protection supervisory 
authorities in a better position
to account for their work to the European Commission, 
European Parliament and national parliaments. The 
data obtained in this standardised way can then be used 
by the authorities as an internal management tool. 
Furthermore, this data is intended to serve as a basis for 
resource requests to the relevant budgetary authorities. 
176 33rd AR No. 11.1
At the same time, a corresponding process is taking 
place at EDPB level under the leadership of the Enforce-
ment Expert Subgroup of the EDPB. In its work, the ZASt 
endeavours to ensure that work on the data frameworks 
is developed as consistently as possible at national and 
European level.
Contributions to the opinion of the EDPB on the draft 
regulation laying down additional procedural rules for 
the enforcement of the GDPR
As last year, the ZASt was involved in the reporting year 
in contributing to the EDPB’s position on the proposals 
of the European Parliament and the Council of the 
European Union for a European procedural regulation 
to improve the enforcement of the GDPR. The ZASt had 
already contributed in detail to the opinion of the DSK 
and the EDPB on the European Commission‘s first draft 
regulation last year.176 The ZASt has a particular per-
spective on this legislative proposal at European level 
in terms of German data protection supervision, with 
a focus primarily on responsibilities, procedures and 
deadlines and ongoing consideration of the adjustments 
to national procedures and working methods that would 
become necessary or expedient as a result of the requi-
rements of the procedural regulation.
As soon as a final regulation is adopted in the trialogue 
of the European legislature, the ZASt sees its role as 
submitting proposals for suitable adjustments to existing 
processes and coordination mechanisms to the DSK‘s Or-
ganisation & Structure working group. The aim here will 
be to adapt the European process steps of cross-border 
cooperation, which are likely to become more complex, 
in such a way that the German data protection supervi-
sion bodies can continue to participate in the European 
cooperation and coherence process in a coordinated 
manner, with a high level of quality and in compliance 
with the new European deadlines.
